# DRP-W Pre-writes — Civil Litigation

Paper `drp-w`, DRP written, Tue 1 Dec 2026, 2:30–4:30pm. Subject 0 Civil Litigation.
(Written Advocacy is to have its own Reference Pack; nothing in this file depends on it.)

**How to use in the exam.** Each Topic (`0.I`, `0.II` …) opens with a **Cheatsheet**: bold
questions a set of facts makes you ask, running from broad to specific, with short answers
underneath. Each answer line ends with a link such as `0-12`, which jumps to the Pre-write
`drp-w-0-12` and prints its page. If you know a keyword (an Order and rule, a case name, a
form number, a phrase like "substituted service"), Ctrl-F it: every Cheatsheet line, title
and Keywords line is text. Orders and rules are written `O 9 r 16(1)(b)`; search `9 r 16`
if the full form fails.

**Pre-writes** are I-CRUPAC paragraphs to adapt and retype. The bold part labels (**I**,
**C**, **R**, **P**, **A**, **C**) are for finding your place only — don't type them. Swap
the square-bracket placeholders for the facts. The **Rule** part is short enough to retype;
the **Rule in full** box under it quotes the rule's own words, limb by limb, for checking
and for picking the limbs the facts engage. The **Application** part is a pattern (which
facts trigger the rule and how to run it), not a worked answer. Cases are cited by name with
the paragraph the syllabus gives.

The *Source note* under each Pre-write is for checking only and is never retyped. Source
aliases (SYL, L1–L6, ROC, SCPD, SCJA, Cyril-Exam, PYP21–25 …) are defined in `outline.md`;
SYL is also cited by syllabus paragraph `[n]`. **Versions:** every Order and rule, PD
paragraph and section cited was checked on 8 Nov 2026 against the current texts (Rules of
Court on SSO as at 1 Apr 2026; Supreme Court and State Courts PDs as last updated 15 Jul
2026; SCJA on SSO as at 1 Jul 2024; other Acts current on SSO). Page cites such as `ROC p86`
still point to the library copies in `authorities/`, which predate later amendments; where
the current text differs, the box quotes the current text and says so. There is no "law as
at" cut-off in the Course Handbook or the syllabus. Where a source disagrees with the
course materials, the Source note says so in a `Conflict:` sentence; open points are
flagged for verification.

**Opening line for any civil procedure answer:** These proceedings, commenced after
1 April 2022, are governed by the Rules of Court 2021 (O 1 r 2(3)(a) ROC 2021), which are to
be given a purposive interpretation to achieve the Ideals of fair access to justice,
expeditious proceedings, cost-effective work proportionate to the claim, efficient use of
court resources and fair and practical results (O 3 r 1(1)–(2)).
*Source note: ROC p34 (O 1 r 2(3)), p53 (O 3 r 1); SYL p1–2 [9].*

**Answer shape for a civil procedure question:** (1) which court and which mode (originating
claim or originating application), and where the case is in the timeline; (2) the step or
application the client needs, and whether it goes in the SAPT or needs the Court's approval
first (O 9 r 9(7)–(8)); (3) the Order and rule, with its limbs, the deadline counted from
the facts (O 3 r 3) and the Practice Direction paragraph and Form; (4) the cases on how the
Court exercises its discretion; (5) apply each limb to the facts, flagging what more you
need to know; (6) conclude, and give the next procedural step: what to file, by when, with
what supporting affidavit, and the costs risk.
*Source note: SYL p1 (Learning Objectives and Competencies are in LP p1); model answers in
PYP22-Xav Q1–Q6 follow the same rule → deadline → test → conclusion pattern; Cyril-Exam.*

**Which rule?** Client wants to sue: which court 0-1, limitation 0-6, offer of amicable
resolution 0-7, forum or arbitration clause 0-8, documents needed first 0-9, OC or OA 0-10.
Starting: originating claim 0-11, validity and renewal 0-12, originating application 0-13.
Serving: personal 0-14, substituted 0-15, ordinary or dispensed 0-16, deadline to serve
0-17, out of Singapore 0-18 to 0-19. Defendant served: statement of claim 0-20, notice of
intention 0-21, defence 0-22, default judgment 0-23, challenge jurisdiction 0-24 and 0-30,
counterclaim 0-25, reply 0-26, OA affidavits 0-27. Case management: case conference 0-28
to 0-29, AB4D 0-31, SAPT 0-32, approval to apply outside the SAPT 0-33. Pre-trial
applications: security for costs 0-34 to 0-35, particulars 0-36, amendment 0-37 to 0-39,
strike out 0-40 to 0-41, summary judgment 0-42 to 0-43, question of law 0-44. Documents:
0-45 to 0-50 (pre-action or non-party 0-9). Witnesses and trial: AEIC 0-51, expert 0-52,
order to attend 0-53, absent witness or video link 0-54, absent party 0-55. Interim relief:
0-56 to 0-61 (Mareva 0-59, search order 0-60). After judgment: further arguments 0-62,
appeal track and time 0-63, which appellate court 0-64, permission 0-65, security for the
appeal 0-66, appeal from a District Court or Magistrate's Court 0-84, enforcement 0-67 to 0-69, stay 0-70. Costs: 0-71 to 0-75 (offers 0-75). Background
only (not examinable, SYL p1): SICC 0-76, pre-action protocols and party status (minor,
bankrupt, estate, representative action) 0-77, converting an OA 0-78, adding parties and
consolidation 0-79, judgment on admissions and third party notices 0-80, AEIC form and
objections, expert reports, independent witnesses 0-81, permission-to-appeal procedure 0-82,
non-party costs, Bullock and Sanderson orders, assessment of costs 0-83.

Contents: 0.I Courts 1–4, 76 · 0.II Pre-action 5–9, 77 · 0.III Commencing 10–13, 78 ·

pre-trial applications 32–39, 79 · 0.VIII Striking out and summary judgment 40–44, 80 ·

## 0.I Courts, jurisdiction and sources

- **Which court do I sue in?**
  - Supreme Court: General Division (first instance), Appellate Division and Court of Appeal (appeals) → [0-1](#drp-w-0-1)
  - District Court's specific jurisdiction up to the limit: recovery of land (annual value or rent), trusts, mortgages, specific performance, partnerships, fraud or mistake, distress, interpleader (State Courts Act ss 25–29) → [0-1](#drp-w-0-1)
  - State Courts: District Court up to $250,000, Magistrate's Court up to $60,000 (the limits are not exceeded at those sums); Small Claims Tribunals up to $20,000, or $30,000 by agreement; above $250,000, General Division (or abandon the excess, or agree, to stay in the District Court) → [0-1](#drp-w-0-1)
- **Does the General Division have jurisdiction over this defendant?**
  - Served in Singapore, or served out of Singapore as the ROC authorise, or the defendant submits (SCJA s 16(1)) → [0-1](#drp-w-0-1)
  - Foreign defendant who will not submit: serve out with the Court's approval (O 8 r 1) → [0-18](#drp-w-0-18)
- **What can the Court do?**
  - Powers in written law and the First Schedule (e.g. para 5: preserve property, evidence and assets) (SCJA s 18) → [0-2](#drp-w-0-2)
  - No express rule: Court may do what is necessary to ensure justice or prevent abuse, if consistent with the Ideals (O 3 r 2(2)) → [0-2](#drp-w-0-2)
  - A party breached the Rules, an order or a PD: waive, reject documents, dismiss or stay, late fee, costs (O 3 r 2(4)) → [0-2](#drp-w-0-2)
  - Order made without notice or by fraud: apply to set aside within 14 days (O 3 r 2(8)–(10)) → [0-2](#drp-w-0-2)
  - Decide on the papers without oral arguments (not where oral evidence is given, unless all agree); asynchronous hearing; summary dismissal of a hopeless appeal from the Registrar (SCJA ss 17B–17C) → [0-2](#drp-w-0-2)
- **Which rules apply?**
  - ROC 2021 for proceedings begun on or after 1 April 2022; ROC 2014 for older (legacy) cases (O 1 r 2(3)) → [0-3](#drp-w-0-3)
  - The Ideals: purposive reading; parties must help achieve them (O 3 r 1) → [0-3](#drp-w-0-3)
- **When does a deadline fall?**
  - "Within 14 days after" X: day 1 is the day after X; a deadline on a non-court day moves to the next court day (O 3 r 3) → [0-4](#drp-w-0-4)
  - Need more time: Court may extend before or after expiry; once by written consent for up to 14 days (O 3 r 4) → [0-4](#drp-w-0-4)
- **Is the SICC an option? (background only — not examinable per SYL p1)**
  - International and commercial action the General Division could hear; an SICC clause is exclusive unless it says otherwise; transfer between the General Division and the SICC; ROC 2021 does not apply (SCJA ss 18A–18M; O 1 r 2(10)) → [0-76](#drp-w-0-76)

### drp-w-0-1 — Which court to sue in; jurisdiction of the General Division
*Keywords: which court, State Courts, State Courts Act s 3, recovery of immovable property, s 25, equity jurisdiction, trust, mortgage, specific performance, partnership, s 26, distress for rent, s 28, interpleader, s 29, General Division, High Court, District Court, Magistrate's Court, District Court limit, Magistrate's Court limit, $250,000, $60,000, Small Claims Tribunal, jurisdiction in personam, submission to jurisdiction, SCJA s 16, SCJA s 17, SCJA s 3, Appellate Division, Court of Appeal*

**I** The issue is which court [Client] should commence proceedings in, and whether that court has jurisdiction over [D]. **C** [Client] should sue in the [General Division of the High Court / District Court / Magistrate's Court], because the claim is for [$X], and the General Division has jurisdiction over [D] under s 16(1) of the Supreme Court of Judicature Act 1969 ("SCJA") once [D] is [served in Singapore / served out of Singapore as the Rules of Court 2021 authorise / submits to the jurisdiction].
**R** The Supreme Court comprises the General Division of the High Court, the Appellate Division of the High Court and the Court of Appeal (s 3 SCJA). The General Division has jurisdiction to hear and try any action in personam where the defendant is served with an originating claim or other originating process in Singapore in the manner prescribed by the Rules of Court, or outside Singapore in the circumstances authorised by and in the manner prescribed by the Rules of Court, or where the defendant submits to its jurisdiction (s 16(1) SCJA). A District Court may not hear an action where the amount claimed, or the value of the subject matter, exceeds the District Court limit of $250,000, and a Magistrate's Court may not where it exceeds the Magistrate's Court limit of $60,000 (ss 2, 19(4), 52(1A) State Courts Act 1970); a claimant may abandon the excess to sue in the District Court, or the parties may agree in a signed memorandum to its jurisdiction (ss 22–23).

> **Rule in full**
> - Section 16(1) SCJA states that the General Division has jurisdiction to hear and try any action in personam where
>   - (a) the defendant is served with an originating claim or any other originating process
>     - (i) in Singapore in the manner prescribed by Rules of Court or Family Justice Rules; or
>     - (ii) outside Singapore in the circumstances authorised by and in the manner prescribed by Rules of Court or Family Justice Rules; or
>   - (b) the defendant submits to the jurisdiction of the General Division.
> - Section 16(2): without limiting subsection (1), the General Division has such jurisdiction as is vested in it by any other written law.
> - Section 17(1) lists specific civil jurisdiction "without limiting section 16" (divorce and matrimonial causes, admiralty, bankruptcy and insolvency, guardianship of infants and of persons and estates of mentally disordered persons, probate and administration, recording mediated settlements under the Mediation Act 2017 and the Singapore Convention on Mediation Act 2020).
> - Section 17A(1): the General Division has no jurisdiction over civil proceedings within the Syariah Court's jurisdiction under s 35(2)(a), (b) or (c) of the Administration of Muslim Law Act 1966 where all parties are Muslims or were married under Muslim law.
> - Section 3: the General Division, the Appellate Division and the Court of Appeal are superior courts of record.
> - State Courts Act 1970 s 2: "District Court limit" means $250,000, and "Magistrate's Court limit" means $60,000 (or other amounts specified by order).
> - s 19(2): a District Court has the General Division's jurisdiction in personam where the defendant is served in or (as the Rules authorise) outside Singapore, or submits; s 19(3): this excludes supervisory and revisionary jurisdiction, judicial review and prerogative orders, jurisdiction vested exclusively elsewhere, passing off, and jurisdiction excluded by written law.
> - s 19(4): subject to ss 22–23, a District Court has no jurisdiction where (a) the amount claimed exceeds the District Court limit; or (b) any remedy or relief sought concerns a subject matter whose value exceeds that limit.
> - s 22: the claimant may abandon the excess, and the District Court then has jurisdiction, but the claimant cannot recover more than the limit and the judgment fully discharges the cause of action.
> - s 23: the parties may agree, by a memorandum signed by them or their solicitors, that the District Court has jurisdiction despite the excess.
> - State Courts Act s 3(1)–(1A): the State Courts are the District Courts, Magistrates' Courts, Coroners' Courts, Small Claims Tribunals and Employment Claims Tribunals; District Courts and Magistrates' Courts have the jurisdiction conferred by the Act and other written law.
> - s 25: without limiting s 19, a District Court may hear an action for the recovery of immovable property (with or without rent or mesne profits, whether or not title is disputed) where (a) the annual value in the Valuation List does not exceed the District Court limit; (b) the annual rent does not exceed that limit, or the monthly rent one-twelfth of it; or (c) if neither can be ascertained, the last transacted price does not exceed 10 times that limit.
> - s 26: equity jurisdiction, each up to the District Court limit: (b) execution of a trust or a declaration that a trust subsists; (c) foreclosure or redemption of a mortgage or enforcing a charge or lien; (d) specific performance, rectification, delivery up or cancellation of an agreement for the sale, purchase or lease of property; (f) dissolution or winding up of a partnership (not an LLP); (g) relief against fraud or mistake.
> - s 28: jurisdiction under the Distress Act 1934 where the rent distrained does not exceed the District Court limit.
> - s 29: relief by interpleader (a) where a bailiff executing a District Court writ or order faces a third party's claim to the goods or proceeds; (b) where the stakeholder's liability does not exceed the District Court limit and 2 or more parties make adverse claims; and the General Division may transfer an interpleader within that limit to a District Court.
> - s 54: a Magistrate's Court judgment or order has the same effect, for appeal or otherwise, and is enforced in the same way, as a District Court's.
> - s 52(1A): a Magistrate's Court has no jurisdiction where (a) there is no money claim; (aa) the action is in passing off; (b) the amount claimed exceeds the Magistrate's Court limit; or (c) any other relief concerns a subject matter worth more than that limit.
> - Small Claims Tribunals Act 1984 s 2: the "prescribed limit" is $20,000 and the "prescribed extended limit" $30,000; s 5: a tribunal hears specified claims served in Singapore, not exceeding $20,000 (or up to $30,000 if the parties agree by signed memorandum), brought within 2 years of accrual, excluding property damage from motor accidents; s 38: appeal to the General Division only on a question of law or jurisdiction, and only with the District Court's permission, whose decision is final.

**P** The General Division also has an inherent jurisdiction to hear and punish for contempt of court (*Li Shengwu v Attorney-General* [2019] 1 SLR 1081 at [98]–[113]).

**A** First, value the claim: [Client] claims [$X] for [breach of contract]. [Since $X exceeds the District Court limit, the General Division is the proper court / Since $X does not exceed $250,000, the District Court is the proper court, and suing in the General Division risks costs consequences; if $X is slightly above $250,000, [Client] may abandon the excess to sue in the District Court (s 22 State Courts Act)]. Second, check jurisdiction over [D]. If [D] is in Singapore, personal service here founds jurisdiction under s 16(1)(a)(i). If [D] is abroad, the claim must be served out of Singapore with the Court's approval under O 8 r 1, which founds jurisdiction under s 16(1)(a)(ii) (see drp-w-0-18). If [D] files a defence on the merits without challenging jurisdiction, [D] submits under s 16(1)(b); a notice of intention to contest, or a defence that only challenges jurisdiction, is not a submission (O 6 r 6(4), r 7(6); see drp-w-0-24). Third, check for a forum or arbitration clause (see drp-w-0-8). **C** Therefore [Client] should commence proceedings in the [court], which will have jurisdiction over [D] once [D] is [served / submits].

*Source note: SCJA p9 (s 3), p20–22 (ss 16, 17, 17A); State Courts limits L1 s6 and State Courts Act ss 2, 19, 22, 23, 52 and Small Claims Tribunals Act ss 2, 5, 38 read on SSO (current as at 8 Nov 2026); State Courts Act ss 3, 25, 26, 28, 29, 54 read on SSO (current as at 18 Nov 2026, extraction subagent; SYL [4]–[5]); L1 s6 says "less than" $250,000/$60,000, but the Act's test is whether the claim "exceeds" the limit, so a claim of exactly $250,000 is within the District Court; SYL p1 [1]–[6], [17]; Li Shengwu SYL p1 [3(a)(i)]; O 6 r 6(4), r 7(6) ROC p71–73.*

### drp-w-0-2 — The Court's powers: SCJA ss 17B–18, First Schedule and O 3 r 2
*Keywords: powers of the court, general powers, SCJA s 17B, decide without oral arguments, asynchronous hearing, SCJA s 17C, summary dismissal of appeal from Registrar, SCJA s 18, First Schedule, paragraph 5, preservation of assets, O 3 r 2, no express provision, non-compliance, waive, late filing fee, $50, dismiss or stay, set aside order made without notice, 14 days, inherent power, Li Shengwu*

**I** The issue is whether the Court has power to [make the order sought / deal with [Party]'s non-compliance with [rule/order]]. **C** The Court [has / lacks] that power under [s 18 and the First Schedule of the SCJA / O 3 r 2(2) / O 3 r 2(4)] of the Rules of Court 2021, which [provides / provide] that [the Court may …].
**R** The General Division has the powers vested in it by written law and those set out in the First Schedule, to be exercised in accordance with the Rules of Court (s 18 SCJA). It may decide matters in its original civil jurisdiction without hearing oral arguments, but not any part where oral evidence is given unless all parties consent (s 17B), and a Judge may summarily dismiss an appeal from the Registrar whose every issue has already been decided against the appellant in an earlier matter, after letting the appellant show cause (s 17C). Where the Rules are silent, the Court may do whatever it considers necessary to ensure that justice is done or to prevent an abuse of its process, so long as this is not prohibited by law and is consistent with the Ideals (O 3 r 2(2)). Where a party fails to comply with the Rules, an order or a practice direction, the Court may waive the non-compliance, reject documents, dismiss, stay or set aside proceedings, impose a late filing fee or make costs orders (O 3 r 2(4)).

> **Rule in full**
> - Section 17B SCJA: (1) the General Division may decide any matter in its original civil jurisdiction without hearing oral arguments, other than a matter prescribed by the Rules of Court; (2) this does not allow any part of a proceeding where oral evidence is given (including any part of a trial) to be conducted without an oral hearing, unless all parties consent; (3)–(4) it may direct that such a matter be heard asynchronously by exchange of written correspondence, unless that would be inconsistent with its duty to conduct the proceedings fairly; (5) it may still hear oral arguments.
> - Section 17C SCJA: (1) a Judge sitting in the General Division may, on his or her own motion, summarily dismiss any appeal (or part of one) against a Registrar's decision in civil proceedings if
>   - (a) every issue in the appeal has already been decided by the General Division, the Appellate Division or the Court of Appeal in an earlier matter in which the appellant was involved, so the appeal has no merit; and
>   - (b) any conditions prescribed by the Rules of Court are met;
>   - (2) the Judge must first give the appellant a reasonable opportunity to show cause and consider its representations.
> - Section 18 SCJA: (1) the General Division has the powers vested in it by any written law for the time being in force; (2) without limiting (1), it has the powers set out in the First Schedule; (3) those powers are to be exercised in accordance with any written law or Rules of Court relating to them.
> - First Schedule, para 5: power before or after any proceedings are commenced to provide for
>   - (a) the interim preservation of property which is the subject matter of the proceedings by sale or by injunction or the appointment of receiver or the registration of a caveat or a lis pendens or in any manner whatsoever;
>   - (b) the preservation of evidence by seizure, detention, inspection, photographing, the taking of samples, the conduct of experiments or in any manner; and
>   - (c) the preservation of assets for the satisfaction of any judgment which has been or may be made.
> - Other First Schedule powers include prerogative orders (para 1), partition and sale of land (paras 2–3), interpleader (para 4) and interest (para 6).
> - O 3 r 2(1): unless the context otherwise requires and subject to any other written law, all requirements in the Rules are subject to the Court's discretion to order otherwise in the interests of justice, even if expressed using "must", "is to" or "shall".
> - O 3 r 2(2): where there is no express provision in the Rules or any other written law, the Court may do whatever it considers necessary on the facts to ensure that justice is done or to prevent an abuse of the process of the Court, so long as it is not prohibited by law and is consistent with the Ideals.
> - O 3 r 2(3): in exercising any power, the Court may impose any condition or give appropriate directions.
> - O 3 r 2(4): where there is non-compliance with the Rules, any other written law, the Court's orders or directions or any practice directions, the Court may
>   - (a) subject to (5), waive the non-compliance;
>   - (b) disallow or reject the filing or use of any document;
>   - (c) refuse to hear any matter or dismiss it without a hearing;
>   - (d) dismiss, stay or set aside any proceedings and give the appropriate judgment or order even though the non-compliance could be compensated by costs, if the non-compliance is inconsistent with any of the Ideals in a material way;
>   - (e) impose a late filing fee of $50 for each day a document remains unfiled after the deadline, excluding non-court days;
>   - (f) make costs orders or any other appropriate orders.
> - O 3 r 2(5): non-compliance with a written law other than the Rules may be waived only if that law allows it.
> - O 3 r 2(8): the Court may, on its own accord or on application, if in the interests of justice, revoke any judgment or order obtained, or set aside anything done,
>   - (a) without notice to, or in the absence of, the party affected;
>   - (b) without complying with the Rules or any order of Court;
>   - (c) contrary to any written law; or
>   - (d) by fraud or misrepresentation.
> - O 3 r 2(9)–(10): the application may be made by or for the party affected and must be taken out within 14 days after the applicant knows or should have known of the ground.

**P** The General Division's inherent jurisdiction includes the power to hear and punish for contempt of court (*Li Shengwu v Attorney-General* [2019] 1 SLR 1081 at [98]–[113]).

**A** Identify which power the facts need. (1) An express power in a rule or the First Schedule comes first: for example, preserving the disputed goods pending trial falls within para 5(a), and freezing assets within para 5(c) (see drp-w-0-59). (2) If no rule covers the step, rely on O 3 r 2(2), and show that the order is necessary for justice or to prevent abuse and is consistent with the Ideals (e.g. ordering production of documents referred to in a pleading before the SAPT: see drp-w-0-48). (3) If the other side has missed a deadline or breached an order, O 3 r 2(4) gives a menu; argue for (d) only if the breach is inconsistent with the Ideals in a material way, otherwise expect waiver with costs under (a) and (f). (4) If an order was made against [Client] without notice, apply under O 3 r 2(8)(a) within 14 days of learning of it. **C** Therefore the Court [may / should] [make the order / impose the consequence] under [provision].

*Source note: SCJA p24–25 (ss 17B–17C, inserted by Act 25 of 2021 from 1 Apr 2022; checked against SSO current as at 18 Nov 2026: unchanged), p25–26 (s 18), p91–92 (First Schedule paras 1–6); O 3 r 2 ROC p53–55; Li Shengwu SYL p1 [3(a)(i)]; SYL p1 [3(a)(ii)], [7]; O 3 r 2 as alternative basis for production, SYL p17 [130].*

### drp-w-0-3 — Which rules apply; sources of civil procedure; the Ideals
*Keywords: Rules of Court 2021, ROC 2021, 1 April 2022, commenced on or after, legacy case, ROC 2014, revoked Rules of Court, transitional provisions, First Schedule, O 1 r 2(3), Ideals, O 3 r 1, purposive interpretation, proportionate, Practice Directions, Supreme Court Practice Directions 2021, practice circulars, Order 2 overview*

**I** The issue is which procedural rules govern [the proceedings / the appeal]. **C** The Rules of Court 2021 apply, because the proceedings were commenced on [date], on or after 1 April 2022 (O 1 r 2(3)(a)).
**R** The Rules of Court 2021 apply to all civil proceedings in the Supreme Court or the State Courts commenced on or after 1 April 2022, including appeals arising from them (O 1 r 2(3)(a)), and with the necessary modifications to appeals to the Court of Appeal or Appellate Division filed on or after that date from decisions in older proceedings (O 1 r 2(3)(b)). The Rules are to be given a purposive interpretation and seek to achieve the Ideals; the Court must seek to achieve them in all its orders, and the parties have a duty to assist (O 3 r 1).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 1 r 2(3): subject to this Rule, these Rules
>   - (a) apply to and in relation to all civil proceedings in the Supreme Court or the State Courts which are commenced on or after 1 April 2022, including appeals arising from those proceedings; and
>   - (b) apply with the necessary modifications to every appeal to the Court of Appeal or the Appellate Division, and every originating application to those courts, filed on or after 1 April 2022, relating to a decision of a lower court made in proceedings to which the revoked Rules of Court apply.
> - O 1 r 2(2): the saving and transitional provisions in the First Schedule have effect (legacy cases continue under the revoked Rules of Court 2014).
> - O 3 r 1(1): the Rules are to be given a purposive interpretation.
> - O 3 r 1(2): the Rules seek to achieve the following Ideals:
>   - (a) fair access to justice;
>   - (b) expeditious proceedings;
>   - (c) cost-effective work proportionate to
>     - (i) the nature and importance of the action;
>     - (ii) the complexity of the claim as well as the difficulty or novelty of the issues and questions it raises; and
>     - (iii) the amount or value of the claim;
>   - (d) efficient use of court resources;
>   - (e) fair and practical results suited to the needs of the parties.
> - O 3 r 1(3): the Court must seek to achieve the Ideals in all its orders or directions.
> - O 3 r 1(4): all parties have the duty to assist the Court and to conduct their cases in a manner which will help to achieve the Ideals.
> - Digest 1 (the Courts' overview of ROC 2021, SYL [9(d)]): ROC 2021 applies to proceedings, including appeals, commenced on or after 1 April 2022, with SICC proceedings excepted (paras 2–4); new terms: writ → originating claim, originating summons → originating application, plaintiff → claimant, leave → permission (paras 6–7).
> - Sources of civil procedure: the SCJA and the State Courts Act; the ROC 2021 (Order 2 gives an overview of the process); case law; the Supreme Court Practice Directions 2021 (the Forms are in Appendix A); circulars from the Courts and the Law Society.

**P** Cases decided under ROC 2014 remain relevant where the underlying principle has not changed; the syllabus flags each such case (e.g. *Iskandar bin Rahmat v Attorney-General* [2022] 2 SLR 1018 at [17]–[19] on striking out; *Gillingham James Ian v Fearless Legends Pte Ltd* [2024] 6 SLR 697 at [14] on pre-action production).

**A** Check the date the originating claim or originating application was filed. On or after 1 April 2022, the ROC 2021 apply, and old-regime labels in the question (writ, summons for directions, discovery, garnishee order) should be translated into the new terms (originating claim, case conference and SAPT, production of documents, attachment of debt). Before that date, the ROC 2014 govern as a legacy case. Then use the Ideals as the touchstone for every discretionary argument: for example, an application brought late or outside the SAPT runs against expeditious proceedings and efficient use of court resources, while a disproportionate document request runs against cost-effective work (O 3 r 1(2)(b)–(d)). **C** Therefore the Rules of Court 2021 govern, and the Court will exercise its discretion in the light of the Ideals.

*Source note: O 1 r 2 ROC p34–35; O 3 r 1 ROC p53; SYL p1–2 [8]–[12]; Digest 1 (judiciary.gov.sg, paras 1–7 read 18 Nov 2026 by an extraction subagent; the para 8 flowchart is an image and was not read); L1 s8; Iskandar SYL p13 [99(b)]; Gillingham SYL p4 [21(b)].*

### drp-w-0-4 — Calculating time; extending or shortening time
*Keywords: calculation of time, O 3 r 3, within 14 days after, period begins immediately after, clear days, month means calendar month, non-court day, 6 days or less, Interpretation Act does not apply, extension of time, O 3 r 4, extend after expiry, consent in writing, 14 days, deadline*

**I** The issue is [when [Party]'s deadline to [file the defence] expires / whether [Party] can obtain more time]. **C** The deadline is [date], calculated under O 3 r 3 of the Rules of Court 2021, [and [Party] may obtain an extension by consent for up to 14 days once, or otherwise from the Court under O 3 r 4].
**R** Time under the Rules is calculated under O 3 r 3, not the Interpretation Act: a period "after" a date begins immediately after that date; a month is a calendar month; periods of 6 days or less exclude non-court days; and a deadline falling on a non-court day moves to the next court day (O 3 r 3). The Court may extend or shorten any period, before or after it expires, and a period to serve, file or amend a pleading or other document may be extended once by written consent for up to 14 days without a court order (O 3 r 4).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 3 r 3(1): the Interpretation Act does not apply to the calculation of time in these Rules.
> - O 3 r 3(2): "month" means a calendar month unless the context otherwise requires.
> - O 3 r 3(3): where an act must be done within a specified period after or from a specified date, the period begins immediately after that date.
> - O 3 r 3(4): where an act must be done within or not less than a specified period before a specified date, the period ends immediately before that date.
> - O 3 r 3(5): where an act must be done a specified number of clear days before or after a date, at least that number of days must intervene between the day of the act and that date.
> - O 3 r 3(6): if the period is 6 days or less, any non-court day is excluded.
> - O 3 r 3(7): where the time prescribed by the Rules, or by any judgment, order or direction, expires on a non-court day, the act is in time if done on the next day that is not a non-court day.
> - O 3 r 4(1): the Court may extend or shorten the period within which a person is required by the Rules or by any judgment, order or direction to do any act in any proceedings.
> - O 3 r 4(2): unless the Rules otherwise provide, the Court may extend the period whether the application is made before or after it expires.
> - O 3 r 4(3): the period within which a person is required by the Rules, or by any order, to serve, file or amend any pleading or other document may be extended once by consent in writing for a maximum period of 14 days without an order of the Court.

**P** No case is cited in the syllabus; the calculation follows the text of O 3 r 3. Model answers state the deadline as a date (e.g. an originating claim issued on [date] for service in Singapore is valid for service for 3 months beginning with that date: O 6 r 3(1)(b)).

**A** Take the trigger date in the facts ([the statement of claim was served on Monday 2 November]). Day 1 is the next day, so "21 days after service" ends on [Monday 23 November]; if that is a Saturday, Sunday or public holiday, the defence is in time if filed on the next court day. For short periods (e.g. "3 days after service" before applying for an enforcement order, O 22 r 2(3)), skip non-court days. If [Party] needs more time to serve a pleading, first seek the other side's written consent to a single extension of up to 14 days (O 3 r 4(3)); failing that, apply to Court, ideally before expiry, explaining the delay (O 3 r 4(1)–(2)). Note that costs of an extension application are usually borne by the applicant (O 21 r 3(4); see drp-w-0-72). **C** Therefore [Party] must [act] by [date], and may [extend by consent / apply to Court] if more time is needed.

*Source note: O 3 rr 3–4 ROC p55–56; O 6 r 3(1) ROC p69; O 22 r 2(3) L5 s6; costs of extension application L6 s16 (O 21 r 3(4)). Not a syllabus paragraph; added because every deadline answer needs it.*

### drp-w-0-76 — The Singapore International Commercial Court (SICC) (background only — not examinable per SYL p1)
*Keywords: SICC, Singapore International Commercial Court, international and commercial, SCJA s 18A, s 18D, s 18F, jurisdiction agreement, exclusive jurisdiction, transfer to the SICC, s 18J, foreign lawyer, foreign law on submissions, O 1 r 2(10), ROC 2021 does not apply, background only*

**I** The issue is whether [Client]'s dispute with [D] can be heard in the Singapore International Commercial Court ("SICC"). **C** The SICC [has / lacks] jurisdiction because the action [is / is not] international and commercial in nature and is one the General Division could hear in its original civil jurisdiction (s 18D(1) SCJA).
**R** The SICC is a division of the General Division (s 18A SCJA). It may hear any action that is international and commercial in nature, that the General Division may hear in its original civil jurisdiction, and that satisfies any conditions the Rules of Court prescribe (s 18D(1)). Parties who agree to submit to the SICC are taken, unless they expressly agree otherwise, to submit to its exclusive jurisdiction, to carry out its judgments without undue delay and to waive recourse to foreign courts against them (s 18F). A case may be transferred between the General Division and the SICC (s 18J). The Rules of Court 2021 do not apply to SICC proceedings begun on or after 1 April 2022 unless the Court otherwise directs (O 1 r 2(10)).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 18A: the SICC is a division of the General Division.
> - s 18D(1): the SICC has jurisdiction to hear and try any action that satisfies all of the following:
>   - (a) the action is international and commercial in nature;
>   - (b) the action is one that the General Division may hear and try in its original civil jurisdiction;
>   - (c) the action satisfies such other conditions as the Rules of Court may prescribe.
> - s 18D(2): it also hears international commercial arbitration proceedings, pre-commencement applications for relief for SICC proceedings, and international and commercial corporate insolvency, restructuring or dissolution proceedings, each subject to the Rules' conditions.
> - s 18D(3): its jurisdiction extends to added parties, counterclaims and third party proceedings if the action as a whole stays international and commercial.
> - s 18F(1)–(2): unless the agreement expressly provides otherwise, parties agreeing to submit to the SICC agree
>   - (a) to its exclusive jurisdiction;
>   - (b) to carry out its judgments and orders without undue delay; and
>   - (c) to waive any recourse to a court or tribunal outside Singapore against them, and against their enforcement, so far as validly waivable.
> - s 18G: one Judge or three Judges; s 18I: the General Division's original civil powers, except First Schedule para 1 and Family Division powers.
> - s 18J: the SICC may transfer a case to the General Division, and the General Division may transfer a case to the SICC, in accordance with the Rules of Court.
> - s 18K–18L: the SICC may disapply Singapore rules of evidence and decide foreign law on submissions instead of proof, in cases the Rules prescribe; s 18M: registered foreign lawyers may appear.
> - O 1 r 2(10): unless the Court otherwise directs, ROC 2021 does not apply to SICC proceedings commenced on or after 1 April 2022, to General Division cases transferred to the SICC, or to applications to transfer them.

**P** No case is set for the SICC in the syllabus; it is listed for background only (SYL [3(c)]).

**A** Ask whether the claim is international (foreign parties, foreign performance, foreign law) and commercial, and whether the contract has an SICC jurisdiction clause; if so, [Client] may file in the SICC, and the clause is exclusive unless it says otherwise (s 18F). If the case is already in the General Division, consider a transfer (s 18J). Note that the SICC has its own procedural rules, not ROC 2021. **C** Therefore [Client] [may commence in / may seek a transfer to] the SICC under s 18D(1) [and s 18J] SCJA.

*Source note: SCJA p26–32 (ss 18A–18M, library copy in force from 1 Dec 2023); O 1 r 2(10) ROC p37–38; SYL p1 [3(c)]. SICC proceedings follow the SICC's own procedural rules (Singapore International Commercial Court Rules 2021), not ROC 2021 (O 1 r 2(10)); those rules are not in the library and were not read (background only), so this Pre-write states no SICC procedure. Rule text from the library copies, checked against SSO (current version as at 18 Nov 2026; extraction subagent): unchanged since 2021.*

## 0.II Pre-action considerations

- **Before taking the case**
  - Engagement terms in writing, conflict check, AML check, instructions documented → [0-5](#drp-w-0-5)
- **Is there a claim, and is it in time?**
  - Identify the cause of action and its elements → [0-6](#drp-w-0-6)
  - Contract or tort: 6 years from accrual (LA s 6(1)(a)) → [0-6](#drp-w-0-6)
  - Negligence, latent damage: 6 years, or 3 years from knowledge, long-stop 15 years (LA ss 24A(3), 24B) → [0-6](#drp-w-0-6)
  - Personal injury: 3 years from accrual or knowledge (LA s 24A(2)) → [0-6](#drp-w-0-6)
  - Fraud, concealment or mistake: time runs from discovery or reasonable diligence (LA s 29; *Chua Teck Chew*) → [0-6](#drp-w-0-6)
  - Counterclaim or set-off: deemed brought when the action began (LA s 31) → [0-6](#drp-w-0-6)
  - Limitation about to expire: issue an originating claim endorsed generally (O 6 r 5(4)) → [0-11](#drp-w-0-11)
- **Must I try to settle first?**
  - Yes: offer of amicable resolution before suing unless reasonable grounds not to; in writing; open at least 14 days (O 5 rr 1–2) → [0-7](#drp-w-0-7)
  - Refusing to try ADR risks costs (O 21 r 4(c); PD 53) → [0-73](#drp-w-0-73)
  - Court may order parties to attempt amicable resolution (O 5 r 3) → [0-7](#drp-w-0-7)
- **Is there a forum or arbitration clause?**
  - Exclusive Singapore jurisdiction clause; non-exclusive clause; no clause; arbitration clause → [0-8](#drp-w-0-8)
- **Client lacks information to sue (who to sue, where the property went, whether a claim exists)?**
  - Pre-action production of documents or information (O 11 r 11; *ED&F Man*, *Dorsey*, *Gillingham*) → [0-9](#drp-w-0-9)
  - Same rule for production from a non-party after action begins; non-party gets reasonable costs (O 11 r 11(3)) → [0-9](#drp-w-0-9)
- **Pre-action protocol, or a party with special status? (background only — not examinable per SYL p1)**
  - Medical negligence protocol (PD 163, Appendix H); State Courts protocols for personal injury, motor accident and defamation claims → [0-77](#drp-w-0-77)
  - Minor below 21 or person lacking capacity: litigation representative acting by a solicitor (O 4 r 2; O 44 r 3); group with a common interest: representative action (O 4 r 6); dead defendant, no grant: sue the estate and apply for a representative (O 4 r 4); bankrupt: Official Assignee's sanction first (IRDA s 401(1); *Aathar Ah Kong*) → [0-77](#drp-w-0-77)

### drp-w-0-5 — Managing the client relationship before action
*Keywords: engagement letter, terms of engagement, conflict check, conflict of interest, anti-money laundering, AML, customer due diligence, instructions, attendance note, documenting instructions, pre-action checklist*

**I** The issue is what [Firm] must do before acting for [Client] in the intended proceedings. **C** Before commencing proceedings, [Firm] should document the terms of engagement, run a conflict check and anti-money laundering checks, and take and record [Client]'s instructions.
**R** The course identifies four steps in managing the relationship with a client before starting proceedings: documenting the terms of engagement; ensuring there are no conflicts of interest (proper conflict checks must be done before engagement); running anti-money laundering checks; and taking and documenting the client's instructions.

> **Rule in full**
> - Managing the relationship with the client (SYL [16(a)]):
>   - (i) documenting the terms of engagement;
>   - (ii) ensuring there are no conflicts of interest — for this module, the candidate should know that proper conflict checks must be done pre-engagement; the substantive conflict rules are beyond the module;
>   - (iii) running anti-money laundering checks — the candidate should know that AML checks must be done; how they are done is beyond the module;
>   - (iv) taking and documenting the client's instructions.
> - Then ask: is there a cause of action (0-6)? Is the limitation period current (0-6)? Has amicable resolution been tried (0-7)? Which court (0-1)? Is there a dispute resolution clause (0-8)?

**P** No case is set for this point in the syllabus; the conflict and AML rules themselves belong to the Ethics paper, not this one.

**A** Where the facts show a new client who wants to sue at once, list the steps briefly and move on to the substantive procedure: [Firm] should issue an engagement letter setting out scope and fees, check its records for any conflict with [D] and other parties before accepting instructions, carry out the required customer due diligence on [Client], and take written instructions on [the claim, the relief sought and settlement authority]. Flag anything in the facts that calls for more care (e.g. [Client] is introduced by a third party who will fund the claim, which also matters for security for costs under O 9 r 12(2)(b): see drp-w-0-34). **C** Once these steps are done, [Firm] can advise on the merits, limitation and the pre-action offer of amicable resolution.

*Source note: SYL p3 [16(a)]; L1 s10.*

### drp-w-0-6 — Cause of action and limitation
*Keywords: cause of action, limitation period, time bar, Limitation Act s 6, 6 years, contract, tort, accrual, s 24A, personal injuries, 3 years, date of knowledge, latent damage, s 24B, 15 years, long-stop, s 29, fraud, concealment, mistake, reasonable diligence, s 31, set-off, counterclaim, Chua Teck Chew Robert v Goh Eng Wah*

**I** The issue is whether [Client]'s claim for [breach of contract / negligence] against [D] is time-barred. **C** The claim [is / is not] time-barred, because under s [6(1)(a) / 24A] of the Limitation Act 1959 the action must be brought within [6 years from accrual / 3 years from the date of knowledge], and the cause of action accrued on [date].
**R** Actions founded on contract or tort may not be brought after 6 years from the date the cause of action accrued (s 6(1)(a) Limitation Act). For negligence, nuisance or breach of duty, a personal injury claim must be brought within 3 years from accrual or from the claimant's date of knowledge, whichever is later (s 24A(2)); other such claims within 6 years from accrual or 3 years from knowledge, whichever is later (s 24A(3)), subject to a 15-year long-stop (s 24B). Where the action is based on fraud, the right of action is concealed by fraud, or relief is sought from the consequences of mistake, time does not run until the claimant discovered it or could with reasonable diligence have discovered it (s 29(1)). A counterclaim or set-off is deemed brought when the action was commenced (s 31).

> **Rule in full**
> - Section 6(1): subject to the Act, the following actions shall not be brought after the expiration of 6 years from the date on which the cause of action accrued:
>   - (a) actions founded on a contract or on tort;
>   - (b) actions to enforce a recognizance;
>   - (c) actions to enforce an award;
>   - (d) actions to recover any sum recoverable by virtue of any written law other than a penalty or forfeiture.
> - Section 6(3): an action upon a judgment shall not be brought after 12 years from the date the judgment became enforceable; arrears of interest on a judgment debt are not recoverable after 6 years from when the interest became due.
> - Section 24A(1): applies to any action for damages for negligence, nuisance or breach of duty (whether the duty arises by contract, by written law or independently).
> - Section 24A(2): where the damages include damages for personal injuries, the action shall not be brought after the expiration of
>   - (a) 3 years from the date on which the cause of action accrued; or
>   - (b) 3 years from the earliest date on which the claimant has the knowledge required for bringing an action for damages in respect of the relevant injury, if that period expires later.
> - Section 24A(3): any other action to which the section applies shall not be brought after the expiration of
>   - (a) 6 years from the date on which the cause of action accrued; or
>   - (b) 3 years from the earliest date on which the claimant (or anyone in whom the cause of action was vested before him) first had both the knowledge required and a right to bring such an action, if that period expires later.
> - Section 24A(4): the knowledge required means knowledge (a) that the injury or damage was attributable to the act or omission alleged to be negligence, nuisance or breach of duty; (b) of the identity of the defendant; (c) where the act or omission was another person's, of that person's identity and the additional facts supporting the action; and (d) of material facts about the injury or damage which would lead a reasonable person who had suffered it to consider it sufficiently serious to justify proceedings against a defendant who did not dispute liability and could satisfy a judgment.
> - Section 24B(1): an action for negligence, nuisance or breach of duty to which s 24A applies shall not be brought after 15 years from the starting date (the last act or omission alleged to be negligent and to which the damage is attributable), even if the cause of action has not yet accrued (s 24B(3)).
> - Section 29(1): where (a) the action is based upon the fraud of the defendant or his agent; (b) the right of action is concealed by the fraud of any such person; or (c) the action is for relief from the consequences of a mistake, the limitation period shall not begin to run until the claimant has discovered the fraud or the mistake, or could with reasonable diligence have discovered it.
> - Section 29(2): this does not allow recovery of property bought for valuable consideration by an innocent purchaser.
> - Section 31: any claim by way of set-off or counterclaim is deemed a separate action commenced on the same date as the action in which it is pleaded.

**P** Reasonable diligence under s 29 does not mean doing everything possible; it means doing what an ordinary prudent person would reasonably do in the circumstances, having regard to expense and difficulty, and it depends largely on the facts (*Chua Teck Chew Robert v Goh Eng Wah* [2009] 4 SLR(R) 716 at [25]–[33]).

**A** First, identify the cause of action and its elements, and whether [Client] can prove each (if not, consider pre-action production: drp-w-0-9). Second, fix the accrual date: in contract, the date of breach ([D] failed to pay on [date]); in tort, the date damage was suffered. Third, apply the period: [6 years from [date] ends on [date]]. Fourth, check for extensions: [the defect was latent and [Client] only learned of it on [date], so s 24A(3)(b) gives 3 years from then]; [the defendant concealed the breach, so s 29(1)(b) postpones the start until [Client] discovered it or could have with reasonable diligence]. Fifth, if the period is about to expire, file an originating claim endorsed generally at once (O 6 r 5(4); drp-w-0-11), and note that a new claim added by amendment after the period expires faces O 9 r 14(4) (drp-w-0-39). For a defendant, note that a counterclaim is treated as brought when the claimant's action began (s 31). **C** Therefore the claim [is in time and must be filed by [date] / is time-barred unless s [29] postpones the start of the period].

*Source note: LA p6–7 (s 6), p17–18 (s 24), p19–21 (ss 24A–24B), p24–25 (ss 29, 31) — LA is the informal consolidation in force from 1 Apr 2022; SYL p3 [16(b)–(c)]; L1 s11; Chua Teck Chew ratio as stated in Cyril-Notes ("Pre-action considerations", reasonable diligence) and Cyril-Exam (same heading).*

### drp-w-0-7 — Amicable resolution before and during the action (Order 5)
*Keywords: amicable resolution, Order 5, O 5 r 1, duty to consider amicable resolution, offer of amicable resolution, before commencing action, reasonable grounds, offer in writing, open for 14 days, O 5 r 2, without prejudice save as to costs, not made known to Court until costs, O 5 r 3, Court may order ADR, sealed document, mediation, letter of demand*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] must take any step to resolve the dispute amicably before commencing proceedings against [D]. **C** Yes. Under O 5 r 1(2) of the Rules of Court 2021, [Client] is to make an offer of amicable resolution before commencing the action, unless it has reasonable grounds not to; the offer must be in writing and open for at least 14 days (O 5 r 2).
**R** A party has a duty to consider amicable resolution before commencing and during any action or appeal, and is to make an offer of amicable resolution before commencing the action unless it has reasonable grounds not to (O 5 r 1(1)–(2)). An offer to settle, or to resolve the dispute other than by litigation, in whole or in part, counts (O 5 r 1(3)); it must be in writing and open for acceptance for a reasonable period of at least 14 days, and its terms may not be revealed to the Court until costs are dealt with (O 5 r 2). A party must not reject an offer without reasonable grounds (O 5 r 1(4)), and the Court may order the parties to attempt amicable resolution (O 5 r 3).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 5 r 1(1): a party to any proceedings has the duty to consider amicable resolution of the party's dispute before the commencement and during the course of any action or appeal.
> - O 5 r 1(2): a party is to make an offer of amicable resolution before commencing the action unless the party has reasonable grounds not to do so.
> - O 5 r 1(3): an offer of amicable resolution means making an offer to settle the action or appeal or making an offer to resolve the dispute other than by litigation, whether in whole or in part.
> - O 5 r 1(4): a party must not reject an offer of amicable resolution unless the party has reasonable grounds to do so.
> - O 5 r 2(1): an offer of amicable resolution and any rejection must be in writing.
> - O 5 r 2(2): the offer must be open for acceptance within a reasonable period of time and in any case for at least 14 days, unless the parties otherwise agree.
> - O 5 r 2(3): the terms of an offer made and not accepted must not be relied upon or made known to the Court until after the Court has determined the merits and is dealing with costs.
> - O 5 r 2(4): an offer that states no expiry date expires once the Court has determined the merits, unless the offeror has stated otherwise.
> - O 5 r 3(1): the Court may order the parties to attempt to resolve the dispute by amicable resolution.
>   - (2) in deciding, the Court must have regard to the Ideals and all relevant circumstances, including whether any party has refused to attempt amicable resolution;
>   - (3) if a party says it does not wish to attempt amicable resolution, the Court may order it to submit a sealed document setting out its reasons;
>   - (4) the sealed document is opened only after the merits are determined, and may be referred to on costs;
>   - (5) the Court may suggest solutions at any time.
> - Costs sanction: the Court may disallow or reduce a successful party's costs, or order it to pay costs, if it has not discharged its duty to consider amicable resolution or to make an offer in accordance with Order 5 (O 21 r 4(c)); solicitors should advise clients on ADR and on adverse costs orders for unreasonable refusal (PD 53).

**P** No case is set on Order 5 in the syllabus. The sanction is in costs: a successful party who ignored Order 5 may have its costs disallowed or reduced, or be ordered to pay costs (O 21 r 4(c)).

**A** On the facts, [Client] has [only sent chasers / not made any offer]. Before filing, [Client] should send [D] a written offer of amicable resolution, for example [an offer to settle at $X, or to mediate at the Singapore Mediation Centre], open for at least 14 days (O 5 r 2(1)–(2)). Consider whether [Client] has reasonable grounds not to make an offer under O 5 r 1(2): [the limitation period expires in days, so there is no time; or [D] is dissipating assets and must not be tipped off before an urgent Mareva application]. If so, record the grounds, because they may be tested on costs. If [D] rejects the offer, the rejection must be in writing and [D] needs reasonable grounds (O 5 r 1(4)). The offer's terms stay confidential until costs (O 5 r 2(3)). **C** Therefore [Client] should make a written offer of amicable resolution open for at least 14 days before commencing proceedings, failing which it risks an adverse costs order under O 21 r 4(c).

*Source note: O 5 rr 1–3 ROC p67–68; O 21 r 4(c) L6 s13; PD 53 SYL p31 [168(c)(ii)]; SYL p3 [16(d)]; tested PYP23 Q1.2, PYP25 Q1.1.*

### drp-w-0-8 — Dispute resolution clauses: jurisdiction and arbitration clauses
*Keywords: dispute resolution clause, exclusive jurisdiction clause, non-exclusive jurisdiction clause, choice of court, governing law, arbitration clause, stay in favour of arbitration, forum conveniens, no jurisdiction clause, submission, service out*

**I** The issue is whether any dispute resolution clause affects where and how [Client] can sue [D]. **C** [The clause / The absence of a clause] means that [Client] [may sue in the Singapore courts / must arbitrate / should sue in [forum]], because [reason].
**R** Before suing, check whether the contract contains a dispute resolution clause, such as an exclusive or non-exclusive jurisdiction clause or an arbitration clause, and whether it applies to the dispute. A Singapore jurisdiction clause supports the Court's jurisdiction and is a factor for service out; an arbitration clause will usually lead to the action being stayed in favour of arbitration; and the absence of a clause does not stop the Court from approving service out if Singapore is the appropriate forum.

> **Rule in full**
> - The syllabus asks only: "Are there any dispute resolution clauses (such as exclusive/non-exclusive jurisdiction clauses, arbitration clauses) that are applicable?" (SYL [18]).
> - Where the defendant is abroad, the clause feeds into service out of Singapore: the claimant must show the Court has jurisdiction or is the appropriate court (O 8 r 1(1)), and among the PD 63(3) nexus factors is a claim on a contract that is governed by Singapore law or contains a term that the Court will have jurisdiction to hear and determine any action in respect of the contract (PD 63(3)(d)(iii)–(iv); see drp-w-0-18).
> - Service out under a contract may be effected in the manner contractually agreed (O 8 r 2(2)(a); see drp-w-0-19).
> - Approval to serve out does not require a jurisdiction clause; the governing law being Singapore law is relevant to nexus (SQ p117–118 rubric).
> - A defendant who wants another forum or arbitration challenges jurisdiction, or asks the Court not to exercise it, in its defence (O 6 r 7(4)–(5); drp-w-0-24).

**P** The 2023-S rubric credits the points that approval to serve out "may be granted in the absence of a jurisdiction clause" and that Singapore governing law is relevant to nexus (SQ p117–118). No case on jurisdiction or arbitration clauses is set in the syllabus; the stay-for-arbitration rule belongs to the Arbitration paper (`drp-m`).

**A** Read the clause. (1) Exclusive Singapore jurisdiction [cl 3 in PYP21]: sue in Singapore; [D] has agreed to the forum, which makes a challenge on forum non conveniens grounds very hard; rely on it in the O 8 r 1 affidavit if [D] is abroad. (2) Non-exclusive Singapore jurisdiction [PYP25 Q1]: Singapore is available but not mandatory; [D] may still argue another forum is clearly more appropriate, so build the PD 63 nexus and forum conveniens case (drp-w-0-18). (3) No clause [PYP23 Q1, SQ 2023-S]: approval to serve out may still be granted; rely on Singapore governing law, the place the contract was made or performed, and the location of witnesses and documents. (4) Arbitration clause: litigation risks a stay; advise [Client] to commence arbitration instead. **C** Therefore [Client] [should commence proceedings in the General Division and rely on cl [x] / should arbitrate under cl [x]].

*Source note: SYL p3 [18]; L1 s11; O 8 r 1(1), r 2 ROC; SQ p48 Q1.2, p117–118 (rubric); tested PYP23 Q1.1, PYP25 Q1.1, SQ 2023-S Q1.2. PD 63(3)(d) SCPD p73.*

### drp-w-0-9 — Pre-action production and production against a non-party (O 11 r 11)
*Keywords: pre-action production, pre-action discovery, pre-action interrogatories, production against non-party, non-party discovery, O 11 r 11, identify possible parties, trace property, any other lawful purpose, interests of justice, cannot be compelled in law, non-party costs, relevant and necessary, viable claim, Ching Mun Fong v Standard Chartered Bank, ED&F Man Capital Markets v Straits (Singapore), Dorsey James Michael v World Sport Group, Gillingham James Ian v Fearless Legends, Norwich Pharmacal*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can obtain [documents / information] from [D / the non-party] before commencing proceedings [or: from a non-party in the existing proceedings]. **C** [Client] may apply under O 11 r 11 of the Rules of Court 2021, and the Court [is likely / unlikely] to order production, because the [documents] are [relevant and necessary for [Client] to decide whether it has a viable claim / to identify the wrongdoer / to trace its property].
**R** The Court may order production of documents and information before proceedings begin, or against a non-party, to identify possible parties, to enable a party to trace its property, or for any other lawful purpose, in the interests of justice (O 11 r 11(1)). It must not order production that cannot be compelled in law (O 11 r 11(2)), and a non-party is entitled to all reasonable costs of the application (O 11 r 11(3)). The applicant must show that the documents or information are relevant and necessary for it to commence proceedings or to determine whether it has a viable claim.

> **Rule in full**
> - O 11 r 11(1): the Court may order the production of documents and information before the commencement of proceedings or against a non-party
>   - to identify possible parties to any proceedings,
>   - to enable a party to trace the party's property, or
>   - for any other lawful purpose,
>   - in the interests of justice.
> - O 11 r 11(2): the Court must not order a document to be produced if its production cannot be compelled in law.
> - O 11 r 11(3): a non-party is entitled to all reasonable costs arising out of such an application.
> - Case factors (decided under ROC 2014 pre-action discovery; the rationale is unchanged):
>   - the documents or information must be relevant, and necessary for the applicant to commence proceedings or to determine whether it has a viable claim (*ED&F Man*; *Gillingham*);
>   - the Court weighs proportionality, other ways of getting the information and the degree of intrusion (*Dorsey*);
>   - if the applicant can already start the action without the material, the Court may refuse (*Dorsey*);
>   - no order if there is no likely prospect of proceedings being commenced in Singapore (*ED&F Man* at [34]–[36]).

**P** The Court will not order pre-action discovery if there is no likely prospect of proceedings being commenced in Singapore (*ED&F Man Capital Markets Ltd v Straits (Singapore) Pte Ltd* [2020] 2 SLR 695 at [34]–[36]; see also *Ching Mun Fong v Standard Chartered Bank* [2012] 4 SLR 185). Production may be ordered where a prospective claimant lacks enough information to know whether it has a viable cause of action or whom to sue, but not where it can already plead its case (*Dorsey James Michael v World Sport Group Pte Ltd* [2014] 2 SLR 208). The overarching rationale of O 11 r 11 is the same as that of its ROC 2014 predecessor (*Gillingham James Ian v Fearless Legends Pte Ltd* [2024] 6 SLR 697 at [14]).

**A** Identify the purpose under r 11(1): [Client] suspects [a conspiracy between D1 and D2] but has [no concrete evidence, only a theory] (PYP21 Q1), so the purpose is to determine whether a viable claim exists, or [to identify the account holder who received the money], or [to trace [Client]'s funds]. Then show relevance and necessity: the [board minutes / bank records] would show [whether D1 ever intended to pay], which [Client] cannot plead without them. Show that Singapore proceedings are likely ([the contract has a Singapore jurisdiction clause]). Address the counter-arguments: a fishing expedition on a bare theory will fail; if [Client] can already sue on [the clause 2 claim] it may need to sue first and seek production in the action. Narrow the request to specific documents to meet proportionality. If the target is a non-party (e.g. [the bank]), [Client] must pay its reasonable costs (r 11(3)), and documents protected by privilege cannot be ordered (r 11(2)). Procedure: before action, by originating application with supporting affidavit (O 6 r 1(3)(b): an application to the Court under written law); in an existing action, by summons, within the SAPT unless the Court approves otherwise (O 9 r 9(4)(k), (8); drp-w-0-33). **C** Therefore [Client] should apply under O 11 r 11 for [specific documents], and the application [is likely to succeed if confined to documents necessary to decide whether to sue / is likely to fail as a fishing exercise]; [Client] bears the costs of the application and, if against a non-party, its reasonable costs.

*Source note: O 11 r 11 ROC p112; SYL p4 [21], p18 [134]–[136]; L1 s12; case propositions: ED&F Man and Ching Mun Fong SYL p4 [21(b)]; Gillingham SYL p4 [21(b)]; Dorsey proposition from Cyril-Notes and Cyril-Exam ("Pre-action production") and Nic-Muggers (pre-action production) (secondary); relevance-and-necessity test Cyril-Notes (secondary); mode of application: PD 74(2) requires an O 11 r 11 application against a network service provider to be made by originating application in Form 15 (current SCPD, epd2021, 15 Jul 2026); for other pre-action applications no action yet exists, so an originating application follows from O 6 r 1(3); tested PYP21 Q1, Q5 (non-party tapes).*

### drp-w-0-77 — Pre-action protocols and the special status of a party (background only — not examinable per SYL p1)
*Keywords: pre-action protocol, medical negligence protocol, PD 163, Appendix H, personal injury protocol, motor accident, defamation protocol, State Courts PD 39, 40, 108, bankrupt, Official Assignee, sanction, locus standi, IRDA s 401, Aathar Ah Kong Andrew v OUE Lippo Healthcare, minor, below 21, person lacking capacity, Mental Capacity Act, litigation representative, O 4 r 2, O 44 r 3, representative action, numerous persons common interest, O 4 r 6, estate of deceased, personal representatives of deceased, O 4 r 4, Public Trustee, background only*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] must follow a pre-action protocol, and whether [Client's / D's] status as [a bankrupt / a minor / a person lacking capacity / a group / a deceased's estate] affects how the action is brought. **C** [Client] must [comply with the medical negligence protocol in Appendix H of the Supreme Court Practice Directions / sue through a litigation representative (O 4 r 2) / sue as a representative group (O 4 r 6) / sue the estate and apply to appoint a representative (O 4 r 4)].
**R** In medical negligence claims in the General Division the parties must comply with the protocol in Appendix H, and non-compliance that causes unnecessary costs may be visited with costs orders, including indemnity costs (PD 163; State Courts PDs paras 39, 40 and 108 set protocols for personal injury and non-injury motor accident, medical negligence and defamation claims). A person below 21 (outside O 4 r 1(a)) or who lacks capacity under the Mental Capacity Act must be represented by a litigation representative, who must act by a solicitor (O 4 r 2(1); O 44 r 3). Numerous persons with a common interest may sue or be sued as a group through a representative (O 4 r 6(1)). Where a defendant has died and no grant has been made, the action may be brought against the "personal representatives of [name] deceased", and the claimant must apply during the validity of the originating process for an order appointing a representative (O 4 r 4(1)–(2)). A bankrupt who sues without the Official Assignee's prior sanction lacks locus standi (s 401(1) IRDA; *Aathar Ah Kong Andrew v OUE Lippo Healthcare Ltd* [2021] SGCA 48 at [27]–[34]).

> **Rule in full**
> - PD 163(1): parties in medical negligence claims must comply with the Protocol at Appendix H; one party's breach does not exempt the others.
> - PD 163(2): if non-compliance led to unnecessary costs, the Court may (a) disallow the defaulting party's costs even if it succeeds; (b) order it to pay the other side's costs; (c) order those costs on an indemnity basis. PD 163(3): compliance is also weighed on interest.
> - O 4 r 2(1): must be represented by a litigation representative —
>   - (a) a person below 21 who does not come under r 1(a) (18 to 20 where s 36 Civil Law Act applies);
>   - (b) a person who lacks capacity within the Mental Capacity Act in relation to property and affairs.
> - O 4 r 2(2)–(3): the litigation representative must be able to be a party in his or her own name and must have no interest adverse to the person represented.
> - O 4 r 3(1)(a); O 44 r 3(1)–(3): a person under disability may only sue, defend or counterclaim by a litigation representative, who must act by a solicitor.
> - O 4 r 4(1)–(3): action against "personal representatives of (defendant's name) deceased"; claimant must apply, within the validity period for service, for an order appointing a representative (or adding the personal representative if a grant exists); if the Public Trustee is appointed, serve on the Public Trustee.
> - O 4 r 6(1)–(5): numerous persons with a common interest may sue or be sued as a group; suing members must consent in writing and be listed with the originating process; the judgment binds those listed.

**P** An undischarged bankrupt may not start, continue or defend an action, or appeal, without the Official Assignee's previous (prior) sanction, which cannot be given retrospectively; without it the bankrupt lacks locus standi (*Aathar Ah Kong Andrew v OUE Lippo Healthcare Ltd* [2021] SGCA 48 at [27]–[34]).

**A** Before filing, check (i) the claim type: a medical negligence claim in the General Division engages the Appendix H protocol, and breach risks the PD 163(2) costs orders; (ii) the parties' status: [Client is 17 / has dementia] → appoint a litigation representative acting by a solicitor; [Client is an undischarged bankrupt] → obtain the Official Assignee's sanction first; [D has died, no grant] → sue the estate and apply under O 4 r 4(2); [many investors with the same claim] → a representative action with written consents and a list. **C** Therefore [Client] should [comply with the protocol / proceed through a litigation representative / obtain sanction / sue the estate] before commencing.

*Source note: PD 163 SCPD p198 (Appendix H not extracted); O 4 rr 1–4, 6 ROC p58–64; O 44 r 3 ROC p429; SYL p3–4 [19]–[20]. Aathar Ah Kong read on eLitigation [2021] SGCA 48 at [27]–[35] (18 Nov 2026, extraction subagent): IRDA s 401(1) and Bankruptcy Act s 131(1) are materially identical; "previous" sanction means prior sanction, which cannot be given after the event. The State Courts PD protocol paragraphs are cited as SYL [19] gives them (background only; not read). ROC text checked against SSO (current version as at 18 Nov 2026; extraction subagent): unchanged since 2021; PD text from the library copy.*

## 0.III Commencing the action

- **Originating claim or originating application?**
  - Material facts in dispute → originating claim (O 6 r 1(2)) → [0-10](#drp-w-0-10)
  - Rules or written law require an OA, an application to Court under written law, or solely or mainly construction or a question of law with no factual dispute → originating application (O 6 r 1(3)) → [0-10](#drp-w-0-10)
- **Drafting the originating claim**
  - Form 8; personal injury needs a medical report and statement of special damages (O 6 r 5(1)–(2)) → [0-11](#drp-w-0-11)
  - Endorse with a Form 9 statement of claim; general endorsement only if limitation expires within 14 days, or in a special case (O 6 r 5(3)–(4)) → [0-11](#drp-w-0-11)
- **How long is it valid?**
  - Issued when the Registrar numbers, signs, seals and dates it; valid for service for 3 months, or 6 months if it is to be served out of Singapore (O 6 rr 2, 3(1)) → [0-12](#drp-w-0-12)
  - Not served in time: apply to extend; at most twice, 3 months each, except in a special case; good reason needed (O 6 r 3(2)–(5); *Lim Hong Kan*) → [0-12](#drp-w-0-12)
- **Drafting the originating application**
  - Form 15 (to be served) or Form 16 (no service); supporting affidavit with all necessary or material evidence (O 6 rr 11, 13) → [0-13](#drp-w-0-13)
- **Disputes of fact in an originating application? (background only — not examinable per SYL p1)**
  - Further affidavits, cross-examination, or conversion into an originating claim with directions (O 15 r 7(5)–(6)) → [0-78](#drp-w-0-78)

### drp-w-0-10 — Choosing the mode: originating claim or originating application (O 6 r 1)
*Keywords: mode of commencement, originating claim, originating application, OC, OA, O 6 r 1, material facts in dispute, construction of document, question of law, application under written law, writ, originating summons, wrong mode, conversion*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] should commence proceedings by originating claim or originating application. **C** [Client] must commence by [originating claim / originating application] under O 6 r [1(2) / 1(3)(c)] of the Rules of Court 2021, because [the material facts are in dispute / the dispute turns solely on the construction of [clause x] and the facts are agreed].
**R** A claimant may commence proceedings by originating claim or originating application unless the Rules or written law provide otherwise (O 6 r 1(1)). It must use an originating claim where the material facts are in dispute (O 6 r 1(2)). It must use an originating application where the Rules or written law require it, where the proceedings are an application to the Court under written law, or where they concern solely or primarily the construction of any written law, instrument or document or a question of law and the material facts are not in dispute (O 6 r 1(3)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 6 r 1(1): unless these Rules or any written law otherwise provide, a claimant may commence proceedings by an originating claim or an originating application.
> - O 6 r 1(2): a claimant must commence proceedings by an originating claim where the material facts are in dispute.
> - O 6 r 1(3): a claimant must commence proceedings by an originating application where
>   - (a) these Rules or any written law require it;
>   - (b) the proceedings concern an application made to the Court under any written law; or
>   - (c) the proceedings concern solely or primarily the construction of any written law, instrument or document or some question of law and the material facts are not in dispute.
> - The course's order of questions (L1 s14): (1) does written law or the ROC require an OA, or is it an application under written law? If yes, OA. (2) If not, are material facts in dispute? If yes, OC. (3) If not, is the sole or primary issue construction or a question of law? If yes, OA.
> - An OA wrongly begun where facts are disputed may be converted to an OC (O 15 r 7(6)(c); background only).

**P** No case is set on the choice of mode; apply the text of O 6 r 1 in the order the course gives (L1 s14).

**A** Ask the three questions in order. (1) Does a statute or the Rules require an originating application, or is [Client] applying under a statute (e.g. [an application under the Companies Act / for pre-action production under O 11 r 11])? If yes, use an OA (Form 15 or 16). (2) Are the material facts in dispute? [D denies liability and says the goods were not of the contract description / D alleges a variation of the delivery clause], so facts are disputed and an OC is required. (3) If the facts are agreed and the only question is [what clause x means], an OA is required. For a debt claim, even where [Client] thinks [D] has no defence, use an OC (with a Form 9 statement of claim), then apply for summary judgment once the defence is in (drp-w-0-42). **C** Therefore [Client] must commence by [originating claim in Form 8 endorsed with a statement of claim / originating application in Form 15 supported by affidavit].

*Source note: O 6 r 1 ROC p68–69; SYL p4–5 [22]–[23], [27], [32]; L1 s14, s17.*

### drp-w-0-11 — The originating claim: form, endorsement and annexures (O 6 r 5(1)–(4))
*Keywords: originating claim, Form 8, endorsement, general endorsement, concise description of the claim, statement of claim, Form 9, endorsed generally, limitation expires within 14 days, special case, personal injuries, medical report, statement of special damages, O 6 r 5*

**I** The issue is how [Client]'s originating claim should be prepared and endorsed. **C** The originating claim must be in Form 8 and should be endorsed with a statement of claim in Form 9; [Client] may endorse it generally with a concise description of the claim only because [the limitation period expires within 14 days of issue] (O 6 r 5(1), (3)–(4)).
**R** An originating claim must be in Form 8, and a personal injury claim must annex a medical report and a statement of the special damages claimed (O 6 r 5(1)–(2)). It may be endorsed generally with a concise description of the claim or with a statement of claim in Form 9, but, except in a special case, it may be endorsed generally only if the limitation period will expire within 14 days after issue (O 6 r 5(3)–(4)). If endorsed generally, the statement of claim must be served within 14 days after the originating claim is served (O 6 r 5(5)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 6 r 5(1): an originating claim must be in Form 8.
> - O 6 r 5(2): if the claim is for personal injuries, the claimant must annex a medical report and a statement of the special damages claimed to the originating claim.
> - O 6 r 5(3): an originating claim may be endorsed generally with a concise description of the claim or with a statement of claim in Form 9.
> - O 6 r 5(4): except in a special case, an originating claim may be endorsed generally only if the limitation period for the cause of action will expire within 14 days after the originating claim is issued.
> - O 6 r 5(5): where the originating claim is endorsed generally, a statement of claim must be served within 14 days after the originating claim has been served.
> - Admiralty proceedings follow different rules (O 33 r 2).

**P** No case is set; the default is a full Form 9 statement of claim, and a general endorsement is the exception (O 6 r 5(4)).

**A** If the facts show the limitation period [ends on [date], within 14 days of the intended issue date], [Client] may issue an originating claim endorsed generally ("The claimant's claim is for the sum of $X due under the contract dated [date] …") to stop time running, and must then serve a Form 9 statement of claim within 14 days after serving the originating claim. Otherwise, [Client] must endorse a full statement of claim (see drp-w-0-20 on what it must plead). If the claim is for personal injuries, annex the medical report and the statement of special damages, or the claim is irregular. **C** Therefore [Client] should issue a Form 8 originating claim endorsed with [a Form 9 statement of claim / a general endorsement, followed by a statement of claim within 14 days of service].

*Source note: O 6 r 5 ROC p70; SYL p4 [24], p7 [44]; L1 s15–s16, s25.*

### drp-w-0-12 — Issue, validity and renewal of the originating process (O 6 rr 2–3)
*Keywords: issue of originating claim, deemed issued, Registrar numbers signs seals and dates, validity, 3 months, 6 months if served out of Singapore, valid for service, renewal, extension of validity, O 6 r 3, twice, not more than 3 months each time, special case, good reason, expired originating claim, renewed for service, Lim Hong Kan v Mohd Sainudin bin Ahmad, Kleinwort Benson v Barbrak, 6 months*

**I** The issue is whether [Client]'s originating claim, issued on [date], is still valid for service and, if not, whether its validity can be extended. **C** The originating claim is valid for service for [3 months (service in Singapore) / 6 months (service out of Singapore)] beginning with [issue date], that is until [date] (O 6 r 3(1)); [Client] should apply to extend its validity under O 6 r 3(2), which the Court may grant [twice at most, by up to 3 months each time,] for good reason.
**R** An originating claim or originating application is deemed issued when the Registrar numbers, signs, seals and dates it (O 6 r 2), and is valid for service for 6 months beginning with the date of issue if it is to be served out of Singapore (with the Court's approval, or where approval is not required under a contract), and for 3 months in any other case (O 6 r 3(1)). If it has not been served on all or any of the defendants, an application may be made, before or after expiry, to extend its validity (O 6 r 3(2)), for a period beginning the day after it would otherwise expire (O 6 r 3(3)). Except in a special case, the Court may extend validity only twice and by not more than 3 months each time (O 6 r 3(4)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 6 r 2: an originating claim or an originating application is deemed issued when the Registrar numbers, signs, seals and dates it.
