> - O 6 r 3(1) (as amended by S 780/2023 with effect from 1 Dec 2023): subject to this Rule, an originating claim or an originating application is valid for service —
>   - (a) where it is to be served out of Singapore (i) with the court's approval under Order 8, Rule 1(2); or (ii) where the court's approval is not required under Order 8, Rule 1(3), for 6 months beginning with the date of its issue; or
>   - (b) in any other case, for 3 months beginning with the date of its issue.
> - Before 1 Dec 2023 every originating process was valid for 3 months only; past papers set before then (e.g. PYP23 Q1.3) assume 3 months.
> - O 6 r 3(2): an application may be made to extend the validity of the originating claim or originating application if it has not been served on all or any of the defendants before or after it expires.
> - O 6 r 3(3): the Court may order the validity to be extended by a period beginning with the day next following that on which it would otherwise expire.
> - O 6 r 3(4): except in a special case, the Court may extend the validity only twice and by not more than 3 months each time.
> - O 6 r 3(5): an originating process whose validity has been extended must be endorsed "Renewed for service for ____ months from _______ by order of Court dated ________" before it is served.
> - O 6 r 3(6): r 3(1) and (4) do not apply to Admiralty originating claims.
> - Service out: the O 8 r 1 affidavit must state whether the validity of the originating process needs to be extended (O 8 r 1(2)(c)), and the extension may be sought in the same summons as the approval to serve out (PD 63(4); drp-w-0-18).

**P** The power to extend the validity of a writ (now an originating process) should only be exercised for good reason, and whether there is good reason depends on all the circumstances; difficulty in effecting service is one possible reason, and the balance of hardship between the parties is relevant (*Lim Hong Kan v Mohd Sainudin bin Ahmad* [1992] SGCA 8 at [17]). Saving costs can be a good reason where the burden of those costs on the party is real, as for a menial labourer already facing an adverse costs order; it is "not the mere fact of a saving in costs that is relevant but their burden on the party concerned" (*Lim Hong Kan* at [26]). In *Kleinwort Benson Ltd v Barbrak Ltd* [1987] AC 597 (HL) a bank issued one writ against 164 defendants to stop time running, then deferred service to save costs while a test action decided the principle, hoping the rest would pay without being sued; the House of Lords allowed the bank's appeals and restored both extensions, so saving costs without prejudice to the defendants can be good reason. These cases were decided under the old Rules, but the White Book at [6/3/3]–[6/3/4] suggests they remain applicable under ROC 2021.

**A** First ask where the defendant is to be served. For service in Singapore, count 3 months: issued [1 July], so valid until [30 September] ("beginning with" the date of issue includes the issue date). For service out of Singapore, count 6 months under the current O 6 r 3(1)(a). (PYP23 Q1.3 was set in 2023, when the period was 3 months for every case: issued 23 July, valid until 22 October, so on 21 October the claimant had to apply at once. Under the current rule an originating claim served in Australia with approval is valid until 22 January, so a week's delay needs no extension; say so, and note the older answer.) Apply by summons, ideally before expiry; the rule permits an application after expiry, but the explanation must then be stronger. Show good reason: [the defendant is abroad and service through the foreign process agent takes time / two reasonable attempts at personal service failed and substituted service is being sought], and that [D] suffers no prejudice. Note that "settlement talks" alone, as in PYP22 Q1, are a weak reason, and that the colleague's "6 months" there is wrong because the defendant is in Singapore: 6 months applies only to process served out of Singapore (O 6 r 3(1)(a)). If this is already the third extension, [Client] must show a special case. Once renewed, endorse the originating process with the renewal words before service. **C** Therefore [Client] should apply under O 6 r 3(2) before [date] to extend the validity of the originating claim by up to 3 months, explaining [the good reason]; if serving out, it may ask for the extension in the same summons as the approval to serve out (PD 63(4)).

*Source note: O 6 rr 2–3 ROC p69; SYL p5 [25]–[26], [30]–[31]; L1 s16; Lim Hong Kan and Kleinwort Benson SYL p5 [26(d)]; Lim Hong Kan [1992] SGCA 8 at [17], [26]–[28] read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026; the SLR citation could not be matched online); Kleinwort Benson: facts from the judgment at [2]–[4], [11]–[12] and outcome at [1] (Lord Keith: "would allow these appeals", the Court of Appeal having set both extensions aside), read on vLex 9 Nov 2026, which cuts off before Lord Brandon's reasoning; the "saving costs at no prejudice to the defendants" ratio is from case summaries, not the speech itself; Cyril-Notes' account (164 defendants, saving costs) matches; tested PYP22 Q1, PYP23 Q1.3, PYP21 Q2.*

### drp-w-0-13 — The originating application: forms and supporting affidavit (O 6 rr 11, 13)
*Keywords: originating application, Form 15, Form 16, need not be served, supporting affidavit, all the evidence necessary or material, O 6 r 11, O 6 r 13, reasonable steps to serve, 14 days, 28 days*

**I** The issue is how [Client]'s originating application should be prepared. **C** It must be in Form [15 / 16] and supported by an affidavit containing all the evidence necessary or material to the claim (O 6 rr 11(1)–(3), 13).
**R** An originating application must be in Form 15 (where it has to be served) or Form 16 (where it need not be served on anyone, where written law permits), and must be supported by affidavit (O 6 r 11(1)–(3)). The affidavit must contain all the evidence that is necessary or material to the claim or to the defence (O 6 r 13). Issue, duration and renewal follow the same rules as an originating claim (O 6 rr 2–3), and reasonable steps to serve it with the supporting affidavit must be taken within 14 days of issue in Singapore, or 28 days if out of Singapore (O 6 r 11(4)–(5)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 6 r 11(1): subject to these Rules and any other written law, an originating application must be in Form 15 or Form 16, whichever is appropriate, and must be supported by affidavit.
> - O 6 r 11(2): Form 15 is to be used when the originating application has to be served.
> - O 6 r 11(3): Form 16 is to be used when the originating application need not be served on anyone, where permitted by any written law.
> - O 6 r 11(4): if it is to be served in Singapore, reasonable steps to serve the originating application and the supporting affidavit on the defendant must be made as soon as possible and in any event within 14 days after issue.
> - O 6 r 11(5): if it is to be served out of Singapore, the same within 28 days after issue.
> - O 6 r 13: an affidavit filed in an originating application must contain all the evidence that is necessary or material to the claim or to the defence.
> - For the defendant's side (affidavit within 21 days or 5 weeks, no further affidavits except in a special case, counterclaim in the affidavit), see drp-w-0-27.

**P** No case is set on the claimant's affidavit; the requirement that it contain all necessary or material evidence reflects that an OA is decided on affidavit evidence without a trial, and further affidavits are allowed only in a special case (*CZD v CZE* [2023] 5 SLR 806 at [19]–[21]; see drp-w-0-27).

**A** Choose the form: [the application under s [x] of [Act] must be served on [D]], so Form 15. Draft the supporting affidavit to put in all the evidence [Client] relies on now ([the agreement, the correspondence, the facts showing the statutory conditions are met]), because further affidavits will not be allowed later except in a special case. Then serve the OA and affidavit personally (O 6 r 4) within the O 6 r 11(4)–(5) window. **C** Therefore [Client] should file a Form [15] originating application supported by an affidavit exhibiting all the evidence material to its claim, and take reasonable steps to serve both within [14 / 28] days.

*Source note: O 6 rr 11, 13 ROC p73–74; SYL p5 [28]–[31]; L1 s17; CZD v CZE SYL p9 [61].*

### drp-w-0-78 — Converting an originating application into an originating claim (O 15 r 7(6)(c)) (background only — not examinable per SYL p1)
*Keywords: convert originating application, conversion to originating claim, disputes of fact in affidavits, O 15 r 7(5), O 15 r 7(6), further affidavits, cross-examination of deponents, wrong mode of commencement, costs schedule PD 137, background only*

**I** The issue is what the Court may do where [Client]'s originating application raises disputes of fact on the affidavits. **C** The Court may order the originating application to be converted into an originating claim, with the necessary directions (O 15 r 7(6)(c)).
**R** Originating applications are decided on affidavit evidence and submissions, without oral evidence or cross-examination, unless written law or the Court directs otherwise (O 15 r 7(5)). Where the Court considers that there are disputes of fact in the affidavits, it may order further affidavits, cross-examination of the makers of the affidavits, conversion of the originating application into an originating claim with the necessary directions, or any other appropriate order (O 15 r 7(6)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 15 r 7(5): unless written law or the Court otherwise directs, originating applications and summonses are decided on affidavit evidence and oral or written submissions, without oral evidence or cross-examination.
> - O 15 r 7(6): where the Court is of the view that there are disputes of facts in the affidavits, it may order any of the following:
>   - (a) the parties to file and serve further affidavits;
>   - (b) the makers of the affidavits to be cross-examined;
>   - (c) the originating application to be converted into an originating claim, and with the necessary directions;
>   - (d) any other appropriate order.
> - PD 137(1)(a): costs schedules are filed for trials of originating claims, including those converted from originating applications.

**P** No case is set in the syllabus for this point (SYL [32]).

**A** If [D]'s affidavit squarely contradicts [Client]'s on [whether the loan was repaid], the dispute cannot be resolved on paper: ask the Court for cross-examination if the dispute is narrow (r 7(6)(b)), or for conversion into an originating claim if it goes to the heart of the case (r 7(6)(c)), with directions for pleadings. Choosing the right mode at the start avoids this (O 6 r 1; drp-w-0-10). **C** Therefore the Court may convert the originating application into an originating claim under O 15 r 7(6)(c).

*Source note: O 15 r 7(5)–(6) ROC p136; PD 137(1) SCPD p178–179; SYL p5 [32]. ROC text from the 2021 library copy, checked against SSO (current version as at 18 Nov 2026; extraction subagent): unchanged since 2021.*

## 0.IV Service

- **Must this document be served personally?**
  - Originating claim or originating application: yes, on each defendant (O 6 r 4) → [0-14](#drp-w-0-14)
  - Other documents: personal service only if a rule, written law or the Court requires it, or the server chooses; otherwise ordinary service (O 7 r 1(1)) → [0-14](#drp-w-0-14)
- **How is personal service done, and by whom?**
  - Individual: leave a copy with the person; entity: with the chairperson, president, secretary, treasurer or other officer; or as written law or agreement allows (O 7 r 2(1)) → [0-14](#drp-w-0-14)
  - Company: also leave at or post by registered post to the registered office (Companies Act s 387) → [0-14](#drp-w-0-14)
  - Process server, solicitor, solicitor's employee, unrepresented litigant, or anyone the Registrar allows (O 7 r 2(2)) → [0-14](#drp-w-0-14)
- **Defendant evading service?**
  - Substituted service if personal service is impractical; method must be effective in bringing it to notice; 2 reasonable attempts first; affidavit; serve within 14 days of order (O 7 r 7; PD 65) → [0-15](#drp-w-0-15)
- **Ordinary service, dispensing with service, deemed time**
  - Ordinary service: address, email, fax (both sides' solicitors agree), as agreed, as directed, as written law allows (O 7 r 3) → [0-16](#drp-w-0-16)
  - Court may dispense with personal service, ordinary service or service altogether (O 7 r 1(2); *Madison Pacific*, *Xu Xiangrong*) → [0-16](#drp-w-0-16)
  - Served after 5pm: deemed served the next day (O 7 r 8) → [0-16](#drp-w-0-16)
- **How fast must I serve?**
  - Reasonable steps as soon as possible, and within 14 days of issue (Singapore) or 28 days (out of Singapore) (O 6 r 5(6)–(7), r 11(4)–(5)) → [0-17](#drp-w-0-17)
  - Not served by the first case conference: dismissal, or serve or apply for substituted service within 14 days (O 9 r 5; PD 64) → [0-17](#drp-w-0-17)
- **Defendant abroad?**
  - Court's approval needed unless a contract allows service out: summons without notice + affidavit (jurisdiction or appropriate court; where D is; validity) (O 8 r 1) → [0-18](#drp-w-0-18)
  - Appropriate court: good arguable case of sufficient nexus (PD 63(3) factors), forum conveniens, serious question to be tried (PD 63(2)) → [0-18](#drp-w-0-18)
  - Without notice: full and frank disclosure (*Manharlal*, *CZD v CZE*) → [0-18](#drp-w-0-18)
  - Manner: as contractually agreed, Civil Procedure Convention (including the Hague Service Convention through the Registrar, O 8 r 4A), foreign government, foreign judicial authority, Singapore consul, or foreign law; nothing contrary to foreign law; translation (O 8 r 2) → [0-19](#drp-w-0-19)
  - Malaysia or Brunei: also via the local court (O 8 r 3); State Courts process (O 8 r 4); foreign State via MFA (O 8 r 6) → [0-19](#drp-w-0-19)

### drp-w-0-14 — Personal service: when required, who serves and how
*Keywords: personal service, O 6 r 4, served personally on each defendant, O 7 r 1, O 7 r 2, leaving a copy, natural person, entity, chairperson, secretary, officer, process server, solicitor's employee, Companies Act s 387, registered office, registered post, memorandum of service, Form 12*

**I** The issue is how [Client] must serve the originating claim on [D]. **C** The originating claim must be served personally on [D] under O 6 r 4 of the Rules of Court 2021, by [leaving a copy with [D] personally / leaving it with a director or the company secretary of [D Pte Ltd], or leaving it at or sending it by registered post to [D Pte Ltd]'s registered office under s 387 of the Companies Act 1967].
**R** Subject to written law and the Rules, an originating claim or originating application must be served personally on each defendant (O 6 r 4). Personal service is effected on a natural person by leaving a copy with that person, on an entity by leaving a copy with its chairperson, president, secretary, treasurer or other officer, according to any written law, or in any agreed manner (O 7 r 2(1)). It may be effected by a court process server, a solicitor, a solicitor's employee, an unrepresented litigant or that litigant's employee, or anyone the Registrar allows (O 7 r 2(2)). A document may be served on a company by leaving it at or sending it by registered post to its registered office (s 387 Companies Act).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 6 r 4: subject to the provisions of any written law and these Rules, an originating claim or an originating application must be served personally on each defendant.
> - O 7 r 1(1): any document required to be served may be served by
>   - (a) personal service, where expressly required by the Rules or any written law, or where the Court orders it, or where the serving party decides to do so voluntarily; or
>   - (b) ordinary service.
> - O 7 r 2(1): personal service of a document is effected
>   - (a) on a natural person by leaving a copy with that person, or the person's agent if that person is an overseas principal under r 4;
>   - (b) on any entity by leaving a copy with the chairperson or president of the entity, or the secretary, treasurer or other officer;
>   - (c) on any person or entity according to the requirements of any written law; or
>   - (d) in any manner agreed with the person or entity to be served.
> - O 7 r 2(2): the following persons may effect personal service:
>   - (a) a process server of the Court;
>   - (b) a solicitor;
>   - (c) a solicitor's employee;
>   - (d) a litigant who is not legally represented or such a person's employee;
>   - (e) any other person that the Registrar may allow in a particular case or generally.
> - O 7 r 2(3): if the court process server effects service, the Registrar must notify the requesting person of the fact and manner of service.
> - Companies Act s 387: a document may be served on a company by leaving it at or sending it by registered post to the registered office of the company.
> - After service, file a memorandum of service (Form 12), which is needed before default judgment (O 6 r 6(6); drp-w-0-23).

**P** No case is set on the mechanics; the syllabus and lecture point to the text of O 6 r 4, O 7 r 2 and s 387 (SYL [33]–[35], [40]; L1 s19).

**A** Identify the defendant. If [D] is an individual living [at his home in Singapore], a solicitor's clerk should hand him a copy (O 7 r 2(1)(a)); leaving it in the letterbox is not personal service. If [D] is a company, either leave it with a director or the company secretary (O 7 r 2(1)(b)) or leave it at or post it by registered post to the registered office shown in its ACRA search (s 387). If [D] avoids service after reasonable attempts, apply for substituted service (drp-w-0-15). Then file the memorandum of service. **C** Therefore [Client] should serve the originating claim personally on [D] by [method], acting through [its solicitor's employee], within the time in O 6 r 5(6) (drp-w-0-17).

*Source note: O 6 r 4 ROC p69; O 7 rr 1–2 ROC p74–76; Companies Act s 387 CA p531 (informal consolidation in force from 1 Jul 2023); SYL p5–6 [33]–[35], [40]; L1 s19; Form 12 SYL p7 [50(a)]; tested PYP21 Q2, PYP22 Q1.*

### drp-w-0-15 — Substituted service (O 7 r 7; PD 65)
*Keywords: substituted service, impractical to serve personally, O 7 r 7, PD 65, 2 reasonable attempts, affidavit, effective in bringing to notice, posting on door, AR registered post, email, electronic means, advertisement, last resort, ICA, SLA, HDB, ACRA search, within 14 days after the order, evasion of service*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] may serve [D] by substituted service. **C** [Client] may apply for substituted service under O 7 r 7 of the Rules of Court 2021 if personal service is impractical, but must first make 2 reasonable attempts at personal service and show by affidavit why they were reasonable (PD 65(2)); [here only one attempt has been made, so [Client] is not yet ready to apply].
**R** If a document must be served personally and it is impractical to do so, a party may apply to serve it by substituted service, and the Court may order any method effective in bringing it to the person's notice, including electronic means; substituted service is to be effected within 14 days after the order (O 7 r 7). The applicant should persuade the Court that the method will probably be effective, should make 2 reasonable attempts at personal service before applying, and must explain by affidavit why those attempts were reasonable (PD 65(1)–(2)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 7 r 7(1): if a document is required to be served personally and it is impractical to serve it personally, a party may apply to serve it by substituted service.
> - O 7 r 7(2): the Court may order any method of substituted service that is effective in bringing the document to the notice of the person to be served, including the use of electronic means.
> - O 7 r 7(3): substituted service is to be effected within 14 days after the order of the Court.
> - PD 65(1): the applicant should persuade the Court that the proposed mode will probably be effective in bringing the document to the notice of the person to be served.
> - PD 65(2): 2 reasonable attempts at personal service should be made before an application for substituted service is filed; the applicant must demonstrate by affidavit why he or she believes the attempts made were reasonable.
> - PD 65(3): consider other modes, such as AR registered post or electronic means (email or Internet transmission), in addition to or instead of posting on doors or gates.
> - PD 65(4): an application to serve by posting at an address or by AR registered post should contain evidence (e.g. IRAS, SLA, HDB or ACRA search results) that the person is resident or can be located there.
> - PD 65(5): service by AR registered post is deemed effected when the postal service delivered, or attempted to deliver, the document.
> - PD 65(6): for service by email, show that the email account belongs to the person and is currently active.
> - PD 65(7): advertisement in the Straits Times (and a vernacular paper if language literacy is unknown) is a last resort, with evidence of literacy in the newspaper's language.
> - Substituted service, and the application for it, is on the list of applications that need no prior approval (O 9 r 9(7)(b)).

**P** Substituted service under O 7 r 7 "is distinct from dispensation of personal service under O 7 r 1(2)" (*Xu Xiangrong v Fu Xianwei* [2025] SGHC 95 at [38]; see drp-w-0-16).

**A** Count the attempts. [One attempt at D's home on 6 July failed] (PYP22 Q1), so a second reasonable attempt is needed first, ideally at a different time of day (e.g. [an evening or weekend]: PD 64(2)(b) treats the day and time of attempts as relevant). Then file the application (a summons, with no prior approval needed: O 9 r 9(7)(b)) with an affidavit that: describes each attempt (date, time, who answered, what was said); explains why they were reasonable; gives evidence that [D] lives at [address] (PD 65(4) searches); and proposes a mode likely to reach [D], such as [posting at the door plus AR registered post, or email to [D]'s active address used in the parties' correspondence (PD 65(6))]. Once the order is made, effect substituted service within 14 days (O 7 r 7(3)), and remember that the originating claim's 3-month validity keeps running (drp-w-0-12). **C** Therefore [Client] should make a second reasonable attempt at personal service and, if it fails, apply under O 7 r 7 with a PD 65 affidavit for substituted service by [mode].

*Source note: O 7 r 7 ROC p78; PD 65(1)–(7) SCPD p76–77; PD 64(2) SCPD p75; O 9 r 9(7)(b) ROC p87; Xu Xiangrong [2025] SGHC 95 at [38] read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026; the syllabus spells it "Xu Xiang Rong"); SYL p6 [36]; L1 s19; tested PYP22 Q1 (model: PYP22-Xav Q1), PYP25 Q5 (WA affidavit).*

### drp-w-0-16 — Ordinary service; dispensing with service; time of service
*Keywords: ordinary service, O 7 r 3, usual or last known address, solicitor's business address, registered office, email address provided, fax, agreed manner, Court may direct, electronic means, dispense with service, O 7 r 1(2), Madison Pacific Trust v PT Dewata Wibawa, Xu Xiang Rong v Fu Xianwei, time of service, O 7 r 8, after 5pm, deemed served next day*

**I** The issue is [how [Party] may serve [document] / whether service can be dispensed with / when service was effected]. **C** [The document may be served by ordinary service under O 7 r 3 / The Court may dispense with service under O 7 r 1(2) / Service at [7.45pm] is deemed effected on the following day under O 7 r 8(2)].
**R** A document not requiring personal service may be served by ordinary service: left at or posted to the person's usual or last known address or solicitor's business address (or an entity's registered or principal office), by email to an address the party provided, by fax between solicitors who agree, in an agreed manner, as the Court directs, or as written law provides (O 7 r 3). In an appropriate case the Court may dispense with personal service, ordinary service, or service altogether (O 7 r 1(2)). Service before 5pm is effected that day; after 5pm, the following day (O 7 r 8).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 7 r 3: ordinary service of a document may be effected
>   - (a) by leaving the document at or posting it to
>     - (i) for a natural person, the person's usual or last known address or the business address of the person's solicitor; or
>     - (ii) for an entity, its registered or principal office or, if none exists, its last known place of business or its solicitor's address;
>   - (b) by electronic mail at the electronic mail address provided by the party to be served;
>   - (c) by fax, but only if (i) both the serving party and the party to be served act by solicitor; and (ii) the solicitor for the party to be served indicates willingness to accept service at a specified fax number and the document is sent to it;
>   - (d) in any manner agreed between the parties;
>   - (e) in any manner which the Court may direct, including electronic means; or
>   - (f) in any manner provided under any written law.
> - O 7 r 1(2): the Court may, in an appropriate case, dispense with personal service or with ordinary service or with service altogether.
> - O 7 r 8(1): subject to written law and the Rules (including Order 28 on electronic filing), service effected before 5pm on a day is deemed effected that day.
> - O 7 r 8(2): service effected after 5pm on a day is deemed effected on the following day.

**P** It is an appropriate case to dispense with personal service "if personal service can reasonably be expected to be difficult or disproportionately time-consuming and the mode of ordinary service to be employed would be as effective in ensuring that the recipient is notified of the documents"; no prior attempts at personal service are required, and the Court may direct ordinary service by any effective means, including email (*Madison Pacific Trust Ltd v PT Dewata Wibawa* [2024] SGHC 184 at [56]–[58]; there, email service on the Singapore solicitors of a defendant in Indonesia was upheld). Dispensing with personal service is distinct from substituted service (*Xu Xiangrong v Fu Xianwei* [2025] SGHC 95 at [38]), and where the documents are served on someone in Singapore, no approval to serve out is needed (*Madison Pacific* at [61]–[62]; *Xu Xiangrong* at [39]–[40]).

**A** For an interlocutory document (e.g. [a summons and affidavit after D has appointed solicitors]), use ordinary service on [D's solicitors] by [email to the address they gave] (O 7 r 3(b)); personal service is not needed. For the originating process, which must be served personally, apply under O 7 r 1(2) to dispense with personal service where personal service can reasonably be expected to be difficult or disproportionately time-consuming (e.g. [D is overseas but has Singapore solicitors acting for it]) and the proposed ordinary service (e.g. [email to those solicitors]) would be as effective in notifying [D] (*Madison Pacific* at [57]); prior attempts at personal service are not required; contrast substituted service, which is about choosing another effective method when personal service is impractical (drp-w-0-15). On timing, if [the process server left the papers at 7.45pm on 4 December], service is deemed on 5 December, and every deadline counts from then (O 7 r 8(2); O 3 r 3). **C** Therefore [Party] may serve by [ordinary service / apply to dispense with service], and service is deemed effected on [date].

*Source note: O 7 rr 1, 3, 8 ROC p74–78; SYL p6 [37]–[39]; L1 s19–s20; Madison Pacific [2024] SGHC 184 (Chua Lee Ming J) at [55]–[62] and Xu Xiangrong [2025] SGHC 95 (Tan Siong Thye SJ) at [38]–[40], read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026); tested PYP25 Q5 (evening attempts, WA).*

### drp-w-0-17 — Duty to take reasonable steps to serve; failure to serve by the case conference
*Keywords: reasonable steps to serve, as soon as possible, within 14 days after issue, within 28 days, out of Singapore, O 6 r 5(6), O 6 r 5(7), O 6 r 11(4), O 6 r 11(5), failure to serve, O 9 r 5, dismiss the action, second case conference, serve within 14 days, apply for substituted service, PD 64, affidavit relating to service attempts, expeditiously*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] has served the originating claim fast enough and what happens if it has not. **C** [Client] had to take reasonable steps to serve [D] as soon as possible and within [14 / 28] days after the originating claim was issued (O 6 r 5(6) / (7)); if it has not served [D] by the first case conference, the Court may dismiss the action or order service, or an application for substituted service, within 14 days (O 9 r 5).
**R** If an originating claim is to be served in Singapore, reasonable steps to serve must be made as soon as possible and in any event within 14 days after issue; if out of Singapore, within 28 days (O 6 r 5(6)–(7)); the same applies to an originating application and its affidavit (O 6 r 11(4)–(5)). If the claimant attends the first case conference without having served, the Court may dismiss the action if not satisfied that reasonable steps were taken expeditiously, or fix a second case conference and order service, or an application for substituted service, within 14 days, extending validity if needed; failure to serve by the second case conference may lead to dismissal (O 9 r 5).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 6 r 5(6): if the originating claim is to be served in Singapore, reasonable steps to serve on the defendant must be made as soon as possible and, in any event, within 14 days after the originating claim is issued.
> - O 6 r 5(7): if it is to be served out of Singapore, reasonable steps must be made as soon as possible and, in any event, within 28 days after issue.
> - O 6 r 11(4)–(5): the same for an originating application and its supporting affidavit (14 days in Singapore; 28 days out of Singapore).
> - O 9 r 5(1): if the claimant attends the case conference but has not served the originating claim or originating application on the defendant, the Court
>   - (a) may dismiss the action if it is not satisfied that the claimant has taken reasonable steps to effect service expeditiously; or
>   - (b) may fix a second case conference and order the claimant to
>     - (i) serve the originating process within 14 days after the date of the first case conference; or
>     - (ii) apply for substituted service within that time,
>   - and extend the validity of the originating process if necessary.
> - O 9 r 5(2): if the claimant fails to serve the originating process by the second case conference, the Court may dismiss the action.
> - PD 64(2): whether reasonable steps were taken depends on the facts; non-exhaustive factors:
>   - (a) the mode(s) chosen to effect personal service;
>   - (b) the day and time personal service was attempted;
>   - (c) the number of attempts;
>   - (d) the reason(s) each attempt failed;
>   - (e) whether an application for substituted service has been or will be taken out.
> - PD 64(3): the Court may require an affidavit relating to service attempts showing that the steps taken were reasonable and expeditious.

**P** No case is set; the test is factual, guided by the PD 64(2) factors.

**A** Count from issue: issued [1 July], so reasonable steps had to start within 14 days, by [15 July] (O 3 r 3). Here [one attempt was made on 6 July, then service was "paused" for settlement talks until 26 September] (PYP22 Q1). Pausing for negotiations is not a step towards service; the claimant risks dismissal at the first case conference (8 weeks after issue: O 9 r 1(1)(a)) under O 9 r 5(1)(a) unless it can show reasonable steps. Advise: make further attempts at once (varying day and time), apply for substituted service if they fail, and prepare an affidavit of service attempts addressing each PD 64(2) factor. Also extend the originating claim's validity before it expires (3 months for service in Singapore: drp-w-0-12). **C** Therefore [Client] has [not] complied with O 6 r 5(6), and should [serve or apply for substituted service immediately] to avoid dismissal under O 9 r 5.

*Source note: O 6 r 5(6)–(7) ROC p70; O 6 r 11(4)–(5) ROC p73; O 9 r 5 ROC p85; PD 64 SCPD p75; SYL p6 [41]; L1 s20; Conflict: Cyril-Exam ("Reasonable steps to serve") gives 24 days for service out; O 6 r 5(7) ROC says 28 days, and the pack follows the ROC and L1 s20; tested PYP21 Q2, PYP22 Q1.*

### drp-w-0-18 — Service out of Singapore: the Court's approval (O 8 r 1; PD 63)
*Keywords: service out of jurisdiction, service out of Singapore, O 8 r 1, Court's approval, leave to serve out, summons without notice, supporting affidavit, appropriate court, has jurisdiction, where the defendant may be found, validity of originating process, PD 63, good arguable case, sufficient nexus, forum conveniens, serious question to be tried, PD 63(3) factors, contract made in Singapore, governed by Singapore law, jurisdiction clause, breach in Singapore, tort in Singapore, full and frank disclosure, Manharlal Trikamdas Mody v Sumikin Bussan, CZD v CZE, approval not required under contract*

**I** The issue is whether, and how, [Client] can serve the originating claim on [D] in [Australia]. **C** [Client] must obtain the Court's approval under O 8 r 1 of the Rules of Court 2021, by a summons without notice supported by an affidavit showing that the Singapore Court is the appropriate court to hear the action: a good arguable case of sufficient nexus to Singapore, that Singapore is the forum conveniens, and a serious question to be tried (PD 63(2)).
**R** An originating process may be served out of Singapore with the Court's approval if it can be shown that the Court has jurisdiction or is the appropriate court to hear the action (O 8 r 1(1)). The claimant must apply by summons without notice supported by an affidavit stating why the Court has jurisdiction or is the appropriate court, where the defendant is or probably may be found, and whether the validity of the originating process needs to be extended (O 8 r 1(2)). Approval is not needed if a contract between the parties allows service out (O 8 r 1(3)). To show the Court is the appropriate court, the affidavit should show a good arguable case of sufficient nexus to Singapore, that Singapore is the forum conveniens, and a serious question to be tried on the merits (PD 63(2)), referring to the PD 63(3) nexus factors.

> **Rule in full**
> - O 8 r 1(1): an originating process or other court document may be served out of Singapore with the Court's approval if it can be shown that the Court has the jurisdiction or is the appropriate court to hear the action.
> - O 8 r 1(2): to obtain approval, the claimant must apply by summons without notice supported by affidavit which must state
>   - (a) why the Court has the jurisdiction or is the appropriate court to hear the action;
>   - (b) in which country or place the defendant is, or probably may be found; and
>   - (c) whether the validity of the originating process needs to be extended.
> - O 8 r 1(3): the Court's approval is not required if service out of Singapore is allowed under a contract between the parties.
> - O 8 r 1(4): once approval is granted for the originating process, no further approval is needed for other court documents.
> - PD 63(4) (added after the 1 Apr 2022 version): the claimant should consider whether to apply also to extend the validity of the originating process; if it can show the process will not or is not likely to be served out of Singapore before it expires, it is to consider seeking the extension in a single summons together with the application for approval to serve out, for a period appropriate to the time likely needed for service.
> - Validity of process to be served out: 6 months from issue (O 6 r 3(1)(a); drp-w-0-12).
> - PD 63(2): to show the Court is the appropriate court, include relevant information showing that
>   - (a) there is a good arguable case that there is sufficient nexus to Singapore;
>   - (b) Singapore is the forum conveniens; and
>   - (c) there is a serious question to be tried on the merits of the claim.
> - PD 63(3), non-exhaustive nexus factors, including:
>   - (a) relief is sought against a person domiciled, ordinarily resident or carrying on business in Singapore, or who has property in Singapore;
>   - (b) an injunction is sought ordering the defendant to do or refrain from doing anything in Singapore;
>   - (c) the claim is against a person duly served in or outside Singapore, and a person outside Singapore is a necessary or proper party;
>   - (d) the claim is to enforce, rescind or otherwise affect a contract, or for damages or other relief for breach of a contract which
>     - (i) was made in Singapore, or as a result of an essential step taken in Singapore;
>     - (ii) was made by or through an agent trading or residing in Singapore for a principal outside Singapore;
>     - (iii) is by its terms or by implication governed by Singapore law; or
>     - (iv) contains a term that the Court will have jurisdiction over any action on the contract;
>   - (e) the claim is for a breach committed in Singapore of a contract made in or outside Singapore;
>   - (f) the claim is founded on a tort constituted at least in part by an act or omission in Singapore, or for damage suffered in Singapore caused by a tort wherever committed;
>   - (g)–(t) other heads, including (o) restitution or fiduciary claims arising from acts in Singapore, (p) a cause of action arising in Singapore, (q) contribution or indemnity for a liability enforceable here, and (r) matters in which the defendant has submitted or agreed to submit to the Court's jurisdiction.
> - Application without notice: the applicant must disclose all material matters within its knowledge, even if prejudicial to its claim.

**P** An applicant for approval without notice owes a duty of full and frank disclosure (*Manharlal Trikamdas Mody v Sumikin Bussan International (HK) Ltd* [2014] 3 SLR 1161 at [78]–[84], decided under ROC 2014); under ROC 2021 the Court reaffirmed that in any application without notice the applicant must disclose all matters within its knowledge which might be material, even if prejudicial to its claim (*CZD v CZE* [2023] 5 SLR 806 at [46]). Approval may be granted without a jurisdiction clause, and Singapore governing law is relevant to nexus (SQ 2023-S rubric).

**A** First, check O 8 r 1(3): if the contract provides for service out (e.g. [an agreed address for service abroad]), no approval is needed. Otherwise apply by summons without notice (Form 2; no prior approval needed, O 9 r 9(7)(c)) with an affidavit covering O 8 r 1(2)(a)–(c). On nexus, map facts to PD 63(3): [the contract was concluded at a trade fair in Singapore] (63(3)(d)(i)); [it is governed by Singapore law] (63(3)(d)(iii)); [it has a non-exclusive Singapore jurisdiction clause] (63(3)(d)(iv)); [payment was due to a Singapore bank account, so the breach occurred in Singapore] (63(3)(e)). On forum conveniens, point to the governing law, where the contract was made and performed, and where witnesses and documents are. On the merits, show a serious question to be tried: [D accepted the goods and has not paid $X]. Disclose matters adverse to [Client], such as [D's complaint that the containers were not food grade under Australian standards]; failure risks the approval being set aside. State whether validity needs extending (O 8 r 1(2)(c)); if service abroad will take more than 6 months, ask for the extension in the same summons (PD 63(4); drp-w-0-12). Then serve in a manner O 8 r 2 permits (drp-w-0-19). **C** Therefore [Client] should apply by summons without notice under O 8 r 1(2), and approval is likely because there is a good arguable case of nexus under PD 63(3)(d), Singapore is the natural forum, and there is a serious question to be tried.

*Source note: O 8 r 1 ROC p78–79; PD 63(1)–(3) SCPD p72–75; O 9 r 9(7)(c) ROC p87; SYL p6–7 [42]; L1 s21–s22 (Form 2); Manharlal and CZD v CZE SYL p7 [42(e)]; SQ p48 Q1.2 and rubric p117–118; PD 63(4) and O 6 r 3(1)(a): current SCPD (epd2021-supremecourt.judiciary.gov.sg, last updated 15 Jul 2026) and SSO ROC (as at 1 Apr 2026); Cyril-QS's "SCPD 63(4)" is correct for the current PD (the 1 Apr 2022 library copy predates it); tested PYP23 Q1.1, PYP25 Q1.2, SQ 2023-S Q1.2, PYP21 Q2.*

### drp-w-0-19 — How to serve out of Singapore (O 8 rr 2–4, 6)
*Keywords: method of service out, O 8 r 2, manner contractually agreed, Civil Procedure Convention, Third Schedule, foreign government, judicial authority, consular authority, law of the foreign country, contrary to the laws of the foreign country, translation, certified translation, Malaysia, Brunei, O 8 r 3, State Courts process, O 8 r 4, foreign State, O 8 r 6, State Immunity Act, Ministry of Foreign Affairs, left at front door, registered office abroad, Australian law*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] may serve the originating claim on [D] in [Australia] by [leaving it at D's registered office / at the front door of D's office]. **C** That method is proper only if it is a manner contractually agreed between the parties, or is permitted by the law of [Australia], under O 8 r 2(1)(a) or (f); [Client] should obtain [Australian] legal advice to confirm it.
**R** Once approval is obtained, service out may be effected in the manner contractually agreed, under a Civil Procedure Convention, through the foreign government or judicial authority if willing, through a Singapore consular authority, or according to the law of the foreign country (O 8 r 2(1)). Nothing may be done contrary to the law of the foreign country (O 8 r 2(6)), and documents need a certified translation unless English is an official language (O 8 r 2(4)–(5)). A defendant in Malaysia or Brunei may also be served through the local court (O 8 r 3).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 8 r 2(1): where the Court's approval has been obtained under r 1(2), service out of Singapore may be effected
>   - (a) according to the manner contractually agreed between the parties;
>   - (b) where there is a Civil Procedure Convention governing service in the foreign country —
>     - (i) for the Hague Service Convention, in accordance with r 4A or r 6, or (subject to any objection by the receiving Contracting State) by the methods in (a), (c), (d), (e) or (f);
>     - (ii) for any other Civil Procedure Convention, according to the manner provided in that convention;
>     - (iii) where both apply, under either (i) or (ii);
>   - (c) through the government of the foreign country if that government is willing to effect service;
>   - (d) through the judicial authority of the foreign country if that authority is willing to effect service;
>   - (e) through a Singapore consular authority in the foreign country seeking the assistance of the relevant authority in that foreign country to effect service;
>   - (f) according to the manner provided by the law of that foreign country.
> - O 8 r 2(2): unless a convention, treaty or foreign authority requires the documents to come from the Singapore Government or judicial authority, the serving party, who must engage a solicitor, may send them to the entities in (c)–(e).
> - O 8 r 2(3): where they must be sent from the Singapore Government, the solicitor sends them to the Registrar to forward to the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, stating the method of service.
> - O 8 r 2(4)–(5): every document served abroad must be accompanied by a translation into the official language (unless English is an official language), certified by a qualified translator giving full name, address and qualifications.
> - O 8 r 2(6): nothing is to be done under this Rule that is contrary to the laws of the foreign country.
> - O 8 r 3: where the defendant is in Malaysia or Brunei Darussalam, the originating process (a) may be served under r 2; or (b) may be sent by the Registrar to the Magistrate, Registrar or other officer of a court with civil jurisdiction where the defendant is, and if returned endorsed with service and an affidavit of service, is deemed duly served.
> - O 8 r 4: State Courts originating process to be served out (other than Malaysia or Brunei) goes from the State Courts Registrar to the Supreme Court Registrar and is served under the Supreme Court rules.
> - O 8 r 4A (new from 1 Dec 2023): service under Article 3 of the Hague Service Convention on a person (not a State) in a Contracting State with a known address: file in the Registry a request in Part 1 of the Model Form, with a sealed copy, a summary (Part 3 of the Model Form), translations under r 2(4)–(5) and copies; the Registrar sends them to that State's central authority; r 4A prevails over inconsistent provisions of Order 8.
> - O 8 r 6: to serve a foreign State (State Immunity Act s 16), after approval, file a request for the Ministry of Foreign Affairs to arrange service, a sealed copy and a certified translation, through a solicitor; or use a method the State agreed (s 14(6) State Immunity Act); if the State is a Hague Service Convention Contracting State, add a summary in Part 3 of the Model Form (r 6(6)–(7)).

**P** The 2023-S rubric credits: proper if contractually agreed, or if permitted by the relevant foreign (Australian) law; the need to ask whether A agreed to the method; and the advice to obtain Australian legal advice (SQ p117).

**A** Work down O 8 r 2(1). (a) Did the contract provide a method or address for service? [The facts are silent: ask.] (b) Is [Australia] party to a Civil Procedure Convention in the Third Schedule, or to the Hague Service Convention? If the Hague Service Convention applies, service may go through the Registrar to that State's central authority (O 8 r 4A), or by the other methods unless that State has objected (O 8 r 2(1)(b)(i)); check its declarations. (c)–(e) Service through the foreign government, its courts or a Singapore consul is slow but safe. (f) Is leaving the papers at [D]'s registered office or front door a manner permitted by [Australian] law? This needs [Australian] legal advice; if permitted, service is valid; if not, it is irregular and [D] may apply to set it aside, which needs no prior approval (O 9 r 9(7)(d)). Check translation: none is needed if English is an official language of [the foreign country] (O 8 r 2(4)). Remember the 28-day reasonable-steps limit (O 6 r 5(7)) and the 6-month validity of process served out (O 6 r 3(1)(a)). **C** Therefore leaving the originating claim at [D]'s office was proper only if [D] agreed to it or [Australian] law permits it; [Client / Z] should obtain [Australian] legal advice and, if the service is irregular, [apply to set it aside / re-serve properly].

*Source note: O 8 rr 2–4, 6 ROC p79–83, read with the current text on SSO (as at 1 Apr 2026): O 8 r 2(1)(b), (e), r 4A and r 6(6)–(7) were amended or added by S 780/2023 with effect from 1 Dec 2023, so the 2021 library copy is out of date on them; O 9 r 9(7)(d) ROC p87; SYL p7 [43]; L1 s23; SQ p48 Q1.1 and rubric p117; tested PYP25 Q1.3, SQ 2023-S Q1.1, PYP23 Q1.1, PYP21 Q2.*

## 0.V Pleadings and the defendant's response

- **Statement of claim**
  - Endorsed on the originating claim; if endorsed generally, serve it within 14 days after serving the originating claim (O 6 r 5(4)–(5)) → [0-20](#drp-w-0-20)
  - Form 9: material facts; particulars of misrepresentation, fraud, breach of trust, wilful deceit, undue influence and states of mind; no evidence, law or argument; relief, interest, costs; certified true by client and solicitor → [0-20](#drp-w-0-20)
  - "Can I just plead that a representation was made, with no details?" No: particulars required; and see amendment risks → [0-20](#drp-w-0-20), [0-37](#drp-w-0-37)
- **Defendant served: what next, and by when?** (counted from service of the statement of claim)
  - Notice of intention to contest or not contest (Form 10): 14 days (Singapore) / 21 days (out); not a submission to jurisdiction (O 6 r 6(1)–(4)) → [0-21](#drp-w-0-21)
  - Defence (Form 13): 21 days (Singapore) / 5 weeks (out) (O 6 r 7(1)–(3)) → [0-22](#drp-w-0-22)
  - Want to contest jurisdiction (another forum, arbitration)? Defence stating the ground instead of the merits; not a submission (O 6 r 7(4)–(6)) → [0-24](#drp-w-0-24)
  - Counterclaim: with the defence, Form 13 (O 6 r 8); claimant's defence to counterclaim within 14 days (O 6 r 9) → [0-25](#drp-w-0-25)
- **Defendant missed a deadline**
  - No notice, or notice says not contesting: judgment in Form 11 + memorandum of service Form 12 (O 6 r 6(5)–(6); PD 108) → [0-23](#drp-w-0-23)
  - No defence: judgment in default of defence, Form 14 (O 6 r 7(7)); no defence to counterclaim (O 6 r 9(3)) → [0-23](#drp-w-0-23)
  - Interest at 5.33% from the originating process (O 6 r 6(7), r 7(8)) → [0-23](#drp-w-0-23)
- **Reply or further pleadings?**
  - None without the Court's approval at a case conference; not ordered if they merely deny or repeat (O 6 r 10; O 9 r 15) → [0-26](#drp-w-0-26)
- **Originating application: the defendant's side**
  - Defendant's affidavit 21 days / 5 weeks; jurisdiction challenge by affidavit; no further affidavits except in a special case; counterclaim inside the affidavit (O 6 rr 12–14; *CZD v CZE*) → [0-27](#drp-w-0-27)

### drp-w-0-20 — The statement of claim: time, form and contents (Form 9)
*Keywords: statement of claim, SOC, Form 9, O 6 r 5(3), O 6 r 5(5), within 14 days after service, material facts, particulars of misrepresentation, fraud, breach of trust, wilful deceit, undue influence, condition of mind, not plead evidence, points of law, legal arguments, relief, interest, contractual interest, costs, certification, true to the best of knowledge, plead oral representation, bare pleading*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can plead [that Mrs Y represented the premises were approved for use as a pub] without further detail. **C** No. A statement of claim in Form 9 must plead the material facts and, for a misrepresentation, its particulars; a bare assertion that a representation was made would be deficient and exposed to a request for particulars or striking out.
**R** An originating claim may be endorsed with a statement of claim in Form 9 (O 6 r 5(3)); if it was endorsed generally, the statement of claim must be served within 14 days after the originating claim is served (O 6 r 5(5)). Form 9 requires a succinct and precise account of the facts in numbered paragraphs; the material facts, particulars of any misrepresentation, fraud, breach of trust, wilful deceit or undue influence, and particulars of any alleged condition of mind (other than knowledge) must be pleaded; evidence, points of law and legal argument must not be; and the relief, interest and costs claimed must be stated. The claimant and solicitor certify that the statements are true to the best of the claimant's knowledge and belief.

> **Rule in full**
> - O 6 r 5(3): an originating claim may be endorsed generally with a concise description of the claim or with a statement of claim in Form 9.
> - O 6 r 5(5): where the originating claim is endorsed generally, a statement of claim must be served within 14 days after the originating claim has been served.
> - Form 9 (Statement of Claim, O 6 r 5(3)):
>   - 1. provide a succinct and precise account of the facts justifying the claim in numbered paragraphs;
>   - 2. the following must be pleaded
>     - (a) material facts;
>     - (b) particulars of any misrepresentation, fraud, breach of trust, wilful deceit or undue influence; and
>     - (c) particulars of facts where there is an allegation as to the condition of the mind of any person, whether any disorder or disability of mind, or any malice, fraudulent intention or other condition of mind except knowledge;
>   - 3. the following should not be pleaded
>     - (a) evidence by which material facts are to be proved;
>     - (b) points of law; and
>     - (c) legal arguments and submissions;
>   - 4. claims: the relief or remedy, including interest and costs, in numbered paragraphs;
>   - 5. whether the interest claimed is contractual, the rate and the period;
>   - 6. whether the costs claimed are contractual and the amount;
>   - 7. for personal injuries, enclose a medical report and statement of special damages; a claim for provisional damages must be pleaded;
>   - certification: the claimant certifies that all statements are true to the best of his knowledge and belief, and the solicitor certifies that he has informed the claimant of that obligation.

**P** Particulars must distinguish material facts, which must be pleaded, from evidence, which must not (*Sharikat Logistics Pte Ltd v Ong Boon Chuan* [2011] SGHC 196; see drp-w-0-36). Under ROC 2021, any amendment of material facts may lead the Court to draw inferences (O 9 r 14(6); drp-w-0-37).

**A** For a misrepresentation claim, plead: who made the representation ([Mrs Y]), to whom ([A]), when and where ([at the viewing on [date]]), how ([orally / in the listing]), its words or substance ([that the premises were approved by URA for use as a pub]), that it was false ([URA's letter of [date] shows approval for general F&B only]), reliance and inducement ([A signed the lease on [date] because of it]), and loss. A bare plea that "Mrs Y represented the premises were approved" omits the particulars Form 9 para 2(b) requires; [D] will seek further and better particulars (O 9 r 13) or strike out. If [A] cannot recall the details, plead what he can truthfully certify, and get instructions from documents first: pleading "oral representations" now and changing it later is risky because the client certifies the pleading as true, and an amendment to material facts may lead the Court to draw adverse inferences (O 9 r 14(6)), apart from needing permission and costs (drp-w-0-37 to 0-38). State the relief, whether interest is contractual (e.g. [7% under cl 17]) and costs. **C** Therefore [A] should plead the representation with full particulars in a Form 9 statement of claim, not a bare allegation.

*Source note: O 6 r 5(3), (5) ROC p70; Form 9 (Forms: SUPCT_new PDs (Appendix A - Form 9).pdf p1); SYL p7 [44]–[46]; L1 s25–s26; O 9 r 14(6) ROC p92; Sharikat SYL p12 [89(b)]; tested PYP23 Q2.1, PYP25 Q2.1, PYP22 Q3 (interest not pleaded).*

### drp-w-0-21 — Notice of intention to contest or not contest (O 6 r 6(1)–(4))
*Keywords: notice of intention to contest or not contest, Form 10, O 6 r 6, within 14 days after the statement of claim is served, 21 days served out of Singapore, appearance, entering appearance, not a submission to jurisdiction, waiver of improper service*

**I** The issue is what [D] must do first after being served with the originating claim and statement of claim, and by when. **C** [D] must file and serve a notice of intention to contest or not contest in Form 10 within [14 days (served in Singapore) / 21 days (served out of Singapore)] after the statement of claim was served, that is by [date] (O 6 r 6(1)–(3)); doing so does not submit [D] to the jurisdiction (O 6 r 6(4)).
**R** A defendant served in Singapore must file and serve a notice of intention to contest or not contest within 14 days after the statement of claim is served; one served out of Singapore, within 21 days (O 6 r 6(1)–(2)). The notice is in Form 10 (O 6 r 6(3)), and filing and serving it is not a submission to jurisdiction or a waiver of any improper service (O 6 r 6(4)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 6 r 6(1): a defendant who is served an originating claim in Singapore must file and serve a notice of intention to contest or not contest within 14 days after the statement of claim is served on the defendant.
> - O 6 r 6(2): a defendant who is served out of Singapore must file and serve such a notice within 21 days after the statement of claim is served on the defendant.
> - O 6 r 6(3): the notice must be in Form 10.
> - O 6 r 6(4): the filing and service of such a notice is not treated as a submission to jurisdiction or a waiver of any improper service of the originating claim.
> - O 6 r 6(5)–(7): consequences of default (drp-w-0-23).

**P** The 2023-S rubric credits "notice of intention to contest within 21 days of service of the statement of claim" for a defendant served in Australia (SQ p118).

**A** Fix the trigger: time runs from service of the statement of claim, not of the originating claim (they are usually the same day). [B was served in Australia on 28 October], so the notice is due 21 days later, on [18 November] (O 3 r 3(3)). [D] should file it even though [D] intends to challenge jurisdiction or prefers [the Australian courts], because O 6 r 6(4) protects [D] from being treated as submitting, and failing to file exposes [D] to default judgment (O 6 r 6(5)). The next step is the defence (drp-w-0-22) or a defence stating the jurisdictional challenge (drp-w-0-24). **C** Therefore [D] should file and serve a Form 10 notice by [date], indicating it intends to contest, without thereby submitting to the jurisdiction.

*Source note: O 6 r 6(1)–(4) ROC p70–71; SYL p7 [47]–[49]; L1 s27–s28; SQ p118 rubric; tested PYP23 Q1.4, PYP25 Q1.4, SQ 2023-S Q1.3.*

### drp-w-0-22 — The defence on the merits: time, form and contents (Form 13)
*Keywords: defence, Form 13, O 6 r 7, within 21 days after the statement of claim is served, 5 weeks, served out of Singapore, paragraph-by-paragraph response, denial, non-admission, admission, matters to be specifically pleaded, limitation, illegality, fraud, performance, release, surprise, extension by consent 14 days*

**I** The issue is when and in what form [D] must file its defence. **C** [D] must file and serve its defence in Form 13 within [21 days (served in Singapore) / 5 weeks (served out of Singapore)] after service of the statement of claim, that is by [date] (O 6 r 7(1)–(3)).
**R** A defendant served in Singapore must file and serve a defence within 21 days after the statement of claim is served; one served out of Singapore, within 5 weeks (O 6 r 7(1)–(2)). The defence must be in Form 13 (O 6 r 7(3)), which requires a paragraph-by-paragraph response to the statement of claim, explaining denials and non-admissions and the essential facts relied on, and specifically pleading any matter (such as performance, release, limitation, fraud or illegality) that makes the claim unmaintainable, might take the claimant by surprise, or raises new issues of fact.

> **Rule in full**
> - O 6 r 7(1): a defendant who is served in Singapore must file and serve a defence to the originating claim within 21 days after the statement of claim is served on the defendant.
> - O 6 r 7(2): a defendant who is served out of Singapore must file and serve a defence within 5 weeks after the statement of claim is served.
> - O 6 r 7(3): the defence must be in Form 13.
> - Form 13 (Defence / Defence and Counterclaim / Defence to Counterclaim):
>   - 1. a paragraph-by-paragraph response to the whole statement of claim (the last pleading incorporates all previous pleadings);
>   - 2–4. state whether the defendant contends the Court has no jurisdiction, or should not exercise jurisdiction, and whether the proceedings should be stayed or struck out or other relief granted;
>   - 5–6. state whether the defendant admits the claim and will pay, or admits it but offers an alternative remedy;
>   - 7. succinctly and precisely explain denials and non-admissions and the essential facts relied on;
>   - 8. state specifically any matter (e.g. performance, release, any relevant statute of limitation, fraud or any fact showing illegality)
>     - (a) which makes any aspect of the statement of claim unmaintainable;
>     - (b) which, if not specifically stated, might take the claimant by surprise; or
>     - (c) which raises issues of fact not arising out of the statement of claim;
>   - 9. plead material facts and particulars of misrepresentation, fraud, breach of trust, wilful deceit, undue influence and conditions of mind;
>   - 10. do not plead evidence, points of law, or legal arguments.
> - O 3 r 4(3): the time to file a pleading may be extended once by written consent for up to 14 days.

**P** No case is set. The defence fixes the issues for trial: a defence that pleads bare denials without explaining them invites a request for particulars (drp-w-0-36) or summary judgment (drp-w-0-42 to 0-43).

**A** Count from service of the statement of claim: [served in Singapore on [date] → defence due [date + 21 days]; served in Australia on 28 October → due 5 weeks later, [2 December]]. Plead to every paragraph. Specifically plead the matters Form 13 para 8 requires, such as [illegality of the moneylending transactions, duress, the alleged variation of the delivery clause, or a limitation defence], or they may be shut out. If [D] needs more time, seek a single 14-day extension by written consent (O 3 r 4(3)) before the deadline, otherwise apply to Court. If [D] has a counterclaim, it must be filed with the defence (drp-w-0-25). If [D] wants to contest jurisdiction, file a defence stating that ground instead (drp-w-0-24). **C** Therefore [D] must file and serve a Form 13 defence by [date], responding to each paragraph and specifically pleading [matters].

*Source note: O 6 r 7(1)–(3) ROC p71; Form 13 (Forms: SUPCT_new PDs (Appendix A - Form 13).pdf p1); O 3 r 4(3) ROC p56; SYL p8 [51]–[53]; L1 s29–s30; tested PYP23 Q1.4, PYP25 Q1.4, SQ 2023-S Q1.3.*

### drp-w-0-23 — Default judgment: no notice of intention, no defence, no defence to counterclaim
*Keywords: default judgment, judgment in default, failure to file notice of intention, Form 11, memorandum of service, Form 12, judgment in default of defence, Form 14, O 6 r 6(5), O 6 r 6(6), O 6 r 7(7), O 6 r 9(3), PD 108, draft judgment, note of costs, interest 5.33%, O 6 r 6(7), O 6 r 7(8), setting aside default judgment, O 3 r 2(8), O 9 r 6*

**I** The issue is what [Client] can do because [D] has not [filed a notice of intention to contest / filed a defence] in time. **C** [Client] may apply for judgment against [D] in Form [11 / 14] under O 6 r [6(5) / 7(7)] of the Rules of Court 2021, filing a memorandum of service in Form 12 with a draft judgment and a note of costs (PD 108).
**R** If the defendant fails to file and serve a notice of intention to contest or not contest in time, or states that it does not intend to contest all or some claims, the claimant may apply for judgment in Form 11, filing a memorandum of service in Form 12 (O 6 r 6(5)–(6)). If the defendant fails to file and serve a defence in time, the claimant may apply for judgment in default of defence in Form 14 (O 6 r 7(7)); the defendant may do the same if the claimant fails to file a defence to counterclaim (O 6 r 9(3)). The Court may award interest at 5.33% a year from the date of the originating process to judgment (O 6 r 6(7), r 7(8)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 6 r 6(5): if the defendant fails to file and serve a notice within the prescribed time, or states in the notice that it does not intend to contest all or some of the claims, the claimant may, subject to (6), apply for judgment to be given against the defendant in Form 11.
> - O 6 r 6(6): the claimant must file a memorandum of service in Form 12 when applying for judgment under (5).
> - O 6 r 6(7) and r 7(8): when giving judgment under these Rules the Court may direct interest from the date of the originating process to the date of judgment at 5.33% per year.
> - O 6 r 7(7): if the defendant fails to file and serve a defence within the prescribed time, the claimant may apply for judgment in default of defence in Form 14.
> - O 6 r 9(3): if the claimant fails to file and serve a defence to the counterclaim in time, the defendant may apply for judgment in default of defence on the counterclaim in Form 14.
> - PD 108(1): the procedure where (a) no notice is filed and served, (b) a notice says the defendant does not intend to contest, or (c) no defence or defence to counterclaim is filed and served, is an application in Form 11 or 14.
> - PD 108(2): the applicant must also file a memorandum of service (Form 12), a draft of the judgment sought, a note of costs (where applicable under Appendix 1 to Order 21), and (for possession of immovable property) a certificate or affidavit that no Order 52 r 1 relief is sought.
> - PD 108(3)–(4): the Court may require an affidavit stating the time, place and method of service and the default.
> - O 9 r 6: if both parties attend the first case conference and no defence has been served though time has expired, the Court may enter judgment in default of defence; it may set aside or vary it, or in a special case extend time for the defence.
> - O 9 r 9(7)(e)–(f): applications for default judgment need no prior approval.

**P** No case is set. A judgment entered without notice to, or in the absence of, the party affected may be set aside under O 3 r 2(8) within 14 days of the party learning of it (O 3 r 2(10); see drp-w-0-2), and a default judgment entered at a case conference may be set aside or varied (O 9 r 6(2)).

**A** Confirm the deadline has passed: [statement of claim served on [date]; notice due 14 / 21 days later; none filed]. Then file the Form 11 (or Form 14) application with the Form 12 memorandum of service, a draft judgment and a note of costs, and ask for interest at 5.33% from [issue date] (or contractual interest if pleaded). Default judgment is "as of right" only if service was regular, so check the service evidence first (PD 108(3)). For [D]: if default judgment has been entered, act quickly to set it aside (O 3 r 2(8)(a), within 14 days of learning of it), explaining the default and showing a defence on the merits. **C** Therefore [Client] may apply for judgment in Form [11 / 14] with a Form 12 memorandum of service, and the Court may award interest at 5.33% a year.

*Source note: O 6 r 6(5)–(7), r 7(7)–(8), r 9(3) ROC p70–72; PD 108 SCPD p133–134 (current text checked on epd2021, 15 Jul 2026: PD 108(2) now limits the note of costs to cases under Appendix 1 to Order 21); O 9 r 6 ROC p85; O 9 r 9(7)(e)–(f) ROC p87; O 3 r 2(8)–(10) ROC p54–55; SYL p7 [50], p8 [55], [57(c)]; L1 s28, s32, s34; setting aside default judgment is not a syllabus point (Cyril-Exam, Written Advocacy chapter); tested PYP25 Q1.4.*

### drp-w-0-24 — Challenging jurisdiction in the defence or defendant's affidavit
*Keywords: challenge to jurisdiction, jurisdictional challenge, defence stating the ground, no jurisdiction, should not exercise jurisdiction, forum non conveniens, arbitration agreement, O 6 r 7(4), O 6 r 7(5), O 6 r 7(6), O 6 r 12(3)–(5), not a submission to jurisdiction, Beltran Julian Moreno v Terraform Labs, set aside service, stay of proceedings, Form 13 para 2–4*

**I** The issue is how [D], who prefers [the Australian courts / arbitration], can contest the Singapore Court's jurisdiction without submitting to it. **C** [D] need not file a defence on the merits; it must file and serve, within [21 days / 5 weeks] after service of the statement of claim, a defence stating the ground on which it challenges the jurisdiction (that the Court has no jurisdiction or should not exercise it), and that defence is not a submission to jurisdiction (O 6 r 7(4)–(6)).
**R** A defendant challenging jurisdiction, whether because the parties agreed to arbitrate or on any other ground, need not file a defence on the merits but must file and serve a defence stating the ground of challenge (O 6 r 7(4)). The challenge may be that the Court has no jurisdiction or that it should not exercise jurisdiction (O 6 r 7(5)), and such a defence is not a submission to jurisdiction (O 6 r 7(6)). The same applies to a defendant's affidavit in an originating application (O 6 r 12(3)–(5)). The Court then gives directions at the case conference for the jurisdictional application (O 9 r 7; drp-w-0-30).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 6 r 7(4): if the defendant is challenging the jurisdiction of the Court on the ground that the parties have agreed to refer their dispute to arbitration or on any other ground, the defendant need not file and serve a defence on the merits but must file and serve a defence stating the ground on which the defendant is challenging the jurisdiction.
> - O 6 r 7(5): the challenge to jurisdiction may be for the reason that
>   - (a) the Court has no jurisdiction to hear the action; or
>   - (b) the Court should not exercise jurisdiction to hear the action.
> - O 6 r 7(6): a defence filed under (4) is not treated as a submission to jurisdiction.
> - O 6 r 12(3)–(5): the same for an originating application, by the defendant's affidavit stating the ground; (4)(b) reads "the Court should not exercise jurisdiction because it is not the appropriate Court to hear the action"; an affidavit under (3) is not a submission.
> - Form 13 paras 2–4: state whether the defendant contends the Court has no jurisdiction, or should not exercise it, and whether the proceedings should be stayed or struck out.
> - Sequence: notice of intention (O 6 r 6; not a submission) → defence stating the ground (O 6 r 7(4)) → case conference: the Court deals with jurisdiction first and directs the application within 14 days, reply 14 days later, hearing within 14 days after (O 9 r 7).

**P** The procedure for a jurisdictional challenge under ROC 2021 is: the defendant files a defence in Form 13 stating the jurisdictional ground; the Court fixes a case conference; at the case conference it must first deal with the objection, directing the application and affidavit within 14 days, the reply within 14 days and a hearing within 14 days after affidavits close (O 9 r 7); and if the challenge fails, it directs a defence on the merits (*Beltran, Julian Moreno v Terraform Labs Pte Ltd* [2023] SGHC 340 at [51], citing O 2 rr 6–7). The words "need not" in O 6 r 7(4) are not permissive: a defendant challenging jurisdiction files only a Defence (Jurisdiction), not a defence on the merits as well (*Beltran* at [52]–[54]).

**A** Choose the ground. (1) No jurisdiction (O 6 r 7(5)(a)): e.g. [service out was irregular because approval was not obtained, or the method breached Australian law]. (2) Should not exercise jurisdiction (O 6 r 7(5)(b)): e.g. [Australia is clearly the more appropriate forum: the goods were classified under Australian standards and the witnesses are there], or [the contract has an arbitration clause]. Then timeline for [B, served in Australia on 28 October]: notice of intention within 21 days ([18 November]); defence stating the ground within 5 weeks ([2 December]); first case conference about 12 weeks after the originating claim was issued (O 9 r 1(1)(b)); then the jurisdiction application within 14 days after that case conference, the claimant's reply affidavit within 14 days after, and a hearing within 14 days after affidavits close (O 9 r 7(2)). Do not plead to the merits or take steps that look like submission (e.g. a counterclaim) before the challenge is decided. **C** Therefore [D] should file a Form 10 notice by [date] and a Form 13 defence stating its jurisdictional challenge by [date], and then make the jurisdiction application on the timetable the Court directs under O 9 r 7(2).

*Source note: O 6 r 7(4)–(6) ROC p71–72; O 6 r 12(3)–(5) ROC p73–74; O 9 r 7 ROC p85–86; Form 13 paras 2–4 (Forms: Appendix A - Form 13); SYL p8 [54], p9 [60], p10 [69]–[70]; L1 s31, s38, s45; Beltran [2023] SGHC 340 (Hri Kumar Nair J) at [47]–[54], read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026); SQ p118 rubric; tested PYP23 Q1.4, PYP25 Q1.4, SQ 2023-S Q1.3.*

### drp-w-0-25 — Counterclaim and defence to counterclaim (O 6 rr 8–9)
*Keywords: counterclaim, defence and counterclaim, Form 13, O 6 r 8, file with the defence, personal injuries counterclaim, defence to counterclaim, O 6 r 9, within 14 days, no reply needed to merely deny, judgment in default on counterclaim, Limitation Act s 31*

**I** The issue is how [D] may bring its claim against [Client] in the existing action, and how [Client] must respond. **C** [D] must file and serve its counterclaim with its defence, in Form 13 (O 6 r 8); [Client] must then file and serve a defence to counterclaim within 14 days (O 6 r 9(1)).
**R** A defendant who intends to counterclaim must file and serve the counterclaim with the defence, in Form 13, annexing a medical report and statement of special damages if it is for personal injuries (O 6 r 8). The claimant need not reply merely to deny the defence, but must file and serve a defence to the counterclaim in Form 13 within 14 days after the defence and counterclaim are served (O 6 r 9(1)–(2)), failing which the defendant may apply for judgment in default on the counterclaim in Form 14 (O 6 r 9(3)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 6 r 8(1): if the defendant intends to counterclaim against the claimant, the defendant must file and serve the counterclaim with the defence.
> - O 6 r 8(2): the counterclaim must be in Form 13.
> - O 6 r 8(3): if the counterclaim is for personal injuries, the defendant must annex a medical report and a statement of the special damages claimed.
> - O 6 r 9(1): the claimant need not file a reply to the defence if the claimant merely wishes to deny assertions without adding anything material, but must file and serve a defence to the counterclaim within 14 days after the defence and counterclaim is served on the claimant.
> - O 6 r 9(2): the defence to counterclaim must be in Form 13.
> - O 6 r 9(3): if the claimant fails to file and serve a defence to counterclaim in time, the defendant may apply for judgment in default on the counterclaim in Form 14.
> - Form 13 (Counterclaim): state the jurisdictional basis of the counterclaim (and any statutory source), the facts, the relief, interest and costs.
> - Limitation Act s 31: a counterclaim is deemed a separate action commenced on the same date as the action in which it is pleaded.
> - Summary judgment is available on a counterclaim (O 9 r 17(11)–(12)), and the Court may stay enforcement of a summary judgment until trial of a counterclaim (O 9 r 17(9)).

**P** No case is set. Because a counterclaim is treated as brought when the claimant's action began (s 31 Limitation Act), a defendant may counterclaim even if a fresh action would by then be time-barred.

**A** If [D] has a cross-claim (e.g. [for defective work or wrongful termination]), plead it in the same Form 13 document as the defence and serve both together, within the 21-day or 5-week deadline (drp-w-0-22); a counterclaim served later needs the Court's approval as an amendment. If [D] is challenging jurisdiction, it should not counterclaim, since that invokes the Court's jurisdiction. For [Client], diarise 14 days from service of the defence and counterclaim for the defence to counterclaim; no reply to the defence is needed unless it adds something material (O 6 r 10; drp-w-0-26). **C** Therefore [D] should file its counterclaim with its defence in Form 13 by [date], and [Client] must file a defence to counterclaim within 14 days after service, by [date].

*Source note: O 6 rr 8–9 ROC p72; Form 13 counterclaim paras (Forms: Appendix A - Form 13); LA p24–25 (s 31); O 9 r 17(9), (11)–(12) L2 s21, s24; SYL p8 [56]–[57]; L1 s33–s34; tested PYP25 Q1.4.*

### drp-w-0-26 — Further pleadings (O 6 r 10; O 9 r 15)
*Keywords: further pleadings, reply, reply to defence, pleadings beyond the defence, approval at case conference, O 6 r 10, O 9 r 15, merely deny or repeat, adding anything material, rejoinder*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] may file a reply to [D]'s defence. **C** Only with the Court's approval at a case conference, which will be given only if the reply adds something material and is not a mere denial or repetition (O 6 r 10; O 9 r 15).
**R** Parties must seek the Court's approval at the case conference to file any pleading beyond the defence or defence to counterclaim, where it is necessary for certain matters to be pleaded, and no further pleadings may be filed unless the Court so orders (O 6 r 10). The Court decides whether a further pleading is necessary and must not order one that merely denies or repeats assertions in earlier pleadings without adding anything material (O 9 r 15).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 6 r 10(1): parties must seek the approval of the Court at the case conference to file any further pleadings beyond the defence or defence to counterclaim, in cases where it is necessary for certain matters to be pleaded.
> - O 6 r 10(2): no further pleadings may be filed unless the Court otherwise orders at the case conference.
> - O 6 r 9(1): the claimant need not file a reply to the defence if it merely wishes to deny assertions without adding anything material.
> - O 9 r 15(1): the Court must decide whether it is necessary to file a pleading beyond the defence or defence to counterclaim.
> - O 9 r 15(2): the Court must not order further pleadings to be filed if they merely deny or repeat assertions in earlier pleadings without adding anything material.
> - Filing further pleadings is also a SAPT matter (O 9 r 9(4)(g)).

**P** No case is set; the rules reflect the Ideals of expeditious and cost-effective proceedings (O 3 r 1(2)).

**A** Ask what the reply would add. If [D]'s defence raises [a variation of the contract, an estoppel, or a limitation defence] and [Client] needs to plead a positive answer (e.g. [the variation was never agreed / waiver / s 29 postponement]), seek approval at the case conference, explaining the material matter to be pleaded. If [Client] would only deny the defence, no reply is needed: the denial is implied (O 6 r 9(1)). **C** Therefore [Client] [should ask the Court at the case conference for approval to file a reply pleading [matter] / need not file a reply].

*Source note: O 6 rr 9(1), 10 ROC p72–73; O 9 r 15 ROC p92; O 9 r 9(4)(g) ROC p86; SYL p8 [58]; L1 s35.*

### drp-w-0-27 — Originating application: the defendant's affidavit, further affidavits and counterclaim
*Keywords: originating application, defendant's affidavit, O 6 r 12, within 21 days, 5 weeks, introduce evidence, challenge jurisdiction by affidavit, no further affidavits, special case, O 6 r 12(6), O 3 r 5(6), CZD v CZE, counterclaim in originating application, O 6 r 14, O 6 r 13, all the evidence necessary or material*

**I** The issue is what [D] must file in response to [Client]'s originating application, and whether either side may file further affidavits. **C** If [D] wishes to adduce evidence, it must file and serve its affidavit within [21 days / 5 weeks] after service of the originating application and affidavit (O 6 r 12(1)–(2)), containing all evidence necessary or material to its defence (O 6 r 13) and any counterclaim (O 6 r 14); no further affidavits may be filed except in a special case (O 6 r 12(6)).
**R** A defendant served in Singapore who wishes to introduce evidence must file and serve its affidavit within 21 days after service of the originating application and affidavit; one served out of Singapore, within 5 weeks (O 6 r 12(1)–(2)). A defendant challenging jurisdiction files an affidavit stating the ground instead, which is not a submission (O 6 r 12(3)–(5)). The affidavit must contain all necessary or material evidence (O 6 r 13), and a counterclaim must be included in it with its evidence (O 6 r 14). Except in a special case, no further affidavits may be filed after the defendant's affidavit on the merits (O 6 r 12(6)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 6 r 12(1): a defendant served in Singapore must file and serve the defendant's affidavit within 21 days after being served with the claimant's originating application and affidavit, if the defendant wishes to introduce evidence.
> - O 6 r 12(2): a defendant served out of Singapore, within 5 weeks.
