> - O 6 r 12(3): a defendant challenging jurisdiction (arbitration agreement or any other ground) need not file an affidavit on the merits but must file and serve an affidavit stating the ground.
> - O 6 r 12(4): the challenge may be that (a) the Court has no jurisdiction; or (b) the Court should not exercise jurisdiction because it is not the appropriate Court.
> - O 6 r 12(5): an affidavit under (3) is not a submission to jurisdiction.
> - O 6 r 12(6): except in a special case, no further affidavits may be filed after the defendant files the defendant's affidavit on the merits.
> - O 6 r 13: an affidavit filed in an originating application must contain all the evidence that is necessary or material to the claim or to the defence.
> - O 6 r 14: a defendant who intends to counterclaim in an originating application must include it in the defendant's affidavit together with all the evidence necessary for the counterclaim.
> - O 3 r 5(6) (applications in an action): except in a special case, no further affidavits after the opposing party's affidavit under O 3 r 5(5).

**P** O 3 r 5(6) intends that "there is to be only one round of affidavits from each party, except in a 'special case'"; new issues raised in an opponent's affidavit may amount to a special case if they could not reasonably have been within the applicant's contemplation when it filed its affidavit, but an applicant cannot adopt a wait-and-see approach (*CZD v CZE* [2023] SGHC 86, reported [2023] 5 SLR 806, at [19]–[21]). The same approach fits the parallel rule for originating applications in O 6 r 12(6).

**A** Diarise [D]'s deadline: [served in Singapore on [date] → affidavit due [date + 21 days]]. [D] must put in all its evidence now, including any counterclaim (O 6 r 14), because it cannot count on a second affidavit. If [Client] wants to reply to new matters in [D]'s affidavit, it must show a special case: argue that [D]'s affidavit raised [a new allegation of fraud / new documents] that [Client] could not have anticipated and must answer for the Court to decide fairly, consistent with the Ideals; a wish to bolster existing evidence will not do. **C** Therefore [D] must file one affidavit containing all its evidence and any counterclaim by [date], and [Client] may reply only if it shows a special case.

*Source note: O 6 rr 12–14 ROC p73–74; O 3 r 5(5)–(6) ROC p56; SYL p9 [59]–[63]; L1 s37–s40; CZD v CZE [2023] SGHC 86 (Chua Lee Ming J) at [18]–[21], read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026); the judgment concerns O 3 r 5(6), and applying it to O 6 r 12(6) is the syllabus's pairing (SYL p9 [61]); Conflict: Cyril-Exam ("Originating Application") cites O 6 r 12(4) for the OA counterclaim; the ROC puts it in O 6 r 14, which the pack follows.*

## 0.VI Case conferences, jurisdiction challenges and AEICs before documents

- **When is the first case conference, and what is it for?**
  - 8 weeks after issue (served in Singapore) or 12 weeks (served out); earlier or later on request or the Court's own accord; as many as needed (O 9 r 1) → [0-28](#drp-w-0-28)
  - The Court takes control, sets timelines, gives directions (O 9 r 2); "confidential" or "without prejudice" communications not disclosed to the trial court (O 9 r 3) → [0-28](#drp-w-0-28)
  - File the Pre-Case Conference Questionnaire (Form B6) about 1 week before the first RCC (PD 56(1)–(2)); expert witness template Form B7 (PD 56(8)) → [0-28](#drp-w-0-28)
  - Intend to call an expert? Tell the Court at the case conference (O 9 r 21(1)) → [0-28](#drp-w-0-28), [0-52](#drp-w-0-52)
- **Someone missed the case conference or a step before it**
  - Nobody or the claimant absent: action may be dismissed; defendant absent: judgment on proof of service; set aside for valid reasons (O 9 r 4) → [0-29](#drp-w-0-29)
  - Originating process not served: dismissal, or serve or seek substituted service within 14 days (O 9 r 5) → [0-17](#drp-w-0-17)
  - No defence by the first case conference: default judgment (O 9 r 6) → [0-29](#drp-w-0-29)
- **Defendant challenging jurisdiction**
  - Court deals with jurisdiction first; application in 14 days, reply 14 days, hearing within 14 days (O 9 r 7) → [0-30](#drp-w-0-30)
- **AEICs before documents (AB4D)**
  - After pleadings, before exchange of documents, the Court may order witness lists and AEICs (O 9 r 8(1)); in practice AB4D is the default unless the key documents are only with the other side (Digest 4 para 12); then no requested-document production except in a special case (O 11 r 3(3)) → [0-31](#drp-w-0-31)

### drp-w-0-28 — Case conferences: timing, purpose, non-disclosure, Pre-Case Conference Questionnaire
*Keywords: case conference, first case conference, Registrar's case conference, RCC, O 9 r 1, 8 weeks, 12 weeks, O 9 r 2, take control, timelines, directions, O 9 r 3, without prejudice, confidential, non-disclosure to trial judge, Pre-Case Conference Questionnaire, PCQ, Form B6, Form 6 of Appendix B, PD 56, list of issues, expert witness template, Form B7, O 9 r 21, intend to rely on expert evidence*

**I** The issue is when the first case conference will be held and what [Client] must do for it. **C** The first case conference will be held [8 / 12] weeks after the originating claim was issued (O 9 r 1(1)), around [date]; [Client] must file the Pre-Case Conference Questionnaire in Form B6 about a week before it (PD 56(2)), and should raise there any intended expert evidence (O 9 r 21(1)).
**R** A case conference is held 8 weeks after the originating process is issued where the defendant is served in Singapore, or 12 weeks where it is served out, though the Court may hold it earlier or later and hold as many as it thinks appropriate (O 9 r 1). At a case conference the Court takes control of the proceedings, sets timelines and gives directions (O 9 r 2). Communications stated or marked "confidential" or "without prejudice" are not disclosed to the trial court (O 9 r 3). Each party must file a Pre-Case Conference Questionnaire, to the extent possible, 1 week before the first Registrar's case conference (PD 56(2)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 1(1): a case conference is to be held
>   - (a) where the defendant is to be served in Singapore, 8 weeks after the originating claim or originating application is issued; or
>   - (b) where it is to be served out of Singapore, 12 weeks after issue.
> - O 9 r 1(2): the Court may hold a case conference earlier or later at a party's request or on its own accord.
> - O 9 r 1(3): the Court may hold as many case conferences as it thinks appropriate, at any stage, including appeals.
> - O 9 r 1(4): as a general rule the Registrar conducts it, but may refer any matter to the assigned Judge or any Judge.
> - O 9 r 2: at a case conference, the Court is to take control of and set the timelines and give directions for the proceedings.
> - O 9 r 3: subject to the law of evidence, a communication made in the course of a case conference must not be disclosed to the Court conducting the trial if it (a) has been stated by any party to be "confidential" or "without prejudice"; or (b) has been so marked by the Registrar or Judge.
> - O 9 r 21(1): the parties are to inform the Court during the case conference if they intend to rely on expert evidence; (2) the Court must then consider the matters in Order 12.
> - PD 56(1)–(2): the PCQ facilitates discussion of issues, timelines and milestones; unless the Court otherwise directs, each party must file it in Form B6 of Appendix B, to the extent possible 1 week before the first RCC, after conferring on agreed positions.
> - PD 56(4): the PCQ is discussed at the first RCC.
> - PD 56(5)–(6): the Court may direct a List of Issues, a neutral tool to define the scope of production and of factual and expert evidence.
> - PD 56(8): before the RCC where expert evidence is discussed, parties fill in the Expert Witness Template (Form B7 of Appendix B).
> - PD 56(10)–(11): parties may have to say at the RCC whether they object to an order for AEICs before documents under O 9 r 8(1) (PCQ question 5a).

**P** The case conference is the "command centre" that sets the timelines and tone of the proceedings (Civil Justice Commission Report, quoted at L1 s41). No case is set on these rules.

**A** Compute the date: [originating claim issued 23 July, served out in Australia] → first case conference about 12 weeks later, [15 October] (O 9 r 1(1)(b)); note it may be fixed earlier or later. Before it: confer with the other side and file the PCQ (Form B6) a week before, stating [Client]'s position on matters such as AEICs before documents (Q5a), expert evidence and timelines. At the conference, raise any intended expert evidence (O 9 r 21(1)); a party that stays silent and then announces an expert shortly before trial (PYP22 Q5) risks having the Court refuse approval (drp-w-0-52). Settlement discussions with the Registrar marked "without prejudice" stay away from the trial judge (O 9 r 3). **C** Therefore [Client] should prepare for a first case conference on about [date], file the PCQ a week before, and use the conference to secure the directions it needs.

*Source note: O 9 rr 1–3 ROC p83–84; O 9 r 21 ROC p95; PD 56(1)–(11) SCPD p56–58; SYL p10 [64]–[66], [68], [72]; L1 s41–s43; SQ p118 rubric ("CC about 12 weeks after issue, possibly earlier or later"); tested PYP25 Q1.4, SQ 2023-S Q1.3, PYP22 Q5 (expert not raised at case conference).*

### drp-w-0-29 — Absence of parties at a case conference; no defence by the first case conference
*Keywords: absence of parties, O 9 r 4, claimant absent, no party attends, dismiss the action, defendant absent, judgment upon proof of service, set aside dismissal, valid reasons, O 9 r 6, no defence by first case conference, judgment in default of defence, extend time special case*

**I** The issue is the consequence of [Party] failing to attend the case conference [or failing to file a defence by it]. **C** The Court may [dismiss the action / give judgment for [Client] on proof of service] under O 9 r [4 / 6], subject to the defaulting party's right to have it set aside for valid reasons.
**R** If no party attends a case conference, or the claimant is absent, the Court may dismiss the action; if the claimant attends but the defendant is absent, the Court may give judgment for the claimant upon proof of service; the Court may set aside or vary the dismissal or judgment on proof of valid reasons for the absence (O 9 r 4). If both parties attend and no defence has been served though time has expired, the Court may enter judgment in default of defence, which it may set aside or vary, or in a special case extend time (O 9 r 6).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 4(1): if no party attends the case conference or if the claimant is absent, the Court may dismiss the action.
> - O 9 r 4(2): if the claimant attends but the defendant is absent, the Court may give judgment for the claimant upon proof of service of the originating claim or originating application on the defendant.
> - O 9 r 4(3): the Court may set aside or vary the dismissal or default judgment on proof that there were valid reasons for the absence of the defaulting party.
> - O 9 r 5: claimant attends but has not served the originating process (see drp-w-0-17).
> - O 9 r 6(1): if both parties in an originating claim attend and no defence is served by the first case conference when the time for it has expired, the Court may enter judgment against the defendant in default of a defence.
> - O 9 r 6(2): the Court may set aside or vary such a default judgment.
> - O 9 r 6(3): the Court may, in a special case, extend time for the defence after the first case conference, failing which the defendant is deemed not to have served a defence and the claimant is entitled to judgment.
> - O 9 r 6(4): this applies with modifications to a claimant's default on a defence to counterclaim.

**P** No case is set. Appearance at a hearing may be in person or by counsel (*Lam Hwa Engineering & Trading Pte Ltd v Yang Qiang* [2013] 2 SLR 524 at [19], cited in the course for absence at trial).

**A** Identify who was absent. If [D] did not attend although served (proof: the memorandum of service), [Client] can ask for judgment under O 9 r 4(2). If [Client] missed it [because of a diary error], expect dismissal under O 9 r 4(1), and apply promptly to set it aside, giving the valid reason with evidence (O 9 r 4(3)). If [D] attended but has filed no defence and time has run, [Client] can ask for default judgment at the conference (O 9 r 6(1)); [D] should ask for a short extension, showing a special case (O 9 r 6(3)). **C** Therefore the Court may [dismiss / give judgment], and the defaulting party should apply to set it aside, showing valid reasons.

*Source note: O 9 rr 4–6 ROC p84–85; SYL p10 [67]; L1 s42; Lam Hwa L3 s20; Conflict: SYL [67] cites O 9 r 5 for "orders … in the event of the absence of parties"; in the ROC, absence of parties is O 9 r 4 and r 5 is failure to serve (L1 s42 cites r 4). The pack follows the ROC text and covers both.*

### drp-w-0-30 — Directions for a jurisdictional challenge at the case conference (O 9 r 7)
*Keywords: jurisdictional challenge, challenge to jurisdiction, O 9 r 7, deal with jurisdiction first, application within 14 days after the case conference, affidavit in reply within 14 days, hearing within 14 days after affidavits, no further affidavits without approval, summons Form 1, O 3 r 5*

**I** The issue is the procedure and timelines for [D]'s challenge to the Court's jurisdiction after its defence stating that challenge. **C** At the case conference the Court must first deal with the objection, directing [D] to file its application and supporting affidavit within 14 days, [Client] to reply within 14 days after, and a hearing within 14 days after all affidavits are filed (O 9 r 7(2)).
**R** If both parties attend the case conference and a defence (or defendant's affidavit) has been filed, the Court must first deal with any objection to its jurisdiction (O 9 r 7(1)). Where the defendant is challenging jurisdiction, the Court must direct it to file and serve the application with supporting affidavit within 14 days after the case conference, direct the claimant to file any reply affidavit within 14 days after that (no further affidavits without approval), and, unless it orders otherwise, fix the hearing no later than 14 days after all affidavits are filed (O 9 r 7(2)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 7(1): if both the claimant and defendant attend the case conference and (a) a defence has been filed and served (originating claim), or (b) the defendant's affidavit has been filed and served (originating application), the Court must first deal with any objection to its jurisdiction.
> - O 9 r 7(2): if the defendant is challenging the jurisdiction of the Court, the Court must
>   - (a) direct the defendant to file and serve the necessary application with supporting affidavit within 14 days after the date of the case conference;
>   - (b) direct the claimant to file and serve any affidavit in reply within 14 days thereafter, with no further affidavits to be filed without the Court's approval; and
>   - (c) unless the Court otherwise orders, fix the hearing of the application on a date no later than 14 days after all affidavits have been filed and served.
> - The application is a summons under O 3 r 5 (Form 1), supported by affidavit.
> - The challenge is raised first in the defence or defendant's affidavit (O 6 r 7(4), r 12(3); drp-w-0-24); AEICs before documents and the SAPT come only after it is dealt with (O 9 r 8(1)).

**P** The 2023-S rubric awards a mark each for: the direction to file the application and affidavit within 14 days of the case conference, the reply affidavit 14 days later, and the hearing no later than 14 days after affidavits close (SQ p118). The same four-step procedure (defence stating the ground, case conference, the O 9 r 7(2) timetable, then a defence on the merits if the challenge fails) is set out in *Beltran, Julian Moreno v Terraform Labs Pte Ltd* [2023] SGHC 340 at [51].

**A** Build the timeline from the facts: notice of intention (21 days after service of the statement of claim, if served out); defence stating the ground (5 weeks); first case conference (about 12 weeks after issue, if served out); then [D]'s summons and affidavit within 14 days of that conference, [Client]'s reply affidavit 14 days later, and the hearing within a further 14 days. Advise [D] to put all its evidence on [forum non conveniens / the arbitration clause / defective service] into its first affidavit, since further affidavits need approval. **C** Therefore [D]'s jurisdiction application must be filed by [case conference + 14 days], [Client] replies by [+14 days], and the hearing will be fixed within 14 days after that.

*Source note: O 9 r 7 ROC p85–86; O 3 r 5 ROC p56; SYL p10 [69]–[70]; L1 s45; SQ p118 rubric; Beltran SYL p8 [54(d)]; tested SQ 2023-S Q1.3, PYP23 Q1.4.*

### drp-w-0-31 — AEICs before documents (AB4D) (O 9 r 8)
*Keywords: AEICs before documents, AB4D, affidavits of evidence-in-chief before production, O 9 r 8(1), list of witnesses, simultaneous or sequential, Digest 4, default, Pre-Case Conference Questionnaire question 5a, Registrar's Circular No 1 of 2025, O 11 r 3(3), special case, witnesses tailoring evidence*

**I** The issue is whether the Court will order the parties to exchange AEICs before producing documents, and what that means for [Client]. **C** The Court may order the parties to file their lists of witnesses and AEICs before any exchange of documents (O 9 r 8(1)); the Courts' Digest 4 says such an order "will be the default", and and once made, requested documents may be ordered only in a special case (O 11 r 3(3)).
**R** Once any jurisdictional challenge is dealt with, or if there is none, after pleadings are filed and served but before any exchange of documents, the Court may order the parties to file and serve their lists of witnesses and the AEICs of all or some witnesses, simultaneously or in any sequence (O 9 r 8(1)). If it does not, it proceeds to the SAPT (O 9 r 8(2)). The rationale is to stop witnesses adjusting their evidence to match disclosure, to crystallise the key issues and to narrow the scope of production.

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 8(1): if the application to challenge the jurisdiction of the Court has been dealt with or where there is no challenge, after pleadings have been filed and served but before any exchange of documents, the Court may, in any particular case, order the parties to file and serve their lists of witnesses and the affidavits of evidence-in-chief of all or some of the witnesses simultaneously or in any sequence.
> - O 9 r 8(2): where the Court does not exercise this power, it will proceed to consider the matters in r 9 (the SAPT).
> - O 11 r 3(3): where the Court has ordered AEICs to be filed and served before production, production of requested documents may not be ordered except in a special case.
> - PD 56(10)–(11) and Form B6 question 5a: parties must indicate whether their case is appropriate for AEICs before production of documents.
> - Digest 4 (Rules of Court 2021, judiciary.gov.sg) para 12: "an order for AB4D will be the default and is not confined only to exceptional circumstances"; it may not be appropriate (a) where highly relevant documents necessary for a party to prepare its evidence are in the possession or control of the other party only; or (b) where a party's evidence is incomplete because of the other party's default. The syllabus also points to Registrar's Circular No 1 of 2025 on drafting AEICs.

**P** No case is set. The rationale, per Digest 4 paras 11–12 and SYL [71]: avoiding witnesses adjusting their evidence to match disclosed documents, crystallising the key issues and streamlining the SAPT, and reducing the scope of disclosure, potentially obviating expert evidence.

**A** At the case conference, take a position on PCQ Q5a. Argue for AB4D where [the dispute turns on what was said at meetings, and the witnesses' independent recollection matters], or where it would narrow an otherwise sprawling production exercise. Argue against where [Client] cannot prepare its witnesses' evidence without documents held only by [D] (e.g. [D's internal records of the variation]); if AB4D is ordered anyway, [Client] must later show a special case to get requested documents (O 11 r 3(3)), so ask now for the specific documents it needs. **C** Therefore [the Court is likely to order AB4D; [Client] should prepare its witness list and AEICs early and seek, before the order, any documents essential to its witnesses' evidence].

*Source note: O 9 r 8 ROC p86; O 11 r 3(3) ROC p109–110; PD 56(10)–(11) SCPD p57–58; SYL p10 [71]–[73]; L1 s47; O 9 r 8(1) unchanged in the current ROC (SSO, as at 1 Apr 2026): the power is discretionary ("may"); Digest 4 paras 10–14 read on judiciary.gov.sg (8 Nov 2026) state the default as a matter of practice, so both are right; Cyril-Exam and Nic-Muggers (2023–25) describe only the discretion.*

## 0.VII The SAPT and pre-trial applications

- **"Must I wait for the SAPT?"**
  - After AB4D (or if none is ordered), the Court orders a single application pending trial covering everything needed to proceed expeditiously (O 9 r 9(1)–(4)) → [0-32](#drp-w-0-32)
  - SAPT and affidavit within 21 days after the case conference; reply 21 days later; SAPT checklist (Form B8) (O 9 r 9(5); PD 56(12)–(16)) → [0-32](#drp-w-0-32)
  - No approval needed for the carve-out list: injunction or search order, substituted service, service out, setting aside service, default judgment, summary judgment, striking out the whole action or defence, stay, enforcement, permission to appeal … (O 9 r 9(7)) → [0-33](#drp-w-0-33)
  - Anything else outside the SAPT: approval by letter (Request, Form B9) explaining why it is needed now; nothing in the 14 days before trial except a special case with the trial Judge's approval (O 9 r 9(8)–(11); PD 66) → [0-33](#drp-w-0-33)
- **Security for costs**
  - Grounds: claimant ordinarily resident abroad; nominal or non-party-funded claimant likely unable to pay; address omitted, wrong or changed to evade; company claimant unable to pay (O 9 r 12(1)–(2); Companies Act s 388) → [0-34](#drp-w-0-34)
  - Discretion: just to order it, considering strength of claim, enforceability, assets, residence (*Cova*, *Creative Elegance*, *Tjong Very Sumito*) → [0-35](#drp-w-0-35)
- **Pleading too vague?**
  - Further and better particulars if necessary on the facts; material facts, not evidence; Summary Table if more than 5 categories (O 9 r 13; PD 68) → [0-36](#drp-w-0-36)
- **Amending a pleading**
  - By written agreement up to 14 days before trial; otherwise with permission; no amendment within 14 days of trial except a special case; later events in a special case; inferences from amended facts (O 9 r 14) → [0-37](#drp-w-0-37)
  - Will permission be granted? Three-step framework (*Wang Piao*, *Maag*); real issues and procedural fairness; no second bite (*Sheagar*, *Review Publishing*) → [0-38](#drp-w-0-38)
  - Limitation has expired: correct a party's name, change capacity, or new cause of action from the same or substantially the same facts (O 9 r 14(4); *Multistar*, *Mer Vue*) → [0-39](#drp-w-0-39)
- **Add or remove a party; consolidate or stay actions? (background only — not examinable per SYL p1)**
  - Add or remove claimants or defendants; an added defendant is sued from the date of the amendment (O 9 r 10; SAPT O 9 r 9(4)(a)) → [0-79](#drp-w-0-79)
  - Consolidate, joint or consecutive trial, or stay one action: common question of law, same factual situation, or appropriate (O 9 r 11; SAPT O 9 r 9(4)(b)) → [0-79](#drp-w-0-79)
  - Security from a non-party funder or assignee (O 9 r 12(3)–(4)) → [0-34](#drp-w-0-34)

### drp-w-0-32 — The single application pending trial (SAPT): when, what, timelines
*Keywords: single application pending trial, SAPT, O 9 r 9, all matters necessary for the case to proceed expeditiously, list of matters, addition of parties, security for costs, further and better particulars, amendment of pleadings, striking out part, production of documents, interim relief, expert evidence, 21 days after the case conference, affidavit in reply 21 days, SAPT checklist, Form B8, PD 56(12)–(16), Registrar's case conference, RCC*

**I** The issue is whether [Client]'s application for [security for costs / amendment / particulars / production] must be brought within the single application pending trial, and when. **C** Yes. [Application] is a matter for the SAPT (O 9 r 9(4)([x])), which the Court will direct after AB4D or, if none is ordered, at that stage; [Client] must file its SAPT with a supporting affidavit within 21 days after the relevant case conference, and the other side replies within 21 days (O 9 r 9(5)).
**R** After the parties comply with an AB4D order, or if none is made, the Court must consider all matters needed to bring the proceedings to a conclusion in accordance with the Ideals (O 9 r 9(1)) and, as far as possible, order each party to make a single application pending trial dealing with all matters necessary for the case to proceed expeditiously (O 9 r 9(2)–(3)). The non-exhaustive list includes addition or removal of parties, consolidation, security for costs, further and better particulars, amendment, further pleadings, striking out part of an action or defence, judgment on admissions, questions of law, production of documents, interim relief and expert evidence (O 9 r 9(4)). The applicant files its application and affidavit within 21 days after the case conference, and the other party replies within 21 days (O 9 r 9(5)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 9(1): after the parties notify the Court that they have complied with an order under r 8(1), or if the Court does not exercise that power, the Court must consider all matters necessary to bring the proceedings to a conclusion in accordance with the Ideals.
> - O 9 r 9(2): as far as possible, the Court must order a single application pending trial to be made by each of the parties.
> - O 9 r 9(3): the single application must deal with all matters that are necessary for the case to proceed expeditiously.
> - O 9 r 9(4): those matters include
>   - (a) addition or removal of parties; (b) consolidation of actions; (c) division of issues at trial; (d) security for costs; (e) further and better particulars of pleadings; (f) amendment of pleadings; (g) filing of further pleadings; (h) striking out of part of an action or of the defence; (i) judgment on admission of facts; (j) determination of questions of law or construction of documents; (k) production of documents; (l) interim relief; (m) expert evidence and assessors; (n) independent witness and interested non-parties; and (o) independent counsel.
> - O 9 r 9(5): the Court must order the applying party to file and serve its application and supporting affidavit within 21 days after the case conference, and the other party to file and serve an affidavit in reply within 21 days thereafter.
> - O 9 r 9(6): the Court may order written submissions with a bundle of authorities.
> - PD 56(12): at least 2 weeks before the RCC where the SAPT will be discussed, the Court may direct parties to complete the SAPT Checklist (Form B8) indicating their preferred sequence of matters.
> - PD 56(13): the applicant completes columns A–C and serves it; the parties confer; the respondent indicates agreement or its own sequence, with reasons, in column D.
> - PD 56(14): the respondent files the completed checklist at least 1 week before that RCC.
> - PD 56(16): the Court then issues directions on the sequence of matters to be heard.

**P** No case is set on the SAPT itself; its purpose is that the Court considers all matters needed to bring the proceedings to a conclusion in accordance with the Ideals (O 9 r 9(1); L2 s2).

**A** Place the case in the timeline: [pleadings closed; no AB4D order; the next RCC on [date] will deal with the SAPT]. List everything [Client] needs before trial ([security for costs, particulars of the alleged variation, amendment to add contractual interest, production of [documents]]) and put all of it into one SAPT, in the sequence proposed in the Form B8 checklist. Then diarise: checklist served and completed (respondent files it at least 1 week before the RCC); SAPT and affidavit within 21 days after that RCC; reply 21 days later. Applications on the carve-out list (e.g. summary judgment) need not wait for the SAPT (drp-w-0-33). **C** Therefore [Client] should include [application] in its SAPT, to be filed with a supporting affidavit within 21 days after the relevant case conference.

*Source note: O 9 r 9(1)–(6) ROC p86–87; PD 56(12)–(16) SCPD p58–59; SYL p10–11 [74]–[76]; L2 s2–s5; tested PYP22 Q2 ("must C wait for the SAPT"), PYP23 Q1.5.*

### drp-w-0-33 — Applications outside the SAPT: the carve-out list, approval by letter, the 14 days before trial
*Keywords: carve-out list, O 9 r 9(7), no permission required, without the Court's approval, injunction, search order, substituted service, service out, setting aside service, default judgment, summary judgment, striking out whole action or defence, stay of the whole action, stay of enforcement, enforcement order, permission to appeal, transfer, setting aside third party proceedings, committal, O 9 r 9(8), approval by letter, Request, Form B9, PD 66(6)–(8), O 9 r 9(10), 14 days before trial, special case, trial Judge's approval, Hertel Singapore v Cheng Swee Guan*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] needs the Court's approval to file [application] now, [before / after] the SAPT. **C** [No: an application for [summary judgment / striking out of the whole defence / an injunction] is on the O 9 r 9(7) list and may be filed at any time without approval. / Yes: [application] is not on the O 9 r 9(7) list, so [Client] must first obtain the Court's approval by letter under O 9 r 9(8), explaining why it is necessary at this stage.]
**R** No application may be taken out other than as directed at a case conference or with the Court's approval, except for the applications listed in O 9 r 9(7). Approval for other applications must be sought by letter setting out the essence of the intended application and why it is necessary at that stage (O 9 r 9(8)); the Court may deal with it summarily or fix a case conference (O 9 r 9(9)). No application may be taken out from 14 days before trial until the merits are determined, except in a special case with the trial Judge's approval sought by letter (O 9 r 9(10)–(11)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 9(7): no application may be taken out by any party at any time other than as directed at the case conference or with the Court's approval, except an application for
>   - (a) an injunction or a search order (including any incidental matter);
>   - (b) substituted service;
>   - (c) service out of Singapore;
>   - (d) setting aside service of an originating process;
>   - (e) judgment in default of a notice of intention to contest or not contest;
>   - (f) judgment in default of defence;
>   - (g) summary judgment;
>   - (h) striking out of the whole of an action or defence;
>   - (i) stay of the whole action;
>   - (j) stay of enforcement of a judgment or order;
>   - (k) an enforcement order;
>   - (l) permission to appeal;
>   - (m) transfer of proceedings under the State Courts Act;
>   - (n) setting aside third party proceedings; or
>   - (o) permission to make an application for a committal order.
> - O 9 r 9(8): the Court's approval to file further applications other than those directed at a case conference must be sought by letter setting out the essence of the intended application and the reasons why it is necessary at that stage of the proceedings.
> - O 9 r 9(9): the Court may deal with the request summarily or fix a case conference.
> - O 9 r 9(10): no application may be taken out during the period starting 14 days before the commencement of the trial and ending when the Court has determined the merits, except in a special case and with the trial Judge's approval.
> - O 9 r 9(11): that approval must be sought by letter setting out the essence of the application and explaining why there is a special case.
> - PD 66(6)–(7): approval is sought by a Request in Form 9 of Appendix B, specifying the nature and essence of the application, the intended filing date and why it is necessary at that stage.
> - PD 66(8): if the application is to be filed (a) after the SAPT, say why it could not have been dealt with under the SAPT; (b) in the 14 days before trial, say why it is a special case.

**P** An application for further and better particulars is not on the O 9 r 9(7) list, so outside the SAPT it needs the Court's approval by letter explaining why it is necessary at that stage (*Hertel Singapore Pte Ltd v Cheng Swee Guan* [2025] SGHC 138 at [11]–[15]). The Court may reject a request summarily without hearing the other party, but it cannot allow the request without giving the other party an opportunity to be heard, by letter or at a case conference, because the right to be heard is a rule of natural justice (*Hertel* at [16]–[24], especially [23]).

**A** Check the list first. Summary judgment (O 9 r 9(7)(g)) and striking out the whole defence (O 9 r 9(7)(h)) can be filed now, without waiting for the SAPT (PYP22 Q2, PYP23 Q3.1). Striking out only part of the defence, or amending the statement of claim, is not on the list: before the SAPT, [Client] must file a Request (Form B9) setting out [the essence of the amendment: adding a claim for 7% contractual interest under cl 17] and why it is needed now ([to avoid a later, separate application and to let D plead to it]) (PYP22 Q3); after the SAPT, also explain why it could not have been in the SAPT. Within 14 days of trial, only a special case with the trial Judge's approval will do. **C** Therefore [Client] [may file [application] immediately / must first obtain the Court's approval by a Form B9 Request under O 9 r 9(8)].

*Source note: O 9 r 9(7)–(11) ROC p87–88; PD 66(4)–(8) SCPD p78–79; SYL p11 [77]–[78]; L2 s6–s7; Hertel [2025] SGHC 138 (Chua Lee Ming J) at [11]–[24], read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026); the current PD 66(6) names the form "Form B9" (epd2021, last updated 15 Jul 2026), matching L2 s7; tested PYP22 Q2, Q3, PYP23 Q3.1, PYP25 Q3.1, PYP21 Q3–Q4.*

### drp-w-0-34 — Security for costs: the grounds (O 9 r 12; Companies Act s 388)
*Keywords: security for costs, O 9 r 12, ordinarily resident out of the jurisdiction, foreign claimant, nominal claimant, funded by a non-party, reason to believe unable to pay costs, address not stated, incorrectly stated, changed address to evade, company claimant, Companies Act s 388, credible testimony, stay until security given, counterclaim, O 9 r 12(6), SAPT, O 9 r 9(4)(d), non-party security, O 9 r 12(3)*

**I** The issue is whether [D] can obtain security for its costs from [Claimant]. **C** [D] may apply for security because [Claimant] [is ordinarily resident out of Singapore (O 9 r 12(1)(a)) / is a company and there is reason to believe it will be unable to pay [D]'s costs (O 9 r 12(2); s 388 Companies Act)], and the application should be made in the SAPT (O 9 r 9(4)(d)).
**R** A defendant may apply for security for its costs if the claimant is ordinarily resident out of the jurisdiction; is a nominal claimant suing for another's benefit, or is funded by a non-party, and there is reason to believe it will be unable to pay the defendant's costs; or has not stated, has incorrectly stated, or has changed its address to evade the consequences of the litigation (O 9 r 12(1)). If the claimant is a company, s 388 of the Companies Act also applies (O 9 r 12(2)): the Court may require security where it appears by credible testimony that there is reason to believe the company will be unable to pay the defendant's costs if the defence succeeds, and stay the proceedings until it is given.

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 12(1): the defendant may apply for security for the defendant's costs of the action if the claimant
>   - (a) is ordinarily resident out of the jurisdiction;
>   - (b) is a nominal claimant who is suing for some other person's benefit (but not suing in a representative capacity) or is being funded by a non-party, and there is reason to believe that the claimant will be unable to pay the defendant's costs if ordered to do so; or
>   - (c) has not stated or has incorrectly stated the claimant's address in the originating claim or originating application, or has changed the claimant's address during the course of the proceedings, so as to evade the consequences of the litigation.
> - O 9 r 12(2): if the claimant is a company, section 388 of the Companies Act also applies.
> - Companies Act s 388(1): where a corporation is claimant in any action or other legal proceeding, the court may, if it appears by credible testimony that there is reason to believe that the corporation will be unable to pay the costs of the defendant if successful in the defendant's defence, require sufficient security to be given for those costs and stay all proceedings until the security is given.
> - O 9 r 12(3)–(4): security may be sought from a non-party who took an assignment for a share of the proceeds, or funds the claimant for a share or while instigating the action (background only); by summons served personally on the non-party.
> - O 9 r 12(6): "claimant" and "defendant" mean the persons in those positions, including on a counterclaim.
> - Security for costs is a SAPT matter (O 9 r 9(4)(d)).

**P** Whether a claimant is "ordinarily resident" depends on physical presence, mental attitude and purpose, with a degree of continuity; a person may be ordinarily resident in more than one place, and residence in Singapore weighs strongly against security (*Tjong Very Sumito v Chan Sing En* [2011] 4 SLR 580 at [22]–[50], decided under ROC 2014). Once a ground is made out, the Court decides whether it is just to order security (drp-w-0-35).

**A** Find the ground. [X Pte Ltd is a Singapore company whose ACRA-filed audited accounts show losses for two years] (PYP23 Q1.5): r 12(1)(a) does not apply (X is local), so rely on r 12(2) and s 388, using the accounts as credible testimony that there is reason to believe X cannot pay [D]'s costs. [A claimant who lives in Australia with no assets here]: r 12(1)(a). [A claimant funded by a third party with no assets]: r 12(1)(b). Timing: security is a SAPT matter, so raise it at the case conference and include it in the SAPT; apply early, since delay counts against the applicant in the discretion (drp-w-0-35). If a counterclaim is the real claim, the roles reverse (r 12(6)). **C** Therefore [D] has a ground under [O 9 r 12(2) and s 388 / O 9 r 12(1)(a)] and should apply in its SAPT for security of [$X], with proceedings stayed until it is given.

*Source note: O 9 r 12 ROC p89–90; Companies Act s 388 CA p536–537; O 9 r 9(4)(d) ROC p86; SYL p11–12 [83]–[85], [87]; L2 s13; Tjong Very Sumito SYL p11 [84(a)], ratio from Cyril-Exam ("Security for costs") and Nic-Muggers 5.1.3 (secondary); tested PYP23 Q1.5, PYP21 Q6 (context).*

### drp-w-0-35 — Security for costs: when the Court will order it (the discretion)
*Keywords: security for costs, discretion, two-stage framework, Cova Group Holdings v Advanced Submarine Networks, enlivened, just to order security, Ideals, Creative Elegance (M) Sdn Bhd v Puay Kim Seng, Tjong Very Sumito v Chan Sing En, strength of claim, ease of enforcement, reciprocal enforcement, assets in Singapore, residence, delay, stifling a genuine claim, quantum*

**I** The issue is whether the Court will exercise its discretion to order [Claimant] to give security, and in what amount. **C** The Court is [likely / unlikely] to order security, because [Claimant has no assets in Singapore and a judgment for costs would be hard to enforce in [country] / Claimant has substantial assets here and a strong claim].
**R** Security for costs involves two stages: first, whether a ground under O 9 r 12 (or s 388 of the Companies Act) is made out so that the discretion is enlivened; second, whether it is just to order security, having regard to the Ideals and all the circumstances. Relevant factors include the strength of the claimant's case, how easily a costs order could be enforced against the claimant, whether the claimant has assets in Singapore, and whether it is also resident here.

> **Rule in full**
> - Stage 1: is a ground made out (O 9 r 12(1)(a)–(c), r 12(2) with s 388 Companies Act)? (drp-w-0-34)
> - Stage 2: is it just to order security in all the circumstances, having regard to the Ideals (O 3 r 1)? Factors include:
>   - the strength of the claimant's claim (a weak claim favours security; the Court does not try the merits);
>   - ease of enforcing a costs order where the claimant and its assets are;
>   - whether the claimant has assets in Singapore that would answer a costs order;
>   - whether the claimant is ordinarily resident in Singapore as well as elsewhere;
> - Quantum: the Court fixes an amount it thinks just.

**P** The Court applies a two-stage framework: whether its discretion is enlivened, and then whether it is just to order security, having regard to the Ideals (*Cova Group Holdings Ltd v Advanced Submarine Networks Pte Ltd* [2023] 5 SLR 1576). In exercising the discretion the Court considers all the circumstances, including the strength of the claim and the ease of enforcing a costs order (*Creative Elegance (M) Sdn Bhd v Puay Kim Seng* [1999] 1 SLR(R) 112 at [13]–[25]; *Tjong Very Sumito v Chan Sing En* [2011] 4 SLR 580 at [51]), both decided under ROC 2014.

**A** Stage 1: [ground]. Stage 2: weigh the factors on the facts. [X's claim for $453,000 for unpaid goods looks strong on the documents], which weighs against security; but [two years of losses and no evidence of assets] mean a costs order may not be satisfied, which weighs for it. If the claimant is abroad, ask whether a Singapore costs order can be enforced there ([e.g. under reciprocal enforcement arrangements]) and whether it has assets here. Ask for an amount proportionate to the costs [D] is likely to incur. **C** On balance, the Court is [likely] to order [Claimant] to provide security of [$X], with the action stayed until it does.

*Source note: SYL p11–12 [84(a)], [86]; L2 s13 ("2 stage approach: Jurisdiction + Discretion"); Cova SYL p12 [86], two-stage framework from Cyril-Exam ("Security for costs", "Present case law per Cova Group Holdings") (secondary); factors from Cyril-Exam and Nic-Muggers 5.1.3 (secondary); tested PYP23 Q1.5.*

### drp-w-0-36 — Further and better particulars (O 9 r 13; PD 68)
*Keywords: further and better particulars, FBP, particulars, O 9 r 13, necessary on the facts of the case, material facts, not evidence, fishing, surprise, Sharikat Logistics v Ong Boon Chuan, Element Six Technologies v IIa Technologies, Access Medical v MHC Medical Network, Summary Table, Form B10, PD 68, more than 5 categories, SAPT, O 9 r 9(4)(e)*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can compel [D] to give further particulars of [its allegation that Client cheated / the alleged variation]. **C** Yes. The Court may order particulars under O 9 r 13 if it considers them necessary on the facts, and particulars of [the allegation] are necessary because [Client] cannot know the case it has to meet without them.
**R** The Court may order a party to serve particulars of any matter stated in its pleading if it is of the opinion that they are necessary on the facts of the case (O 9 r 13). The application is a SAPT matter (O 9 r 9(4)(e)); where more than 5 categories are sought and the application is contested, the parties complete a Summary Table in Form B10 instead of written submissions (PD 68).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 13: the Court may order a party to serve on any other party particulars of any matter stated in the firstmentioned party's pleading if the Court is of the opinion that the particulars are necessary on the facts of the case.
> - O 9 r 9(4)(e): further and better particulars are a SAPT matter.
> - PD 68(1): applies to an application under O 9 r 13 (or for production) where (a) more than 5 categories or sub-categories are sought, or the parties agree it applies; and (b) the application is contested.
> - PD 68(2): the parties complete the Summary Table in Form B10 of Appendix B instead of written submissions, unless the Court allows submissions in exceptional circumstances.
> - Form 9 and Form 13 already require particulars of misrepresentation, fraud, breach of trust, wilful deceit, undue influence and states of mind.

**P** Particulars are ordered to define the material facts a party must plead, not to obtain the evidence by which they will be proved (*Sharikat Logistics Pte Ltd v Ong Boon Chuan* [2011] SGHC 196; *Element Six Technologies Ltd v IIa Technologies Pte Ltd* [2017] SGHCR 16 at [12]). Pre-ROC 2021 cases remain useful, but under ROC 2021 the test is whether the particulars are "necessary on the facts of the case", read with the Ideals (*Access Medical Pte Ltd v MHC Medical Network Pte Ltd* [2023] SGHCR 19 at [18]).

**A** Identify the bare allegation: [D's defence says Client "cheated in an earlier round", with no details of when, how or by whom it was observed] (PYP21 Q4). Particulars of when, where and what conduct is alleged are material facts [Client] needs to answer the allegation and avoid surprise at trial, so they are necessary on the facts. Do not ask for the evidence (e.g. [the names of every witness or the footage itself]); that is for production and AEICs. Procedure: first write to [D] asking for the particulars; if refused, include the request in the SAPT (or seek approval to apply earlier under O 9 r 9(8)); if more than 5 categories and contested, file the PD 68 Summary Table. If the allegation is unsustainable even with particulars, consider striking out (drp-w-0-40 to 0-41). **C** Therefore the Court is likely to order [D] to give particulars of [allegation] under O 9 r 13.

*Source note: O 9 r 13 ROC p90–91; PD 68(1)–(2) SCPD p81–82; O 9 r 9(4)(e) ROC p86; SYL p12 [88]–[89]; L2 s15; Sharikat and Element Six SYL p12 [89(b)], propositions from Cyril-Exam ("Further and better particulars") and Nic-Muggers 5.1.4 (secondary); Access Medical SYL p12 [89(b)], proposition from Cyril-Exam ("Note Access Medical (post-ROC 2021)") (secondary); tested PYP21 Q4, PYP23 Q2.1, PYP25 Q2.1.*

### drp-w-0-37 — Amendment of pleadings: agreement or permission, timing, later events, inferences (O 9 r 14)
*Keywords: amendment of pleadings, amend statement of claim, O 9 r 14, written agreement, not less than 14 days before trial, permission to amend, less than 14 days before trial, special case, events after the originating claim, inferences, material facts amended, O 9 r 14(6), costs of amendment, O 21 r 3(3), SAPT, O 9 r 9(4)(f), approval by letter, amend before defence, leave not required myth*

**I** The issue is what [Client] must do to amend its statement of claim to [add a claim for contractual interest / change the alleged representation], and when. **C** [Client] may amend by written agreement with [D] up to 14 days before trial (O 9 r 14(5)); failing agreement it needs the Court's permission (O 9 r 14(1)), sought in the SAPT or, before then, with the Court's approval by letter (O 9 r 9(4)(f), (8)).
**R** The Court may allow the parties to amend their pleadings (O 9 r 14(1)); any pleading may be amended by written agreement not less than 14 days before trial (O 9 r 14(5)). The Court must not allow an amendment less than 14 days before trial except in a special case (O 9 r 14(3)), may in a special case allow events after the originating claim to be pleaded (O 9 r 14(2)), and may draw appropriate inferences if material facts are amended (O 9 r 14(6)). Amendment is a SAPT matter (O 9 r 9(4)(f)); an application before or after the SAPT needs approval under O 9 r 9(8).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 14(1): the Court may allow the parties to amend their pleadings.
> - O 9 r 14(2): in a special case, the Court may consider events that occurred after the originating claim is filed to be pleaded even though they do not relate back to the date of filing.
> - O 9 r 14(3): the Court must not allow any pleading to be amended less than 14 days before the commencement of the trial except in a special case.
> - O 9 r 14(4): amendments after the relevant limitation period has expired (drp-w-0-39).
> - O 9 r 14(5): despite (1) to (4), any pleading may, by written agreement between the parties, be amended not less than 14 days before the commencement of the trial.
> - O 9 r 14(6): if material facts in the pleadings are amended, the Court may draw the appropriate inferences.
> - O 9 r 14(7): the Rule applies to an originating application with the necessary modifications.
> - O 21 r 3(3): the costs of and occasioned by any amendment made without permission must be borne by the party making it, unless the Court otherwise orders.
> - O 9 r 9(4)(f), (8): amendment is a SAPT matter; outside the SAPT, approval by letter (Request, Form B9).

**P** ROC 2021 contains no right to amend once without permission before the defence; every amendment needs written agreement or the Court's permission (O 9 r 14(1), (5)). For when permission will be granted, see drp-w-0-38.

**A** Steps and timelines. (1) Ask [D] in writing to agree to the amendment; if it agrees, file the amended pleading under O 9 r 14(5) (at least 14 days before trial), bearing the costs occasioned unless the Court orders otherwise (O 21 r 3(3)). (2) If [D] refuses: if the SAPT has not been filed, seek approval by a Form B9 Request explaining why the amendment is needed now, or include it in the SAPT (filed within 21 days after the relevant case conference, reply 21 days later); if after the SAPT, also explain why it was not in the SAPT. (3) Within 14 days of trial, show a special case (O 9 r 14(3)). On the "plead now, amend later" plan (PYP23 Q2.2, PYP25 Q2.2), two difficulties apart from cost and delay: first, if [the alleged oral representation] is later changed to [a written one], the Court may draw adverse inferences from the change in material facts (O 9 r 14(6)), damaging [Client]'s credibility; second, permission is not automatic and may be refused for prejudice or late amendment (drp-w-0-38), and the Form 9 certification means [Client] should not plead what he cannot vouch for. The intern's view that a party may keep amending without leave until the defence is filed (PYP21 Q3) is wrong under ROC 2021. **C** Therefore [Client] should seek [D]'s written agreement and, failing that, apply for permission [in the SAPT / with the Court's prior approval], at least 14 days before trial.

*Source note: O 9 r 14 ROC p91–92; O 21 r 3(3) ROC p232; O 9 r 9(4)(f), (8) ROC p86–87; Form 9 certification (Forms: Appendix A - Form 9); SYL p12–13 [90]–[92], [94]–[96]; L2 s17 (14-day limits); "no right to amend once without permission": O 9 r 14 (unchanged in the current ROC on SSO, as at 1 Apr 2026) provides only for amendment with permission (r 14(1)) or by written agreement (r 14(5)); the ROC 2014 right to amend once before pleadings closed has no counterpart; tested PYP21 Q3, PYP22 Q3, PYP23 Q2.2–2.3, PYP25 Q2.2–2.3.*

### drp-w-0-38 — Amendment: when the Court grants permission
*Keywords: permission to amend, leave to amend, Wang Piao v Lee Wee Ching, three-step framework, Maag Daniel v Lalit Kumar Modi, Sheagar s/o T M Veloo v Belfield International, Review Publishing v Lee Hsien Loong, real issues in controversy, procedural fairness, prejudice, compensable by costs, second bite of the cherry, late amendment, delay, Ideals*

**I** The issue is whether the Court will grant [Client] permission to amend [its statement of claim to add [claim]]. **C** The Court is [likely] to grant permission, because the amendment [will enable the real controversy to be determined and causes [D] no prejudice that costs cannot cure], applying the three-step framework in *Wang Piao v Lee Wee Ching* [2024] 4 SLR 540 at [16]–[19].
**R** The Court may allow amendments (O 9 r 14(1)). In deciding whether to grant permission, the Court asks three questions in turn: first, at what stage of the proceedings the amendment is sought, since the later the application, the stronger the grounds needed; second, whether the amendment would enable the real question or issue in controversy between the parties to be determined; and third, whether it is nonetheless just to allow it, chiefly whether it causes prejudice to the other party that cannot be compensated in costs and whether the applicant is effectively asking for a second bite at the cherry (*Wang Piao v Lee Wee Ching* [2024] 4 SLR 540 at [16]–[19]; *Maag, Daniel v Lalit Kumar Modi* [2024] SGHC 311 at [48]).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 14(1): the Court may allow the parties to amend their pleadings; (3) not less than 14 days before trial except in a special case; (4) special rules after limitation (drp-w-0-39).
> - *Wang Piao* at [16]–[19], the three-step analytical framework:
>   - Step 1 ([16]): "as a threshold question, a court needs to determine the stage of the proceedings in which the amendment to the pleadings is being sought"; the later the application, the stronger the grounds required to justify it.
>   - Step 2 ([17]): "whether the amendments sought 'would enable the real question and/or issue in controversy between the parties to be determined'"; the application must be made in good faith; a useless, merely technical or trivial amendment is disallowed; this involves a rudimentary assessment of the merits (a pleading bad in law raises no real issue).
>   - Step 3 ([18]): "whether it is nonetheless just to allow the amendments", focusing on the opposing party: (a) prejudice that cannot be compensated in costs; and (b) whether the applicant is effectively asking for a second bite at the cherry (*Review Publishing* at [113]).
> - After summary judgment, amendments are allowed sparingly (*Wang Piao* at [41]).
> - At step 2 the striking-out standards inform the inquiry: an amendment that is plainly or obviously unsustainable does not raise a real issue (*Maag* at [49], citing *Iskandar* at [19]).
> - Considerations from the ROC 2014 cases (still cited by the syllabus):
>   - will the amendment enable the real issues in controversy to be determined (substantive justice)?
>   - is it fair to the other party: is there prejudice that costs cannot compensate?
>   - delay alone is not decisive, but a late amendment (especially at or after trial) is more likely to prejudice the other side;
>   - an amendment that is in effect a second bite of the cherry will be refused.

**P** The Court applies the three-step framework in *Wang Piao v Lee Wee Ching* [2024] 4 SLR 540 at [16]–[19] (applied in *Maag, Daniel v Lalit Kumar Modi* [2024] SGHC 311 at [48]–[49]). Its third step adopts the two factors from *Review Publishing Co Ltd v Lee Hsien Loong* [2010] 1 SLR 52 at [113]: prejudice not compensable in costs, and a second bite at the cherry. Earlier, the primary considerations were described as whether the amendment enables the real issues to be determined and whether procedural fairness to the other party is maintained (*Sheagar s/o T M Veloo v Belfield International (Hong Kong) Ltd* [2014] 3 SLR 524 at [116]–[119], decided under ROC 2014).

**A** Stage: [the application is made before the SAPT, long before trial], so ordinary grounds suffice; an application on the eve of trial or after summary judgment needs much stronger grounds. Real issues: [adding the 7% contractual interest under cl 17 of the Settlement Agreement] arises from a document already in the case and lets the Court decide the full extent of [D]'s liability. Fairness: the application is made [before the SAPT and long before trial]; [D] can plead to it, and any extra costs can be met by a costs order (O 21 r 3(3)). It is not a second bite: [Client] is not trying to re-run a point already decided. Contrast an amendment sought [on the eve of trial or after evidence closes], which is more likely to prejudice [D] and be refused or allowed only on terms. **C** Therefore the Court is likely to grant permission to amend, on terms that [Client] bears the costs occasioned by the amendment.

*Source note: O 9 r 14 ROC p91–92; SYL p12 [93(a)]; L2 s17; Sheagar and Review Publishing propositions from Cyril-Exam ("Amendment of pleadings") and Nic-Muggers 5.1.5 (secondary) and PYP22-Xav Q3; Wang Piao [2023] SGHC 216 (Goh Yihan JC; reported [2024] 4 SLR 540) at [16]–[19], [41] and Maag [2024] SGHC 311 (Dedar Singh Gill J) at [48]–[49], read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026); the syllabus cites Maag as [2025] 3 SLR 1093 and Wang Piao at [14]–[19]; the steps are at [16]–[18] and the SLR report of Maag could not be confirmed online; tested PYP22 Q3, PYP23 Q2.2, PYP25 Q2.2.*

### drp-w-0-39 — Amendment after the limitation period has expired (O 9 r 14(4))
*Keywords: amendment after limitation, time-barred, O 9 r 14(4), correct the name of a party, genuine mistake, not misleading, substitute new party, alter capacity, add or substitute new cause of action, same or substantially the same facts, Multistar Holdings v Geocon Piling, MCST Plan No 3322 v Mer Vue Developments, relation back*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can amend to [add a new claim in fraud / correct D's name] although the limitation period for it has expired. **C** The Court may allow the amendment only within O 9 r 14(4): [the new cause of action arises out of the same or substantially the same facts as the existing claim / it corrects a genuine misnomer]; [here it does / does not], so the amendment [may / is unlikely to] be allowed.
**R** Where permission to amend is sought after the relevant limitation period has expired, the Court may allow an amendment to correct a party's name (even if it substitutes a new party) if it was a genuine mistake and not misleading as to identity; to alter the capacity in which a party sues, if it could have sued in that capacity when the claim was issued; or to add or substitute a new cause of action arising out of the same or substantially the same facts as an existing cause of action already pleaded (O 9 r 14(4)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 14(4): where an application for permission to amend is made after the relevant limitation period has expired, the Court may allow the amendment in the following circumstances:
>   - (a) an amendment to correct the name of a party even if its alleged effect will be to substitute a new party, if it was a genuine mistake and was not misleading as to the identity of the party in question;
>   - (b) an amendment to alter the capacity in which a party sues, if the party might have sued in that capacity at the time the originating claim was issued or the counterclaim was made;
>   - (c) an amendment to add or substitute a new cause of action, if the new cause of action arises out of the same or substantially the same facts as an existing cause of action for which relief has already been claimed in the same action.
> - Even within (a)–(c), the general discretion to grant permission still applies (drp-w-0-38).

**P** A new cause of action that relies on new factual material, rather than the same or substantially the same facts already pleaded, will not be allowed after the limitation period has expired (*Multistar Holdings Ltd v Geocon Piling & Engineering Pte Ltd* [2016] 2 SLR 1; *Management Corporation Strata Title Plan No 3322 v Mer Vue Developments Pte Ltd* [2016] 4 SLR 351 at [48]), both decided under ROC 2014.

**A** First, confirm the new claim is time-barred (drp-w-0-6: [the breach occurred more than 6 years ago]). Then match the amendment to a limb. A misnamed defendant ([sued "ABC Pte Ltd" instead of "ABC Holdings Pte Ltd", which knew it was the target]) fits (a). Suing as executor rather than personally fits (b) if [Client] could have sued in that capacity at the start. For a new cause of action, compare facts: [a claim in misrepresentation added to a claim in breach of warranty, based on the same statements and the same transaction] arguably arises from substantially the same facts; [a new claim based on a different meeting and different statements] relies on new factual material and fails (*Mer Vue* at [48]). **C** Therefore the amendment [may be allowed under O 9 r 14(4)([c]) / should be refused because it relies on new facts].

*Source note: O 9 r 14(4) ROC p91; SYL p12 [93(b)]; L2 s17; Multistar and Mer Vue propositions from Cyril-Exam ("Amendment of pleadings") and Nic-Muggers 5.1.5 (secondary).*

### drp-w-0-79 — Adding or removing parties; consolidation, joint or consecutive trials and stays (O 9 rr 10–11) (background only — not examinable per SYL p1)
*Keywords: add a party, remove a party, joinder, O 9 r 10, deemed commenced against added defendant, limitation, O 9 r 10(3), consolidation, joint trial, tried together, one after another, consecutive trials, stay pending another action, O 9 r 11, common question of law, same factual situation, SAPT, O 9 r 9(4)(a)–(b), non-party security, O 9 r 12(3), background only*

**I** The issue is whether [X] can be [added as / removed as] a party, and whether [Action A] and [Action B] can be [consolidated / tried together / one stayed]. **C** The Court may [add / remove] [X] under O 9 r 10(1) and may [consolidate / order a joint or consecutive trial / stay] under O 9 r 11 because [the actions share a common question of law / arise out of the same factual situation], and the application belongs in the SAPT (O 9 r 9(4)(a)–(b)).
**R** The Court may add or remove claimants or defendants, permit a third party notice under Order 10, or direct that the originating process be served on anyone who may have an interest in the action (O 9 r 10(1)). A person who wishes to be added may attend, or ask by letter for, a case conference (O 9 r 10(2)). An added defendant is sued from the date the action is amended (O 9 r 10(3)). The Court may consolidate actions, try them together or one immediately after another, or stay one pending the other, if there is a common question of law, the reliefs claimed concern or arise out of the same factual situation, or it is appropriate (O 9 r 11).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 10(1): the Court may add or remove one or more claimants or defendants, give permission for a defendant to issue a third party notice in accordance with Order 10, or give directions for the originating process to be served on any person who may have an interest in the action.
> - O 9 r 10(2): a person seeking to be added may attend a case conference if aware of it, or seek one by letter to the Court copied to all parties.
> - O 9 r 10(3): where a person is added as a defendant, the action is deemed commenced against that person on the date the amendment is made.
> - O 9 r 11: the Court may order 2 or more actions to be consolidated, tried together or one immediately after another, or any of them stayed pending the determination of the other(s), if it is of the opinion that
>   - (a) there is some common question of law in the actions;
>   - (b) the reliefs claimed in the actions concern or arise out of the same factual situation; or
>   - (c) it is appropriate to do so.
> - O 9 r 9(4)(a)–(b): addition or removal of parties and consolidation are SAPT matters.
> - O 9 r 12(3)–(4): security from a funding or assignee non-party is covered in drp-w-0-34.

**P** No case is set in the syllabus for these points (SYL [79]–[82]).

**A** Raise the point at the case conference and include it in the SAPT (O 9 r 9(4)); outside the SAPT, ask for approval by letter (O 9 r 9(8); drp-w-0-33). Adding a defendant: because the action runs against [X] only from the amendment (r 10(3)), check limitation against [X] at that date (drp-w-0-6). Two actions over [the same collapsed building]: same factual situation, so seek consolidation or a joint trial to save costs and avoid inconsistent findings (r 11(b)). **C** Therefore [Client] should seek, in its SAPT, an order [adding X / consolidating the actions] under O 9 r [10 / 11].

*Source note: O 9 rr 9(4), 10, 11 ROC p86–89; SYL p11 [79]–[82], [85]. ROC text from the 2021 library copy, checked against SSO (current version as at 18 Nov 2026; extraction subagent): unchanged since 2021.*

## 0.VIII Striking out, summary judgment and questions of law

- **The other side's case is hopeless: strike out or summary judgment?**
  - Strike out the whole action or defence: no approval needed (O 9 r 9(7)(h)); part only: in the SAPT (O 9 r 9(4)(h)) → [0-40](#drp-w-0-40)
  - No reasonable cause of action or defence, on the pleading alone, no evidence (O 9 r 16(1)(a), (2)) → [0-40](#drp-w-0-40)
  - Abuse of process: groundless, collateral purpose, sham, vexatious repetition (O 9 r 16(1)(b); *Gabriel Peter*, *Chee Siok Chin*) → [0-41](#drp-w-0-41)
  - Interests of justice (O 9 r 16(1)(c); *Iskandar*); striking out affidavits (O 9 r 16(4); *Asian Eco*) → [0-41](#drp-w-0-41)
- **Summary judgment (claimant, or defendant on a counterclaim)**
  - After the defence; within 28 days of its service unless the Court permits; no approval needed (O 9 r 17(1), (13); O 9 r 9(7)(g)) → [0-42](#drp-w-0-42)
  - Affidavits: applicant all evidence; respondent 14 days; reply 14 days; no more without approval (O 9 r 17(2)–(6)) → [0-42](#drp-w-0-42)
  - Test: applicant's prima facie case, then respondent must show a triable issue or other reason for trial; mere assertions fail (*M2B*, *Horizon Capital*, *Ling Yew Kong*, *Ritzland*) → [0-43](#drp-w-0-43)
  - Orders: dismiss, unconditional permission to defend, judgment, conditional permission if dubious; stay pending counterclaim (O 9 r 17(7), (9)) → [0-43](#drp-w-0-43)
- **The case turns on a point of law or the meaning of a document**
  - Court may decide it without a trial, on application or its own accord; judgment if it disposes of the matter (O 9 r 19; *UOB v Lippo Marina*, *Babbobox*) → [0-44](#drp-w-0-44)
- **Admissions to enter judgment on, or a third party to bring in? (background only — not examinable per SYL p1)**
  - Judgment on admissions of fact in pleadings or other documents (O 9 r 18(2)) → [0-80](#drp-w-0-80)
  - Third party notice (Form 20 or 21) for contribution, indemnity, related relief or a shared question; no permission if begun by originating claim and before the defence; else Form 22 with affidavit; directions by Form 23 summons (O 10 rr 1–4) → [0-80](#drp-w-0-80)

### drp-w-0-40 — Striking out: procedure, and "no reasonable cause of action or defence" (O 9 r 16(1)(a))
*Keywords: striking out, strike out, O 9 r 16, no reasonable cause of action, no reasonable defence, no evidence admissible, O 9 r 16(2), whole of action or defence, O 9 r 9(7)(h), part of action or defence, SAPT, O 9 r 9(4)(h), stay or dismiss, judgment entered, Envy Asset Management v Lau Lee Sheng, plainly unsustainable, originating application as pleading*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can have [D's defence / the claim] struck out as disclosing no reasonable [defence / cause of action]. **C** [Client] may apply under O 9 r 16(1)(a), without the Court's prior approval because it seeks to strike out the whole [defence] (O 9 r 9(7)(h)); the application [is likely to succeed / fail] because, taking the pleaded facts as true, [the defence raises no answer known to law / the pleading does disclose an arguable case].
**R** The Court may order any or part of any pleading to be struck out or amended on the ground that it discloses no reasonable cause of action or defence, is an abuse of process, or it is in the interests of justice to do so, and may stay or dismiss the action or enter judgment accordingly (O 9 r 16(1)). No evidence is admissible on the first ground (O 9 r 16(2)). An application to strike out the whole action or defence needs no prior approval (O 9 r 9(7)(h)); striking out part is a SAPT matter (O 9 r 9(4)(h)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 16(1): the Court may order any or part of any pleading to be struck out or amended, on the ground that
>   - (a) it discloses no reasonable cause of action or defence;
>   - (b) it is an abuse of process of the Court; or
>   - (c) it is in the interests of justice to do so,
>   - and may order the action to be stayed or dismissed or judgment to be entered accordingly.
> - O 9 r 16(2): no evidence is admissible on an application under (1)(a).
> - O 9 r 16(3): the Rule applies to an originating application as if it were a pleading.
> - O 9 r 16(4): the Court may order any affidavit or other document filed in Court to be struck out or redacted on the ground that (a) the party had no right to file it; (b) it is an abuse of process; or (c) it is in the interests of justice.
> - O 9 r 9(7)(h): striking out the whole of an action or defence needs no approval; O 9 r 9(4)(h): striking out part is a SAPT matter.

**P** The bar for striking out is high and the power is exercised very sparingly; the applicant must show the claim is obviously unsustainable, so that it is impossible, not just improbable, for it to succeed (*Envy Asset Management Pte Ltd v Lau Lee Sheng* [2024] SGHC 38 at [17]). Under ground (a) the test is whether the pleading, taking its facts as true, shows some chance of success or raises a question fit to be decided at trial (*Envy Asset* at [18], citing *Gabriel Peter & Partners v Wee Chong Jin* [1997] 3 SLR(R) 649 at [21] and *Iskandar bin Rahmat v Attorney-General* [2022] 2 SLR 1018 at [17]). Counsel should identify which limb of O 9 r 16 is relied on and argue to that limb's standard (*Envy Asset* at [21]–[22]).

**A** Read [D]'s defence on its face. If it pleads [only a bare denial with no facts], or [a defence that is no answer in law, e.g. that the debt is not payable because the claimant is "a foreign company"], it discloses no reasonable defence, and no evidence is needed. If the defence turns on disputed facts ([an alleged oral variation], [duress]), ground (a) fails because the pleaded facts are assumed true; consider ground (b) (drp-w-0-41) or, better, summary judgment, where evidence is admissible (drp-w-0-42 to 0-43). Procedure: summons (Form 1) with affidavit (O 3 r 5), filed at any time if it targets the whole defence; if only part, include it in the SAPT or seek approval. Costs follow the event (drp-w-0-72). **C** Therefore [Client] should apply to strike out [the whole defence] under O 9 r 16(1)(a), and the Court [is likely / unlikely] to strike it out and enter judgment.

*Source note: O 9 r 16 ROC p92; O 9 r 9(4)(h), (7)(h) ROC p86–87; O 3 r 5 ROC p56; SYL p13 [97]–[99(a)]; L2 s19; Envy Asset [2024] SGHC 38 (Goh Yihan J) at [16]–[22], read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026); the syllabus cites it as "[2024] 4 SLR" without a page and no SLR page was found online; tested PYP21 Q4, PYP22 Q2.*

### drp-w-0-41 — Striking out: abuse of process and the interests of justice (O 9 r 16(1)(b)–(c), (4))
*Keywords: abuse of process, O 9 r 16(1)(b), improper use of the court's machinery, collateral purpose, ulterior motive, delay tactics, sham, manifestly groundless, vexatious, relitigation, multiplicity of proceedings, Gabriel Peter & Partners v Wee Chong Jin, Chee Siok Chin v Minister for Home Affairs, interests of justice, O 9 r 16(1)(c), Iskandar bin Rahmat v Attorney-General, Leong Quee Ching Karen v Lim Soon Huat, plainly or obviously unsustainable, striking out affidavits, O 9 r 16(4), Asian Eco Technology v Deng Yiming*

**I** The issue is whether [D's defence / the action / D's affidavit] should be struck out as an abuse of process or in the interests of justice. **C** [It should / should not] be struck out under O 9 r 16(1)(b) [and (c)], because [it is put forward only to delay judgment / it relitigates an issue already decided / it is plainly unsustainable].
**R** The Court may strike out any pleading that is an abuse of process of the Court, or where it is in the interests of justice to do so (O 9 r 16(1)(b)–(c)), and may strike out or redact any affidavit or document on the same grounds or where the party had no right to file it (O 9 r 16(4)). "Abuse of process" is broad and covers the improper use of the Court's machinery, including proceedings that are manifestly groundless or serve no useful purpose, are brought for a collateral purpose, involve deception or a sham, or are vexatious multiple or successive proceedings.

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 16(1)(b): the pleading is an abuse of process of the Court.
> - O 9 r 16(1)(c): it is in the interests of justice to strike it out.
> - O 9 r 16(4): any affidavit or document filed in Court may be struck out or redacted where (a) the party had no right to file it; (b) it is an abuse of process; or (c) it is in the interests of justice.
> - Unlike (1)(a), evidence is admissible on (b) and (c).
> - Categories of abuse (*Chee Siok Chin* at [34], as summarised in the seniors' notes):
>   - proceedings that are manifestly groundless or without foundation, or serve no useful purpose;
>   - proceedings brought for a collateral or improper purpose;
>   - deception or a sham;
>   - multiple or successive proceedings that are vexatious or oppressive.

**P** "Abuse of process" engages considerations of public policy and the interests of justice, and the Court will prevent the improper use of its machinery (*Gabriel Peter & Partners v Wee Chong Jin* [1997] 3 SLR(R) 649 at [22]; *Chee Siok Chin v Minister for Home Affairs* [2006] 1 SLR(R) 582 at [34]). These ROC 2014 cases remain relevant under ROC 2021 (*Iskandar bin Rahmat v Attorney-General* [2022] 2 SLR 1018 at [17]–[19]; *Leong Quee Ching Karen v Lim Soon Huat* [2022] SGHC 309 at [23]). Under the "interests of justice" limb, a pleading may be struck out if it is plainly or obviously unsustainable (*Iskandar* at [19]). An affidavit may be struck out or redacted under O 9 r 16(4) where the party had no right to file it, it is an abuse of process, or it is in the interests of justice; the *Iskandar* tests apply to r 16(4)(b)–(c), and these grounds "should not be construed too widely" (*Asian Eco Technology Pte Ltd v Deng Yiming* [2023] SGHC 260 at [11], [16], [18]). Irrelevant material is struck out only where that serves a wider purpose, such as removing scandalous allegations, saving substantial trial time or costs, or preventing a collateral attack (at [20]–[21], reading O 15 r 25(1) in that light).

**A** Identify the abuse on the facts: [settlement correspondence suggests D's defence is filed only to drag out proceedings] (PYP22 Q2); [D's defence repeats allegations a court has already rejected]; [the action is brought to pressure D into a commercial concession]. Evidence of the improper purpose is needed and suspicion is not enough: [here, the only evidence is C's impression from settlement talks, so the ground is weak]. Under (c), argue that [the defence of illegality is plainly unsustainable because the agreements are not moneylending transactions on their face]. If [D's affidavit contains scandalous or irrelevant material], apply under r 16(4) to strike out or redact it. Where the dispute is factual, summary judgment is usually the better route (drp-w-0-42). **C** Therefore [Client] [has / lacks] grounds to strike out [the defence] as an abuse of process [and in the interests of justice], and should [also / instead] apply for summary judgment.

*Source note: O 9 r 16(1), (4) ROC p92; SYL p13 [99(b)–(d)]; L2 s19; Gabriel Peter, Chee Siok Chin, Iskandar and Leong Quee Ching SYL p13 [99]; abuse categories and "plainly or obviously unsustainable" from Cyril-Exam ("Striking out") (secondary); PYP22-Xav Q2 (Gabriel Peter at [22]); Asian Eco SYL p13 [99(d)], read on eLitigation [2023] SGHC 260 at [11]–[21] (18 Nov 2026, extraction subagent); tested PYP21 Q4, PYP22 Q2.*

### drp-w-0-42 — Summary judgment: when to apply and the affidavit timetable (O 9 r 17)
*Keywords: summary judgment, O 9 r 17, after defence filed and served, no defence to a claim, part of a claim, except as to amount of damages, within 28 days after service of the defence, O 9 r 17(13), Court otherwise permits, no permission required, O 9 r 9(7)(g), claimant's affidavit all evidence, defendant's affidavit 14 days, reply affidavit 14 days, no further affidavits, counterclaim summary judgment, O 9 r 17(11), fast way to obtain judgment*

**I** The issue is how [Client] can obtain judgment quickly against [D], and the procedure and timelines. **C** [Client] should apply for summary judgment under O 9 r 17, which needs no prior approval (O 9 r 9(7)(g)), within 28 days after [D]'s defence was served, that is by [date] (O 9 r 17(13)), with an affidavit containing all the evidence necessary or material to the claim.
**R** After the defence is filed and served, the claimant may apply for summary judgment on the ground that the defendant has no defence to a claim, part of a claim, or a claim except as to the amount of damages (O 9 r 17(1)). The claimant's affidavit must contain all necessary or material evidence; the defendant, if disputing, files its affidavit within 14 days after service; the claimant may reply within 14 days; and no further affidavits may be filed without approval (O 9 r 17(2)–(6)). Unless the Court permits, the application may not be made more than 28 days after service of the defence (or defence to counterclaim) (O 9 r 17(13)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 17(1): the claimant may apply for summary judgment against any defendant after the defence has been filed and served in an originating claim on the ground that the defendant has no defence to
>   - (a) a claim;
>   - (b) a particular part of a claim; or
>   - (c) a claim or part of a claim, except as to the amount of any damages claimed.
> - O 9 r 17(2): the claimant's affidavit must contain all the evidence that is necessary or material to the claim.
> - O 9 r 17(3): if the defendant disputes the application in any way, it must file and serve its affidavit within 14 days after service of the claimant's application and affidavit.
> - O 9 r 17(4): the defendant's affidavit must contain all the evidence that is necessary or material to the defence.
> - O 9 r 17(5): if the claimant disputes anything in the defendant's affidavit, it must file and serve its affidavit within 14 days after service of the defendant's affidavit.
> - O 9 r 17(6): no further affidavits may be filed without the Court's approval.
> - O 9 r 17(11)–(12): a defendant who has served a counterclaim, and received a defence to it, may apply for summary judgment on the counterclaim; (2)–(10) apply with modifications.
> - O 9 r 17(13): except where the Court otherwise permits, an application under this Rule may not be made after the expiry of 28 days after the date of service of the defence or defence to counterclaim.
> - O 9 r 9(7)(g): summary judgment needs no prior approval.

**P** Summary judgment exists to allow a claimant to obtain judgment quickly where there is plainly no defence to the claim (*Ling Yew Kong v Teo Vin Li Richard* [2014] 2 SLR 123 at [29]–[36]).

**A** Timeline: [R's defence was filed and served on 1 November] (PYP23 Q3.1), so the application must be filed by [29 November] unless the Court permits otherwise; summary judgment is only available once the defence is in, and only in an originating claim. File the summons (Form 1) and an affidavit exhibiting all the evidence, e.g. [the contract, proof of payment, and the correspondence showing the variation was never agreed]. [D] then has 14 days to respond; [Client] 14 days to reply. If the defence is also legally hopeless, add an application to strike out (drp-w-0-40). For the test and likely outcome, see drp-w-0-43.
