Costs: the successful party usually gets costs (drp-w-0-72). **C** Therefore [Client] should file a summary judgment application under O 9 r 17 by [date], without needing the Court's prior approval.

*Source note: O 9 r 17 ROC p92–94; O 9 r 9(7)(g) ROC p87; SYL p13–14 [100]–[102], [106]; L2 s21–s22; Ling Yew Kong SYL p14 [103] with purpose from Cyril-Exam ("Summary judgment") and PYP22-Xav Q2 (secondary); tested PYP21 Q4, PYP22 Q2, PYP23 Q3.1, PYP25 Q3.1.*

### drp-w-0-43 — Summary judgment: the test, the possible orders, counterclaims
*Keywords: summary judgment test, prima facie case, burden shifts, triable issue, fair probability of a bona fide defence, reasonable ground, some other reason for trial, mere assertion, equivocal, M2B World Asia Pacific v Matsumura Akihiko, Horizon Capital Fund v Ollech David, Ling Yew Kong v Teo Vin Li Richard, Ritzland Investment v Grace Management, conditional permission to defend, dubious defence, payment into court, unconditional permission to defend, stay of enforcement pending counterclaim, O 9 r 17(7), O 9 r 17(9)*

**I** The issue is whether [Client]'s summary judgment application will succeed, and what orders the Court may make. **C** [Client] has a prima facie case for judgment because [D accepted the goods and has not paid]; [D]'s defence [of a "failed publicity campaign" / of an oral variation] is a bare assertion contradicted by the documents and raises no triable issue, so the Court is likely to grant judgment under O 9 r 17(7)(c) [or, at most, conditional permission to defend under r 17(7)(d)].
**R** The applicant must first show a prima facie case for summary judgment; if it does, the burden shifts to the respondent to show a triable issue or question, or some other reason for a trial (*M2B World Asia Pacific Pte Ltd v Matsumura Akihiko* [2015] 1 SLR 325 at [17]–[19]). The Court may dismiss the application, give unconditional permission to defend, give judgment, or give permission to defend on conditions if the defence is of a dubious nature (O 9 r 17(7)), and may stay enforcement of a summary judgment until the trial of a counterclaim (O 9 r 17(9)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 17(7): the Court may
>   - (a) dismiss the application;
>   - (b) grant permission to defend to the defendant without any conditions;
>   - (c) grant judgment to the claimant; or
>   - (d) grant permission to defend to the defendant with conditions if the defence or any issue raised therein is of a dubious nature.
> - O 9 r 17(8): after summary judgment on part, the claimant may proceed with the rest of the claim or against other defendants.
> - O 9 r 17(9): the Court may order a stay of enforcement of any summary judgment against a defendant until after the trial of the defendant's counterclaim (and likewise against a claimant on a counterclaim: r 17(11)–(12)).
> - O 9 r 17(10): the Court may set aside or vary a summary judgment against a defendant absent at the hearing.
> - Principles (*M2B* at [17]–[19]; L2 s23):
>   - the applicant must show a prima facie case for summary judgment, failing which the application is dismissed;
>   - if it does, the burden shifts: the respondent must show a triable issue or question, or that there should be a trial for some other reason;
>   - mere assertions, or assertions that are equivocal, imprecise or inherently improbable, will not raise a triable issue.

**P** The applicable principles under O 9 r 17 are set out in *Horizon Capital Fund v Ollech David* [2023] SGHC 164 at [58]–[60], where a bare point of law that is plainly misconceived was held not to resist summary judgment. The two-stage burden comes from *M2B World Asia Pacific Pte Ltd v Matsumura Akihiko* [2015] 1 SLR 325 at [17]–[19]. A defendant who shows a reasonable ground of defence, or a fair probability of a bona fide defence, gets unconditional permission to defend (*Ritzland Investment Pte Ltd v Grace Management & Consultancy Services Pte Ltd* [2014] 2 SLR 1342 at [43]–[47]), and the "other reason" for a trial must relate to the merits, not merely disapproval of the claimant's conduct (*Ling Yew Kong v Teo Vin Li Richard* [2014] 2 SLR 123 at [29]–[36]). *M2B*, *Ritzland* and *Ling Yew Kong* were decided under ROC 2014.

**A** Stage 1, prima facie case: [the contract, the invoice for $350,000, and M's part payment of $50,000 with no complaint at the time]. Stage 2, triable issue: test [D]'s defence against the documents. [M says P used "unheard-of bloggers" instead of A-list influencers]: if the contract defines "A-list" and contemporaneous messages show M approved the bloggers, the defence is a bare assertion contradicted by the documents and fails; if the term is undefined and there is genuine evidence of complaint, there is a triable issue on breach. [R's alleged oral variation "subject to stock"] is contradicted by correspondence showing it was never agreed, so it is an assertion of the kind *M2B* rejects. Possible outcomes: judgment; conditional permission to defend (e.g. payment into court of the sum claimed) if the defence is dubious; unconditional permission if there is a real dispute. If [D] has a genuine counterclaim, the Court may give judgment but stay enforcement until it is tried (r 17(9)). An order on summary judgment by a Judge is appealable only with permission in some cases (drp-w-0-65). **C** Therefore [Client] is likely to obtain [summary judgment / conditional permission to defend], and its chances of success are [good].

*Source note: O 9 r 17(7)–(12) ROC p93–94; SYL p14 [103]–[106]; L2 s23–s24; M2B L2 s23 and SYL p14 [103]; Horizon Capital SYL p14 [103] with proposition from Cyril-Exam ("Summary judgment") (secondary); Ritzland and Ling Yew Kong propositions from Cyril-Exam and Nic-Muggers 6.3 (secondary); payment into court or security for the sum claimed as the typical condition: SCJA Fifth Schedule para 3(b) ("permission to defend … on condition that the party … pays into court or gives security for the sum claimed"), SCJA p97; tested PYP22 Q2, PYP23 Q3.1, PYP25 Q3.1, PYP21 Q4.*

### drp-w-0-44 — Deciding a question of law or construction without a trial (O 9 r 19)
*Keywords: question of law, construction of document, O 9 r 19, without a trial, determination of question of law, Court's own accord, fully determine the action, give judgment or dismiss, United Overseas Bank v Lippo Marina Collection, Babbobox v Epiq Singapore, suitable for determination, no fact-finding, saves time and costs, SAPT, O 9 r 9(4)(j)*

**I** The issue is whether the Court can decide [the meaning of clause x / whether the agreements are illegal moneylending] without a trial. **C** Yes. Under O 9 r 19 the Court may decide any question of law or construction of a document without a trial or hearing on the facts, on application or of its own accord, whether or not that decision fully determines the action, and may give judgment if it does.
**R** On a party's application or of its own accord, the Court may decide any question of law or the construction of any document arising in an action without a trial or hearing on the facts, whether or not the decision will fully determine the action (O 9 r 19(1)). Where the decision fully determines the matter or any claim or issue (subject only to appeal), the Court may give judgment, dismiss the action or make any appropriate order (O 9 r 19(2)). The question must be suitable for determination without a full trial, which usually means no disputed facts need to be found.

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 19(1): upon a party's application or on the Court's own accord, the Court may decide any question of law or the construction of any document arising in any action without a trial or hearing on the facts, whether or not such decision will fully determine the action.
> - O 9 r 19(2): where the Court's decision fully determines (subject only to any appeal) the entire matter or any claim or issue therein, the Court may give judgment or dismiss the action or make any order that is appropriate.
> - Determination of questions of law or construction is a SAPT matter (O 9 r 9(4)(j)).
> - Suitability factors (from *UOB v Lippo Marina* at [12]–[18], as summarised in the seniors' notes):
>   - the question must be suitable for determination without a full trial (no fact-finding needed);
>   - the parties must have had an opportunity to be heard on it;
>   - the determination should fully determine the matter, or at least save time and costs;
>   - novel questions of considerable public importance are less suitable.

**P** Two conjunctive requirements apply: (a) the issue must be suitable for determination without a full trial; and (b) resolving it must fully determine the whole cause or matter, or at least one claim or issue in it (*Hyflux Ltd v SM Investments Pte Ltd* [2020] 4 SLR 1265 at [34]). The Court keeps a residual discretion, the overriding consideration being whether summary determination would save time and costs (*Manas Kumar Ghosh v MSI Ship Management* [2021] 4 SLR 935 at [18]), and a suitable question of law is one based on facts that are agreed or undisputed and duly pleaded (*United Overseas Bank Ltd v Lippo Marina Collection Pte Ltd* [2016] 2 SLR 597 at [15]). These principles were accepted by the parties and applied under ROC 2021 in *Babbobox Pte Ltd v Epiq Singapore Pte Ltd* [2026] SGHC 66 at [5]–[7], where the Court declined to decide the meaning of "Work Product" summarily because it turned on disputed facts ([50]–[52]).

**A** Is the issue purely legal or about construction, on facts that are not in dispute? [Whether an "A-list influencer" clause requires a minimum follower count] may be a question of construction if the contract and its background are agreed; but [whether the delivery clause was orally varied] is factual and unsuitable. Would deciding it dispose of the claim or a major issue? [If the exclusion clause covers the loss, the claim fails entirely], which saves the cost of a trial. Procedure: include it in the SAPT (O 9 r 9(4)(j)), or ask the Court to decide it of its own accord at a case conference; if the decision disposes of the claim, ask for judgment or dismissal under r 19(2). Contrast summary judgment (evidence, triable issue) and striking out (pleading only). **C** Therefore [Client] should ask the Court to determine [question] under O 9 r 19, which, if decided in its favour, would [fully determine the action].

*Source note: O 9 r 19 ROC p94–95; O 9 r 9(4)(j) ROC p86; SYL p14 [107]–[108]; L2 s26; UOB v Lippo Marina L2 s26, SYL p14 [108], suitability factors from Cyril-Exam ("Determination of questions of law") (secondary); Babbobox [2026] SGHC 66 (Andre Maniam J) at [5]–[7], [50]–[52], read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026); note that [5]–[7] record the principles as the parties accepted them, citing Hyflux, Manas Kumar Ghosh and UOB v Lippo Marina.*

### drp-w-0-80 — Judgment on admissions; third party proceedings (O 9 r 18; O 10) (background only — not examinable per SYL p1)
*Keywords: judgment on admissions, admission of fact in pleadings, O 9 r 18(2), agreed statement of facts, Mycitydeal, third party notice, contribution, indemnity, O 10 r 1, Form 20, Form 21, Form 22, Form 23, permission to issue third party notice, before serving defence, O 10 r 2 affidavit, service of third party notice, O 10 r 3, third party directions, O 10 r 4, set aside third party notice, Chancery Law Corp, SAPT, O 9 r 9(4)(i), O 9 r 9(7)(n), background only*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can [obtain judgment on [D]'s admissions / bring [X] into the action by a third party notice]. **C** [Client] may [apply for judgment on the admissions in [D]'s [defence] (O 9 r 18(2)) / issue a third party notice against [X] claiming [contribution / indemnity] (O 10 r 1(1)(a))], [without / with] the Court's permission (O 10 r 1(2)).
**R** Where a party admits facts in its pleadings or other documents, the Court may, on oral or written application, give judgment on those admissions (O 9 r 18(2)). A defendant who claims contribution or indemnity from a non-party, or related relief substantially the same as the claimant's, or wants a connected question decided as against the non-party too, may issue a third party notice in Form 20 or 21 after filing any required notice of intention to contest (O 10 r 1(1)). Permission is needed unless the action began by originating claim and the notice is issued before the defence is served (O 10 r 1(2)). Once served, the third party is a party to the action (O 10 r 1(3)). The defendant must then apply by summons in Form 23 for directions (O 10 r 4(1)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 9 r 18(1): the Court must direct the parties to agree as many material facts as possible in an agreed statement of facts. O 9 r 18(2): where admissions of fact are made in a party's pleadings or other documents, the Court may, on application made orally or in writing, give judgment on those admissions. Judgment on admission is a SAPT matter (O 9 r 9(4)(i)).
> - O 10 r 1(1): a defendant may issue a third party notice (Form 20 or Form 21) where it
>   - (a) claims against a non-party any contribution or indemnity;
>   - (b) claims against a non-party any relief or remedy relating to or connected with the original subject matter and substantially the same as some relief claimed by the claimant; or
>   - (c) requires any question or issue relating to or connected with the original subject matter to be determined not only between claimant and defendant but also between either or both of them and a non-party.
> - O 10 r 1(2): no third party notice without the Court's permission unless the action was begun by originating claim and the notice is issued before the defendant serves its defence.
> - O 10 r 1(3): on service, the third party is a party to the action as if sued in the ordinary way by the defendant.
> - O 10 r 2: permission by summons without notice in Form 22, with an affidavit stating (a) the claimant's claim; (b) the stage reached; (c) the applicant's claim or the question to be decided, and the facts relied on; (d) the third party's name and address.
> - O 10 r 3(1)–(4): the order may fix the period to issue the notice; serve it with the originating process and any pleadings; it is served like an originating claim (or originating application).
> - O 10 r 4(1)–(2): the defendant applies for directions by summons in Form 23 served on all parties, in an originating claim only after the third party files its notice of intention to contest or not contest (Form 10); if the defendant does not, the third party may apply for directions or to set the notice aside, not earlier than 14 days after its notice of intention (originating claim) or after service of the notice on it (originating application).
> - O 10 r 4(3)–(5): the Court may enter judgment against the third party if liability is established, order issues tried as it directs, or dismiss and end the third party proceedings; give the third party permission to defend or take part at trial; make any directions for binding the third party; and vary them at any time.
> - O 9 r 9(7)(n): setting aside third party proceedings needs no approval to apply outside the SAPT.

**P** For judgment on admissions, the admission must clearly admit all the facts needed to make out the cause of action, so that no findings of fact are needed; qualified or inconsistent statements are not enough (*Mycitydeal Ltd (trading as Groupon UK) v Villas International Property Pte Ltd* [2014] SGHC 196, reported [2014] 4 SLR 1077, at [66]–[69], decided under ROC 2014). Third party proceedings aim to let all issues on the subject-matter be raised in one set of proceedings, avoiding delay and duplicated costs, and to prevent inconsistent decisions on common issues (*Chancery Law Corp v Management Corporation Strata Title Plan No 1024* [2015] SGHC 66 at [47], decided under ROC 2014).

**A** Admissions: if [D]'s defence admits [the contract, delivery and the unpaid invoice of $X] and pleads only an unconnected set-off, apply in the SAPT for judgment on the admitted sum. Third party: [D, sued for a defective roof, says its subcontractor X did the work]: claim indemnity or contribution against X under r 1(1)(a); if [D] has not yet served its defence in an originating claim, no permission is needed (r 1(2)); otherwise apply without notice in Form 22 with the r 2(2) affidavit. Serve the notice with the originating claim and pleadings, then take out the Form 23 summons for directions once X files its notice of intention. **C** Therefore [Client] should [apply for judgment on admissions under O 9 r 18(2) / issue a third party notice under O 10 r 1 and seek directions under O 10 r 4].

*Source note: O 9 r 18 ROC p94; O 9 r 9(4), (7) ROC p86–87; O 10 rr 1–4 ROC p101–103; SYL p14–15 [109]–[116]; Nic-Muggers 7.1–7.3 (secondary, consistent); Mycitydeal ([2014] SGHC 196) and Chancery Law Corp ([2015] SGHC 66) read on eLitigation (18 Nov 2026, extraction subagent). Conflict: SYL [116(b)] says the defendant's application for directions must be made "within 14 days" of the third party's notice of intention, failing which the third party may apply; O 10 r 4(2) only says the third party may apply "not earlier than 14 days after" — followed here in the rule's own words. ROC text from the 2021 library copy, checked against SSO (current version as at 18 Nov 2026; extraction subagent): unchanged since 2021.*

## 0.IX Production of documents

- **What is a "document", and what principles govern production?**
  - Evidence Act s 3 definition; electronically stored information counts (*Sanae Achar*) → [0-45](#drp-w-0-45)
  - Sue on the strength of your own case; privacy and confidentiality are not given up; broader discovery by agreement or where it could aid fair disposal (O 11 r 1) → [0-45](#drp-w-0-45)
- **Initial production (old "general discovery")**
  - Ordered at a case conference; within 14 days exchange a list and copies of documents relied on, known adverse documents, and any agreed or ordered broader category (O 11 r 2(1)) → [0-46](#drp-w-0-46)
  - "Known adverse" includes documents a party ought reasonably to know are adverse (O 11 r 2(2)) → [0-46](#drp-w-0-46)
  - Common documents need not be exchanged; unproduced documents cannot be relied on (O 11 r 2(4)–(5)) → [0-46](#drp-w-0-46)
- **Asking for specific documents (old "specific discovery")**
  - Properly identify them and show they are material to the issues (O 11 r 3(1); *Lutfi Salim*); Summary Table if more than 5 categories (PD 68) → [0-47](#drp-w-0-47)
  - Not held: affidavit saying so and what became of them (O 11 r 3(2)); after an AB4D order, only in a special case (O 11 r 3(3)) → [0-47](#drp-w-0-47)
- **The Court's own power; non-parties; documents referred to in pleadings**
  - Court may order any party or non-party to produce at any time (O 11 r 4); includes documents referred to in pleadings and affidavits (*Interactive Digital Finance*, *eSave*) → [0-48](#drp-w-0-48)
  - Non-party or pre-action production (O 11 r 11) → [0-9](#drp-w-0-9)
- **What cannot be ordered, or withheld?**
  - Train of inquiry documents; private or internal correspondence (unless known adverse or a special case); privilege; public interest (O 11 r 5; *Cachet*) → [0-49](#drp-w-0-49)
  - Privileged documents stay privileged even if leaked; confidentiality is no ground to withhold (O 11 rr 8–9) → [0-49](#drp-w-0-49)
- **After production**
  - Continuing duty: produce within 14 days of coming into possession or control (O 11 r 6) → [0-50](#drp-w-0-50)
  - Non-compliance: dismissal or strike out, adverse inference, contempt, no reliance (O 11 r 7) → [0-50](#drp-w-0-50)
  - Use in other proceedings only with consent or approval; inspect originals within 14 days (O 11 rr 10, 12) → [0-50](#drp-w-0-50)

### drp-w-0-45 — What is a document; Order 11's principles; broader discovery (O 11 r 1)
*Keywords: document, definition of document, Evidence Act s 3, electronically stored documents, electronic documents, metadata, Sanae Achar v Sci-Gen, O 11 r 1, sue on strength of own case, not weakness of defendant's case, privacy and confidentiality, broader scope of discovery, agreement to broaden, interests of justice, aid in disposing fairly of the proceedings, O 11 r 1(5)*

**I** The issue is whether [the WhatsApp messages / server logs] are "documents" that may be ordered to be produced, and on what principles the Court decides production. **C** They are documents, since electronically stored information falls within the definition (*Sanae Achar v Sci-Gen Ltd* [2011] 3 SLR 967 at [10]); the Court decides production in light of the Ideals and the O 11 r 1(2) principles, unless broader discovery is sought.
**R** Order 11 sets the basic production obligations and does not affect any agreement to broaden them, or the Court's power to allow broader discovery in the interests of justice (O 11 r 1(1), (3)); that is so where broader discovery could aid in disposing fairly of the proceedings (O 11 r 1(4)). In exercising its powers the Court bears in mind the Ideals and two principles: a claimant sues on the strength of its own case, not the weakness of the defendant's; and a party does not give up its right to privacy and confidentiality by suing or being sued (O 11 r 1(2)). On an application for broader discovery, only the Ideals and r 1(4) apply (O 11 r 1(5)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 11 r 1(1): the Order sets out the basic requirements of the parties' obligations to produce and exchange documents and does not affect (a) any agreement between the parties to broaden them; or (b) the Court's power to allow a broader scope of discovery in the interests of justice.
> - O 11 r 1(2): in exercising its power under the Order, the Court must bear in mind, in addition to the Ideals,
>   - (a) that a claimant is to sue and proceed on the strength of the claimant's case and not on the weakness of the defendant's case;
>   - (b) that a party who sues or is sued in court does not thereby give up the party's right to privacy and confidentiality in its documents and communications.
> - O 11 r 1(3): the Court may allow a broader scope of discovery where it determines that it is in the interests of justice.
> - O 11 r 1(4): it will be in the interests of justice where broader discovery could aid in disposing fairly of the proceedings.
> - O 11 r 1(5): on an application for broader discovery, r 1(2) does not apply and the Court must have regard only to the Ideals and r 1(4).
> - Evidence Act 1893 s 3(1): "document" includes, in addition to a document in writing, (a) any map, plan, graph or drawing; (b) any photograph; (c) any label, marking or other writing identifying or describing anything; (d) any disc, tape, soundtrack or other device embodying sounds or other data (not visual images) capable of being reproduced; (e) any film, negative, tape, disc or other device embodying visual images capable of being reproduced; and (f) any paper or other material bearing marks, figures, letters, symbols or perforations meaningful to those qualified to interpret them.

**P** Electronically stored documents are "documents" for the purposes of production (*Sanae Achar v Sci-Gen Ltd* [2011] 3 SLR 967 at [10]).

**A** First, confirm the material is a document: [emails, chat messages, spreadsheets and CCTV footage] are electronically stored and so qualify. Second, frame the request within the basic obligations (r 2 and r 3: drp-w-0-46 to 0-47). If it goes further (e.g. [all of D's internal communications about the project]), either obtain [D]'s agreement or ask for broader discovery, arguing it "could aid in disposing fairly of the proceedings" (r 1(4)); on such an application the strength-of-own-case and privacy principles do not apply (r 1(5)). Otherwise expect [D] to invoke r 1(2): [Client] cannot use production to find a case it does not already have. **C** Therefore the [messages] are documents, and production will be ordered [within the basic categories / as broader discovery only if it could aid in disposing fairly of the proceedings].

*Source note: O 11 r 1 ROC p108; SYL p15–16 [117]–[118]; L2 s35–s37; Sanae Achar L2 s35 and SYL p15 [117]; Evidence Act s 3 read on SSO (current as at 8 Nov 2026).*

### drp-w-0-46 — Initial production: timing, scope, known adverse documents, non-production (O 11 r 2)
*Keywords: initial production, general discovery, O 11 r 2, case conference, within 14 days, list of documents, copy of documents, documents relied on, known adverse documents, ought reasonably to know are adverse, broader scope agreed or ordered, possession or control, common electronic format, documents common to the parties, O 11 r 2(4), may not rely on documents not produced, O 11 r 2(5), Hu Yinan v Peh Chin Hua*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] must disclose [the email from its staff stating that delivery is "naturally subject to stock status"]. **C** Yes. It is a known adverse document, which [Client] must list and produce under O 11 r 2(1)(b), since it undermines [Client]'s case that the delivery clause was never varied.
**R** At a case conference the Court may order the parties, within 14 days, to exchange a list and a copy of all documents in their possession or control that they will rely on, all known adverse documents, and any documents within an agreed or ordered broader scope (O 11 r 2(1)). Known adverse documents include those a party ought reasonably to know are adverse to its case (O 11 r 2(2)). Parties need not exchange documents common to them, to avoid duplication (O 11 r 2(4)), and may not rely on any document not exchanged or produced (O 11 r 2(5)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 11 r 2(1): the Court may, at a case conference, order that the parties must within 14 days after the date of the case conference exchange a list of and a copy of all documents in their possession or control which fall within one or more of the following categories:
>   - (a) all documents that the party in question will be relying on;
>   - (b) all known adverse documents;
>   - (c) where applicable, documents within a broader scope of discovery (i) as agreed between the parties; or (ii) as ordered by the Court.
> - O 11 r 2(2): "known adverse documents" includes documents which a party ought reasonably to know are adverse to the party's case.
> - O 11 r 2(3): a copy may be in paper form or, if electronic, in a common electronic format the other party can use.
> - O 11 r 2(4): to comply with the order, it is not necessary for the parties to exchange documents common to them that are in their possession or control, to avoid duplication and to save costs.
> - O 11 r 2(5): the parties may not rely on any document that was not exchanged or produced under this Order.
> - O 11 r 6: continuing duty to produce within 14 days of a document coming into possession or control (drp-w-0-50).

**P** Under ROC 2021, production is carried out by exchanging a list of documents together with copies of all the documents described in it, and the scope of what a party has produced is fixed by that list and those copies (*Hu Yinan v Peh Chin Hua* [2026] SGHC 103 at [24]–[25]); a document identified in the list counts as "produced" for inspection of originals under O 11 r 12 (*Hu Yinan* at [26]). The list should describe documents specifically, or by class, sufficiently to identify them (*Hu Yinan* at [18]).

**A** Is the document adverse? [The email from P's own staff conceding delivery was "subject to stock status"] supports R's variation defence, so it is a known adverse document, and at least one P "ought reasonably to know" is adverse (r 2(2)). Is it excused because R, as recipient, already has a copy (PYP23 Q3.2)? Rule 2(4) only spares the parties from exchanging copies of documents common to them, to avoid duplication; it does not let a party conceal the existence of an adverse document, so the safer course is to list it, since production is by "a list of and a copy of" the documents (r 2(1); *Hu Yinan* at [24]) and r 2(4) speaks only of not exchanging the documents themselves (open point, no authority: r 2(1) orders the exchange of "a list of and a copy of" the documents and r 2(4) says only that it is "not necessary for the parties to exchange documents common to them", so the text does not say whether the list entry may also be left out; ROC text checked 9 Nov 2026, and no judgment, PD or judiciary.gov.sg guidance on r 2(4) was found). If it surfaced only after the exchange, the continuing duty requires production within 14 days of it coming into P's possession or control (r 6). Failing to produce exposes P to the r 7 sanctions (drp-w-0-50), and P cannot rely on documents it has not produced (r 2(5)). **C** Therefore the intern is wrong: [Client] must disclose the email as a known adverse document, at least by listing it, and within 14 days if it has only now surfaced.

*Source note: O 11 rr 2, 6 ROC p108–111; SYL p16 [119]–[123]; L2 s38–s39; Hu Yinan [2026] SGHC 103 (Chua Lee Ming J) at [18], [23]–[26], read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026); the listing point on common documents is the pack's reading of r 2(1) and r 2(4); tested PYP23 Q3.2, PYP22 Q4.*

### drp-w-0-47 — Production of requested documents (O 11 r 3)
*Keywords: requested documents, specific discovery, O 11 r 3, specific document or class of documents, properly identifies, material to the issues, possession or control, affidavit of non-possession, O 11 r 3(2), AEICs before documents, special case, O 11 r 3(3), Summary Table, Form B10, PD 68, Lutfi Salim bin Talib v British and Malayan Trustees, SAPT, O 9 r 9(4)(k)*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can obtain [the surveillance tapes / D's board minutes] from [D]. **C** [Client] may ask the Court under O 11 r 3(1) to order [D] to produce them, and the Court is likely to do so if [Client] properly identifies them and shows they are material to the issues, as [the tapes] are to [D's allegation that Client cheated].
**R** The Court may order a party to produce the original or a copy of a specific document or class of documents in its possession or control if the requesting party properly identifies them and shows they are material to the issues in the case (O 11 r 3(1)). If the documents are not in the party's possession or control, the Court may order an affidavit saying so and explaining what became of them (O 11 r 3(2)). Except in a special case, the Court may not order requested documents before the parties comply with an AB4D order (O 11 r 3(3)). Where more than 5 categories are sought and contested, the parties complete a Summary Table (PD 68).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 11 r 3(1): the Court may order any party to produce the original or a copy of a specific document or class of documents (the requested documents) in the party's possession or control, if the requesting party
>   - (a) properly identifies the requested documents; and
>   - (b) shows that the requested documents are material to the issues in the case.
> - O 11 r 3(2): if the requested documents are not in the party's possession or control, the Court may order that party to file an affidavit stating this, whether it had them previously and, if so, when it parted with them and what has become of them.
> - O 11 r 3(3): except in a special case, if the Court orders AEICs to be filed before any exchange of documents, it must not exercise its power under this Rule before the parties comply with that order.
> - PD 68: Summary Table (Form B10) instead of written submissions where more than 5 categories or sub-categories are sought and the application is contested.
> - Production is a SAPT matter (O 9 r 9(4)(k)); the limits in O 11 r 5 apply (drp-w-0-49).

**P** On a request under O 11 r 3, the respondent may answer by affidavit that the documents do not exist, are privileged or are not or no longer in its possession or control, and that affidavit is generally conclusive; the old "reasonable suspicion" test has no place under ROC 2021, and the Court will not go behind the affidavit unless it is plain and obvious from the documents produced, the affidavits, the pleadings or other objective evidence that the documents must exist or have existed, must be or have been in the respondent's possession or control, or are not protected from production (*Lutfi Salim bin Talib v British and Malaysian Trustees Ltd* [2024] SGHC 85 at [17]–[34], especially [32]). The high threshold reflects the Ideals and discourages fishing for a "smoking gun" ([33]–[34]); the affidavit is conclusive only for the application, and the requesting party may still cross-examine at trial and seek adverse inferences ([41]).

**A** Identify the documents precisely ("[the CCTV recordings of the Featured Restaurants on the night of the second round, 5–6 pm]"), not "all documents relating to the show". Show materiality: [D's defence alleges P cheated in that round, and the footage would show whether she did], so it goes directly to a pleaded issue. Check possession or control: [if the restaurants, not D1, hold the tapes, D1 may not have control; seek an O 11 r 3(2) affidavit, and consider an order against the restaurants as non-parties under O 11 r 4 or r 11 (drp-w-0-48, drp-w-0-9)]. Timing: include the request in the SAPT, unless AB4D has been ordered, in which case a special case is needed. If the tapes may be erased, consider a preservation order (drp-w-0-61). **C** Therefore the Court is likely to order [D] to produce [the requested documents] under O 11 r 3(1).

*Source note: O 11 r 3 ROC p109–110; PD 68 SCPD p81–82; SYL p16–17 [124]–[128]; L2 s40; Lutfi Salim [2024] SGHC 85 (Chua Lee Ming J) at [17]–[34], [41], read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026); the syllabus calls the defendant "British and Malayan Trustees Ltd", the judgment "British and Malaysian Trustees Limited"; tested PYP21 Q5.*

### drp-w-0-48 — The Court's general power to order production; non-parties; documents referred to in pleadings or affidavits (O 11 r 4)
*Keywords: Court's power to order production, O 11 r 4, of its own accord, at any time, any party or non-party, copy of any document, possession or control, documents referred to in pleadings, documents referred to in affidavits, before the SAPT, Interactive Digital Finance v Credit Suisse, eSave AG v eSave APAC, O 3 r 2, non-party production*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can obtain [the contract referred to in paragraph 5 of D's defence / documents held by a non-party] before the SAPT. **C** Yes. The Court may, of its own accord and at any time, order any party or non-party to produce a copy of any document in its possession or control (O 11 r 4), and this power extends to documents referred to in pleadings and affidavits, even before the SAPT.
**R** Subject to O 11 rr 5, 8 and 9, the Court may, of its own accord and at any time, order any party or non-party to produce a copy of any document in that person's possession or control (O 11 r 4). The Court has power under O 11 r 4, or alternatively O 3 r 2, to order production of documents referred to in pleadings before the SAPT, although ROC 2021 has no express equivalent of the old rule on inspecting documents mentioned in pleadings and affidavits.

> **Rule in full**
> - O 11 r 4: subject to Rules 5, 8 and 9, the Court may, of its own accord and at any time, order any party or non-party to produce a copy of any document that is in the person's possession or control.
> - Limits: r 5 (train of inquiry, private or internal correspondence, privilege, public interest), r 8 (privilege), r 9 (confidentiality no bar) (drp-w-0-49).
> - Alternative source: O 3 r 2(2) (no express provision: the Court may do what is necessary for justice, consistent with the Ideals) (drp-w-0-2).
> - Non-party production on application: O 11 r 11; the non-party gets its reasonable costs (drp-w-0-9).

**P** The Court has power under O 11 r 4 (or alternatively O 3 r 2) to order production of documents referred to in pleadings before the SAPT, despite the absence of an equivalent to the ROC 2014 rule on inspection of documents mentioned in pleadings and affidavits (*Interactive Digital Finance Ltd v Credit Suisse AG* [2023] 5 SLR 1735 at [30]–[33], [35]); the principle extends to documents referred to in affidavits (*eSave AG v eSave APAC Pte Ltd* [2026] SGHCR 17 at [76], which also summarises the approach).

**A** If [D's defence relies on "the addendum dated [date]"] or [D's affidavit refers to "the board's approval"], ask [D] to produce it; if refused, apply for an order under O 11 r 4 citing *Interactive Digital Finance* and *eSave*, since [Client] cannot meet a pleaded case built on a document it has not seen. Approval to file before the SAPT may be needed under O 9 r 9(8), unless the Court acts at a case conference of its own accord. For a non-party (e.g. [the Featured Restaurants holding the tapes]), the Court may order production under r 4, or [Client] may apply under r 11 and pay the non-party's reasonable costs. **C** Therefore the Court may order [D / the non-party] to produce [document] under O 11 r 4, including before the SAPT.

*Source note: O 11 r 4 ROC p110; O 3 r 2(2) ROC p53; SYL p17 [129]–[130]; L2 s41; Interactive Digital Finance and eSave SYL p17 [130]; tested PYP21 Q5 (tapes held by the restaurants).*

### drp-w-0-49 — Limits on production; privilege; confidentiality (O 11 rr 5, 8, 9)
*Keywords: no order for production, train of inquiry, O 11 r 5(1), special case, private or internal correspondence, internal emails, WhatsApp, instant messaging, O 11 r 5(2), known adverse documents, Cachet Multi Strategy Fund v Feng Shi, privilege, legal advice privilege, litigation privilege, public interest, O 11 r 5(3), privileged document not relied on, O 11 r 8, inadvertent disclosure, confidential documents, O 11 r 9, confidentiality no ground to withhold*

**I** The issue is whether [D] can refuse to produce [its internal emails / its solicitors' advice / confidential pricing records]. **C** [D may refuse to produce privileged documents (O 11 r 5(3)), and need not produce private or internal correspondence unless it is a known adverse document or a special case (O 11 r 5(2)); but it cannot refuse merely because documents are confidential (O 11 r 9)].
**R** Except in a special case, the Court must not order production of documents that merely lead to a train of inquiry (O 11 r 5(1)), or of a party's private or internal correspondence (including email and instant messages), unless it is a special case or the correspondence are known adverse documents (O 11 r 5(2)). Subject to written law, it must not order production of privileged documents or where production would be contrary to the public interest (O 11 r 5(3)). A privileged document must not be relied on without the privilege-holder's consent or the Court's approval, and does not lose its privilege if disclosed or taken inadvertently or unlawfully (O 11 r 8). Confidentiality is not a ground to withhold or object to production (O 11 r 9).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 11 r 5(1): except in a special case, the Court must not order production of any document that merely leads a party on a train of inquiry to other documents.
> - O 11 r 5(2): the Court must not order production of any document that is part of a party's private or internal correspondence, in paper or electronic form (including email, SMS or any instant messaging service), wherever stored, unless
>   - (a) it is a special case; or
>   - (b) such correspondence are known adverse documents.
> - O 11 r 5(3): subject to any written law, the Court must not order production of any document which is subject to any privilege or where its production would be contrary to the public interest.
> - O 11 r 8(1): a document which was at any time subject to any privilege must not be relied on unless the party entitled to the privilege consents or the Court approves.
> - O 11 r 8(2): it does not lose its privilege or confidentiality even if disclosed or taken inadvertently or unlawfully by anyone.
> - O 11 r 9(1): a party required to produce documents may not withhold or object to production on the ground that a document is confidential.
> - O 11 r 9(2): a confidential document does not lose its confidentiality if disclosed or taken inadvertently or unlawfully.
> - Any document produced may be used only in these proceedings (O 11 r 10; drp-w-0-50).

**P** It is not the requesting party's duty to show that internal documents may include known adverse documents; the requested party must deny the existence or possession of known adverse documents (including internal ones), assert privilege or another reason for non-disclosure, or disclose them (*Cachet Multi Strategy Fund SPC v Feng Shi* [2024] SGHC 327 at [24]).

**A** Sort the documents. [D's internal emails between its sales staff about the delivery terms] are internal correspondence: [Client] cannot get them unless they are known adverse documents or it shows a special case; but under *Cachet* [D] must itself say whether any of them are known adverse documents and produce those. [Emails with D's lawyers seeking advice] are privileged and cannot be ordered (r 5(3)); if [Client] received one by mistake, it must not rely on it without consent or approval (r 8). [D's confidential pricing data], if material and within an order, must be produced; confidentiality can be managed by redaction or a confidentiality order, but is no ground to refuse (r 9). Requests that would only open a train of inquiry ("[all documents that might lead to evidence of the variation]") fail except in a special case. **C** Therefore [D] must produce [category], may withhold [privileged category], and need not produce [internal category] unless it contains known adverse documents.

*Source note: O 11 rr 5, 8, 9 ROC p110–111; SYL p17 [131]–[133], p18 [139]; L2 s42–s43; Cachet SYL p17 [132]; tested PYP21 Q5 (as context), PYP22 Q4.*

### drp-w-0-50 — Continuing duty to produce; non-compliance; use in other proceedings; inspecting originals (O 11 rr 6, 7, 10, 12)
*Keywords: continuing duty to produce, O 11 r 6, within 14 days after the document comes into possession or control, newly discovered documents, adverse documents found late, non-compliance with production order, O 11 r 7, dismiss action, strike out defence, adverse inference, contempt of court, not rely on document, use in other proceedings, O 11 r 10, implied undertaking, collateral use, prohibit use, inspection of original, O 11 r 12, within 14 days of request*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] must now disclose [documents it found after production was completed], and the consequences if it does not. **C** Yes. Under O 11 r 6, a party ordered to produce documents remains under a duty to produce any such document within 14 days after it comes into its possession or control at any time in the proceedings; failing that, the Court may impose the sanctions in O 11 r 7, including striking out and adverse inferences.
**R** Where the Court has ordered production under r 2, 3 or 4, the party remains under a duty to produce any of those documents within 14 days after it comes into its possession or control at any time in the proceedings (O 11 r 6). If a party fails to comply with a production order, the Court may dismiss the action or strike out the defence, draw an adverse inference, punish for contempt, or bar reliance on documents within the order (O 11 r 7). Documents produced under compulsion must not be relied on in other proceedings without the producing party's consent or the Court's approval (O 11 r 10). A party may ask to inspect originals, which must be arranged within 14 days (O 11 r 12).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 11 r 6: where the Court makes an order under Rule 2, 3 or 4, the party required to produce the documents remains under a duty to produce any of those documents within 14 days after that document comes into the party's possession or control at any time in the course of the proceedings.
> - O 11 r 7: if any party fails to comply with any order under this Order, the Court may
>   - (a) order that the action be dismissed or that the defence be struck out and judgment be entered accordingly;
>   - (b) draw an adverse inference or make any such order as the Court deems fit;
>   - (c) punish that party for contempt of court if the order has been served on that party's solicitor, though that party may show it was not notified or did not know of the order; or
>   - (d) order that that party may not rely on any document within the scope of the order unless the Court approves.
> - O 11 r 10(1): any document produced under this Order or by compulsion of law must not be relied on in other proceedings by the other parties or non-parties unless the producing party consents or the Court otherwise approves.
> - O 11 r 10(2): the party who used or produced any document may apply to prohibit its use for any purpose other than that case.
> - O 11 r 12(1)–(3): on request, the producing party must arrange inspection of the original at a mutually convenient time and place, within 14 days unless otherwise agreed, failing which the requesting party may apply to compel it.

**P** The rule against collateral use reflects the implied undertaking in *Riddick v Thames Board Mills Ltd* [1977] QB 881, which applies to documents obtained under compulsion. No other case is set on these rules.

**A** Apply r 6 to the facts: [C found communications between his son and D that support D's duress case] (PYP22 Q4) after r 2 and r 3 production had ended. The earlier advice that "the discovery stage is over" is wrong: the order under r 2 covered known adverse documents (r 2(1)(b)), so C must produce them within 14 days of coming into possession or control of them. If C withholds them, D may later ask the Court to strike out the claim, draw an adverse inference, or treat it as contempt (r 7), and C's credibility will suffer. Separately, [D] may not use [Client]'s produced documents in [another dispute] without consent or approval (r 10), and either side may inspect originals on request within 14 days (r 12). **C** Therefore [Client] must produce the [documents] within 14 days after obtaining them, or risk the sanctions in O 11 r 7.

*Source note: O 11 rr 6, 7, 10, 12 ROC p110–112; SYL p18 [137]–[142]; L2 s43; Riddick SYL p21 [146(e)]; tested PYP22 Q4 (model: PYP22-Xav Q4), PYP23 Q3.2.*

## 0.X Witnesses and evidence at trial

- **How does a witness give evidence-in-chief?**
  - By AEIC as a general rule; orally only in a special case with the Court's permission (O 15 r 16(1)–(2); *Basil Anthony Herman*) → [0-51](#drp-w-0-51)
  - AEIC holds all material facts; no new facts orally unless they arose later; only admissible evidence (O 15 r 16(4)–(5)) → [0-51](#drp-w-0-51)
- **Expert evidence**
  - Expert = specialised knowledge from training, study or experience; duty to the Court overrides the client (O 12 r 1) → [0-52](#drp-w-0-52)
  - No expert evidence without the Court's approval; only if it contributes materially and the issue cannot be resolved by agreed facts or submissions (O 12 r 2); raise it at the case conference (O 9 r 21) → [0-52](#drp-w-0-52)
  - One common expert as far as possible; one expert per issue; court expert in a special case; panels (O 12 rr 3, 7; *Sanum*) → [0-52](#drp-w-0-52)
- **Witness will not come voluntarily**
  - Order to attend court or produce documents (Form 29), personal service in Singapore at least 28 days before the hearing; cannot be served abroad (O 15 r 4(1)–(3); PD 76) → [0-53](#drp-w-0-53)
  - Setting aside: irrelevant, oppressive or abusive (*Lao PDR v Sanum*, *Ong Jane Rebecca*, *BVU v BVX*); revocation and review within 14 days (O 15 r 4(4)–(6)) → [0-53](#drp-w-0-53)
- **Witness absent or abroad at trial**
  - AEIC cannot be used unless the maker attends for cross-examination, or the parties agree (O 15 r 16(3)) → [0-54](#drp-w-0-54)
  - Evidence by video link (Evidence Act s 62A; *Sonica*, *Anil Singh Gurm*, *Wang Xiaopu*); letter of request where foreign law requires (O 15 r 5) → [0-54](#drp-w-0-54)
  - Non-party witnesses stay outside court until called (O 15 r 4(13)–(14)) → [0-54](#drp-w-0-54)
- **A party does not turn up**
  - Hearings: attend in person or by solicitor; the Court may dismiss or make any order (O 15 r 3); trial: proceed, give judgment or dismiss (O 15 r 8(3)–(4)) → [0-55](#drp-w-0-55)
- **AEIC form and objections, expert reports, Court's own witnesses? (background only — not examinable per SYL p1)**
  - AEIC: all material facts, only relevant and admissible evidence, in English, Form 31 with photograph (O 15 rr 16(4)–(5), 18, 19, 25; O 3 r 7; PD 78–83) → [0-81](#drp-w-0-81)
  - Objecting to AEIC contents: notice at least 28 days before the hearing; ruled on at trial (O 15 r 16(6); PD 84) → [0-81](#drp-w-0-81)
  - Expert's overriding duty to the Court; agreed issues and assumed facts; what the report must contain (O 12 rr 1(2)–(3), 4, 5) → [0-81](#drp-w-0-81)
  - Independent witness, interested non-party, independent counsel (amicus curiae) (O 9 rr 22–23) → [0-81](#drp-w-0-81)

### drp-w-0-51 — Fact witnesses: evidence by AEIC; oral evidence only in a special case (O 15 r 16)
*Keywords: affidavit of evidence-in-chief, AEIC, O 15 r 16, general rule, trial on affidavits, cross-examination, re-examination, special case, oral evidence-in-chief, dispense with AEIC, all material facts, no new facts in oral evidence, admissible evidence only, objections to AEIC 28 days, Basil Anthony Herman v Premier Security Cooperative*

**I** The issue is whether [witness X] may give evidence-in-chief orally at trial instead of by AEIC. **C** Only in a special case, with the Court's permission (O 15 r 16(2)); [no special case appears here / here [reason] may amount to one], so [X] should give evidence by AEIC.
**R** As a general rule, the trial of an originating claim is decided on the witnesses' AEICs, cross-examination, re-examination and submissions (O 15 r 16(1)). In a special case the Court may allow evidence-in-chief to be given orally instead (O 15 r 16(2)). An AEIC must contain all material facts, which may not be departed from or supplemented in oral evidence unless they arose afterwards, and only evidence admissible in law (O 15 r 16(4)–(5)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 15 r 16(1): as a general rule, the trial in an originating claim, assessment of damages or value, and taking of accounts must be decided on the basis of the witnesses' AEICs, cross-examination, re-examination and oral or written submissions.
> - O 15 r 16(2): in a special case, the Court may allow a witness' evidence-in-chief to be given orally instead of by AEIC.
> - O 15 r 16(3): an AEIC may not be used if the maker does not attend Court for cross-examination, unless the parties otherwise agree (drp-w-0-54).
> - O 15 r 16(4): an AEIC must contain all material facts, which may not be departed from or supplemented by new facts in oral evidence unless the new facts occurred after the AEIC was made.
> - O 15 r 16(5): an AEIC must contain only evidence that is admissible in law.
> - O 15 r 16(6): a party objecting to the contents of an AEIC must give notice in the prescribed form at least 28 days before the first hearing date (background).
> - Purpose of the AEIC scheme: a fair and expeditious disposal, saving costs and eliminating surprise (SYL [143(a)]).

**P** Where a party opposes another party's application to call a witness to give oral evidence without an AEIC, the objection must be taken at that application; it cannot be raised collaterally at trial (*Basil Anthony Herman v Premier Security Cooperative Ltd* [2010] 3 SLR 110 at [78], decided under ROC 2014).

**A** Ask why an AEIC is not possible. A special case might be [a hostile non-party witness who will not cooperate in preparing an affidavit and must be compelled by an order to attend court (drp-w-0-53)], or [a witness who cannot read or write and whose evidence is short]. Mere convenience is not enough, given the purpose of avoiding surprise and saving costs. If permission is granted, the other side should object then, not at trial (*Basil Anthony Herman*). Remind [Client] that its witnesses cannot add new material facts orally later, so the AEICs must be complete. **C** Therefore [X] must give evidence by AEIC unless [Client] obtains the Court's permission by showing a special case under O 15 r 16(2).

*Source note: O 15 r 16 ROC p142–144; SYL p19 [143(a)–(b)], [143(c)], [143(f)]; L3 s5–s6, s10; Basil Anthony Herman SYL p19 [143(b)], proposition from Cyril-Exam ("Witnesses and evidence") (secondary); tested PYP25 Q3.3 (as context).*

### drp-w-0-52 — Expert evidence: who is an expert, the Court's approval, number of experts (O 12)
*Keywords: expert evidence, expert witness, O 12 r 1, specialised knowledge, training study or experience, duty to the Court overrides, O 12 r 2, no expert evidence unless the Court approves, contribute materially, agreed statement of facts, lacks specialised knowledge, lacks impartiality, O 12 r 3, one common expert, one expert per issue, court expert, special case, panel of experts, O 12 r 7, O 9 r 21, inform the Court at case conference, expert witness template, Sanum Investments v ST Group, foreign law expert, handwriting expert, absolute right to call expert*

**I** The issue is whether [D] may call [a cryptocurrency analyst / Professor XY, a handwriting expert] to give expert evidence at trial. **C** [D] has no right to call an expert: no expert evidence may be used without the Court's approval, which will be given only if it will contribute materially to an issue that cannot be resolved by agreed facts or submissions (O 12 r 2(1)–(3)); [here it is unlikely / likely] to be approved because [reason].
**R** An expert is a person with scientific, technical or other specialised knowledge based on training, study or experience, whose duty to assist the Court overrides any obligation to the party instructing or paying them (O 12 r 1). No expert evidence may be used without the Court's approval; the Court must not approve it unless it will contribute materially to the determination of an issue that cannot be resolved by an agreed statement of facts or submissions on agreed materials, and may reject an expert lacking the necessary knowledge or impartiality (O 12 r 2). As far as possible the parties must agree on one common expert, and a party may not rely on more than one expert per issue except in a special case with approval (O 12 r 3). Parties must tell the Court at the case conference if they intend to rely on expert evidence (O 9 r 21(1)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 12 r 1(1): an expert is a person with scientific, technical or other specialised knowledge based on training, study or experience.
> - O 12 r 1(2)–(3): the expert has a duty to assist the Court on the matters within his or her expertise; that duty overrides any obligation to the person who instructs or pays the expert.
> - O 12 r 2(1): no expert evidence may be used in Court unless the Court approves.
> - O 12 r 2(2): the parties must consider whether expert evidence will contribute materially to the determination of any issue that relates to scientific, technical or other specialised knowledge and whether that issue can be resolved by an agreed statement of facts or by submissions based on mutually agreed materials.
> - O 12 r 2(3): the Court must not approve expert evidence unless it will contribute materially to the determination of any issue in the case and the issue cannot be resolved in the manner stated in (2).
> - O 12 r 2(4): the Court may disallow or reject expert evidence if the expert lacks the requisite specialised knowledge or lacks impartiality.
> - O 12 r 3(1): as far as possible, parties must agree on one common expert.
> - O 12 r 3(2): except in a special case and with approval, a party may not rely on more than one expert for any issue.
> - O 12 r 3(3): in a special case, the Court may appoint a court expert in addition to or in place of the parties' experts; (4) the Court gives directions on appointment, questioning and remuneration; (5) different rules for simplified trials in the State Courts.
> - O 12 r 7: where there is more than one expert, the Court may order them to testify as a panel, comment on each other's views, and be cross-examined in any sequence.
> - O 9 r 21(1)–(2): parties are to inform the Court during the case conference if they intend to rely on expert evidence, and the Court then considers Order 12.
> - PD 56(8): Expert Witness Template (Form B7 of Appendix B) before the RCC where expert evidence is discussed.

**P** A party's proposed expert may be found not to qualify: a foreign-law "expert" whose knowledge of that law was too limited was rejected (*Sanum Investments Ltd v ST Group Co Ltd* [2020] 3 SLR 225 at [31]–[33]). Solicitors must bring the guidelines on expert evidence to the expert's attention (*Pacific Recreation Pte Ltd v S Y Technology Inc* [2008] 2 SLR 491, background).

**A** Approval: [D's solicitors never raised expert evidence at any case conference, as O 9 r 21(1) required, and announce it two weeks before trial claiming an "absolute right"] (PYP22 Q5). There is no such right (O 12 r 2(1)). [The state of the cryptocurrency market when the settlement was signed] does not bear on [whether the debt is due or whether there was duress], so it will not contribute materially to any issue (O 12 r 2(3)); C should object. Contrast [an alleged forged signature on the addendum] (PYP23 Q3.3): handwriting comparison needs specialised knowledge and cannot be resolved by agreed facts, so approval is likely; P should raise it at the case conference, fill in the expert witness template, try to agree a common expert with R (O 12 r 3(1)), and ensure Professor XY is qualified and impartial (O 12 r 2(4)), with a report setting out the agreed issues and facts. **C** Therefore the Court [is unlikely to approve [D]'s crypto expert / is likely to approve a handwriting expert, preferably a common one].

*Source note: O 12 rr 1–3, 7 ROC p112–117; O 9 r 21 ROC p95; PD 56(8) SCPD p57; SYL p19–20 [144(a)–(c)]; L3 s7–s9; Sanum SYL p19 [144(a)] with proposition from Cyril-Exam ("Expert witnesses") (secondary); Pacific Recreation SYL p20 [144(e)] and L3 s9; tested PYP22 Q5 (model: PYP22-Xav Q5), PYP23 Q3.3.*

### drp-w-0-53 — Order to attend court or produce documents (O 15 r 4)
*Keywords: order to attend court, order to produce documents, subpoena, subpoena to testify, subpoena duces tecum, Form 29, O 15 r 4, Registrar issues, ministerial act, personal service in Singapore, at least 28 days before the hearing, cannot be served out of Singapore, revoke, review within 14 days, setting aside, irrelevant, abuse of process, Lao People's Democratic Republic v Sanum Investments, Ong Jane Rebecca v Lim Lie Hoa, Basil Anthony Herman, BVU v BVX, reasonable compensation, produce documents only, Riddick undertaking, PD 76, hostile witness*

**I** The issue is how [Client] can secure the evidence of [Mr D, M's director], who is unlikely to testify voluntarily. **C** [Client] should request the Registrar to issue an order to attend court in Form 29 and serve it personally on [Mr D] in Singapore at least 28 days before the trial (O 15 r 4(1)–(3)); [Mr D] would then be compelled to attend, though he could apply to set it aside.
**R** A party may request the Registrar to issue an order to attend court or produce documents in Form 29, stating whether the witness is to give oral evidence or produce documents or both (O 15 r 4(1)–(2)). It must be served on the witness by personal service in Singapore at least 28 days before the hearing (O 15 r 4(3)). The Registrar may revoke it on any person's application or of his own motion, and a dissatisfied party may seek review by a Judge within 14 days (O 15 r 4(4)–(6)). A witness producing documents only need not attend if the documents are produced, and a complying witness may claim reasonable compensation from the requesting party (O 15 r 4(8)–(9)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 15 r 4(1): the parties may request the Registrar to issue an order to attend court or an order to produce documents in Form 29 to any witness, stating whether the witness is to give oral evidence or to produce documents or both.
> - O 15 r 4(2): the order must state the requesting party and what the witness is to do.
> - O 15 r 4(3): it must be served by the requesting party on the witness by personal service in Singapore at least 28 days before the hearing.
> - O 15 r 4(4): the Registrar may revoke it upon application by any person or on the Registrar's own motion.
> - O 15 r 4(5)–(6): a party dissatisfied with the Registrar's decision may apply to a Judge of the General Division or a District Judge for review, by summons with affidavit, within 14 days after the decision.
> - O 15 r 4(7): the order continues in effect until the conclusion of the hearing.
> - O 15 r 4(8): a witness ordered only to produce documents need not attend personally if all the documents are produced as ordered.
> - O 15 r 4(9): a complying witness may claim reasonable compensation for time and expenses from the requesting party.
> - O 15 r 4(10)–(12): prison witnesses (Prisons Act s 38).
> - ROC 2021, like ROC 2014, does not permit service of such orders out of Singapore (SYL [146(b)]).
> - Documents obtained under compulsion are subject to the implied undertaking in *Riddick v Thames Board Mills Ltd* [1977] QB 881.

**P** Issuing the order is a ministerial act of the Registry (*The Lao People's Democratic Republic v Sanum Investments Ltd* [2013] 4 SLR 947 at [17]). An order to produce documents is not a substitute for discovery: the documents must be relevant, material and necessary (*Lao PDR v Sanum* at [24]). An order may be set aside where it is used frivolously or to embarrass (*Ong Jane Rebecca v Lim Lie Hoa* [2003] 1 SLR(R) 457), where the witness is not shown to be needed (*Basil Anthony Herman v Premier Security Cooperative Ltd* [2010] 3 SLR 110 at [50]–[51]), or where the documents sought are clearly irrelevant, or the order is an abuse of process or issued for a collateral purpose, as where it is used to re-open an arbitrated dispute (*BVU v BVX* [2019] SGHC 69 at [103]–[107]). These cases were decided under ROC 2014.

**A** [Mr D's comment that the bloggers "would fit the bill"] is directly relevant to [whether M approved the influencers]. Because he is M's director and unlikely to give an AEIC for P, P should: (1) request a Form 29 order to attend court (and, if useful, to produce [the meeting notes]); (2) serve it personally on him in Singapore at least 28 days before trial; (3) ask the Court's permission for his evidence-in-chief to be given orally, since a hostile witness will not sign an AEIC (O 15 r 16(2); drp-w-0-51); and (4) offer reasonable compensation. If [Mr D] is abroad, the order cannot be served out; consider video-link evidence or a letter of request (drp-w-0-54). Expect M to apply to set the order aside; answer by showing the evidence is relevant, material and needed. **C** Therefore P should obtain and personally serve a Form 29 order to attend court on [Mr D] at least 28 days before trial.

*Source note: O 15 r 4 ROC p132–133; SYL p21 [146]; L3 s11; Lao PDR v Sanum L3 s11 and SYL p21 [146(a)], [146(c)], propositions at [17] and [24] from Cyril-Exam ("Order to attend court") (secondary); Ong Jane Rebecca, Basil Anthony Herman and BVU v BVX SYL p21 [146(c)(ii)], propositions from Cyril-Exam and Nic-Muggers 8.5 (secondary); BVU v BVX [2019] SGHC 69 (Ang Cheng Hock JC) at [103]–[107], read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026): the syllabus's "SHJC" and Cyril-Exam's "BVY" are typos; PD 76(1) SCPD p90 (Form 29 filed by the requesting party; issued when sealed by the Registry); tested PYP25 Q3.3.*

### drp-w-0-54 — Witness must attend for cross-examination; video link; letters of request; excluding witnesses
*Keywords: attendance of witnesses at trial, O 15 r 16(3), AEIC not used if maker does not attend, cross-examination, parties agree, live video link, live television link, Evidence Act s 62A, unavailability of witness, technical facilities, unfair prejudice, Sonica Industries v Fu Yu Manufacturing, Peter Rogers May v Pinder Lillian, Bachmeer Capital v Ong Chih Ching, Anil Singh Gurm v J S Yeh, Sahara Energy v Chu Said Thong, Wang Xiaopu v Koh Mui Lee, unwillingness to attend, pandemic, letter of request, O 15 r 5, Hague Evidence Convention, exclusion of witnesses, O 15 r 4(13)*

**I** The issue is whether [witness X], who is [overseas and unwilling / unable] to come to Singapore, can give evidence at trial. **C** [X]'s AEIC cannot be used unless [X] attends for cross-examination or the parties agree (O 15 r 16(3)); [Client] should apply for permission for [X] to testify by live video link under s 62A of the Evidence Act, which the Court [is likely / unlikely] to grant because [reason].
**R** An AEIC may not be used if its maker does not attend Court for cross-examination, unless the parties agree (O 15 r 16(3)). The Court may permit a witness outside Singapore to give evidence by live video or television link if a ground in s 62A(1) of the Evidence Act is satisfied, considering all the circumstances, including the reasons for the witness's unavailability, the facilities at the place where the evidence will be given, and whether any party would be unfairly prejudiced (s 62A(2)); it may make orders on the matters in s 62A(3). Where the foreign law requires a letter of request, one may be sought under O 15 r 5. Non-party witnesses who have not testified must stay outside the courtroom (O 15 r 4(13)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 15 r 16(3): an AEIC may not be used if the maker does not attend Court for cross-examination, unless the parties otherwise agree.
> - Evidence Act 1893 s 62A(1): despite any other provision, a person may, with the court's permission, give evidence through a live video or live television link in any proceedings (other than criminal or contempt proceedings) if
>   - (a) the witness is below 18 years of age;
>   - (b) it is expressly agreed between the parties that evidence may be so given;
>   - (c) the witness is outside Singapore; or
>   - (d) the court is satisfied that it is expedient in the interests of justice to do so.
> - s 62A(2): in deciding whether to permit a witness outside Singapore to give evidence this way, the court is to have regard to all the circumstances, including
>   - (a) the reasons for the witness being unable to give evidence in Singapore;
>   - (b) the administrative and technical facilities and arrangements made at the place where the witness is to give evidence;
>   - (c) whether any party would be unfairly prejudiced; and
>   - (d) any other matter prescribed by rules.
> - s 62A(3): in granting permission the court may order on who may be present with the witness, who must be seen and heard, the stages to which the order applies, the technical method, and any other order necessary in the interests of justice; s 62A(4): it may revoke, suspend or vary the order (e.g. if the link fails or fairness requires).
> - O 15 r 5(1): where permission is sought for a person outside Singapore to give evidence by live video or television link and that jurisdiction's law requires a letter of request, an application may be made for an order in Form 30 for its issue; (2) only in the General Division; (3) by summons with affidavit; (5)–(8) the letter (Hague Recommended Model Form or Form 17), certified translations and a Form 18 undertaking on expenses.
> - O 15 r 4(13): a non-party witness who has not given evidence must remain outside the courtroom until called; (14) after testifying he or she may stay or leave.

**P** The Court balances the factors: an unwillingness to attend physically is relevant but not determinative (*Anil Singh Gurm v J S Yeh & Co* [2020] 1 SLR 555); the pandemic should not be cited blindly as a reason a witness cannot attend (*Wang Xiaopu v Koh Mui Lee* [2022] SGHC 54 at [13]–[17]); and half-hearted efforts to attend in person weigh against permission (*Sahara Energy International Pte Ltd v Chu Said Thong* [2020] SGHC 272 at [3], [29]–[62]). See also *Sonica Industries Ltd v Fu Yu Manufacturing Ltd* [1999] 3 SLR(R) 119, *Peter Rogers May v Pinder Lillian Gek Lian* [2006] 2 SLR(R) 381 and *Bachmeer Capital Ltd v Ong Chih Ching* [2018] 4 SLR 29 (inability through illness or travel restrictions accepted; mere inconvenience not). These cases were decided under ROC 2014.

**A** First, if [X] simply does not come, [X]'s AEIC is out unless [D] agrees (r 16(3)), and [Client] may lose that evidence entirely. So apply early for video-link permission. Reasons: [X is seriously ill and cannot fly] (strong; *Bachmeer*); [X lives abroad and coming would be costly] (moderate; *Peter Rogers May*); [X is merely unwilling] (relevant but not decisive; *Anil Singh Gurm*); [X cites the pandemic without specifics] (weak; *Wang Xiaopu*). Show the arrangements: [a reliable link from a law office in [city], with the documents bundle available to X]. Address prejudice: [D can still cross-examine X in real time]. Check whether the foreign law requires a letter of request (e.g. [some jurisdictions require permission for their residents to testify abroad]); if so, apply under O 15 r 5 in the General Division. **C** Therefore [Client] should apply for permission for [X] to give evidence by video link, which the Court [is likely] to grant given [reason].

*Source note: O 15 r 16(3) ROC p142–143; O 15 r 5 ROC p133–135; O 15 r 4(13)–(14) ROC p133; Evidence Act s 62A(1)–(4) read on SSO (current as at 8 Nov 2026), matching L3 s17; SYL p21–22 [147]; video-link cases SYL p22 [147(b)(ii)], propositions from Cyril-Exam ("Live video link") and Nic-Muggers 9.1.2 (secondary); tested PYP25 Q3.3 (as context).*

### drp-w-0-55 — Absence of a party at a hearing or trial (O 15 rr 3, 8)
*Keywords: absence of party, failure to attend, O 15 r 3, attend in person or by solicitor, dismiss application, any appropriate order, dispense with attendance, decide on documents, O 15 r 8(3), none of the parties attends, dismiss claim and counterclaim, O 15 r 8(4), one party absent, proceed with trial, judgment against absent party, Lam Hwa Engineering v Yang Qiang, set aside*

**I** The issue is what happens because [D] did not attend [the trial / the hearing]. **C** The Court may proceed with the trial in [D]'s absence, give judgment against [D], or make any other appropriate order (O 15 r 8(4)); [D] may then seek to have the judgment set aside by showing a good reason for its absence.
**R** All parties must attend hearings in person, if unrepresented, or by a solicitor, and if a party fails to attend the Court may dismiss its application, action or appeal or make any other appropriate order (O 15 r 3(1)–(2)). The Court may dispense with attendance and decide a matter on the documents, except where oral evidence is given (unless all parties consent) or the hearing must be advertised (O 15 r 3(3)). At trial, if no party attends the Court may dismiss the claim and any counterclaim; if one party is absent, it may proceed with the trial, give judgment against or dismiss the claim of the absent party, or make any other order (O 15 r 8(3)–(4)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 15 r 3(1): all parties must attend the hearing of any matter in person (if not legally represented) or by a solicitor (if legally represented).
> - O 15 r 3(2): if a party fails to attend, the Court may dismiss the party's application, action or appeal or make any other appropriate order against the party.
> - O 15 r 3(3): the Court may dispense with attendance and decide any matter on the documents without oral arguments, except (a) where oral evidence is given at any part of the proceedings, unless all parties consent; and (b) where the hearing must be advertised or published under written law or court order.
> - O 15 r 3(4): matters decided without oral arguments may be heard asynchronously unless that would be unfair.
> - O 15 r 8(3): if none of the parties attends Court when the trial begins, the Court may dismiss the claim and any counterclaim.
> - O 15 r 8(4): if one of the parties does not attend when the trial begins, the Court may proceed with the trial or give judgment against or dismiss the claim of the absent party or make any other appropriate order.
> - O 3 r 2(8)(a): a judgment or order obtained in the absence of the party affected may be revoked or set aside if in the interests of justice, on application within 14 days of knowledge (drp-w-0-2).

**P** A party may appear in person or by counsel; a party is not "absent" if its solicitor attends (*Lam Hwa Engineering & Trading Pte Ltd v Yang Qiang* [2013] 2 SLR 524 at [19]).

**A** Who is absent? If [D and its solicitors] fail to appear on the first day of trial, [Client] may ask the Court to proceed and give judgment on [Client]'s evidence (r 8(4)); if [Client] is absent, its claim may be dismissed. If both are absent, both claim and counterclaim may be dismissed (r 8(3)). If only [D] personally is absent but its counsel attends, [D] is not absent (*Lam Hwa*), though its evidence may suffer if it was to testify. For the absent party: apply promptly to set aside, explaining the absence and showing a real defence (O 3 r 2(8)–(10)). **C** Therefore the Court may [proceed and give judgment against [D] / dismiss [Client]'s claim], subject to an application to set aside.

*Source note: O 15 r 3 ROC p131–132; O 15 r 8(3)–(4) ROC p136; O 3 r 2(8)–(10) ROC p54–55; SYL p22 [148]; L3 s20 (Lam Hwa at [19]).*

### drp-w-0-81 — AEIC content, form and objections; expert reports and duties; independent witnesses and counsel (background only — not examinable per SYL p1)
*Keywords: AEIC contents, material facts, O 15 r 16(4), admissible evidence only, O 15 r 16(5), relevant facts only, O 15 r 25, vulgar or insulting words, English language, translation, O 3 r 7, O 15 r 18, Form 31, photograph, O 15 r 19, PD 78–80, PD 83 non-compliance, exchange of AEICs, O 9 r 8(1), objections to AEIC, 28 days before hearing, O 15 r 16(6), PD 84, Form B13, CES v International Air Transport Association, expert report, list of issues, common set of agreed or assumed facts, O 12 r 4, O 12 r 5, expert's duty to the Court, O 12 r 1(2)–(3), Pacific Recreation, Poh Soon Kiat, HSBC Institutional Trust v Toshin, independent witness, interested non-party, O 9 r 22, independent counsel, amicus curiae, O 9 r 23, background only*

**I** The issue is whether [the AEIC of [W] / the expert report of [E]] complies with the Rules, and how [Client] may object to it. **C** [The AEIC / report] [complies / does not comply] with [O 15 r 16(4)–(5), r 25 / O 12 r 5], and [Client] should file its notice of objections at least 28 days before the first hearing date (O 15 r 16(6); PD 84).
**R** An AEIC must contain all material facts, which may not be departed from or supplemented orally unless they arose later, and only evidence admissible in law (O 15 r 16(4)–(5)); it must contain only relevant facts and nothing vulgar, insulting or intended to belittle (O 15 r 25). It must be in English (or translated), in Form 31 with numbered paragraphs and, for an AEIC in an originating claim, the maker's photograph (O 3 r 7; O 15 rr 18–19; PD 78–80); a non-compliant affidavit may be rejected with costs (PD 83). A party objecting to the contents of AEICs must give notice in the PD form at least 28 days before the first hearing date (O 15 r 16(6); PD 84). An expert's duty to assist the Court overrides any duty to the client (O 12 r 1(2)–(3)); expert evidence is confined to court-approved issues and a common set of agreed or assumed facts (O 12 r 4) and is given in a signed report exhibited in the expert's affidavit with the contents listed in O 12 r 5. The Court may of its own accord call an independent witness, invite written views from an interested non-party, or appoint independent counsel (O 9 rr 22–23).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 15 r 16(4): an AEIC must contain all material facts, which may not be departed from or supplemented by new facts in oral evidence unless the new facts occurred after the AEIC was made. O 15 r 16(5): only evidence admissible in law.
> - O 15 r 25: (1) only relevant facts; (2) not (a) vulgar or insulting words unless in issue, or (b) anything intended to offend or belittle any person or entity.
> - O 3 r 7: documents filed or used in Court must be in English; others need a certified or affidavit-verified translation. O 15 r 18: an affidavit is a statement of evidence in English, signed and affirmed before a commissioner for oaths.
> - O 15 r 19: Form 31, consecutively numbered paragraphs; an AEIC in an originating claim carries a colour photograph (35 mm × 45 mm, taken in the last 12 months, full face). PD 78–80: margins, double spacing, header line, exhibits. PD 83: non-compliant affidavits or exhibits may be rejected and attract costs.
> - O 9 r 8(1): the Court may order AEICs of all or some witnesses to be filed and served simultaneously or in any sequence.
> - O 15 r 16(6): a party objecting to AEIC contents on admissibility or other grounds must file and serve the PD form at least 28 days before the first date of the hearing. PD 84(1)–(3): notice in Form 13 of Appendix B setting out all objections and grounds; adjudication only at the trial or hearing, and an earlier application will be adjourned with costs.
> - O 12 r 1(2)–(3): the expert must assist the Court on matters within his or her expertise; that duty overrides any obligation to the instructing or paying party.
> - O 12 r 4: parties agree the list of issues and the common set of agreed or assumed facts; the Court approves (or decides them if no agreement); evidence is confined to them; issues framed as yes/no questions as far as possible.
> - O 12 r 5(1)–(2): report signed and exhibited in the expert's affidavit, including (a) qualifications; (b) statement of the overriding duty; (c) issues and facts relied on; (d) materials relied on; (e) who did any tests; (f) any range of opinion and reasons; (g) statement of belief of correctness; (h) conclusions and reasons.
> - O 9 r 22: the Court may of its own accord order an independent witness to give evidence orally or by affidavit, with cross-examination directions; may invite an interested or able person or entity to give written views on specific issues, without cross-examination or attendance; and may order parties to pay their reasonable expenses.
> - O 9 r 23: the Court may of its own accord appoint independent counsel (formerly amicus curiae) — a solicitor, a law academic or a person with special knowledge — on specific issues of law, with directions on submissions and attendance.

**P** Solicitors must ensure that the proper bounds of evidentiary affidavits are not transgressed, and must not ask an expert to opine beyond the expert's proper role (*CES v International Air Transport Association* [2020] 4 SLR 44 at [102]–[106]). Solicitors must bring the guidelines on expert evidence to the expert's attention (*Pacific Recreation Pte Ltd v S Y Technology Inc* [2008] 2 SLR 491 at [62]–[89]); see also *Poh Soon Kiat v Desert Palace Inc* [2010] 1 SLR 1129 at [22]–[24] (rule of prudence on expert evidence) and *HSBC Institutional Trust Services (Singapore) Ltd v Toshin Development Singapore Pte Ltd* [2012] 4 SLR 738 at [71] (experts must promptly disclose any significant relationship with an interested party). All four were decided under ROC 2014.

**A** Read [W]'s AEIC for hearsay, opinion, argument or irrelevant and insulting matter (O 15 rr 16(5), 25); list every objection with grounds in the Form 13 notice at least 28 days before trial, and expect the ruling at trial (PD 84(3)).
