Check [E]'s report against each O 12 r 5(2) item, especially the duty statement and any undisclosed tie to [D] (*HSBC v Toshin*); if [E] strays beyond the approved issues or assumed facts, object under O 12 r 4(2). **C** Therefore [Client] should file a notice of objections under O 15 r 16(6) and PD 84 [and challenge the report under O 12 rr 4–5].

*Source note: O 15 rr 16, 18, 19, 25 ROC p142–147; O 3 r 7 ROC p57; O 9 rr 8, 22, 23 ROC p86, 95; O 12 rr 1, 4, 5 ROC p112–115; PD 78, 83, 84 SCPD p92–99; SYL p18–20 [143(c)–(f)], [144(d)–(e)], [145]. Case holdings are as the syllabus states them; the judgments were not read. ROC text checked against SSO (current version as at 18 Nov 2026; extraction subagent): unchanged since 2021; PD text from the library copy.*

## 0.XI Interim relief before trial

- **Where does the power come from; what kind of injunction?**
  - CLA s 4(10) (just or convenient); SCJA s 18(2) and First Schedule para 5; State Courts Act ss 31–32 → [0-56](#drp-w-0-56)
  - Interlocutory or interim injunction (*Tay Long Kee*); freestanding injunction (*Sulzer Pumps*); in aid of foreign proceedings (CLA s 4(10A)) → [0-56](#drp-w-0-56)
- **How do I apply, especially urgently?**
  - Any time, even if not claimed; before the originating process if urgent; OA or summons without notice + affidavit on urgency, why no notice, merits (O 13 r 1(1)–(4)) → [0-57](#drp-w-0-57)
  - Still give at least 2 hours' notice unless that would defeat the purpose (PD 71); affidavit headings, skeletal arguments, note of hearing (PD 73); out of hours (PD 85) → [0-57](#drp-w-0-57)
  - Full and frank disclosure of all material facts, including adverse ones (O 13 r 1(5); *The "Vasiliy Golovnin"*, *Parastate Labs*) → [0-57](#drp-w-0-57)
  - No approval needed to file (O 9 r 9(7)(a)) → [0-33](#drp-w-0-33)
- **Ordinary interlocutory injunction: the test**
  - Serious question to be tried; balance of convenience; undertaking as to damages, fortification (*Group Lease*; *American Cyanamid*) → [0-58](#drp-w-0-58)
- **Defendant moving money or assets out**
  - Mareva (freezing) injunction: good arguable case, real risk of dissipation; Forms 24 (local) and 25 (worldwide); a Judge hears it if contested; ancillary disclosure (O 13 r 1(6)–(7); PD 72; *Bouvier*, *Farooq*, *Sea Trucks*) → [0-59](#drp-w-0-59)
  - Proprietary injunction is different (*Farooq*); foreign proceedings (*Bi Xiaoqiong*; CLA s 4(10A)) → [0-59](#drp-w-0-59)
  - After judgment, in aid of execution (*JTrust*) → [0-69](#drp-w-0-69)
- **Evidence may be destroyed**
  - Search order: extremely strong prima facie case, very serious damage, real possibility of destruction, proportionality; Form 26; supervising solicitor (O 13 r 1(8); PD 72(5); *Computerland*, *Bengawan Solo*, *Asian Corporate Services*, *Arfat*) → [0-60](#drp-w-0-60)
- **Preserve, inspect or sample the property in dispute**
  - Detention, custody, preservation, inspection, entry, fund paid into court; samples and experiments (O 13 rr 2–3; *UMCI*, *Sang Cheol Woo*) → [0-61](#drp-w-0-61)

### drp-w-0-56 — Interim relief: source of the power; interim, interlocutory and freestanding injunctions
*Keywords: source of power, Civil Law Act s 4(10), just or convenient, SCJA s 18(2), First Schedule para 5, State Courts Act ss 31–32, interlocutory injunction, interim injunction, freestanding injunction, Tay Long Kee Impex v Tan Beng Huwah, Sulzer Pumps Spain v Hyflux Membrane, interim relief in aid of foreign proceedings, CLA s 4(10A), s 4(10C), mandatory order*

**I** The issue is whether the Court has power to grant [Client] an injunction pending trial, and of what kind. **C** Yes. The Court may grant an injunction by interlocutory order wherever it appears just or convenient (s 4(10) Civil Law Act), and the General Division has power under s 18(2) and para 5 of the First Schedule of the SCJA to preserve property, evidence and assets pending trial.
**R** A mandatory order or an injunction may be granted, or a receiver appointed, by interlocutory order in all cases in which it appears to the court just or convenient, unconditionally or on terms (s 4(10) CLA). The General Division also has power, before or after proceedings begin, to provide for the interim preservation of the subject matter, of evidence and of assets to satisfy a judgment (s 18(2) and First Schedule para 5 SCJA); the State Courts have corresponding powers (State Courts Act ss 31–32). The court may also grant interim relief in aid of civil or commercial proceedings commenced or to be commenced outside Singapore, if just or convenient (s 4(10A) CLA).

> **Rule in full**
> - CLA s 4(10): a Mandatory Order or an injunction may be granted or a receiver appointed by an interlocutory order of the court, either unconditionally or upon such terms and conditions as the court thinks just, in all cases in which it appears to the court to be just or convenient that such order should be made.
> - CLA s 4(10A): subject to (10B)–(10D), a court has power to grant interim relief in aid of proceedings in any civil or commercial matter (excluding fiscal, monetary or revenue matters) which have been or are to be commenced outside Singapore, if it appears just or convenient.
> - CLA s 4(10C): the court may refuse if (a) its lack of jurisdiction apart from (10A) over the subject matter makes the order inappropriate; or (b) the foreign proceedings cannot give rise to a judgment enforceable in Singapore.
> - CLA s 4(10D): "interim relief" excludes a warrant for arrest of property and provision for obtaining evidence.
> - SCJA s 18(2) and First Schedule para 5: power before or after proceedings are commenced to provide for (a) interim preservation of the subject matter; (b) preservation of evidence; (c) preservation of assets to satisfy any judgment (drp-w-0-2).
> - State Courts Act 1970 s 31(1)–(2): a District Court, in any action within its jurisdiction, may grant such relief as the General Division could, and in particular may grant an injunction whether or not any other relief is claimed; s 32: a District Judge has the powers of a General Division Judge in chambers in civil proceedings pending in the District Court; s 52(1B): similar powers for a Magistrate's Court.

**P** "Interim" and "interlocutory" injunctions are not terms of art: an interim injunction is made in the meantime until something is done, and an interlocutory injunction is made before final disposal of the action; "An interim injunction is an interlocutory injunction, and vice versa", and there is no material difference between them (*Tay Long Kee Impex Pte Ltd v Tan Beng Huwah* [2000] SGCA 22, reported [2000] 1 SLR(R) 786). Contrast a freestanding injunction, where obtaining the injunction is the sole object of the originating process: an injunction restraining a call on a performance bond is a freestanding prohibitory injunction, not an interlocutory one, and the party seeking it need not have a separate cause of action, since it protects existing rights against a call made without bona fides (*Sulzer Pumps Spain, SA v Hyflux Membrane Manufacturing (S) Pte Ltd* [2020] SGHC 122, reported [2020] 5 SLR 634, at [75]–[81]).

**A** Identify the relief: [an order restraining D from selling the disputed machinery until trial] (interlocutory; CLA s 4(10) and First Schedule para 5(a)); [a freezing order over D's bank accounts] (para 5(c); drp-w-0-59); [an order to preserve CCTV footage] (para 5(b); drp-w-0-61). If the main proceedings are abroad ([in Australia]), rely on s 4(10A) and show the foreign judgment will be enforceable here (s 4(10C)(b)). Then apply the procedure (drp-w-0-57) and the test for the type of injunction (drp-w-0-58 to 0-60). **C** Therefore the Court has power to grant [relief] under s 4(10) CLA [and s 18(2) SCJA], and [Client] should apply on the procedure in O 13 r 1.

*Source note: CLA p9–10 (s 4(10)–(10D)), informal consolidation in force from 1 Aug 2022; SCJA p26 (s 18(2)), p92 (First Schedule para 5); SYL p23 [149]–[150(a)], p24 [150(d)] (s 4(10A)); L3 s22; Tay Long Kee [2000] SGCA 22 read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026; the online text has no paragraph numbers, so the syllabus pinpoint [45]–[48] could not be matched); Sulzer SYL p23 [150(a)], read on eLitigation as [2020] SGHC 122 at [75]–[81] (18 Nov 2026, extraction subagent; SLR cite as SYL); State Courts Act ss 31–32 read on SSO (current as at 8 Nov 2026): s 31(2)(a) gives a District Court power to grant an injunction, and s 32 gives a District Judge the chambers powers of a General Division Judge; Conflict: Cyril-Exam ("Interim relief") cites "s 10 Civil Law Act"; the CLA and the syllabus place it in s 4(10), which the pack follows.*

### drp-w-0-57 — Procedure for injunctions and search orders; without-notice applications; full and frank disclosure
*Keywords: Vasiliy Golovin, application for injunction, O 13 r 1, whether or not claimed in originating process, urgent case, before originating process issued, originating application without notice, summons without notice, affidavit, urgency, why defendant should not be informed, merits, not to serve until originating process issued, duty of full and frank disclosure, O 13 r 1(5), material facts, adverse matters, PD 71, 2 hours' notice, defeat the purpose, PD 73, affidavit headings, skeletal arguments, note of hearing, PD 85, out of office hours, duty registrar, The Vasiliy Golovnin, Parastate Labs v Wang Li, Tay Long Kee, discharge of injunction*

**I** The issue is how [Client] should apply urgently for an injunction against [D] and what it must disclose. **C** [Client] may apply, even before issuing its originating claim, by [originating application / summons] without notice supported by an affidavit stating the urgency, why [D] should not be told, and the merits (O 13 r 1(2)–(3)); it must disclose all material facts, including those adverse to it (O 13 r 1(5)), and should give [D] at least 2 hours' notice unless that would defeat the purpose (PD 71).
**R** A party may apply for an injunction or search order whether or not it is claimed in its originating process, and in an urgent case before the originating process is issued (O 13 r 1(1)–(2)). The application may be by originating application or summons without notice, supported by an affidavit stating the urgency, why the defendant should not be informed and the merits (O 13 r 1(3)). The applicant must disclose all material facts it knows or reasonably ought to know, including matters adverse to its case (O 13 r 1(5)). Even a without-notice application requires at least 2 hours' notice to the other parties unless giving notice would or might defeat its purpose, which must be explained in the affidavit (PD 71(2)–(3)). The affidavit must address the PD 73(1) headings, and skeletal arguments are required (PD 73(2)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 13 r 1(1): a party may apply for an injunction or a search order, whether or not a claim for such relief was included in its originating process, counterclaim or third party notice.
> - O 13 r 1(2): in an urgent case, the claimant may apply before the originating process is issued.
> - O 13 r 1(3): the application may be by originating application without notice or summons without notice, supported by an affidavit stating the urgency and explaining why the defendant should not be informed about the application, and the merits.
> - O 13 r 1(4): the Court may order the claimant not to serve the injunction or search order until after the originating process is issued.
> - O 13 r 1(5): a party applying for an injunction or search order has the duty to disclose to the Court all material facts that it knows or reasonably ought to know, including any matter that may affect the merits of its case adversely.
> - O 13 r 1(6)–(8): Form 24 (local asset injunction), Form 25 (worldwide), Form 26 (search order).
> - PD 71(2): an applicant for an injunction without notice must give notice to the other concerned parties before the hearing (email, or by phone in extreme urgency), at least 2 hours except in extreme urgency or with permission, with the date, time, place and relief sought and, if possible, draft papers; if they are absent, counsel tells the Court (a) what attempts were made to notify; (b) what documents were given and when; (c) whether they consent to the hearing in their absence.
> - PD 71(3): (2) need not be followed if notice would or might defeat the purpose of the application, but the reasons must be set out in the affidavit.
> - PD 73(1): the supporting affidavit must include, under clear headings,
>   - (a) reasons for proceeding without notice, including any risk of dissipation, destruction of evidence or other prejudicial conduct;
>   - (b) urgency, including any triggering event;
>   - (c) the factual basis, including past incidents and any evidence of dishonesty or bad faith;
>   - (d) the factual basis for any reasonable defences the opponent may rely on;
>   - (e) any issues of jurisdiction, forum non conveniens or service out;
>   - (f) an undertaking to pay for losses caused by the orders, stating what assets are available to meet it and to whom they belong; and
>   - (g) any other material facts.
> - PD 73(2)–(3): skeletal arguments must be filed and given to the Court and any opponent present; the Court may require a note of the hearing to be served on absent opponents.
> - PD 85: urgent applications outside office hours: contact the Registrar on duty; papers and draft orders ready; undertaking to file by the next working day.
> - Injunction and search order applications need no prior approval (O 9 r 9(7)(a)).

**P** The applicant must disclose all matters within its knowledge which might be material, even if prejudicial to its claim, and must draw the Court's attention to them rather than merely exhibit them (*The "Vasiliy Golovnin"* [2008] 4 SLR(R) 994 at [83]–[110]; *CZD v CZE* [2023] SGHC 86 at [46]); Mareva relief may be refused to an applicant without clean hands, including one that did not make full and frank disclosure; a deliberate, unexplained departure from the prescribed form (omitting standard undertakings) counts against the applicant, and a later concession does not cure it; non-disclosure caught before any injunction is granted may lead the Court to refuse relief at the hearing with notice (*Parastate Labs Inc v Wang Li* [2023] SGHC 153 at [26], [27]–[39]). On breach, the Court may discharge the injunction, or exercise a discretion, sparingly, to continue it or grant a fresh one; where there was deliberate suppression rather than innocent omission, it must be a special case for the injunction not to be discharged (*Tay Long Kee Impex Pte Ltd v Tan Beng Huwah* [2000] SGCA 22).

**A** Urgency: [D is transferring large sums out of its accounts now] (PYP25 Q3.4), so apply at once, without waiting for the SAPT (O 9 r 9(7)(a)); if proceedings have begun, by summons without notice; if not, by originating application without notice (and issue the originating claim promptly). Notice: tipping [D] off may trigger dissipation, so explain in the affidavit why PD 71 notice would defeat the purpose (PD 71(3)). Affidavit: use the PD 73(1) headings, and give the undertaking as to damages with evidence of [Client]'s assets to back it. Disclosure: set out, and highlight, the facts against [Client], such as [M's complaint that P used unknown bloggers, which is M's defence] (PD 73(1)(d)). Failure risks discharge with costs. A contested Mareva application, and any search order application, is heard by a Judge (PD 72(1)). **C** Therefore [Client] should file [a summons without notice] with a PD 73 affidavit making full and frank disclosure and, unless notice would defeat the purpose, give [D] at least 2 hours' notice.

*Source note: O 13 r 1 ROC p117–118; PD 71 SCPD p85–86; PD 72(1) SCPD p86; PD 73 SCPD p87–88; PD 85 SCPD p100; O 9 r 9(7)(a) ROC p87; SYL p23 [150(b)(i)–(ii)]; L3 s23; Vasiliy Golovnin L3 s23, proposition from Cyril-Exam ("Interim relief") and Nic-Muggers 10.1.3 (secondary); Tay Long Kee [2000] SGCA 22 and CZD v CZE [2023] SGHC 86 at [46] read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026); Parastate SYL p23 [150(b)(i)(B)], read on eLitigation [2023] SGHC 153 at [26]–[39] (18 Nov 2026, extraction subagent); PD 72(1) current text (epd2021, 15 Jul 2026); the syllabus spells the case "Vasiliy Golovin"; tested PYP25 Q3.4, PYP21 Q5.*

### drp-w-0-58 — Interlocutory injunction: the test; undertaking as to damages
*Keywords: interlocutory injunction test, American Cyanamid, serious question to be tried, balance of convenience, adequacy of damages, ability to pay, status quo, undertaking as to damages, fortification, Group Lease Holdings v Group Lease Public Co, usual costs order, costs in the cause*

**I** The issue is whether the Court will grant [Client] an interlocutory injunction restraining [D] from [conduct] until trial. **C** The Court is [likely] to grant it, because there is a serious question to be tried on [Client]'s claim that [D is breaching the restraint clause], and the balance of convenience favours an injunction since [damages would not adequately compensate Client for lost customers].
**R** A court will grant an interim prohibitory injunction if (a) there is a serious issue to be tried; and (b) the balance of convenience lies in favour of granting it (*Group Lease Holdings Pte Ltd v Group Lease Public Co Ltd* [2024] SGHC 302 at [39]). The first threshold is low: the claim must not be frivolous or vexatious ([40]). The balance of convenience is approached in three stages: whether damages would adequately compensate the claimant if the injunction is refused; if not, whether damages would adequately compensate the defendant if it is wrongly granted; and only then the balance of convenience proper ([46]). The applicant must ordinarily give an undertaking as to damages, which the Court may require to be fortified.

> **Rule in full**
> - Stage 1 (*Group Lease* at [39]–[42]): is there a serious issue to be tried? The threshold is low (the claim must not be frivolous or vexatious); there is no mini-trial, though the merits must be assessed where granting or refusing the injunction would in effect dispose of the action.
> - Stage 2 (*Group Lease* at [46]): the balance of convenience, in three steps:
>   - (a) would damages adequately compensate the claimant if the injunction is refused? If yes and the defendant can pay, no injunction; if no or in doubt, go to (b);
>   - (b) would damages (under the claimant's undertaking) adequately compensate the defendant if the injunction is wrongly granted? If yes and the claimant can pay, grant; if no or in doubt, go to (c);
>   - (c) the balance of convenience proper: (i) any difference in the extent to which damages are inadequate for each side; (ii) the relative strength of the parties' cases; and (iii) as a last resort, where all else is balanced, preserving the status quo.
> - Undertaking as to damages: the applicant undertakes to pay the respondent's losses if the injunction turns out to have been wrongly granted; the Court may order fortification (e.g. a bank guarantee) (White Book at [13/1/28]–[13/1/33]; PD 73(1)(f)).
> - Costs: in *Group Lease* at [194] the parties agreed that costs of the application be costs in the cause, which the Court accepted as sensible (though not an immutable rule) because the injunction holds the ring pending trial; reserving costs to the trial judge may also be reasonable.

**P** The two-stage test of a serious question to be tried and the balance of convenience applies to interlocutory injunctions generally (*Group Lease Holdings Pte Ltd v Group Lease Public Co Ltd* [2024] SGHC 302 at [39]–[46], applying *American Cyanamid Co v Ethicon Ltd* [1975] AC 396, also reported at [1975] 1 All ER 504).

**A** Stage 1: [Client]'s claim that [D, a former employee, is soliciting customers in breach of cl 12] is arguable on [the signed contract and customer emails], so there is a serious question to be tried. Stage 2: if no injunction, [Client] may lose customers in ways hard to quantify, so damages are inadequate; if an injunction is wrongly granted, [D]'s loss of income can be measured and is covered by [Client]'s undertaking, which [Client] can meet given [its assets]. If evenly balanced, preserve the status quo [by restraining solicitation until trial]. Offer the undertaking as to damages and be ready to fortify it if [D] shows it may be unsupported. **C** Therefore the Court is likely to grant an interlocutory injunction on [Client]'s undertaking as to damages.

*Source note: SYL p24 [150(c)], [150(e)]; L3 s24 (American Cyanamid, 2022 White Book [13/1/28]–[13/1/37]); Group Lease [2024] SGHC 302 (Goh Yihan J) at [39]–[46], [194], read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026); American Cyanamid: [1975] AC 396 and [1975] 1 All ER 504 (cited in L3 s24) are reports of the same House of Lords decision ([1975] UKHL 1); tested PYP25 Q3.4 (as context).*

### drp-w-0-59 — Mareva (freezing) injunction: procedure, ancillary disclosure, principles of grant
*Keywords: Mareva injunction, freezing injunction, freezing order, injunction prohibiting disposal of assets, local Mareva, Form 24, worldwide Mareva, Form 25, O 13 r 1(6)–(7), PD 72, heard by a Judge, undertaking not to inform third parties, good arguable case, assets within jurisdiction, real risk of dissipation, solid evidence, dishonesty, Bouvier Yves Charles Edgar v Accent Delight, Farooq Ahmad Mann v Xia Zheng, ancillary disclosure order, asset disclosure, Sea Trucks Offshore v Roomans, proprietary injunction, Bi Xiaoqiong v China Medical Technologies, foreign proceedings, CLA s 4(10A), moving money out of accounts*

**I** The issue is what [Client] can do to stop [D] from [moving large sums out of its bank accounts] before judgment. **C** [Client] should apply without notice to a Judge for a Mareva injunction in Form [24 / 25] (O 13 r 1(6)–(7); PD 72(1)), with an ancillary asset disclosure order; it is likely to be granted if [Client] shows a good arguable case, assets of [D] [in Singapore / worldwide] and a real risk that [D] will dissipate them to frustrate a judgment.
**R** A Mareva injunction restrains a defendant from disposing of its assets so that a judgment is not rendered nugatory. A local injunction must be in Form 24 and a worldwide one in Form 25 (O 13 r 1(6)–(7)); contested applications are heard by a Judge (PD 72(1)), the prescribed forms should be used, and the applicant should undertake not to inform third parties until the return date (PD 72(1)–(3)). The applicant must show (a) a good arguable case on the merits of its claim and (b) a real risk that the defendant will dissipate its assets to frustrate enforcement of an anticipated judgment; the requirements are the same for a worldwide order, but the threshold of necessity is more exacting (*Farooq Ahmad Mann v Xia Zheng* [2024] SGHC 182 at [21]–[23], applying *Bouvier, Yves Charles Edgar v Accent Delight International Ltd* [2015] 5 SLR 558 at [36]–[37]); the balance of convenience is not part of the Mareva test (*Farooq* at [40]–[42]). The Court may order ancillary disclosure of assets to make the injunction effective (*Sea Trucks Offshore Ltd v Roomans* [2019] 3 SLR 836 at [44]–[55]).

> **Rule in full**
> - Procedure: as for any injunction (O 13 r 1(1)–(5); PD 71, 73; drp-w-0-57); no approval needed (O 9 r 9(7)(a)).
> - O 13 r 1(6): a local injunction prohibiting the disposal of assets in Singapore must be in Form 24; (7) a worldwide injunction must be in Form 25.
> - PD 72(1) (current version): unless otherwise directed, contested applications for injunctions prohibiting the disposal of assets, and applications for search orders, whether made with or without notice, will be heard by a Judge; all other applications for interim injunctions may be heard by a Registrar. (The 1 Apr 2022 version sent all asset-injunction applications to a Judge.)
> - PD 72(2): use Forms 24–26 unless the Judge sees good reason; justify departures in the affidavit.
> - PD 72(3): the applicant should undertake not to inform any third party of the proceedings until after the return date.
> - PD 72(4): apply early enough for the Judge to read the papers.
> - Requirements (*Farooq* at [21]–[23], applying *Bouvier* at [36]–[37]):
>   - a good arguable case on the merits (more than barely capable of serious argument);
>   - the defendant has assets that the order can bite on, in Singapore (local order) or abroad (worldwide order);
>   - a real risk that the defendant will dissipate assets to frustrate a judgment, shown by solid evidence; dishonesty is relevant only if it bears on the risk of dissipation;
>   - a worldwide order demands a more exacting threshold of necessity.
> - Proprietary injunction (*Farooq* at [24]–[25]): granted in support of a proprietary claim to a particular asset, not a personal money claim; governed by the American Cyanamid test (seriously arguable proprietary claim and balance of convenience); no need to show a real risk of dissipation.
> - Ancillary orders: disclosure of assets, production of documents or answers to questions, to make the injunction effective.
> - In aid of foreign proceedings: CLA s 4(10A) (drp-w-0-56).

**P** A Mareva injunction requires a good arguable case on the merits and a real risk that the defendant will dissipate its assets to frustrate enforcement of an anticipated judgment; it operates in personam and gives no proprietary interest; and a worldwide order requires a more exacting threshold of necessity (*Farooq Ahmad Mann v Xia Zheng* [2024] SGHC 182 at [19]–[23], applying *Bouvier, Yves Charles Edgar v Accent Delight International Ltd* [2015] 5 SLR 558 at [36]–[37]). A proprietary injunction is distinct: it supports a claim to a particular asset, follows the American Cyanamid test, and needs no real risk of dissipation (*Farooq* at [24]–[25]). An ancillary asset disclosure order serves to make the Mareva injunction effective (*Sea Trucks Offshore Ltd v Roomans, Jacobus Johannes* [2019] 3 SLR 836 at [44]–[55]). The Court may grant a Mareva injunction in aid of foreign proceedings under s 4(10) CLA, subject to at least two conditions: personal jurisdiction over the defendant, and a reasonable accrued cause of action against the defendant in Singapore (the *Siskina* principle); the Singapore action need not be intended to end in a Singapore judgment, but an applicant who never means to seek relief here may be refused for collateral purpose (*Bi Xiaoqiong v China Medical Technologies, Inc* [2019] SGCA 50, reported [2019] 2 SLR 595, at [62], [66]–[68], [103]–[104], [114]–[120]). Section 4(10A) CLA now gives an express power to grant interim relief in aid of foreign proceedings where just or convenient.

**A** Good arguable case: [M owes $350,000 on the invoice; its "failed campaign" complaint is weak on the documents] (PYP25 Q3.4). Assets: [M's bank accounts in Singapore] (local order, Form 24); [D1's assets mostly abroad] (PYP21 Q5) would need a worldwide order (Form 25) and a stronger case. Real risk of dissipation: point to solid evidence, such as [intelligence that M is moving large sums out of its accounts after the dispute arose, its deteriorating financials, and its being a shell with few assets]; a mere fear is not enough. Ask for an ancillary order requiring [D] to disclose its assets on affidavit. Procedure: summons without notice (heard by a Judge if contested: PD 72(1); no approval needed); PD 73 affidavit with full and frank disclosure (e.g. [D's defence]); undertaking as to damages with evidence of [Client]'s means, and an undertaking not to inform any third party of the proceedings until after the return date (PD 72(3)). If [Client] instead claims [specific funds traced as its own], seek a proprietary injunction. **C** Therefore [Client] should apply to a Judge, without notice and immediately, for a Mareva injunction in Form [24] with an asset disclosure order, which is likely to be granted on the evidence of [dissipation].

*Source note: O 13 r 1(6)–(7) ROC p118; PD 72(1)–(4) SCPD p86; SYL p23–24 [150(b)(iii)], [150(d)]; L3 s25; Bouvier, Farooq, Sea Trucks and Bi Xiaoqiong SYL p24 [150(b)(iii)], [150(d)]; Bi Xiaoqiong read on eLitigation [2019] SGCA 50 (18 Nov 2026, extraction subagent); requirements and Bouvier propositions from Cyril-Exam ("Mareva injunction") and Nic-Muggers 10.2.2 (secondary); Conflict: Cyril-Exam gives three limbs for a domestic Mareva; Nic-Muggers adds a fourth (dissipation would make judgment nugatory) and a five-limb worldwide test; the pack states the common core; Farooq [2024] SGHC 182 (Aedit Abdullah J) at [18]–[25], [40]–[42], read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026); PD 72(1) current text (epd2021, 15 Jul 2026); L3 s25 cites Ernest Ferdinand [2020] SGCA 24 at [58]–[60] for the proprietary distinction; tested PYP25 Q3.4, PYP21 Q5.*

### drp-w-0-60 — Search orders (formerly Anton Piller orders)
*Keywords: search order, Anton Piller order, O 13 r 1(8), Form 26, supervising solicitor, PD 72(5), heard by a Judge, without notice, PD 73, extremely strong prima facie case, very serious damage, real possibility of destruction, proportionate, Computerland Corp v Yew Seng Computers, Bengawan Solo v Season Confectionary, Asian Corporate Services (SEA) v Eastwest Management, Arfat Pannir Selvam v Sharad Selvam Ramachandra, counterfeit goods, destruction of evidence*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can obtain an order to enter [D]'s premises and seize [the counterfeit stock and records] before [D] destroys them. **C** [Client] may apply without notice to a Judge for a search order in Form 26 (O 13 r 1(8); PD 72); it will be granted only if [Client] shows an extremely strong prima facie case, very serious potential damage, and a real possibility that [D] will destroy the evidence, and the order is proportionate.
**R** A search order is applied for on the same procedure as an injunction (O 13 r 1(1)–(5); PD 73), must be in Form 26 (O 13 r 1(8)), is heard by a Judge (PD 72(1)), and must be served by, and carried out in the presence of, an independent supervising solicitor (PD 72(5)). It is an exceptional remedy, granted only where the applicant shows an extremely strong prima facie case, very serious actual or potential damage, clear evidence that the respondent has incriminating material and a real possibility that it will be destroyed, and that the order is proportionate.

> **Rule in full**
> - O 13 r 1(8): a search order must be in Form 26; procedure and disclosure as in O 13 r 1(1)–(5) (drp-w-0-57).
> - PD 72(1): applications for search orders, with or without notice, are heard by a Judge unless otherwise directed.
> - PD 72(5): the order must be served by a supervising solicitor and carried out in his or her presence and under his or her supervision; the supervising solicitor should be experienced, not from the applicant's firm, and familiar with search orders; the supporting affidavit should give his or her identity and experience.
> - PD 73(1): affidavit headings, including the basis for believing evidence will be destroyed and an undertaking as to damages.
> - Requirements (*Asian Corporate Services* at [14], applied in *Arfat* at [34]):
>   - an extremely strong prima facie case;
>   - very serious damage, actual or potential, to the applicant;
>   - clear evidence that the respondent has relevant documents or things and a real possibility that it will destroy them before an inter partes application;
>   - the harm likely to be caused by the order is not excessive or out of proportion to its legitimate object.

**P** A search order is warranted if (a) there is an extremely strong prima facie case; (b) the damage the claimant would suffer would be very serious; (c) there is a real possibility the defendant would destroy relevant documents; and (d) the effect of the order is proportionate to its legitimate object (*Asian Corporate Services (SEA) Pte Ltd v Eastwest Management Ltd (Singapore Branch)* [2006] 1 SLR(R) 901 at [14], applied in *Arfat Pannir Selvam v Sharad Selvam Ramachandra* [2026] SGHC 56 at [34]). Where the order's object is to preserve evidence, the claimant must show it could not prove its case if the evidence were destroyed ([38]–[40]); the risk of destruction may be inferred from the defendant's conduct and propensity, without direct evidence ([41], [47]–[50]); and information found after the order may be considered. See also *Computerland Corp v Yew Seng Computers Pte Ltd* [1991] 2 SLR(R) 379 and *Bengawan Solo Pte Ltd v Season Confectionary Co Pte Ltd* [1994] 1 SLR(R) 448 (where goods are sold openly, there may be no real risk that evidence will be destroyed).

**A** Strong prima facie case: [test purchases show D selling goods bearing Client's registered mark, with identical packaging]. Serious damage: [lost sales and reputational harm from inferior copies]. Real risk of destruction: [D has deleted records before / former staff say D will clear the stock if warned]; contrast [goods sold openly in shops], which suggests no secret to hide (*Bengawan Solo*). Proportionality: limit the order to [the warehouse and the sales ledger], not D's whole business, and consider whether a preservation order (drp-w-0-61) or Mareva would do. Procedure: without notice to a Judge, Form 26, PD 73 affidavit with full disclosure, undertaking as to damages, and an independent supervising solicitor identified in the affidavit. **C** Therefore [Client] [is likely / unlikely] to obtain a search order, and should frame it narrowly with a supervising solicitor.

*Source note: O 13 r 1(8) ROC p118; PD 72(1), (5) SCPD p86; PD 73 SCPD p87–88; SYL p24 [151]; L3 s27; four requirements and Bengawan Solo point from Cyril-Exam ("Search orders") and Nic-Muggers 10.2.3 (secondary); Arfat [2026] SGHC 56 (Tan Siong Thye SJ) at [34]–[53], read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026): an application to set aside a search order, dismissed.*

### drp-w-0-61 — Detention and preservation of property; taking samples (O 13 rr 2–3)
*Keywords: detention of property, custody, preservation, inspection of property, O 13 r 2, enter immovable property, specific fund paid into court, O 13 r 3, samples, experiments, subject matter of the action, may give rise to issues, UMCI v Tokio Marine & Fire Insurance, Sang Cheol Woo v Charles Choi Spackman, CCTV footage, perishable goods*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can obtain an order to [preserve / inspect / sample] [the goods / CCTV recordings / the disputed fund] pending trial. **C** Yes. The Court may order the detention, custody, preservation or inspection of property that is the subject matter of, or may give rise to issues in, the action, and the taking of samples and experiments on it (O 13 rr 2–3).
**R** The Court may order the detention, custody or preservation of any property which is the subject matter of, or may give rise to issues in, an action, and the inspection of such property in a party's possession or control, authorising entry onto a party's land to do so (O 13 r 2(1)–(3)). Where there is a dispute over a party's right to a specific fund, the Court may order it paid into court or otherwise secured (O 13 r 2(4)). The Court may also order samples of such property to be taken and experiments or examinations to be conducted (O 13 r 3).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 13 r 2(1): the Court may order the detention, custody or preservation of any property which is the subject matter of or may give rise to issues in an action.
> - O 13 r 2(2): the Court may order the inspection of any such property in the possession or control of a party.
> - O 13 r 2(3): the Court may authorise any person to enter upon any immovable property in the possession or control of any party to effect an order under (1) or (2).
> - O 13 r 2(4): where there is a dispute as to the right of any party to a specific fund, the Court may order the fund to be paid into Court or otherwise secured.
> - O 13 r 3(1): if any property is the subject matter of or may give rise to issues in an action, the Court may order (a) any sample of the property to be taken; and (b) any experiment or examination to be conducted on the sample.
> - O 13 r 3(2): the Court may authorise entry onto a party's immovable property to effect the order.
> - Procedure: summons supported by affidavit (L3 s26); an interim relief matter for the SAPT (O 9 r 9(4)(l)) unless urgent.
> - Power: SCJA First Schedule para 5(a)–(b) (drp-w-0-2).

**P** Under the ROC 2014 predecessors: the sampling power covers physical property connected with the case, not a process or method of working (nor asking a person to reproduce handwriting), and it does not reach non-parties (*UMCI Ltd v Tokio Marine & Fire Insurance Co (Singapore) Pte Ltd* [2006] SGHC 142, reported [2006] 4 SLR(R) 95, at [20]–[44]); the detention and preservation power covers only property that is the subject-matter of the action or about which a question may arise, and not choses in action such as money in a bank account (*Sang Cheol Woo v Charles Choi Spackman* [2021] SGHC 42 at [102]–[109]).

**A** Identify the property and its link to the issues: [the allegedly non-food-grade containers held in D's warehouse] go to whether the goods met the contract, so [Client] may ask for their preservation and for samples to be tested by an agreed laboratory (O 13 r 3). [The restaurants' CCTV recordings] may be overwritten on a routine cycle, so seek an order preserving them pending production (O 13 r 2(1)), though if held by non-parties, consider production under O 11 rr 4, 11 (drp-w-0-48). [A disputed sale fund held by an agent] can be paid into court (O 13 r 2(4)). Apply by summons with affidavit; if destruction is imminent and secrecy matters, consider a search order instead (drp-w-0-60). **C** Therefore the Court is likely to order [preservation / inspection / sampling] of [the property] under O 13 r [2 / 3].

*Source note: O 13 rr 2–3 ROC p118; SCJA First Schedule para 5 SCJA p92; SYL p25 [152]; L3 s26; UMCI and Sang Cheol Woo SYL p25 [152], both read on eLitigation ([2006] SGHC 142; [2021] SGHC 42) (18 Nov 2026, extraction subagent); Conflict: SYL [152] says Sang Cheol Woo [102]–[109] concern the predecessor of O 13 r 1(1) (O 29 r 1(1)), but those paragraphs concern O 29 r 2 (detention and preservation, now O 13 r 2); O 29 r 1 is at [97]–[101]; followed the judgment; "physical property only" from Cyril-Exam ("Detention and preservation") (secondary); tested PYP21 Q5 (tapes, as context).*

## 0.XII Further arguments and appeals

- **Ask the Judge to reconsider before appealing?**
  - General Division decision after a hearing other than a trial: request further arguments before extraction or the 15th day, whichever is earlier; by letter/Request to the Registrar; deemed refused if no reply in 14 days; time to appeal runs from the outcome (SCJA s 29B; O 18 r 28; PD 112) → [0-62](#drp-w-0-62)
  - Not after a trial (*TG Master*); appellate courts: none after reserving or giving judgment (O 18 r 38); further evidence only for new arguments (*ARW*) → [0-62](#drp-w-0-62)
- **Which appeal rules: Order 18 or Order 19?**
  - Order 18: decisions on applications in an action; Order 19: judgments after trial, assessment, committal, prerogative orders (O 18 r 1; O 19 rr 1, 3) → [0-63](#drp-w-0-63)
  - Notice of appeal (Form 35): 14 days (O 18 r 27) or 28 days (O 19 r 25) → [0-63](#drp-w-0-63)
  - Time runs only after all matters, including costs, are decided; costs count as decided once entitlement is decided, or costs are ordered assessed or reserved, or no order is made; if costs are not decided within 30 days, time runs anyway (O 18 r 3; O 19 r 4) → [0-63](#drp-w-0-63)
  - An appeal does not stay enforcement unless ordered (O 18 r 6; O 19 r 6; SCJA ss 45(1), 60C) → [0-63](#drp-w-0-63), [0-70](#drp-w-0-70)
- **Appellate Division or Court of Appeal?**
  - Default: Appellate Division; Court of Appeal for Sixth Schedule cases (constitutional or administrative law, contempt, arbitration, corporate insolvency, patents, admiralty and shipping, SICC …) (SCJA s 29C; *Noor Azlin*, *Seow Fook Sen*) → [0-64](#drp-w-0-64)
  - Filed in the wrong court: transfer under SCJA ss 29D–29E; apply within 14 days of service of the notice of appeal (O 18 rr 40–41; O 19 rr 39–40) → [0-64](#drp-w-0-64)
- **Can I appeal at all; do I need permission?**
  - No appeal: Fourth Schedule (consent orders; orders on further and better particulars; giving permission to amend (with a limitation exception); refusing security for costs; interrogatories) (SCJA s 29) → [0-65](#drp-w-0-65)
  - Permission needed: $250,000 or less; Fifth Schedule para 3 interlocutory orders (unconditional permission to defend, refusing to strike out, giving security for costs, discovery, refusing a stay, residual interlocutory orders) (SCJA s 29A; *Telecom Credit*, *Zhu Su*) → [0-65](#drp-w-0-65)
  - Permission application: 14 days after the decision (O 18 r 29(1)) → [0-65](#drp-w-0-65)
- **Security and papers for the appeal**
  - Security with the notice of appeal (Form 36 certificate): $15,000 (O 18 r 30) or $20,000 (O 19 r 27) for appeals to the AD/CA; solicitor's undertaking (Form 37), deposit, or as agreed → [0-66](#drp-w-0-66)
- **Appeal from a District Court or Magistrate's Court?**
  - Lies to the General Division (SCJA s 20(b)); permission needed if $60,000 or less, or a Third Schedule order (permission to defend, setting aside default judgment); General Division's permission decision is final (s 21) → [0-84](#drp-w-0-84)
  - Applications: O 18 Div 3; judgments after trial: O 19 Div 2; Form 35 within 14 days; permission from the lower court first, then the General Division, 14 days each; security $3,000 (MC) or $5,000 (DC) (O 18 rr 17–20; O 19 rr 13–16) → [0-84](#drp-w-0-84)
  - Rehearing; further evidence only with permission on special grounds (s 22); Registrar to District Judge: 14 days (O 18 r 15) → [0-84](#drp-w-0-84)
  - Order 20 is for tribunal appeals and cases stated, never State Court appeals (O 20 r 1) → [0-84](#drp-w-0-84)
- **How do I apply for permission to appeal? (background only — not examinable per SYL p1)**
  - Application with written submissions within 14 days; 15-page submissions, 25-page bundle; no affidavit or oral argument; notice of appeal within 14 days of permission (O 18 r 29; O 19 r 26; PD 126) → [0-82](#drp-w-0-82)

### drp-w-0-62 — Further arguments (SCJA s 29B; O 18 r 28; PD 112)
*Keywords: further arguments, request for further arguments, SCJA s 29B, decision after a hearing other than a trial, before extraction, 15th day after the decision, O 18 r 28, letter to the Registrar, Request through eLitigation, PD 112, propose arguments briefly, authorities, 14 days, deemed no further arguments, affirm vary or set aside, time to appeal runs from, TG Master v Tung Kee Development, no further arguments after trial, O 18 r 38, O 19 r 34, appellate court, ARW v Comptroller of Income Tax, further evidence, new arguments, State Courts PD 81*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can ask the Judge to reconsider [the decision on its application] before appealing. **C** Yes. Because the decision was made after a hearing other than a trial, [Client] may request further arguments under s 29B of the SCJA, by letter to the Registrar (O 18 r 28), before the earlier of extraction of the order and the 15th day after the decision; the time to appeal then runs from the Judge's decision on the request.
**R** For a decision of a General Division Judge after any hearing other than a trial, the Judge may hear further arguments if a party or the Judge requests them before the earlier of extraction of the order and the 15th day after the decision; the Judge may then affirm, vary or set aside the decision, and no notice of appeal may be filed until then, with time for appealing running from that date (s 29B SCJA). The request is made by letter (a Request through the Electronic Filing Service) to the Registrar, served on all parties, setting out the proposed arguments briefly with copies of authorities; if the Registrar does not respond within 14 days, the Judge is deemed not to require further arguments (O 18 r 28; PD 112).

> **Rule in full**
> - SCJA s 29B(1): applies to a decision of a Judge in the original or appellate civil jurisdiction of the General Division after any hearing other than a trial of an action.
> - s 29B(2): before any notice of appeal is filed, the Judge may hear further arguments if any party or the Judge requests them before the earlier of (a) the time the judgment or order is extracted; (b) the 15th day after the date of the decision.
> - s 29B(3): after hearing further arguments, the Judge may affirm, vary or set aside the decision.
> - s 29B(4): once a request is made, (a) no notice of appeal may be filed until the Judge (i) affirms, varies or sets aside the decision, or (ii) certifies, or is deemed to certify, that no further arguments are required; and (b) time for the notice of appeal begins on that date.
> - s 29B(5): a party may, but need not, request further arguments before appealing.
> - O 18 r 28(1)–(5): the request is by letter to the Registrar, served on all parties, within the s 29B(2) time; it sets out the arguments briefly with copies of authorities; the Registrar informs the requesting party within 14 days whether the Judge requires further arguments; silence means the Judge does not.
> - PD 112(1): the Request is filed through the Electronic Filing Service, stating the party, the Judge, when the order was made, the provision relied on, the proposed arguments and authorities, with copies.
> - O 18 r 29(2): where permission to appeal is required, the request must also be made before an application for permission is filed.
> - O 18 r 38 (and O 19 r 34): unless the appellate court directs otherwise, no further arguments after it has heard the appeal and reserved judgment, or given its decision.
> - State Courts PD 2021 para 81: all requests for further arguments are made by a "Request for Further Arguments" filed through the Electronic Filing Service, which must (a) state the party making the request; (b) identify the Judge or Registrar who heard the matter; (c) specify when the order was made; (d) state the provision of law under which the request is made; (e) set out the proposed arguments briefly with authorities; and (f) include a copy of each authority; a copy goes to all parties and the Request is addressed to the Registrar.

**P** Further arguments are not available after a trial, as opposed to after a decision on an application (*TG Master Pte Ltd v Tung Kee Development (Singapore) Pte Ltd* [2023] SGHC 64 at [21]–[50]). Further evidence may be allowed in support of new arguments, but not to support or strengthen arguments already made (*ARW v Comptroller of Income Tax* [2019] 1 SLR 499 at [85]–[92], decided under ROC 2014).

**A** Check the kind of decision: [the Judge dismissed Client's summary judgment application in chambers], which is a decision after a hearing other than a trial, so s 29B applies; a judgment after trial is not. Check timing: the decision was on [date]; the request must go in before the order is extracted and by the 15th day, [date]. Content: a short letter/Request with the new point ([a case the Judge was not referred to, or an argument on the construction of cl x not made]) and copies of authorities; do not use it to re-argue the same points with new evidence (*ARW*). Consequence: [Client] cannot file its notice of appeal (or permission application) until the Judge decides or 14 days pass without a response; time then runs from that date. **C** Therefore [Client] may request further arguments by [date], and its time to appeal will run from the Judge's response.

*Source note: SCJA p40–41 (s 29B); O 18 r 28 ROC p173; O 18 r 29(2) ROC p174; O 18 r 38 ROC p185–186; PD 112 SCPD p137; SYL p26 [153]–[156]; L4 s4–s7; TG Master and ARW SYL p26 [154], [156]; State Courts PD 81 read on epd2021-statecourts.judiciary.gov.sg (last updated 15 Jul 2026); O 18 r 28 current text (SSO, as at 1 Apr 2026) differs only in citing "Supreme Court of Judicature Act 1969".*

### drp-w-0-63 — The appeal track (O 18 or O 19); when time runs; no automatic stay
*Keywords: Order 18, Order 19, appeals structure, applications in an action, judgments after trial, O 18 r 1, O 19 r 1, O 19 r 3 definition of judgment and trial, notice of appeal, Form 35, 14 days, O 18 r 27, 28 days, O 19 r 25, time for appeal starts to run, all matters including costs, costs to be assessed, costs reserved, 30 days, costs submissions, bifurcated trial, O 18 r 3, O 19 r 4, appeal not a stay of enforcement, O 18 r 6, O 19 r 6, Division 5, Division 3, extension of time*

**I** The issue is which rules govern [D]'s appeal against [the judgment / the order], and when time to appeal starts to run. **C** The appeal is governed by [Order 19 (Division 3), because it is against a judgment of the General Division after trial / Order 18 (Division 5), because it is against a decision on an application in the action]; time runs only once the Court has determined all matters including costs, and costs count as determined once the Court decides entitlement, orders costs assessed or reserved, or makes no order; if costs are not determined within 30 days, time runs anyway (O [19 r 4 / 18 r 3]).
**R** Order 18 applies to appeals from decisions on applications in an action, and Order 19 to appeals against judgments of the General Division (other than the Registrar's), where "judgment" means a judgment given in a trial, after assessment of damages or taking accounts, in a committal application or prerogative order application (O 18 r 1; O 19 rr 1, 3). For appeals to the Appellate Division or Court of Appeal, the notice of appeal (Form 35) is due within 14 days under Order 18 or 28 days under Order 19 (O 18 r 27; O 19 r 25). Time for appealing or seeking permission does not run until the lower Court has determined all matters, including costs; costs are deemed determined once the Court has decided entitlement (even if the amount is not fixed), ordered assessment, reserved costs, or made no order as to costs; and if costs are not determined within 30 days after all other matters, time starts to run when the 30 days expire, even if costs submissions were directed (O 18 r 3(1)–(2); O 19 r 4(1)–(2), as amended by S 37/2024 from 1 Feb 2024). An appeal does not stay enforcement unless the lower or appellate Court directs (O 18 r 6; O 19 r 6).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 18 r 1(1): Order 18 applies to every appeal from (i) a decision on an application in an action; (ii)–(iii) appeals on such decisions; (iv) a decision of the Registrar other than on an application, including on an assessment of damages, the taking of accounts or the hearing on the merits of an originating application; (iva) a decision of a District Judge or Magistrate at the hearing on the merits of an originating application; (v) an appellate Court's decision on a summons in an appeal; and to applications relating to such appeals ((iv)–(iva) as amended by S 206/2022).
> - O 18 r 1(2): an "application in an action" includes applications after commencement, consequential matters after judgment, and enforcement; it excludes applications taken out or heard on the day of, or during, the hearing on the merits, and matters within Order 19.
> - O 19 r 1(b): Order 19 applies to an appeal against any judgment of the General Division (except a judgment of the Registrar).
> - O 19 r 3: "judgment" means a judgment given by the lower Court (a) in a trial (including where judgment is given or the action dismissed at trial because parties are absent); (b) after damages are assessed or accounts taken; (c) in a committal application; (d) in a prerogative order application; or (e) on appeal against such a judgment; "trial" means the hearing on the merits of an originating claim or originating application, including applications on that day or until judgment.
> - O 18 r 26 / O 19 r 24: Order 18 Division 5 and Order 19 Division 3 govern appeals from the General Division to the Appellate Division or Court of Appeal.
> - O 18 r 27(1): notice of appeal in Form 35 within 14 days after the lower Court's decision (or after the s 29B(4)(b) date if further arguments were requested).
> - O 19 r 25(1): notice of appeal in Form 35 within 28 days after the judgment (or after the s 29B(4)(b) date).
> - O 18 r 27(2) / O 19 r 25(2): the appellate Court may extend time on application at any time; the lower Court only if asked before time expires.
> - O 19 r 4(1): subject to written law and (1A)–(2), unless the Court otherwise orders, time for an appeal or for a permission application does not start to run until after the lower Court has heard and determined all matters in the trial, including costs.
> - O 19 r 4(1A): where the lower Court does not determine costs within 30 days after determining all other matters, time starts to run after the 30 days expire, even if the Court directed costs submissions.
> - O 19 r 4(2)(a): the lower Court is deemed to have determined costs when it has (i) decided the parties' entitlement to costs, even if the amount has not been determined; (ii) ordered that costs be assessed; (iii) ordered that costs be reserved; or (iv) decided that there is no order as to costs or each party bears its own costs.
> - O 19 r 4(2)(b): in a bifurcated trial, time for appealing a distinct portion (including its costs) runs from its determination.
> - (r 4(1A)–(2) were substituted by S 37/2024 with effect from 1 Feb 2024; earlier, only a direction to assess costs was deemed a determination.)
> - O 18 r 3(1)–(2): the same for applications (including the 30-day rule and the deemed determinations), with special rules for a SAPT dealing with several matters (time runs when all matters are decided; Registrar and Judge matters run separately; further arguments postpone time for all matters).
> - O 18 r 6(1) / O 19 r 6(1): except so far as the lower Court or the appellate Court directs, an appeal does not operate as a stay of enforcement or of proceedings; (3) interest runs for the period of any delay.
> - SCJA ss 45(1), 60C(1): an appeal to the Appellate Division or Court of Appeal does not operate as a stay unless ordered.
> - O 20 r 1: Order 20 governs appeals from tribunals and cases stated to the General Division where written law provides for them, not appeals from a State Court. Appeals from the State Courts to the General Division: drp-w-0-84.

**P** The course sums up the divide as: Order 18 for applications, Order 19 for judgments (L4 s11; Cyril-Exam).

**A** Classify the decision: [judgment for C for US$5.5m after trial] is a "judgment" given in a trial, so Order 19 Division 3 applies (PYP22 Q6(b)); [the Judge's dismissal of P's summary judgment application] is a decision on an application in an action, so Order 18 Division 5 applies. Time: [the judgment was given on 1 November with costs "to be assessed"]; an order that costs be assessed is deemed a determination of costs, so time has already started to run from 1 November and the notice of appeal is due within 28 days (O 19 rr 4(2)(a)(ii), 25) (PYP22 Q6(c), PYP25 Q4.1); [D] need not wait for the assessment. If instead the Judge [called for costs submissions] and has not decided costs within 30 days, time starts to run when the 30 days end (O 19 r 4(1A)). Stay: filing an appeal does not stop enforcement; [D] must apply for a stay (drp-w-0-70) (PYP25 Q4.3). **C** Therefore the appeal falls under Order [19], the notice of appeal must be filed by [date], and it will not stay enforcement unless the Court so orders.

*Source note: O 18 rr 1, 3, 6, 26–27 ROC p153–157, p172–173; O 19 rr 1, 3, 4, 6, 24–25 ROC p189–192, p205–206; O 20 r 1 ROC p227; SCJA p52 (s 45(1)), p62 (s 60C(1)); SYL p26–27 [157]–[159(a)]; Digest 6A (judiciary.gov.sg, last updated 14 Dec 2021, read 18 Nov 2026: Order 18 for applications, Order 19 for judgments after trial; 14- and 28-day notices; permission within 14 days), consistent with the rules above; L4 s11–s15, s24–s25; tested PYP22 Q6(b)–(c), PYP25 Q4.1, Q4.3, PYP21 Q8.*

### drp-w-0-64 — Which appellate court: Appellate Division or Court of Appeal; transfer of a wrongly filed appeal
*Keywords: Appellate Division, Court of Appeal, allocation of appeals, SCJA s 29C, Sixth Schedule, constitutional or administrative law, contempt, arbitration, insolvency restructuring dissolution, patents, admiralty, shipping, SICC, case relating to, Noor Azlin binte Abdul Rahman v Changi General Hospital, Seow Fook Sen Aloysius v Rajah & Tann, reasonable relationship, wrong court, transfer, SCJA s 29D, s 29E, O 18 r 40, O 18 r 41, O 19 r 39, O 19 r 40, 14 days after service of notice of appeal, more appropriate, medical negligence, negligence judgment*

**I** The issue is to which court [D] must appeal against the General Division's [judgment / decision], and what happens if it filed in the wrong court. **C** The appeal lies to the [Appellate Division, because the case relates to [negligence / contract], which is not a Sixth Schedule subject / Court of Appeal, because it arises from a case relating to [arbitration / insolvency]] (s 29C SCJA); an appeal filed in the wrong court can be transferred, and [Client] may apply within 14 days after service of the notice of appeal.
**R** An appeal against a decision of the General Division in its civil jurisdiction is to be made to the Appellate Division, unless the Sixth Schedule or other written law provides for the Court of Appeal (s 29C SCJA). The Sixth Schedule sends to the Court of Appeal appeals arising from cases relating to constitutional or administrative law, contempt of court, arbitration, corporate insolvency, restructuring or dissolution, patents and admiralty or shipping law, appeals from the SICC, and appeals under specified statutes. The Court of Appeal may transfer an appeal from the Appellate Division to itself, or from itself to the Appellate Division, on its own motion or on application; an application on the ground that the appeal was filed in the wrong court must be made within 14 days after service of the notice of appeal (ss 29D–29E SCJA; O 18 rr 40–41; O 19 rr 39–40).

> **Rule in full**
> - SCJA s 29C(1): subject to (2), an appeal against a decision of the General Division in its original or appellate civil jurisdiction is to be made to the Appellate Division.
> - s 29C(2): it is to be made to the Court of Appeal if the Sixth Schedule or any other written law so provides; (3) s 29C creates no right of appeal.
> - Sixth Schedule para 1: appeals to the Court of Appeal where
>   - (a) the appeal arises from a case relating to constitutional or administrative law;
>   - (b) contempt of court;
>   - (c) the law of arbitration;
>   - (d) the insolvency, restructuring or dissolution of a corporation, LLP or VCC sub-fund;
>   - (e) the law of patents;
>   - (ea) admiralty law or shipping law;
>   - (f) a decision of the SICC;
>   - (g)–(h) election matters;
>   - (i)–(j) appeals under listed statutes (including s 10(4) International Arbitration Act, s 90(3) Patents Act) or any written law providing for appeal to the Court of Appeal;
>   - (k)–(l) decisions under the Mediation Act 2017 and the Singapore Convention on Mediation Act 2020;
>   - each "(even if the appeal does not raise any issue relating to" that area of law).
> - s 29D(1)–(2): the Court of Appeal may transfer to itself an appeal made to the Appellate Division, on its own motion, on a reference by the Appellate Division, or on a party's application (i) on the ground that the appeal was not made in accordance with s 29C, or (ii) on grounds in the Rules (O 18 r 40(1): it is more appropriate for the Court of Appeal to hear it).
> - O 18 r 40(3): an application on the wrong-court ground must be filed within 14 days after service of the notice of appeal; (4) on the "more appropriate" ground, within 14 days after written submissions are due; (5) factors for "more appropriate": national or public importance, point of law of public importance, complexity and novelty, a relevant Court of Appeal decision, conflicting decisions, significance of the result.
> - s 29E and O 18 r 41: the Court of Appeal may transfer to the Appellate Division an appeal made to it, on its own motion (where the appeal was not made under s 29C, no issue engages the Sixth Schedule, or all Sixth Schedule issues are settled law) or on a party's application on the wrong-court ground, within 14 days after service of the notice of appeal.
> - O 19 rr 39–40 contain the equivalent provisions for Order 19 appeals (ROC p222–224).

**P** The Sixth Schedule looks to the type of case from which the appeal arises, not merely the issues raised on appeal (*Noor Azlin binte Abdul Rahman v Changi General Hospital Pte Ltd* [2021] SGCA 59 at [22]–[38]); a case "relates to" a Sixth Schedule subject where there is a reasonable relationship between the case and that subject, through its legal issues or the application of law to its facts (*Seow Fook Sen Aloysius v Rajah & Tann Singapore LLP* [2022] SGCA 40 at [7]–[16]).

**A** Identify the subject of the case: [a negligence claim for $850,000 by BB Ltd against Mr G] (PYP23 Q4.1), [a medical negligence claim against Doctor T] (PYP25 Q4.2), or [a contract claim for prize money] (PYP21 Q8) is not within any Sixth Schedule category, so the appeal lies to the Appellate Division (s 29C(1)). Contrast [a challenge to an arbitral award] or [a winding-up dispute], which go to the Court of Appeal even if the appeal raises no arbitration or insolvency issue. If [D] filed in the wrong court (e.g. [to the Court of Appeal in a contract case]), [Client] may apply to the Court of Appeal to transfer the appeal to the Appellate Division within 14 days after service of the notice of appeal (s 29E(2)(b); O 18 r 41(2) / O 19 r 40), or the Court of Appeal may do so of its own motion; costs of the application should be sought against [D]. **C** Therefore the appeal should be made to the [Appellate Division], and a wrongly filed appeal can be transferred on an application made within 14 days of service of the notice of appeal.

*Source note: SCJA p41–42 (ss 29C–29E), p98–100 (Sixth Schedule); O 18 rr 40–41 ROC p187–189; SYL p27 [159(b)]; L4 s28–s30; Noor Azlin [2021] SGCA 59 at [33]–[35], [37] read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026): allocation follows the area of law of the case, not the individual issues raised; the case was a medical negligence suit allocated to the Appellate Division by default; Seow Fook Sen SYL p27 [159(b)], "reasonable relationship" from Cyril-Exam ("Appeals", Step 4) and Nic-Muggers 11.4 (secondary); O 19 rr 39–40 ROC p222–224 (mirror O 18 rr 40–41) and L4 s29–s30; tested PYP23 Q4.1, PYP25 Q4.2, PYP22 Q6(d), PYP21 Q8.*

### drp-w-0-65 — Is there a right of appeal; is permission needed (SCJA ss 29, 29A; Fourth and Fifth Schedules)
*Keywords: right of appeal, no appeal, SCJA s 29, Fourth Schedule, consent order, further and better particulars, permission to amend, refusing security for costs, interrogatories, permission to appeal, leave to appeal, SCJA s 29A, $250,000, Fifth Schedule, interlocutory orders, unconditional permission to defend, refusing to strike out, giving security for costs, discovery, refusing a stay, costs only, residual interlocutory application, paragraph 3(l), Telecom Credit v Midas United, Zhu Su v Three Arrows Capital, VietJet Aviation v FW Aviation, DRH v DRI, O 18 r 29, 14 days, appellate court grants permission*

**I** The issue is whether [D] may appeal against [the decision], and whether it needs permission. **C** [D] [cannot appeal, because the decision is [an order refusing security for costs] in para 1(j) of the Fourth Schedule (s 29 SCJA) / may appeal as of right, because the amount in dispute exceeds $250,000 and the decision is not in the Fifth Schedule / needs the appellate court's permission under s 29A and para [3(x)] of the Fifth Schedule], [applied for within 14 days (O 18 r 29(1))].
**R** No appeal lies against a decision of the General Division where written law makes it final, or in a case in the Fourth Schedule (s 29 SCJA). Permission is required where written law requires it, where the amount in dispute or value of the subject matter at the hearing (excluding interest and costs) does not exceed $250,000 (subject to Fifth Schedule exceptions), and in the cases in paras 3, 4(1) and 5(1) of the Fifth Schedule (s 29A(1)); permission is sought from the appellate court (s 29A(2); Fifth Schedule para 1), within 14 days of the decision for an Order 18 appeal (O 18 r 29(1)).

> **Rule in full**
> - SCJA s 29: an appeal cannot be brought against a decision of the General Division in its original or appellate civil jurisdiction (a) where written law makes it final or non-appealable; (b) in a case in the Fourth Schedule.
> - Fourth Schedule para 1 (no appeal):
>   - (b) a judgment or order made by consent of the parties;
>   - (h) an order giving or refusing further and better particulars;
>   - (i) an order giving permission to amend a pleading, except if (i) the application was made after the relevant limitation period expired and (ii) the amendment corrects a party's name, alters capacity, or adds or substitutes a new cause of action;
>   - (j) an order refusing security for costs;
>   - (k) an order giving or refusing interrogatories.
> - Fourth Schedule para 3: no appeal where the parties agreed in signed writing that the decision is final, unless fraud, illegality or a fundamental breach of natural justice is shown.
> - Fourth Schedule para 4 (added by S 505/2024 from 1 Jul 2024): where an action is, with all parties' consent, ordered to proceed under the expedited procedure in the Rules of Court (O 46A), no appeal lies against a Judge's decisions in it except listed ones (including orders on jurisdiction, summary judgment, refusing to set aside default judgment, striking out, post-limitation amendments, giving security for costs, stays, and judgment after trial); and no appeal lies against refusal of a consent request to use that procedure.
> - SCJA s 29A(1): permission is required (a) where written law requires it; (b) subject to Fifth Schedule exceptions, where the amount in dispute or value of the subject matter at the hearing (excluding interest and costs) does not exceed $250,000; (c) subject to exceptions, in the cases in Fifth Schedule paras 3, 4(1) and 5(1).
> - s 29A(2): permission from the court specified by written law, otherwise the court in the Fifth Schedule (the appellate court under s 29C: para 1).
> - Fifth Schedule para 2(2): no permission needed, despite s 29A(1)(b), for decisions in the General Division's original jurisdiction under a law requiring the case to be decided there, or Family Division original decisions.
> - Fifth Schedule para 3 (permission required), including:
>   - (a) an order giving unconditional permission to defend;
>   - (b) permission to defend on condition of payment into court or security, unless the appellant is that party;
>   - (c)–(d) orders setting aside a default judgment unconditionally, or conditionally unless the appellant is the conditioned party;
>   - (e) an order refusing to strike out an action, pleading or part;
>   - (f) where the only issue relates to costs or hearing fees;
>   - (h) an order refusing permission to amend (with the same limitation exception as above);
>   - (i) an order giving security for costs;
>   - (j) an order giving or refusing discovery or inspection of documents;
>   - (k) an order refusing a stay of proceedings;
>   - (l) an order at the hearing of any interlocutory application other than one for summary judgment, setting aside default judgment, striking out, dismissal, particulars, amendment, security for costs, discovery, interrogatories or a stay.
> - Fifth Schedule para 5: permission needed where the parties agreed the decision is final but the appellant alleges fraud, illegality or breach of natural justice.
> - O 18 r 29(1): where permission is required, apply to the appellate court and file and serve the application and supporting documents within 14 days after the lower Court's decision (subject to further arguments: r 29(2)–(3)).

**P** Permission is required under s 29A(1)(c) and para 3(l) of the Fifth Schedule only for an interlocutory order made at the hearing of an interlocutory application; whether an order is interlocutory is tested by asking whether the order, as made, finally disposes of the rights of the parties (*Zhu Su v Three Arrows Capital Ltd* [2024] SGCA 14 at [11]–[12], applying *Bozson v Altrincham UDC*). Both conditions must be met: a refusal to set aside a substituted service order is an interlocutory order on an interlocutory application, so permission is needed (*VietJet Aviation Joint Stock Co v FW Aviation (Holdings) 1 Ltd* [2026] SGHC(A) 11 at [20]–[21]); but an order on an interlocutory application that finally determines a party's rights is appealable as of right (*DRH v DRI* [2026] SGCA 26 at [18]–[24]). The scheme turns on the importance of the order to the substantive outcome: orders refusing a stay or giving unconditional permission to defend need permission because they send the case along the ordinary route to trial, whereas an order granting summary judgment or striking out the action is appealable as of right (*Purwadi v MBF Northern Securities Sdn Bhd* [2025] SGHC(A) 25 at [24]–[25], citing *Telecom Credit Inc v Midas United Group Ltd* [2019] 1 SLR 131 at [6], [18]–[19]).

**A** Step 1, any bar? [The trial Judge dismissed D1's application for security for costs] (PYP21 Q6): an order refusing security is in Fourth Schedule para 1(j), so no appeal lies at all, and the earlier advice that D1 could appeal with permission was wrong. Step 2, permission? [A judgment after trial for $850,000 / US$5.5m]: the amount exceeds $250,000 and no Fifth Schedule head applies, so [D] may appeal as of right (PYP23 Q4.2, PYP22 Q6(a)). [A General Division Judge dismissed P's $350,000 summary judgment application] (PYP25 Q3.2): the amount exceeds $250,000; para 3(l) excludes summary judgment applications from the residual head; but a dismissal, like unconditional permission to defend, sends the case on to trial, and an order giving unconditional permission to defend needs permission under para 3(a); *Purwadi v MBF Northern Securities Sdn Bhd* [2025] SGHC(A) 25 at [24] gives the reason, though it discusses the predecessor provision (para (e) of the Fifth Schedule to the 2007 SCJA); so the safer course is to apply for permission within 14 days (open point, no authority: O 9 r 17(7)(a)–(b) lists dismissal separately from unconditional permission to defend, and para 3(a) names only the latter, so on the text a dismissal over $250,000 is appealable as of right; but *Purwadi* at [24] explains that orders sending the matter "along the ordinary route to trial" need permission, which points the other way; SCJA Fifth Schedule and ROC text checked 9 Nov 2026, and no case deciding it was found, *Lee Wee Ching v Wang Piao* [2024] SGHC(A) 9 being about an appeal from a grant of summary judgment). [An order giving security for costs] needs permission (para 3(i)). Step 3, if permission is needed, apply to the appellate court (the court under s 29C) within 14 days. **C** Therefore [D] [has no right of appeal / may appeal without permission / needs permission from the [Appellate Division], applied for within 14 days].

*Source note: SCJA p39–40 (ss 29, 29A), p97–98 (Fourth and Fifth Schedules), checked against the current SCJA on SSO (as at 1 Jul 2024: only Fourth Schedule para 4 has been added since the library copy); O 18 r 29 ROC p173–177; SYL p27 [159(c)(i)–(ii)]; L4 s14; Telecom Credit, Zhu Su, VietJet and DRH v DRI SYL p27 [159(c)(ii)]; Zhu Su [2024] SGCA 14 at [11]–[12], VietJet [2026] SGHC(A) 11 at [20]–[21] and DRH v DRI [2026] SGCA 26 at [18]–[24] read on eLitigation via an extraction subagent, and Purwadi [2025] SGHC(A) 25 at [23]–[28] read directly (8 Nov 2026); Conflict: Cyril-Exam ("Appeals", Steps 1–2) and Nic-Muggers 11.1 state the amendment exception in Fourth Schedule para 1(i) and Fifth Schedule para 3(h) as "limitation expired OR name/capacity/new cause of action"; the SCJA text requires both ("and"), which the pack follows; tested PYP21 Q6, PYP22 Q6(a), PYP23 Q4.2, PYP25 Q3.2.*

### drp-w-0-66 — Security for the costs of an appeal; the appeal papers
*Keywords: security for costs of appeal, O 18 r 30, $15,000, O 19 r 27, $20,000, certificate for security for costs, Form 36, at the time of filing the notice of appeal, solicitor's undertaking, Form 37, deposit with the Registry, Accountant-General, one set per appeal, each respondent, vary or waive, further security, record of appeal, appellant's case, core bundle, page limits, State Courts appeals $3,000 $5,000*

**I** The issue is whether [D] must provide security for [Client]'s costs to appeal, and how much. **C** Yes. [D] must provide security of [$20,000 (Order 19 appeal against a judgment) / $15,000 (Order 18 appeal against a decision on an application)] for each respondent, and file a certificate for security for costs in Form 36 when filing the notice of appeal (O [19 r 27 / 18 r 30]).
**R** On an appeal to the Appellate Division or Court of Appeal, the appellant must provide security for the respondent's costs of the appeal and file a Form 36 certificate when it files the notice of appeal; the security is a solicitor's undertaking in Form 37, a deposit with the Registry or Accountant-General, or another agreed form; the amount is $15,000 under Order 18 and $20,000 under Order 19, per respondent or set of respondents represented by the same firm; any party may apply to vary or waive it, and the appellate Court may order more (O 18 r 30; O 19 r 27).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 19 r 27(1): the appellant must provide security for the respondent's costs of the appeal and file a certificate for security for costs in Form 36 at the time the appellant files the notice of appeal.
> - O 19 r 27(2): several appellants in one appeal need provide only one set of security.
> - O 19 r 27(3): where there is more than one respondent, security for each respondent (or each set represented by the same firm).
> - O 19 r 27(4): the security must be (a) a solicitor's undertaking in Form 37, filed and served on the respondent; (b) deposited in the Registry or with the Accountant-General; or (c) in any other form acceptable to the parties.
> - O 19 r 27(5): the amount is $20,000.
> - O 19 r 27(6)–(7): any party may apply to vary or waive the amount; the appellate Court may order further security.
> - O 18 r 30(1)–(7): the same for Order 18 Division 5 appeals, with the amount $15,000.
> - State Courts appeals to the General Division: $3,000 (Magistrate's Court action) or $5,000 (District Court action) (O 18 r 20(5) for decisions; O 19 r 16(5) for judgments).
> - Order 19 papers (L4 s26): record of appeal, appellant's case (55 pages), core bundle, bundle of authorities, within 8 weeks after the record of proceedings is available; respondent's case within 28 days of service; reply within 14 days. Order 18 appeals: 35-page written submissions and bundles (L4 s24).

**P** Neither the syllabus nor the lectures cite a case; the amounts are fixed by the Rules (L4 s24–s25).

**A** Classify the appeal (drp-w-0-63). [D's appeal against the trial judgment for US$5.5m] is an Order 19 appeal, so [D] must provide $20,000 security for C's costs, by a Form 37 solicitor's undertaking or a deposit, and file the Form 36 certificate with the Form 35 notice of appeal within 28 days of the judgment (PYP22 Q6(e)). If there are two respondents separately represented, it is $20,000 for each. An appeal against an interlocutory decision under Order 18 requires $15,000. If [D] considers the amount disproportionate, it may apply to vary or waive it; [Client] may ask for further security if its costs of the appeal will be much higher. **C** Therefore [D] must provide security of [$20,000] and file a Form 36 certificate when it files its notice of appeal.

*Source note: O 18 r 30 ROC p177–178; O 19 r 27 ROC p209–210; L4 s17, s24–s26; not a syllabus paragraph (the syllabus focuses on allocation and permission, SYL p26 note to [157]); added because PYP22 Q6(e) tested it; PYP22-Xav Q6(e) gives the same $20,000 and Form 36; State Courts amounts O 18 r 20(5), O 19 r 16(5) ROC and L4 s17; tested PYP22 Q6(e).*

### drp-w-0-84 — Appeals from the State Courts to the General Division (SCJA ss 20–22; O 18 Div 3; O 19 Div 2); the appeals map (O 18 r 1, O 19 r 1, O 20 r 1)
*Keywords: appellate civil jurisdiction of the General Division, SCJA s 20, s 21, s 22, appeal from District Court, appeal from Magistrate's Court, $60,000, permission to appeal, Third Schedule, permission to defend, setting aside default judgment, permission decision final, s 21(2A), rehearing, further evidence special grounds, s 22, O 18 r 15, Registrar to District Judge, O 18 r 17, O 18 r 18, O 18 r 19, O 18 r 20, $3,000, $5,000, O 19 r 13, O 19 r 14, O 19 r 15, O 19 r 16, Form 35, Form 36, Form 37, 14 days, Order 20, tribunal appeals, case stated, O 20 r 1, Digest 6A, Digest 6B, appeals structure*

**I** The issue is whether, and how, [Client] can appeal to the General Division against [the District Judge's / Magistrate's] [decision on the application / judgment after trial]. **C** An appeal lies to the General Division (s 20(b) SCJA), [as of right / only with permission, because the amount in dispute does not exceed $60,000 / because the order is in the Third Schedule] (s 21(1)), and [Client] must file and serve a notice of appeal in Form 35 within 14 days after the decision (O [18 r 17(1) / 19 r 14(1)]), with security of [$3,000 / $5,000] (O [18 r 20 / 19 r 16]).
**R** The General Division's appellate civil jurisdiction includes appeals from District Courts and Magistrates' Courts (s 20(b) SCJA). An appeal lies only with the permission of the lower court or the General Division where the amount in dispute or value of the subject matter (excluding interest and costs) does not exceed $60,000, or the order is one in the Third Schedule; the General Division's decision on permission is final (s 21(1), (2A)). The appeal is a rehearing; the General Division has the lower court's powers, and may admit further evidence only with permission and on special grounds, unless it concerns matters after the decision (s 22). Decisions on applications (and on the merits of an originating application) go up under Order 18 Division 3, and judgments after the trial of an originating claim, assessment or committal under Order 19 Division 2; in each, the notice of appeal (Form 35) is due within 14 days, permission is sought first from the lower court within 14 days, and security (Form 36) is $3,000 (Magistrate's Court) or $5,000 (District Court) (O 18 rr 17, 19, 20; O 19 rr 13–16). Order 20 governs appeals from tribunals and cases stated, not appeals from a State Court (O 20 r 1).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 20 SCJA: the appellate civil jurisdiction of the General Division consists of hearing appeals (a) from Family Courts in quasi-criminal or civil matters; (b) from District Courts and Magistrates' Courts exercising quasi-criminal or civil jurisdiction; (c) from other tribunals as written law prescribes.
> - s 21(1): subject to the Act and other written law, an appeal lies to the General Division from a decision of a District Court or Magistrate's Court only with the permission of that court or the General Division in
>   - (a) any case where the amount in dispute, or the value of the subject matter, at the hearing below (excluding interest and costs) does not exceed $60,000 (or other amount specified by order under s 21(3));
>   - (b) any case specified in the Third Schedule.
> - s 21(2A): an order of the General Division giving or refusing permission under s 21(1) is final.
