> - Third Schedule (permission needed): a District Court or Magistrate's Court order (a) giving unconditional permission to defend; (b) giving conditional permission to defend on payment into court or security, except if the appellant is that party; (c) setting aside a default judgment unconditionally; (d) setting aside a default judgment on condition of payment into court or security, except if the appellant is that party.
> - s 22(1)–(2): appeals are by way of rehearing; the General Division has all the powers and duties of the lower court.
> - s 22(3)–(5): further evidence (oral, affidavit, deposition or otherwise) only with the General Division's permission and on special grounds, except evidence of matters after the decision.
> - s 22(6)–(7): the General Division may draw any inference of fact, give any judgment and make any order, on any part of the decision and in favour of any party, including one who has not appealed.
> - O 18 r 15: Registrar of the State Courts to a District Judge: notice of appeal in Form 35 within 14 days after the Registrar's decision (Division 2).
> - O 18 r 17(1)–(2) (Division 3): appeal to the General Division against a District Judge's or Magistrate's decision on an application at first instance or on the merits of an originating application (words added by S 206/2022), or a District Judge's decision on appeal: Form 35 within 14 days after the decision, or within 14 days after the outcome of a request for further arguments under r 18; the General Division may extend time at any time, the lower court only if asked before time expires.
> - O 18 r 18: further arguments by letter to the Registrar of the State Courts before extraction or the 15th day after the decision, whichever is earlier; no reply within 14 days means no further arguments are required.
> - O 18 r 19 / O 19 r 15: where permission is required, apply to the District Judge or Magistrate (lower court) within 14 days after the decision; if refused, to the General Division within 14 days after the refusal; if granted, Form 35 within 14 days after the grant.
> - O 18 r 20 / O 19 r 16: security with the notice of appeal (Form 36 certificate): $3,000 for an appeal in a Magistrate's Court action; $5,000 in a District Court action; by solicitor's undertaking (Form 37), deposit, or as the parties accept; the appellate Court may vary or waive (and under O 19 r 16(7), order further) security.
> - O 19 r 13 (Division 2): applies to appeals to the General Division against a District Court or Magistrate's Court judgment given (a) in a trial of an originating claim (including where a party is absent; "of an originating claim" added by S 206/2022); (b) after damages are assessed or accounts taken by a District Judge; (c) in a committal application. O 19 r 14(1): Form 35 within 14 days after the judgment.
> - O 18 r 1(1): Order 18 covers appeals from decisions on applications in an action (and further appeals on them), Registrar's decisions on assessments of damages, accounts and OA merits hearings, District Judge's or Magistrate's decisions on OA merits hearings, and appellate summonses (current text, see drp-w-0-63).
> - O 19 r 1: Order 19 covers appeals against (a) District Court or Magistrate's Court judgments in a trial, after assessment or accounts by a District Judge, or in committal; (b) General Division judgments (not the Registrar's); (c) Appellate Division judgments; (d) tribunal appeals to the Appellate Division or Court of Appeal under written law; (f) Medical Registration Act Disciplinary Tribunal decisions.
> - O 20 r 1(1)–(2): Order 20 applies, where written law provides for it, to (a) every appeal against a tribunal's decision (except under O 19 r 41) and (b) every application for, or by way of, a case stated to the General Division; it does not apply to an appeal from a State Court.

**P** The Courts' overview of the appeal routes within and from the State Courts (Digest 6B, 14 Dec 2021, paras 2, 19–25, 43–50) and in the Supreme Court (Digest 6A) matches the rules above. On the further-arguments step, see drp-w-0-62; on appeals from the General Division, drp-w-0-63 to drp-w-0-66.

**A** First, classify the decision: [the District Judge's order striking out the defence] is a decision on an application, so Order 18 Division 3 applies; [the District Court's judgment after trial] falls under Order 19 Division 2. Second, check permission: the amount in dispute below was [$45,000], which does not exceed $60,000, so permission is needed (s 21(1)(a)); or the order [gave the defendant unconditional permission to defend] is in the Third Schedule. Apply to the District Judge within 14 days, and if refused, to the General Division within 14 days (O 18 r 19; O 19 r 15); its decision is final (s 21(2A)). Third, file the Form 35 notice within 14 days of the decision (or of the grant of permission), with security of $5,000 for a District Court action. Fourth, the appeal is a rehearing, so new evidence needs permission and special grounds (s 22(4)). **C** Therefore [Client] [may appeal as of right / needs permission] and must file the notice of appeal by [date] under O [18 r 17 / 19 r 14], with security under O [18 r 20 / 19 r 16].

*Source note: SCJA p33–35 (ss 20–22), p96–97 (Third Schedule), library copy in force from 1 Dec 2023, ss 20–22 checked against SSO (current as at 18 Nov 2026): unchanged; O 18 rr 1, 15, 17–20 ROC p153, p161–165; O 19 rr 1, 13–16 ROC p189, p196–198; O 20 r 1 ROC p227; ROC rules checked against SSO (current as at 18 Nov 2026): O 18 r 17(1) and O 19 r 13(1)(a) amended by S 206/2022 (quoted as amended), the rest unchanged, including the $3,000 / $5,000 security; SYL p1 [3(b)], p26–27 [157]; Digest 6A and Digest 6B (judiciary.gov.sg, last updated 14 Dec 2021, read 18 Nov 2026).*

### drp-w-0-82 — Procedure for seeking permission to appeal (O 18 r 29; O 19 r 26; PD 126) (background only — not examinable per SYL p1)
*Keywords: permission to appeal procedure, leave to appeal, O 18 r 29, O 19 r 26, 14 days after decision, written submissions with application, 15 pages, bundle of documents 25 pages, reply submissions 14 days, no affidavit without permission, no oral arguments, notice of appeal within 14 days of permission, Form 35, further arguments, extension of time, PD 126, Form B24, Form B25, background only*

**I** The issue is how [Client] should apply for permission to appeal against [the Judge's decision of [date]]. **C** [Client] must file and serve its application for permission, with written submissions, on all interested parties within 14 days after the decision (O 18 r 29(1) / O 19 r 26(1)), in the form required by PD 126.
**R** Where permission to appeal is required, the party applies to the appellate Court and files and serves the application and its written submissions (with any bundle of authorities, and optionally a bundle of documents from below) within 14 days after the lower Court's decision; if further arguments were requested, within 14 days after the Judge decides them (O 18 r 29(1)–(3), (7)). The respondent replies within 14 days of service (r 29(8)). Submissions are limited to 15 pages and the bundle of documents to 25 pages; no affidavit is filed without permission and there are no oral arguments unless ordered (r 29(10)–(16)). If permission is granted, the notice of appeal in Form 35 must be filed and served within 14 days (r 29(17)). Order 19 r 26 is in the same terms for appeals under Order 19.

> **Rule in full**
> - O 18 r 29(1) (= O 19 r 26(1)): where permission is required, apply to the appellate Court and file and serve the application and the r 29(7) documents on all parties interested in the appeal within 14 days after the lower Court's decision.
> - r 29(2)–(4): further arguments may be requested under SCJA s 29B before the earliest of extraction, the 15th day after the decision, or the filing of a permission application; if requested, the permission application waits for the Judge's decision on them and is filed within 14 days after it.
> - r 29(5): permission to appeal from the Appellate Division to the Court of Appeal (SCJA s 47(1)) needs a point of law of public importance and that it is appropriate for the Court of Appeal to hear it.
> - r 29(6): if the appellate court decides the other appellate court is the right one, apply there within 14 days of the Registrar's notification.
> - r 29(7): the applicant (a) must file written submissions and any bundle of authorities with the application; (b) may file a bundle of relevant documents from the proceedings below.
> - r 29(8): the opposing party files and serves written submissions (and optionally a bundle) within 14 days after service of the application papers.
> - r 29(10)–(14): submissions 15 pages and bundle of documents 25 pages, unless the Court allows more in special circumstances on payment of fees; one set of submissions per party or firm.
> - r 29(15)–(16): no affidavit without the appellate Court's permission; no oral arguments unless it orders otherwise.
> - r 29(17): if permission is granted, file and serve the notice of appeal in Form 35 within 14 days after the decision granting permission. r 29(18): the appellate Court may extend time.
> - PD 126(1)–(4): the application is in Form 24 or 25 of Appendix B (now B24, B25) (Forms 26–27 for SCJA s 47(1) applications to the Court of Appeal); formatting per PD 120; if no submissions are filed in time, the Court may decide on the papers.

**P** No case is set in the syllabus for the procedure; whether permission is needed is in drp-w-0-65 (*Telecom Credit*, *Zhu Su*).

**A** Count 14 days from the decision (O 3 r 3; drp-w-0-4), or from the Judge's decision on further arguments if [Client] asked for them (drp-w-0-62). File the application in Form B24 or B25 with submissions of no more than 15 pages and a bundle of no more than 25 pages; do not file an affidavit. If out of time, apply to the appellate Court for an extension (r 29(18)). On permission being granted, file the Form 35 notice of appeal within 14 days and provide security (drp-w-0-66). **C** Therefore [Client] must apply for permission by [date] under O 18 r 29 [O 19 r 26], following PD 126.

*Source note: O 18 r 29 ROC p173–175; O 19 r 26 ROC p206–208; PD 126 SCPD p166–167; SYL p27–28 [159(c)(iii)]. SYL [159(c)(iii)(B)] prints "Order 19 Rule 26(1)–29(4)"; read as r 26(1)–(4). O 18 r 29 and O 19 r 26 checked against SSO (current version as at 18 Nov 2026; extraction subagent): unchanged since 2021; PD text from the library copy.*

## 0.XIII Enforcement of court orders

- **Before and during the case**
  - Watch the defendant's ability to pay, so judgment is not a "paper judgment" (SYL [160]) → [0-67](#drp-w-0-67)
- **Judgment unpaid: how do I enforce it?**
  - One enforcement order for one or more modes: seizure and sale (land, goods, shares), delivery or possession, attachment of a debt (including bank deposits) (O 22 r 2(1)–(2)) → [0-67](#drp-w-0-67)
  - Summons without notice, Form 38, not earlier than 3 days after the order is served; affidavit (r 2(4)) with solicitors' undertaking to the Sheriff (O 22 r 2(3)–(4)) → [0-67](#drp-w-0-67)
  - Valid 12 months, extendable before expiry (O 22 r 2(5)–(8)); Sheriff serves notices (Form 40); bank must report within 14 days and hold for 21 days (O 22 r 6) → [0-67](#drp-w-0-67)
  - Joint accounts (*One Investment*, *Timing*); objections within 14 days of the notice (O 22 r 10) → [0-67](#drp-w-0-67)
- **Don't know what assets the debtor has**
  - Examination of enforcement respondent, orally or by affidavit, with documents; officers of an entity; Form 41 with PD 145 questionnaire, personal service; counts as enforcement for a stay (O 22 r 11; *Burgundy*, *PT Bakrie*) → [0-68](#drp-w-0-68)
- **Debtor is moving assets after judgment**
  - Mareva in aid of execution (*JTrust*) → [0-69](#drp-w-0-69)
- **Debtor wants to stop enforcement**
  - Appeal is not a stay (O 18 r 6; O 19 r 6); apply for a stay: special case (O 22 r 13(1)) or pending appeal (SCJA ss 45(1), 60C; *Lee Kuan Yew v Jeyaretnam*, *Axis Megalink*) → [0-70](#drp-w-0-70)

### drp-w-0-67 — The enforcement order: modes, procedure, validity, Sheriff, objections (O 22 rr 1–2, 6, 10)
*Keywords: enforcement order, O 22 r 2, single enforcement order, seizure and sale, writ of seizure and sale, delivery or possession, attachment of debt, garnishee order, bank account, deposit of money, financial institution, Form 38, summons without notice, not earlier than 3 days after service of the court order, supporting affidavit, O 22 r 2(4), solicitors' undertaking to indemnify the Sheriff, valid 12 months, extension, Sheriff, notice of seizure, notice of attachment, Form 40, non-party bank, 14 days to inform Sheriff, 21 days, O 22 r 6, objection, notice of objection, 14 days, O 22 r 10, joint account, One Investment and Consultancy v Cham Poh Meng, Timing Ltd v Tay Toh Hin, condominium, Singapore Land Authority, paper judgment*

**I** The issue is how [Client] can enforce its judgment for [$X] against [D]'s [bank account / condominium / warehouse goods]. **C** [Client] may apply by summons without notice in Form 38 for an enforcement order under O 22 r 2, not earlier than 3 days after serving the judgment on [D], seeking [attachment of the debt represented by D's deposit with DBS and seizure and sale of D's condominium]; [D] may object within 14 days after service of the notice of seizure or attachment (O 22 r 10).
**R** An enforcement applicant may apply for an enforcement order to enforce one or more Court orders (O 22 r 2(1)); the order authorises the Sheriff to seize and sell property, to seize and deliver or give possession of property, to attach a debt due to the enforcement respondent from a non-party (including a bank deposit), or to do anything in the Court order (O 22 r 2(2)). The application is by summons without notice in Form 38, not earlier than 3 days after the Court order is served, supported by an affidavit stating the matters in O 22 r 2(4), including the solicitors' undertaking to indemnify and pay the Sheriff (O 22 r 2(3)–(4)). The order is valid for 12 months and may be extended if an application is made before expiry (O 22 r 2(5)–(8)). An enforcement respondent or other person may object to a seizure or attachment within 14 days after service of the notice (O 22 r 10).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 22 r 1: "Court order" includes any judgment or order for payment of money (including costs), delivery of movable property, title to or possession of immovable property, doing or restraining any act, or declaring rights; "movable property" includes cash, debts, deposits, shares, club memberships and cryptocurrency.
> - O 22 r 2(1): an enforcement applicant may apply for an enforcement order to enforce one or more Court orders, without affecting other methods under written law.
> - O 22 r 2(2): subject to written law, an enforcement order authorises the Sheriff to do one or more of the following:
>   - (a) seizure and sale: seize and sell all property belonging to the enforcement respondent;
>   - (b) delivery or possession: seize and deliver or give possession of property in the respondent's possession or control;
>   - (c) attachment of a debt: attach a debt due to the respondent from any non-party, now or in future, including a deposit with a financial institution, whether or not matured and despite any withdrawal restriction;
>   - (d) anything specified in the Court order.
> - O 22 r 2(3): apply by summons without notice for an enforcement order in Form 38 not earlier than 3 days after the Court order has been served on the enforcement respondent.
> - O 22 r 2(4): the supporting affidavit must state (a) the terms of the order; (b) the respondent's name and address; (c) the date of service; (d) the unpaid terms; (e) reasons for delay if over 6 years; (f) any change of identity; (g) the amount due, recovered, paid and owing; (h) daily interest; (i) description of property to be seized and evidence it belongs to or is held by the respondent; (j) notice to third parties in possession; (k) description of any deposit or debt to be attached and evidence it belongs or is due to the respondent; (l) any sequence of enforcement; (m) the solicitors' undertaking to (i) indemnify the Sheriff, (ii) pay the Sheriff's charges and fees, (iii) deposit money as requested, with a written undertaking exhibited.
> - O 22 r 2(5)–(8): valid for 12 months from issue; extendable for 12 months at a time on application before expiry; priority dates from original issue; ceases on written notice to the Sheriff or an order recording satisfaction.
> - O 22 r 6: the Sheriff carries out the order in the sequence indicated; seizure of land by notice to the SLA and registration within 14 days; attachment of a bank deposit by serving a notice of attachment (Form 40) on the bank; copy to the respondent within 7 days; the non-party must within 14 days tell the Sheriff the amount available and must not deal with it for 21 days (or until an objection is decided), then pay over within 7 days; it may claim $100 costs.
> - O 22 r 10: an objector must within 14 days after service of the notice of seizure or attachment file a notice of objection with the Sheriff, serve it and state its grounds and evidence; if the applicant does not consent within 14 days, the Sheriff may direct the objector to apply to Court within 7 days.
> - PD 140 (requests for the Sheriff's attendance), PD 141 (service of the notice of seizure or attachment on the Sheriff's directions), PD 142 (claims and objections to seizure or attachment) (SCPD p180–181; contents not summarised here).

**P** An order for attachment of a debt (formerly a garnishee order) was refused in respect of a joint account (*One Investment and Consultancy Ltd v Cham Poh Meng* [2016] 5 SLR 923), but *Timing Ltd v Tay Toh Hin* [2020] 5 SLR 974 at [16]–[33] declined to follow it and allowed attachment where there was strong prima facie evidence that all the money in the joint account belonged to the judgment debtor; both were decided under ROC 2014.

**A** Before trial, keep checking whether [D] can pay, to avoid a paper judgment. After judgment: serve the judgment on [D]; wait at least 3 days; file the Form 38 summons without notice with an O 22 r 2(4) affidavit identifying [D's DBS account (evidence: the tranche paid into it)], [D's condominium (SLA title search)], and [the Kallang warehouse equipment (evidence that D owns it)], stating the amount due and daily interest, and exhibiting the solicitors' undertaking to the Sheriff. Ask for the modes in a sequence ([attach the deposits first, then seize and sell the condominium]). For land, register the order within 14 days after the notice of seizure. If [the account is joint with D's spouse], show strong prima facie evidence that the money is all [D]'s (*Timing*). If [the DBS account may be closed] (PYP21 Q9), add other assets or first examine [D] on his assets (drp-w-0-68). [D] can object within 14 days after service of the notice (O 22 r 10), e.g. [that the funds belong to a third party]. **C** Therefore [Client] should apply for an enforcement order in Form 38 for [attachment and seizure and sale], not earlier than 3 days after serving the judgment, and [D] has 14 days after service of the notices to object.

*Source note: O 22 rr 1–2 ROC p252–256; O 22 r 6 ROC p257–260; O 22 r 10 ROC p263–264; SYL p28 [160]–[162]; L5 s5–s6; One Investment and Timing SYL p28 [162(e)]; Conflict: L5 s6 cites "O 22 r 9" for the non-party's duty to inform the Sheriff within 14 days and not to deal with the attached amount; in the ROC those duties are in O 22 r 6(9)–(10) and r 9 concerns the Sheriff's accounts and commission. The pack follows the ROC; tested PYP21 Q9, PYP22 Q7, PYP23 Q4.4.*

### drp-w-0-68 — Examination of the enforcement respondent (O 22 r 11; PD 145)
*Keywords: examination of enforcement respondent, EER, examination of judgment debtor, EJD, O 22 r 11, orally examined in court, affidavit of assets, produce documents, officer of entity, director, Form 41, questionnaire, PD 145, personal service, summons without notice, deemed enforcement, within terms of stay, O 22 r 11(4), PT Bakrie Investindo v Global Distressed Alpha Fund, Burgundy Global Exploration v Transocean, serve officer abroad, full and frank disclosure, Vasiliy Golovnin*

**I** The issue is how [Client] can find out what assets [D] has, beyond [its DBS account]. **C** [Client] may apply under O 22 r 11 for [D] to be examined orally in court and/or to make an affidavit about its assets, and to produce documents; the order must be in Form 41 with the PD 145 questionnaire and served personally on [D] [and on the officer of D to be examined].
**R** An enforcement applicant may apply for the enforcement respondent to be examined orally in court, or to make an affidavit, or both, on the properties it owns beneficially in whole or in part or will be entitled to in future, and the Court may order production of documents (O 22 r 11(1)–(2)). Where the respondent is an entity, the order must name the officer(s) to be examined (O 22 r 11(3)). The order must be in Form 41 and served personally on the respondent and any officer ordered to attend (O 22 r 11(5)), with the PD 145 questionnaire annexed. An application under this rule is deemed to be enforcement of the Court order and so falls within any stay of enforcement (O 22 r 11(4)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 22 r 11(1): the enforcement applicant may apply for the enforcement respondent to be examined orally in court or to make an affidavit or both on the properties which are owned by the enforcement respondent beneficially whether in whole or in part or which it will be entitled to in the future.
> - O 22 r 11(2): the Court may also order the enforcement respondent to produce appropriate documents.
> - O 22 r 11(3): where the enforcement respondent is an entity, the order must state the appointment of the officer or officers to be examined.
> - O 22 r 11(4): an application under this Rule is deemed to be enforcement of a Court order and is within the terms of any written law or any order staying enforcement of that Court order.
> - O 22 r 11(5): the order must be in Form 41 and must be served personally on the enforcement respondent and, for an entity, on any officer ordered to attend.
> - Application: summons without notice supported by affidavit (O 22 r 2(3)–(4); L5 s3), with full and frank disclosure.
> - PD 145(1): a questionnaire in Form B41 or B42 of Appendix B must be annexed to the order when served; the applicant may modify the questions; (2) a respondent who thinks a question unreasonable should raise it with the applicant before the hearing.
> - PD 145(4) (current version): the enforcement respondent need not attend the hearing if (a) it gives its answers to the questionnaire to the enforcement applicant by affidavit or statutory declaration before the hearing; and (b) the applicant agrees to apply for a discharge of the order at the hearing.

**P** The Court's permission is needed to serve an examination order abroad on a foreign officer of the enforcement respondent (*Burgundy Global Exploration Corp v Transocean Offshore International Ventures Ltd* [2014] 3 SLR 381 at [110]–[115]). Under ROC 2021 an examination is deemed enforcement and is caught by a stay (O 22 r 11(4)), unlike the previous position that it was merely an information-gathering tool not held in abeyance by a stay of execution pending appeal (*PT Bakrie Investindo v Global Distressed Alpha Fund 1 Ltd Partnership* [2013] 4 SLR 1116 at [21]–[23], [33]). An applicant without notice must give full and frank disclosure (*The "Vasiliy Golovnin"* [2008] 4 SLR(R) 994 at [83]–[110]).

**A** [BB Ltd knows only of Mr G's DBS account] (PYP23 Q4.3): apply by summons without notice for an order that Mr G attend court to be examined on his assets and file an affidavit of assets, producing [bank statements, property and share records for the last [x] years]. Use Form 41, annex the PD 145 questionnaire (tailored to the facts), and serve personally. If [Mr G] answers the questionnaire fully by affidavit before the hearing, the order may be discharged by agreement without his attendance (PD 145(4)). If [D] is a company (e.g. [D1, with its sole director D2 in Australia]: PYP21 Q9), name D2 as the officer to be examined, and seek permission to serve him abroad (*Burgundy*). If [D] has obtained a stay pending appeal, the examination is stayed too (O 22 r 11(4)); so Doctor T's belief that appealing alone keeps his asset information safe is wrong unless a stay is actually ordered (PYP25 Q4.3). **C** Therefore [Client] should apply under O 22 r 11 for [D] [and its officer] to be examined and to file an affidavit of assets, by a Form 41 order served personally with the PD 145 questionnaire.

*Source note: O 22 r 11 ROC p264–265; PD 145 SCPD p183, read with the current text (epd2021, 15 Jul 2026: Forms B41/B42 and new PD 145(4)); SYL p29 [163]; L5 s3–s4; Burgundy and PT Bakrie L5 s4 and SYL p29 [163]; Vasiliy Golovnin SYL p29 [163(a)(i)]; tested PYP21 Q9, PYP22 Q7, PYP23 Q4.3, PYP25 Q4.3.*

### drp-w-0-69 — Mareva injunction after judgment, in aid of execution
*Keywords: post-judgment Mareva, Mareva in aid of execution, freezing order after judgment, JTrust Asia v Group Lease Holdings, real risk of dissipation, frustrate enforcement, aid execution not oppress, interests of justice, judgment creditor*

**I** The issue is whether [Client], as judgment creditor, can freeze [D]'s assets to stop [D] from putting them beyond reach before enforcement. **C** Yes. The Court may grant a Mareva injunction after judgment in aid of execution, where there is a real risk that [D] will dissipate assets to frustrate enforcement (*JTrust Asia Pte Ltd v Group Lease Holdings Pte Ltd* [2021] SGCA 26 at [21]–[24]).
**R** A Mareva injunction may be obtained after judgment as an aid to execution (SYL [150(d)], [164]). Because the claimant already has judgment, the merits limb is satisfied; the Court looks at whether there is a real risk of dissipation that would frustrate enforcement of the judgment, whether the injunction would aid execution rather than oppress the debtor, and whether it is in the interests of justice (*JTrust* at [21]–[24]). The procedure is the same as for a pre-judgment Mareva (O 13 r 1; PD 71–73; drp-w-0-59).

> **Rule in full**
> - Power: SCJA First Schedule para 5(c) (preservation of assets for satisfaction of a judgment "which has been or may be made"); CLA s 4(10).
> - Procedure: O 13 r 1(1)–(7), Forms 24/25, heard by a Judge if contested (PD 72(1)), PD 73 affidavit, full and frank disclosure (drp-w-0-57, 0-59).
> - Principles (*JTrust* at [21]–[24], as summarised in the seniors' notes):
>   - a real risk that the judgment debtor will dissipate assets so as to deprive the creditor of the fruits of the judgment;
>   - the order must aid execution, not oppress the debtor;
>   - it must be in the interests of justice.

**P** The principles for granting a Mareva injunction after judgment, as an aid to execution, are set out in *JTrust Asia Pte Ltd v Group Lease Holdings Pte Ltd* [2021] SGCA 26 at [21]–[24] (decided under ROC 2014).

**A** [The appeal was dismissed and D1 ignores demands; D1's assets are mostly abroad and its director has left Singapore] (PYP21 Q9). The judgment satisfies the merits. Show a real risk of dissipation: [D1 is a shell, its accounts are deteriorating, and it has ignored the judgment]. Frame the order to aid execution: freeze assets up to the judgment sum plus interest and costs, pair it with an asset disclosure order or an O 22 r 11 examination (drp-w-0-68), and move quickly to an enforcement order (drp-w-0-67). A worldwide order needs stronger justification. **C** Therefore [Client] should apply to a Judge without notice for a post-judgment Mareva injunction up to [$X], together with an asset disclosure order.

*Source note: SYL p24 [150(d)], p29 [164]; SCJA First Schedule para 5(c) SCJA p92; JTrust SYL p29 [164], three requirements from Cyril-Exam ("Post-judgment Mareva") (secondary); tested PYP21 Q9 (as an option).*

### drp-w-0-70 — Stay of enforcement, including pending appeal (O 22 r 13; SCJA ss 45(1), 60C)
*Keywords: stay of enforcement, stay of execution, O 22 r 13, special case, inappropriate to enforce immediately, stay pending appeal, appeal not a stay, O 18 r 6, O 19 r 6, SCJA s 45(1), SCJA s 60C, Lee Kuan Yew v Jeyaretnam Joshua Benjamin, Axis Megalink v Far East Mining, special circumstances, appeal rendered nugatory, no reasonable prospect of recovery, stay for specified period, withdraw seizure, examination of enforcement respondent stayed*

**I** The issue is whether [D] can stop [Client] enforcing the judgment while [D] appeals. **C** Filing an appeal does not stay enforcement (O 19 r 6; s 45(1) SCJA); [D] must apply for a stay, which will be granted only if [D] shows special circumstances, such as that [Client] could not repay the judgment sum if the appeal succeeded.
**R** An appeal does not operate as a stay of enforcement unless the lower or appellate Court so orders (O 18 r 6; O 19 r 6; SCJA ss 45(1), 60C(1)). A party liable under a Court order may apply to stay enforcement, or an enforcement order, if there is a special case making it inappropriate to enforce the order immediately (O 22 r 13(1)); the Court may stay for a specified period or until a specified event, and direct the Sheriff to withdraw or maintain any seizure or attachment (O 22 r 13(2)–(3)). A stay pending appeal may be sought under ss 45(1) and 60C of the SCJA.

> **Rule in full**
> - O 22 r 13(1): the party liable under any Court order may apply for stay of enforcement or stay of any enforcement order or any part of it if there is a special case making it inappropriate to enforce the Court order immediately.
> - O 22 r 13(2): the Court may order a stay for a specified period or until the occurrence of a specified event.
> - O 22 r 13(3): where the Sheriff has seized properties or attached a debt before the stay, the order may direct the Sheriff to withdraw the seizure or attachment or to maintain it without further action.
> - O 18 r 6(1) / O 19 r 6(1): except so far as the lower Court or the appellate Court directs, an appeal does not operate as a stay of enforcement; (3) interest runs for any delay caused.
> - SCJA s 45(1): an appeal to the Appellate Division does not operate as a stay unless the General Division or the Appellate Division so orders; s 60C(1): the same for an appeal to the Court of Appeal.
> - O 22 r 11(4): an examination of the enforcement respondent is enforcement, so a stay covers it (drp-w-0-68).
> - A stay application needs no prior approval (O 9 r 9(7)(j)).

**P** An appeal does not operate as a stay, and the successful party should not be deprived of the fruits of its judgment; the applicant must show special circumstances, something distinctive and out of the way, which usually means a real (not speculative) risk that a successful appeal would be rendered nugatory, for example because there is no reasonable prospect of recovering the money paid or the judgment creditor is likely to become insolvent; a mere offer to pay the judgment sum into court is not enough, and the merits of the appeal are generally irrelevant (*Axis Megalink Sdn Bhd v Far East Mining Pte Ltd* [2024] SGHC 47 at [7]–[11]; *Lee Kuan Yew v Jeyaretnam Joshua Benjamin* [1990] 1 SLR(R) 772 at [6]). Where special circumstances are shown, the Court may grant a stay on conditions, such as payment of the judgment sum into court or to the creditor's solicitors as stakeholders (*Axis Megalink* at [12]–[13], [37]).

**A** [Doctor T believes that appealing alone keeps his assets and asset information safe] (PYP25 Q4.3): that is wrong, because an appeal does not stay enforcement, and [Mr X] may obtain an enforcement order and an examination order meanwhile. To protect himself, Doctor T must apply for a stay, showing special circumstances, e.g. [Mr X is impecunious and could not repay $500,000 if the appeal succeeds]; a wish to avoid paying, or a belief that the appeal is strong, is not enough. If a stay is granted, it also stays any O 22 r 11 examination, and the Court may direct the Sheriff to maintain attachments without further action, or order [the judgment sum paid into court or to Mr X's solicitors as stakeholders] as a condition (*Axis Megalink* at [12]–[13]). An offer to pay into court does not by itself justify a stay (*Axis Megalink* at [10]). **C** Therefore [D] must apply for a stay under [s 45(1) SCJA / O 22 r 13], and will obtain one only by showing special circumstances; until then [Client] may enforce.

*Source note: O 22 r 13 ROC p265–266; O 18 r 6 ROC p156–157; O 19 r 6 ROC p192; SCJA p52 (s 45(1)), p62 (s 60C(1)); O 9 r 9(7)(j) ROC p87; SYL p29 [165]; L5 s2; Lee Kuan Yew v Jeyaretnam L5 s2 and SYL p29 [165], "special circumstances" from Cyril-Exam ("Stay of enforcement") and Nic-Muggers 12.3 (secondary); Axis Megalink [2024] SGHC 47 (Goh Yihan J) at [7]–[13], [37] read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026); a conditional stay was granted there on payment to the creditor's solicitors as stakeholders; tested PYP25 Q4.3.*

## 0.XIV Costs

- **Basic terms**
  - Order 21 governs costs of contentious business (business in or for proceedings before a court or arbitrator) (O 21 r 1; LPA s 2(1); *Legis Point*) → [0-71](#drp-w-0-71)
  - Solicitor-and-client costs vs party-and-party costs; P&P recovery capped at what the client owes its own solicitor (LPA s 112(2)) → [0-71](#drp-w-0-71)
- **Who pays?**
  - No costs without a court order (unless legislation or contract); fixed by the court that heard the matter; payable from the order (O 21 rr 2(3)–(4), 3(1)) → [0-72](#drp-w-0-72)
  - Costs in the Court's discretion, at any stage, having regard to the O 21 r 2(2) factors (O 21 r 2(1)–(2)) → [0-72](#drp-w-0-72)
  - Costs follow the event: the successful party gets costs unless some other order is appropriate (O 21 r 3(2); *Comfort Management*) → [0-72](#drp-w-0-72)
  - Amendment without permission and extensions of time: the party seeking them pays (O 21 r 3(3)–(4)) → [0-72](#drp-w-0-72)
- **Can the winner lose its costs?**
  - Failed issues that added time or cost; unreasonable conduct; no amicable resolution under Order 5; breach of an order, protocol or PD (O 21 r 4; PD 53; *Tullio Planeta*, *De Beers*, *Lim Lie Hoa*, *Raffles Town Club*) → [0-73](#drp-w-0-73)
- **Costs against the lawyer personally**
  - Costs incurred unreasonably: disallow, repay client, indemnify other party; hearing first (O 21 r 6; *Nagaenthran*); fictitious authorities and AI misuse (*Tan Hai Peng Micheal*) → [0-74](#drp-w-0-74)
- **An offer was made and beaten (or not)**
  - No formal offer-to-settle regime in ROC 2021; Order 5 offers revealed at the costs stage and weighed under O 21 rr 2(2)(a), 4 → [0-75](#drp-w-0-75)
- **Unpaid costs order**
  - Court may stay or dismiss the defaulter's application, action or appeal (O 21 r 2(6)) → [0-72](#drp-w-0-72)
- **Costs without an order, against a non-party, set-off, assessment? (background only — not examinable per SYL p1)**
  - Costs under legislation or contract (O 21 rr 3(3)–(5), 9); plead an indemnity costs clause (*Abani Trading*) → [0-83](#drp-w-0-83)
  - Non-party funder or assignee (O 21 r 5); set-off, Bullock or Sanderson order (O 21 r 2(5)) → [0-83](#drp-w-0-83)
  - Fixed or assessed; standard or indemnity basis; interest at 5.33%; bill within 12 months; review within 14 days (O 21 rr 2(3), 10, 17–29) → [0-83](#drp-w-0-83)

### drp-w-0-71 — Costs of contentious business; solicitor-and-client and party-and-party costs
*Keywords: costs, contentious business, Legal Profession Act s 2(1), proceedings before a court or arbitrator, Order 21, O 21 r 1, costs includes fees charges disbursements, solicitor-and-client costs, S&C costs, party-and-party costs, P&P costs, LPA s 112(2), cannot recover more than owed to own solicitor, Legis Point LLC v Tay Choon Ai, pre-action work, Maryani Sadeli v Arjun Permanand Samtani*

**I** The issue is what costs [Client] may recover from [D] if it succeeds, and how they relate to [Client]'s own legal fees. **C** [Client] may recover party-and-party costs from [D] under a costs order (O 21 r 3(1)), which will not cover all of its solicitor-and-client costs, and can never exceed what [Client] owes its own solicitor (s 112(2) LPA).
**R** Order 21 applies to the costs of or incidental to contentious business, which means business done in or for the purposes of proceedings begun before a court or an arbitrator (O 21 r 1(1)–(2); s 2(1) LPA); "costs" includes fees, charges, disbursements, expenses and remuneration. Solicitor-and-client costs are those a party pays its own solicitor; party-and-party costs are those one party pays another under a costs order. A client may not recover from another person, under a costs order, more than the amount it must pay its own solicitor under their agreement (s 112(2) LPA).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 21 r 1(1): the Order applies to the costs of or incidental to contentious business, and to other proceedings where written law provides or the parties consent.
> - O 21 r 1(2): "contentious business" has the meaning in s 2(1) LPA; "costs" includes fees, charges, disbursements, expenses and remuneration.
> - LPA s 2(1): "contentious business" means business done in or for the purposes of proceedings begun before a court of justice or before an arbitrator.
> - S&C costs: payable by a party to its own solicitor for work done in a contentious or non-contentious matter.
> - P&P costs: payable by one party to another party to the proceedings.
> - LPA s 112(2): the client is not entitled to recover from any other person, under any order for payment of costs which are the subject of a costs agreement, more than the amount payable by the client to its own solicitor (or law corporation or LLP) under the agreement.

**P** "Contentious business" includes work done before the writ was filed, such as drafting and pre-action negotiations, and post-writ mediation (*Legis Point LLC v Tay Choon Ai* [2018] 3 SLR 1269 at [79]–[84]). Full recovery of one's own costs from the other side is the exception, not the rule (*Maryani Sadeli v Arjun Permanand Samtani* [2015] 1 SLR 496 at [34], cited in the costs lecture).

**A** Tell [Client] at the outset (and in the engagement letter: drp-w-0-5) that even if it wins it will usually recover only part of its legal spend as P&P costs, fixed by the Court (drp-w-0-72); its pre-action work [letters of demand, the Order 5 offer] is contentious business and can form part of the costs claimed; and it cannot recover from [D] more than it owes its own firm under any fee agreement (s 112(2)). **C** Therefore [Client]'s recoverable costs are party-and-party costs under a court order, capped by what it owes its own solicitors.

*Source note: O 21 r 1(1)–(2) ROC p230; LPA p15 (s 2(1)), p270–271 (s 112) (informal consolidation in force from 17 Apr 2025); SYL p30 [166]–[167]; L6 s4–s6; Legis Point SYL p30 [166] and L6 s4 (L6 cites [73]–[84], [89]–[96]); Maryani Sadeli L6 s6 (not in SYL).*

### drp-w-0-72 — Entitlement to costs under a court order: discretion, factors, costs follow the event
*Keywords: entitlement to costs, costs order needed, O 21 r 3(1), costs in the discretion of the Court, O 21 r 2(1), any stage, factors, O 21 r 2(2), amicable resolution efforts, complexity, proportionality, conduct, stage concluded, fixed by the Court which heard the matter, O 21 r 2(3), costs schedule, assessment, payable from the date of order, O 21 r 2(4), set-off, unpaid costs stay or dismiss, O 21 r 2(6), costs follow the event, successful party, O 21 r 3(2), Comfort Management v OGSP Engineering, amendment costs, O 21 r 3(3), extension of time costs, O 21 r 3(4)*

**I** The issue is whether [Client], having [won the application / succeeded at trial], is entitled to costs from [D]. **C** Yes. Costs are in the Court's discretion, but the Court must order costs in favour of the successful party unless some other order is appropriate (O 21 r 3(2)); [Client] is the successful party because [it obtained judgment for $X], and the Court will fix the costs having regard to the factors in O 21 r 2(2).
**R** Except where legislation or contract provides otherwise, no party may recover costs from another without a court order (O 21 r 3(1)). Costs are in the Court's discretion, which it may exercise at any stage or after the proceedings (O 21 r 2(1)), having regard to all relevant circumstances including the factors in O 21 r 2(2). Costs must be fixed by the Court which heard the matter unless it directs assessment (O 21 r 2(3)), and are payable from the date of the order unless agreed otherwise (O 21 r 2(4)). The Court must order costs in favour of a successful party unless in the circumstances some other order should be made (O 21 r 3(2)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 21 r 2(1): subject to written law, costs are in the discretion of the Court, which may determine all issues of costs at any stage of the proceedings or after their conclusion.
> - O 21 r 2(2): in fixing or assessing costs, the Court must have regard to all relevant circumstances, including
>   - (a) efforts made by the parties at amicable resolution;
>   - (b) the complexity of the case and the difficulty or novelty of the questions involved;
>   - (c) the skill, specialised knowledge and responsibility required of, and the time and labour expended by, the solicitor;
>   - (d) the urgency and importance of the action to the parties;
>   - (e) the number of solicitors involved for each party;
>   - (f) the conduct of the parties;
>   - (g) the principle of proportionality; and
>   - (h) the stage at which the proceedings were concluded.
> - O 21 r 2(3): costs payable by one party to another must be fixed by the Court which heard the matter, after an oral hearing or written submissions, unless it directs an assessment.
> - O 21 r 2(4): costs are payable from the date of the order unless the parties otherwise agree.
> - O 21 r 2(5): the Court may set off parties' costs against each other.
> - O 21 r 2(6): if a party refuses or neglects to pay costs ordered within the time specified, the Court may stay or dismiss any application, action or appeal or make any other order, whether the costs were ordered in the present or related proceedings.
> - O 21 r 3(1): subject to the Order, no party is entitled to recover any costs from another except under an order of the Court.
> - O 21 r 3(2): the Court must, subject to the Order, order the costs of any proceedings in favour of a successful party, except when it appears that some other order should be made as to the whole or part of the costs.
> - O 21 r 3(3): the costs of and occasioned by an amendment made without permission are borne by the amending party unless the Court otherwise orders.
> - O 21 r 3(4): the costs of an application to extend time are borne by the applicant unless the Court otherwise orders.

**P** The "successful party" is identified in substance and reality, by asking who has won; in money claims this usually turns on the net result, and in other claims on whether the claimant obtained something it could not have had without the litigation (*Comfort Management Pte Ltd v OGSP Engineering Pte Ltd* [2022] SGHC 77 at [28]–[39]). The "event" is construed pragmatically (*Alfro-Asia Shipping v Da Zhong Investment* [2004] 3 SLR(R) 274 at [20], cited in the costs lecture).

**A** Identify the event: [Client obtained summary judgment for the full $350,000 / succeeded on the claim but lost on interest]. On the usual rule, [Client] gets costs (O 21 r 3(2)); if success is mixed, the Court may apportion (drp-w-0-73). Ask the Court that heard the matter to fix costs at the end of the hearing, citing the r 2(2) factors ([a simple debt claim resolved at an early stage, so moderate costs]; [D's conduct in raising a sham defence]). Note the special rules: [Client] bears the costs of its own amendment without permission and of any extension it sought (r 3(3)–(4)). If [D] does not pay costs ordered, ask the Court to stay or dismiss [D]'s further applications or appeal (r 2(6)). **C** Therefore [Client], as the successful party, should be awarded its costs of [the application / the action], fixed by the Court.

*Source note: O 21 rr 2–3 ROC p230–232; SYL p30–31 [168(c)(i)–(ii)]; L6 s9–s12, s16, s18; Comfort Management SYL p31 [168(c)(ii)(2)], proposition from Cyril-Exam ("Costs") (secondary); Alfro-Asia L6 s12 (not in SYL); tested PYP21 Q1, Q7 (costs implications asked in every 2021–2022 question).*

### drp-w-0-73 — Departing from the usual order: O 21 r 4 and unreasonable refusal of ADR
*Keywords: adverse costs order against successful party, O 21 r 4, disallow or reduce costs, failed to establish claim or issue, unnecessarily increasing time costs complexity, unreasonable conduct, duty to consider amicable resolution, Order 5, refusal of ADR, refusing mediation, PD 53, non-compliance with order or pre-action protocol or practice direction, issue-based costs, Tullio Planeta v Maoro Andrew G, MCST Plan No 473 v De Beers Jewelry, Lim Lie Hoa v Ong Jane Rebecca, Raffles Town Club v Lim Eng Hock Peter, departures exceptional, indemnity costs*

**I** The issue is whether [Client], although it won, may be deprived of some of its costs, or ordered to pay [D]'s, because [it refused mediation / it lost on two issues / it breached a court order]. **C** Yes. Under O 21 r 4 the Court may disallow or reduce a successful party's costs, or order it to pay costs, where [it unreasonably failed to engage in amicable resolution under Order 5 (r 4(c)) / it raised issues it failed to establish, unnecessarily increasing time and costs (r 4(a))]; such departures are exceptional.
**R** The Court may disallow or reduce a successful party's costs, or order it to pay costs, if it failed to establish a claim or issue it raised and thereby unnecessarily increased the time, costs or complexity of the proceedings; did or omitted anything unreasonably; did not discharge its duty to consider amicable resolution or make an offer under Order 5; or failed to comply with a court order, a relevant pre-action protocol or a practice direction (O 21 r 4). Solicitors should advise clients on potential adverse costs orders for unreasonable refusal to engage in ADR (PD 53(6)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 21 r 4: the Court may disallow or reduce a successful party's costs or order that party to pay costs, if
>   - (a) that party has failed to establish any claim or issue which that party has raised in any proceedings, thereby unnecessarily increasing the amount of time taken, the costs or the complexity of the proceedings;
>   - (b) that party has done or omitted to do anything unreasonably;
>   - (c) that party has not discharged that party's duty to consider amicable resolution of the dispute or to make an offer of amicable resolution in accordance with Order 5; or
>   - (d) that party has failed to comply with any order of court, any relevant pre-action protocol or any practice direction.
> - PD 53(2): it is the professional duty of solicitors to advise clients to consider ADR and to give sufficient information about ADR options (r 17(2)(e) PCR).
> - PD 53(4): ADR must be considered before and during any action or appeal.
> - PD 53(6): attention is drawn to O 21 r 4(c); solicitors should advise their clients on potential adverse costs orders for any unreasonable refusal to engage in ADR.
> - O 5 r 3(3)–(4): a party refusing ADR may be ordered to give reasons in a sealed document, opened on costs (drp-w-0-7).

**P** Departures from the rule that costs follow the event are exceptional (*Tullio Planeta v Maoro Andrew G* [1994] 2 SLR(R) 501 at [21]–[24]). A successful party who loses on one ground is not penalised if that did not increase the time or costs (*Management Corporation Strata Title Plan No 473 v De Beers Jewelry Pte Ltd* [2002] 1 SLR(R) 418 at [57]–[58]). The factors for departing are not exhaustive (*Lim Lie Hoa v Ong Jane Rebecca* [2005] 3 SLR(R) 116 at [60]–[64]), and a party who pursues unsustainable issues at length or misleads the court may be penalised in costs (*Raffles Town Club Pte Ltd v Lim Eng Hock Peter* [2011] 1 SLR 582 at [34]–[38]). All four were decided under ROC 2014.

**A** Run each limb. (a) [Client] won on liability but [pursued a fraud allegation for three trial days and failed]: the Court may disallow the costs of that issue (*De Beers* if it added nothing to time or cost). (b) [Client] [refused to agree an obviously sensible extension / filed unnecessary affidavits]: unreasonable conduct. (c) [Client] [rejected D's mediation proposal without reasons / made no pre-action offer]: the Court may reduce its costs or order it to pay some of [D]'s; check the sealed reasons and the Order 5 correspondence. (d) [Client] [missed directions]. Then weigh: departures are exceptional (*Tullio Planeta*), so expect a percentage reduction rather than reversal unless the conduct was serious. Advise [Client] now to engage with ADR offers to avoid this risk. **C** Therefore the Court [may reduce [Client]'s costs by [x]% / is unlikely to depart from the usual order].

*Source note: O 21 r 4 ROC p232–233; PD 53(2), (4), (6) SCPD p54; SYL p31 [168(c)(ii)(2)]; L6 s13–s15; Tullio Planeta L6 s14–s15; De Beers, Lim Lie Hoa and Raffles Town Club SYL p31, propositions from Cyril-Exam ("Costs") (secondary); Conflict: Cyril-Exam cites Tullio Planeta for the point that an arbitration agreement alone is not enough to deprive a party of costs, while Nic-Muggers 13.3.3 attributes indemnity costs for ignoring an arbitration clause to "Tjong Very Sumito"; the pack relies on Tullio Planeta only for "departures are exceptional", which the course deck states (L6 s14–s15), and on neither senior's arbitration point.*

### drp-w-0-74 — Personal costs orders against solicitors (O 21 r 6)
*Keywords: personal costs order, wasted costs, adverse costs order against solicitor, O 21 r 6, costs incurred unreasonably, disallow solicitor-and-client costs, repay client, indemnify other party, reasonable opportunity to be heard, notice to client, Attorney-General, Nagaenthran a/l K Dharmalingam v Attorney-General, unmeritorious applications, abuse of process, fictitious authorities, AI tools, hallucinated cases, Tan Hai Peng Micheal v Tan Cheong Joo, Ridehalgh v Horsefield, non-delegable duty to verify citations*

**I** The issue is whether [D's solicitor] may be ordered to pay costs personally for [citing non-existent authorities generated by an AI tool / bringing a hopeless application]. **C** Yes. Under O 21 r 6 the Court may order a solicitor who incurred costs unreasonably to [indemnify [Client] for the costs wasted / repay his own client], after giving the solicitor a reasonable opportunity to be heard.
**R** If a solicitor is responsible, personally or through an employee or agent, for incurring costs unreasonably, the Court may disallow the costs between solicitor and client, order the solicitor to repay costs the client was ordered to pay, or order the solicitor to indemnify any other party for costs payable by them (O 21 r 6(1)). The Court must first give the solicitor a reasonable opportunity to be heard (O 21 r 6(2)), may direct notice to the client (O 21 r 6(3)), and may involve the Attorney-General (O 21 r 6(4)).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 21 r 6(1): if the solicitor is responsible, either personally or through an employee or agent, for incurring costs unreasonably in the proceedings, the Court may
>   - (a) disallow the costs as between the solicitor and his or her client in whole or in part;
>   - (b) order the solicitor to repay to his or her client costs which the client has been ordered to pay in the proceedings; and
>   - (c) order the solicitor to indemnify any other party in the proceedings for costs payable by them.
> - O 21 r 6(2): before making an order, the Court must give the solicitor a reasonable opportunity to be heard, orally or by written submissions.
> - O 21 r 6(3): the Court may direct notice to the solicitor's client.
> - O 21 r 6(4): the Court may direct or authorise the Attorney-General to take part, and order the Attorney-General's costs.

**P** Personal costs may be ordered against counsel who bring unmeritorious applications amounting to an abuse of process (*Nagaenthran a/l K Dharmalingam v Attorney-General* [2022] SGCA 44). Where fictitious authorities were cited in closing submissions, the Court, applying O 21 r 6 and the three-stage test in *Ridehalgh v Horsefield* (improper or unreasonable conduct, unnecessary costs caused, and whether it is just to order the solicitor to pay), held that the solicitor on record had a non-delegable duty to verify the authorities, and ordered him and the lawyer who drafted the submissions each to pay $5,000 personally on an indemnity basis; it did not refer them to the Law Society but warned that similar conduct may lead to disciplinary action (*Tan Hai Peng Micheal v Tan Cheong Joo* [2026] SGHC 49 at [34]–[66], [84]).

**A** Identify the unreasonable conduct and whose it was: [D's solicitor cited two cases that do not exist, produced by an AI tool and never checked], wasting [Client]'s time in researching and responding; or [the solicitor filed a hopeless last-minute application to delay execution]. That is conduct for which the solicitor is personally responsible, not the client. Ask the Court for an order under r 6(1)(c) that the solicitor indemnify [Client] for the wasted costs, and note that the Court must first hear the solicitor (r 6(2)). Professional conduct consequences may follow separately (the Ethics paper). **C** Therefore the Court may order [D's solicitor] personally to pay [Client]'s wasted costs under O 21 r 6(1)(c), after hearing the solicitor.

*Source note: O 21 r 6 ROC p233–234; SYL p31 [168(c)(ii)(4)]; Nagaenthran SYL p31, proposition from Cyril-Exam ("Costs") (secondary); Tan Hai Peng Micheal v Tan Cheong Joo [2026] SGHC 49 (S Mohan J, 6 Mar 2026) read on eLitigation (8 Nov 2026) via an extraction subagent; the syllabus spells the parties "Tan Hai Peng Michael" and "Tan Cheong Koo", the judgment "Micheal" and "Joo". The Ridehalgh three-stage test is the pack's summary of [39].*

### drp-w-0-75 — Offers to settle and costs under ROC 2021
*Keywords: offer to settle, Form 33, Order 22A, ROC 2014, beat the offer, offer of amicable resolution, O 5 r 2(3), terms not revealed until costs, without prejudice save as to costs, O 21 r 2(2)(a), efforts at amicable resolution, O 21 r 4(b), O 21 r 4(c), unreasonable rejection, O 5 r 1(4), payment into court, Order 14, Form 27, accept within 14 days*

**I** The issue is what costs consequences follow from [D's unaccepted offer to settle at $400,000, when judgment was for $350,000]. **C** ROC 2021 has no fixed offer-to-settle costs regime of the kind in ROC 2014 O 22A; instead, [D]'s offer is an offer of amicable resolution that the Court will consider when deciding costs, and [Client]'s unreasonable rejection of an offer better than the judgment may lead the Court to reduce its costs or order it to pay [D]'s costs from the date of the offer (O 5 rr 1(4), 2(3); O 21 rr 2(2)(a), 4(b)–(c)).
**R** An offer of amicable resolution must be in writing, open for at least 14 days, and its terms may not be revealed to the Court until it has decided the merits and is dealing with costs (O 5 r 2(1)–(3)); a party must not reject an offer without reasonable grounds (O 5 r 1(4)). In fixing costs the Court must consider the parties' efforts at amicable resolution (O 21 r 2(2)(a)), and may disallow or reduce a successful party's costs, or order it to pay costs, for unreasonable conduct or failure to comply with Order 5 (O 21 r 4(b)–(c)). Separately, a defendant may pay money into court in satisfaction of a debt or damages claim, which the claimant may accept within 14 days (O 14 rr 1, 3); the payment must not be disclosed to the trial court until liability and quantum are decided (O 14 r 7).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 5 r 1(3): an offer of amicable resolution includes an offer to settle the action or appeal, in whole or in part.
> - O 5 r 1(4): a party must not reject an offer of amicable resolution unless it has reasonable grounds to do so.
> - O 5 r 2(1)–(4): in writing; open at least 14 days; terms not relied on or made known to the Court until the merits are decided and the Court is dealing with costs; an offer with no expiry date lapses when the merits are decided unless stated otherwise.
> - O 21 r 2(2)(a): in fixing or assessing costs the Court must have regard to efforts made by the parties at amicable resolution.
> - O 21 r 4(b)–(c): the Court may disallow or reduce a successful party's costs, or order it to pay costs, if it acted unreasonably or did not discharge its Order 5 duties.
> - O 14 r 1(1)–(2): in an action for a debt or damages, a defendant may, after filing its notice of intention to contest or not contest, pay into Court a sum in satisfaction of the cause(s) of action, giving notice in Form 27.
> - O 14 r 3(1): the claimant may accept within 14 days after receiving the notice (before trial) by notice in Form 28; on acceptance, the proceedings on that cause of action are stayed (r 3(4)).
> - O 14 r 7: the payment into court must not be pleaded or disclosed to the trial court until liability and amount have been decided.
> - The ROC 2021, as amended to 1 Apr 2026, contain no provision fixing costs consequences for beating or failing to beat an offer to settle: the only offers they regulate are offers of amicable resolution under Order 5, and payments into court under Order 14.

**P** No case on offers under ROC 2021 is set in the syllabus. PYP21 Q7 (a ROC 2014 paper) turned on the Form 33 offer to settle under the old O 22A; under the 2026 syllabus the same facts are analysed through Order 5 and O 21 rr 2(2)(a) and 4.

**A** Compare the offer with the result: [D offered US$400,000 three months before trial; judgment was for US$350,000]. [Client] did worse at trial than the offer, so [D] will reveal the offer at the costs stage (O 5 r 2(3)) and argue that [Client] rejected it without reasonable grounds (O 5 r 1(4)), so that [Client], though the successful party, should have its post-offer costs disallowed or pay [D]'s post-offer costs (O 21 r 4(b)–(c)). [Client] will answer that [it had reasonable grounds: e.g. the offer excluded interest and costs, or was made before key documents were produced]. The Court has a discretion, not a fixed formula. If [D] had paid money into court instead, [Client] could have accepted within 14 days and stayed the action (O 14 r 3). **C** Therefore the offer does not automatically shift costs, but the Court is likely to reduce [Client]'s costs, or order it to pay [D]'s, for the period after the offer if the rejection was unreasonable.

*Source note: O 5 rr 1–2 ROC p67; O 21 rr 2(2), 4 ROC p230–233; O 14 rr 1, 3, 7 ROC p123–128; not a syllabus paragraph; added because PYP21 Q7 tested offers to settle (ROC 2014 O 22A); the current ROC on SSO (as at 1 Apr 2026, last amended by S 174/2026) was searched on 8 Nov 2026 and has no "offer to settle" provision outside Order 5; neither the syllabus nor the seniors' notes mention one.*

### drp-w-0-83 — Costs without a court order, non-party costs, set-off (Bullock and Sanderson), fixed or assessed costs, basis and assessment (background only — not examinable per SYL p1)
*Keywords: costs under legislation, costs under contract, indemnity costs clause, Abani Trading, Telemedia Pacific, O 21 r 3(3)–(5), O 21 r 9, discontinuance costs, payment into court, costs against non-party, funder, O 21 r 5, set-off of costs, O 21 r 2(5), Bullock order, Sanderson order, Grains and Industrial Products Trading, fixed costs, O 21 r 10, Appendix 1, costs schedule, PD 137, Costs Guidelines, PD 138, Appendix G, payable from date of order, O 21 r 2(4), standard basis, indemnity basis, O 21 r 22, QBE Insurance v Relax Beach, interest on costs, 5.33%, O 21 r 29, bill of costs, 12 months, O 21 r 17, Notice of Dispute, PD 133, Section 1 2 3 costs, Lin Jian Wei v Lim Eng Hock Peter, review by Judge 14 days, O 21 r 27, Shorvon, solicitor and client assessment, LPA s 120, O 21 r 23, background only*

**I** The issue is [whether [Client] can recover costs from [non-party funder X] / how costs should be ordered where [Client] succeeded against only one of two defendants / on what basis [Client]'s costs will be assessed]. **C** [The Court may order costs against X under O 21 r 5 / The Court may make a Bullock or Sanderson order under O 21 r 2(5) / Costs will be assessed on the [standard / indemnity] basis under O 21 r 22].
**R** Costs are recoverable without a court order only where legislation or a contract provides (O 21 r 3(1); e.g. O 21 rr 3(3)–(5), 9). Where just, the Court may order costs against a non-party who took an assignment of, or funded, the claim for a share of the proceeds, or funded and actively encouraged it, after giving the non-party a chance to be heard (O 21 r 5). The Court may set off parties' costs against each other (O 21 r 2(5)); where a claimant reasonably sued two defendants and won against one, it may make a Bullock order (claimant pays the winning defendant and recovers that from the losing defendant) or a Sanderson order (losing defendant pays the winning defendant directly). Costs are fixed by the Court unless it directs assessment (O 21 r 2(3)); fixed sums apply in Appendix 1 cases (O 21 r 10). Assessment is on the standard basis unless the Court orders the indemnity basis (O 21 r 22(1), (4)). Costs carry interest at 5.33% a year (O 21 r 29).

> **Rule in full**
> - O 21 r 3(1): no party may recover costs from another except under a Court order (subject to the Order). Examples of costs without an order: r 3(3) amendment without permission (amending party bears costs); r 3(4) applications to extend time (applicant bears costs); r 3(5) trustee, personal representative or mortgagee (costs out of the fund); r 9 discontinuance or acceptance of a payment into Court (costs to the time of discontinuance or notice; judgment for assessed costs after 48 hours).
> - O 21 r 5(1): where it is just, the Court may order costs against a non-party who has
>   - (a) assigned its right in the action to a party for a share of any recovery;
>   - (b) contributed or agreed to contribute to a party's costs for a share of any recovery; or
>   - (c) contributed or agreed to contribute to a party's costs and actively instigates or encourages that party to continue.
> - O 21 r 5(2): the non-party must first have a reasonable opportunity to be heard, orally or in writing.
> - O 21 r 2(5): the Court may order 2 or more parties' costs to be set off so that only the balance is paid.
> - O 21 r 2(3): costs are fixed by the Court which heard the matter, after an oral hearing or written submissions, unless it directs assessment. O 21 r 10: in Appendix 1 cases, the amounts in that Appendix apply unless the Court otherwise orders. PD 137: costs schedules (Form 34) in trials of originating claims and OAs with cross-examination, filed with closing submissions. PD 138: Costs Guidelines (Appendix G) are a general guide only.
> - O 21 r 2(4): costs are payable from the date of the order unless otherwise agreed; r 2(6): non-payment may lead to a stay or dismissal.
> - O 21 r 22(1): assessment is on the standard basis unless the Court considers the indemnity basis appropriate. r 22(2): standard basis — a reasonable amount for costs reasonably incurred; doubts resolved for the paying party. r 22(3): indemnity basis — all costs except those unreasonable in amount or unreasonably incurred; doubts resolved for the receiving party. r 22(4): no basis stated → standard basis. r 22(5): a General Division claim within a State Court's jurisdiction recovers no more than State Court costs unless ordered otherwise.
> - O 21 r 29: assessed, fixed, agreed and Appendix 1 (Parts 1–3) costs carry interest at 5.33% a year from assessment, order, agreement or judgment.
> - Assessment procedure: bill of costs within 12 months after the whole matter (including appeals) is finally disposed of (r 17); at least 14 days' notice of the assessment (r 18); copy of the bill to every party entitled to be heard within 2 days (r 19); Notice of Dispute (Form B33) at least 14 days before the assessment date (PD 133); Registrar's certificate conclusive unless set aside (rr 25–26); review by a Judge within 14 days, generally without further evidence (rr 27–28).
> - Solicitor-and-client assessment: on order under LPA s 120 or by consent; assessed on the indemnity basis with presumptions in favour of costs the client approved (O 21 r 23(2)).

**P** An indemnity costs clause should generally be specifically pleaded (*Abani Trading Pte Ltd v BNP Paribas* [2014] 3 SLR 909 at [62]–[69]; *Telemedia Pacific Group Ltd v Credit Agricole (Suisse) SA* [2015] 4 SLR 1019 at [30]–[31] on when this is necessary). Bullock and Sanderson orders protect a claimant who reasonably did not know whom to pursue (*Grains and Industrial Products Trading Pte Ltd v Bank of India* [2016] 3 SLR 1308 at [144]–[149]). *QBE Insurance (Singapore) Pte Ltd v Relax Beach Co Ltd* [2023] SGCA 45 at [35]–[57] illustrates the factors for an indemnity costs order. *Lin Jian Wei v Lim Eng Hock Peter* [2011] 3 SLR 1052 ([2011] SGCA 29) at [27]–[30], [34]–[58], [78] is the leading authority on assessment. A review of the Registrar's assessment is heard de novo (*Shorvon Simon v Singapore Medical Council* [2006] 1 SLR(R) 182 at [21]–[22]). All but *QBE* were decided under ROC 2014.

**A** [Client sued A and B, unsure which caused the loss, and failed against A]: ask for a Bullock or Sanderson order so that A's costs are borne by B. [The claim was bankrolled by X for a 30% share]: seek costs against X under O 21 r 5(1)(b), after X is heard. [The contract has an indemnity costs clause]: plead it, and ask for assessment on the indemnity basis. If costs are to be assessed, file the bill within 12 months of final disposal and seek review within 14 days of the Registrar's decision. **C** Therefore [Client] should seek [a Bullock / Sanderson order / costs against X / indemnity costs] under O 21 r [2(5) / 5 / 22].

*Source note: O 21 rr 2, 3, 5, 9, 10 ROC p230–236; rr 17–19, 22, 23, 25–29 ROC p239–245; PD 133, 137, 138 SCPD p175–179; SYL p30–34 [168(a)–(b)], [168(c)(ii)(3), (5)], [169]–[174]; Nic-Muggers 13.2.3.4 (secondary, consistent on Bullock and Sanderson). Case holdings are as the syllabus states them; the judgments were not read. SYL prints Lin Jian Wei as "[2011] SLR 1052"; the volume (3) and neutral citation [2011] SGCA 29 were confirmed by the extraction subagent (18 Nov 2026) from case-citator listings. LPA ss 111, 120, 122, 126, 128 and Singapore Academy of Law Act s 25 are cited from SYL [168], [173]–[174] only, not read. ROC text checked against SSO (current version as at 18 Nov 2026; extraction subagent): unchanged since 2021; PD text from the library copy.*
